# China, Communism and Asia - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches how the oldest continuously governed state in the world lost the capacity to govern itself, how four different political forces tried to rebuild that capacity between 1911 and 1949, why the one that succeeded was the one nobody in 1921 would have predicted, and what that success then did to China, to Asia and to the idea of communism itself. It begins with a single question that organises everything that follows - not "why did communism win?" but "who could build a state?" - because the answer to the second question explains the first, and an answer that reverses the order will misread the whole century. It teaches the late Qing crisis completely: the population rise from 140 million to 410 million between 1741 and 1841, the subsistence failure and banditry that followed, the Opium Wars of 1839-42 and the cession of Hong Kong, the rights and concessions eventually held in about eighty ports and towns, the Taiping Rebellion of 1850-64 and the regional armies whose victory broke the dynasty's monopoly of force, the defeat by Japan in 1894-5 and the loss of Formosa, the leaseholds of 1898, the Boxer Rising of 1898-1900 and the indemnity, and the further losses that followed the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-5. It teaches the revolution of 1911 as a legitimacy collapse rather than a military conquest - the reforms that accelerated provincial separation, the rising at Wuchang in October 1911, the five-year-old emperor Puyi, and the bargain with Yuan Shikai that traded the dynasty for a military dictatorship lasting from 1912 to 1915 - and then the Twenty-One Demands of January 1915, the Warlord Era of 1916-28 and the intellectual awakening that the humiliation produced. It teaches the two parties in parallel: Sun Yat-sen's Three Principles and their internal weakness, the Soviet assistance and the Whampoa Military Academy of 1924, Chiang Kai-shek's leadership after Sun's death in 1925, the Chinese Communist Party's foundation in 1921 as a party of intellectuals, and why two organisations with opposite social bases co-operated at all. It teaches the Northern March from 1926, the captures of Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking by 1927 and of Beijing in 1928, the rupture of 1927 and the purification movement whose toll is recorded in the core account as estimated by some as high as 250,000, and then the rural turn that followed: the five extermination campaigns between 1930 and 1934, the Chinese Soviet Republic proclaimed at Juichin in 1931, the Long March of 1934-5 with its roughly 100,000 starters, 6,000 miles, 368 days and 20,000 survivors at Yenan, and the historiographical quarrel over what that march actually was. It teaches why the peasants moved, through the five recorded reasons - Nationalist inefficiency and corruption, unreformed factory conditions, unrelieved peasant poverty against a restrained communist land policy, the contested New Life Movement of 1934, and above all the absence of effective resistance to Japan - and then the Sian incident of 1936, the full-scale war from 1937, and the growth of the communist base areas from five controlling 12 million people in 1937 to nineteen controlling 100 million by 1945. It teaches the civil-war outcome without hindsight, including the recorded judgement that communist victory was still not inevitable in 1945, the Manchurian complication, the shift from guerrilla to conventional war in 1948, the capture of Beijing in January 1949, the flight to Taiwan and the proclamation at Tiananmen in October 1949. It then teaches the People's Republic as a problem of state formation: the land redistribution of 1950-6 and the sharply conflicting accounts of its violence, collectivisation reaching about 95 per cent of surviving peasants by 1956, nationalisation and the Five Year Plan of 1953, the Hundred Flowers campaign of 1957 and the clampdown that followed, the Great Leap Forward from 1958 with its communes of up to 75,000 people and its 600,000 backyard furnaces, the harvest failures of 1959-61 and the withdrawal of Soviet aid, and the mortality evidence handled with the attribution discipline that this topic requires above all others. It teaches the Cultural Revolution, the Party's own 1981 verdict on it, the Sino-Soviet split from the 1950 treaty through the deterioration after 1956, the charge of revisionism, the frontier claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang, the attack on Vietnam in February 1979, the three grievances of 1984 and the reconciliation of May 1989 - and the opening to the United States that ran alongside it, from the table-tennis invitation of 1971 and the United Nations seat in October 1971 to formal recognition early in 1979 and diplomatic relations effective on 1 January 1979. It teaches the Deng transition to the boundary this topic owns: the succession struggle, the policy changes from June 1978, the Democracy Wall of 1978-9, the household responsibility system, the procurement change of December 1984, the inflation of 1986, the crisis of 1987 and the Tiananmen crackdown of 3-4 June 1989 with its divergently recorded casualty estimates. It teaches Asian communism as four genuinely different histories rather than one export: Korea from the Japanese annexation and the Cairo promise of 1943 to the division at the 38th parallel and the dynastic state that followed; Vietnam from the unified communist party Ho Chi Minh helped to found in February 1930 through Dien Bien Phu in 1954, the elections that never took place, reunification in 1976 and the Doi Moi turn of December 1986; Cambodia from Sihanouk's neutrality through the coup of March 1970, the bombing, the Khmer Rouge years from April 1975 and the Vietnamese intervention of December 1978 to January 1979; and Laos, the quiet case that ended up posing the sharpest definitional question of all. It closes with the comparison to the Russian revolution, drawn as a genuine contrast rather than a false equivalence, and with the answer architecture that converts a century into marks. Four boundaries are held throughout and stated in the lessons: the Korean and Vietnam wars as Cold War theatres belong to the Cold War owner; the anti-colonial transfer of power belongs to the decolonisation owner; the post-1991 order belongs to the end-of-Cold-War owner; and the Russian revolution and Stalinist transformation belong to their own owner and are used here only for comparison.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of the chapters that supply most of the evidence, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter narrates the revolution, then the warlords, then the two parties, then the victory, then the People's Republic decade by decade. That arrangement produces the standard failing script: a chronology with a verdict bolted on at the end. Here the late Qing crisis comes first and is taught as a crisis of *capacity* rather than of morale, because every strong answer on this topic turns on the distinction between removing a government and building a state. The 1911 revolution then follows immediately, so that the reader meets the paradox at full strength - the dynasty fell easily and nothing was solved - before any ideology is introduced. The two parties are taught in adjacent lessons rather than merged, because their social bases were opposite and the reasons for their co-operation and rupture are examinable in themselves. The rural turn is separated from the Long March, because the strategy and the march are constantly confused: one was a decision about where a revolution's social base should be, the other was a retreat that later acquired a political meaning. The reasons the peasants moved come before the Japanese invasion, so that the invasion can be seen doing what it actually did - accelerating a shift that was already under way - rather than causing it from nothing.

After 1949 the sequence changes character. The state-formation lessons come first, because the achievements of the early 1950s are what make the catastrophes of the next two decades intelligible: the same capacity did both. The Hundred Flowers campaign is given its own lesson rather than a paragraph, because it is the hinge between two political styles and because its logic - invite criticism, receive more than expected, close it down - recurs in 1979, 1987 and 1989. The Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution are separated by two lessons, because merging them is the commonest factual error in the topic and because the intellectual quarrel that produced the second grew directly out of the failure of the first. The Sino-Soviet split is placed after both, because it cannot be understood until the reader knows what Mao meant by revisionism and why the charge mattered to him. The Deng transition comes next, then Tiananmen, and only then the rest of Asia - because the Asian cases are best read against a Chinese model the reader already knows well enough to see that none of them copied it. The comparison with Russia is last among the substantive lessons, because a comparison made too early becomes a template, and a template is the thing this topic most needs to avoid.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Four labels run through this session, and separating them matters more here than in almost any other topic on the syllabus, because this is a subject on which confident, fluent and entirely unsourced sentences are very easy to write.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the single core world-history account read for this session, the cross-topic owners consulted for boundaries, the verified question ledgers, or a checked item from the one verification pass recorded in the final ledger | Assert it. Dates, named individuals, institutions, recorded policies and stated outcomes can be written flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated official item, used exactly once in this session and limited there in the text itself | One dated sentence at most, and never as the substance of a history answer |
| **Attributed estimate** | A figure for deaths, killings or famine mortality, always carried with the name of whoever produced it | Attribute it in the sentence. Never state such a figure as an unowned fact |

That fourth label has no symbol because it must be visible in the sentence itself, and it is the single most important discipline in this topic. ⚠️ An answer that writes "the Great Leap Forward killed 45 million people" has made an assertion that no honest source makes in that form; an answer that writes "Frank Dikötter, working in archives opened around 2008, puts the premature deaths at at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962" has made a checkable statement and demonstrated exactly the historiographical awareness that a 20-mark question rewards.

Eight disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where the vocabulary is familiar, the narrative is dramatic, and precision is rare.

**The capacity discipline.** ⚠️ The organising question of the whole period from 1839 to 1949 is not which ideology deserved to win but which organisation could actually govern. ✅ The dynasty lost its monopoly of armed force to regional armies during the Taiping Rebellion; ✅ the republic of 1911 was captured within months by a general; ✅ the Warlord Era meant that provinces were governed by whoever commanded troops in them; ✅ the Nationalist government that reunified the country in 1928 could not collect taxes honestly, control inflation, or reach the villages. ⚠️ Every one of these is a statement about capacity, not about belief. ⚠️ The strongest available thesis on the 1949 outcome follows directly: the Communist Party won because it demonstrated in the countryside and in the anti-Japanese war that it could govern, and the Nationalists demonstrated in the cities that they could not.

**The two-dates discipline.** ✅ Several of this topic's events have two defensible dates, and an examiner's close option will sit exactly on the gap. ✅ The Cultural Revolution is dated 1966-9 in the core world-history account and 1966-76 by both canonical owners, because the first dates the period of mass upheaval and the second dates the whole campaign to Mao's death; ✅ both are stated in Lesson 14 and neither is silently preferred. ✅ The Great Leap Forward is conventionally 1958-61 and the associated mortality is variously bounded to 1959-63 and 1958-62 by different sources, each of which is reported with its own bracket. ✅ The Warlord Era is dated 1916-28 in the core account; ✅ the republic's own descent began earlier, with Yuan's dictatorship from 1912. ⚠️ Where two dates exist, write the one your source supports and say which phenomenon it dates.

**The attribution discipline.** ⚠️ This topic contains three episodes of mass death - the land-reform violence of the early 1950s, the famine of the Great Leap Forward, and the Khmer Rouge years in Cambodia - and one episode of lethal repression, Tiananmen in 1989. ✅ For every one of them the evidence base is contested, and in this session every figure is carried with the name of the historian, official body, journalist or archive that produced it, together with the period it covers. ❌ No figure anywhere in this session is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated, combined across sources or converted from one bracket to another. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to named leaders, party organs, armies and local officials, never to a nation or a people.

**The mechanism discipline.** ⚠️ Six things are routinely called "the communist victory" and they are six different mechanisms. **Organisational survival** is the Long March: not winning, but not being destroyed. **Social-base construction** is land policy in the villages: winning by governing. **Patriotic legitimacy** is the anti-Japanese record: winning by being the more credible national defender. **Administrative contrast** is discipline and honesty against corruption and inflation: winning by comparison. **Military conversion** is the shift from guerrilla to conventional warfare in 1948: winning by fighting. **Adversary collapse** is desertion, surrender and tactical blunder on the Nationalist side: winning because the other side fell over. ⚠️ A good answer names which mechanism it is discussing; a weak one merges all six into "the communists were more popular".

**The regime-and-doctrine discipline.** ⚠️ What communism *is* as a political philosophy - its claims about property, class and the state, its relation to socialism and to social democracy - is not defined in this session, because that doctrine is owned elsewhere in the world-history allocation and is cross-referenced rather than restated. ⚠️ This session owns the *historical record* of communist regimes in China and Asia. ⚠️ The practical consequence for an answer is that a question asking what communism is should be answered from the doctrine owner, while a question asking what communism did in Asia should be answered from here, and the two should not be blended into a single vague paragraph about ideology.

**The date-and-status discipline.** ✅ The historical facts used in this session are stable: the dynasty fell in 1911, the Party was founded in 1921, the alliance broke in 1927, the Long March was 1934-5, the invasion came in 1937, the People's Republic was proclaimed in 1949, the Great Leap began in 1958, the Cultural Revolution began in 1966, reform began after 1978 and the crackdown came in June 1989. ❌ This session does **not** assert any current Chinese economic indicator, growth rate, population figure, military capability, leadership arrangement or diplomatic position, because none of those is stable and a history answer does not need them. ⚠️ Where a live institutional development is genuinely relevant, it appears exactly once, dated, in Lesson 17, and it is limited there in the text itself.

**The divergence discipline.** ⚠️ Asian communism is examined almost entirely through divergence, and the four non-Chinese cases are taught so that each one refutes a different generalisation. ✅ Korea shows that a communist state could be produced by external division rather than internal revolution, and that it existed before the Chinese one. ✅ Vietnam shows that anti-colonial nationalism, not class analysis, could be the primary asset. ✅ Cambodia shows that a regime using the same vocabulary could produce an atrocity that its communist neighbours neither attempted nor tolerated. ✅ Laos shows that a one-party state could abandon socialist economics so completely that whether it remained communist became a genuine question. ⚠️ The decisive empirical evidence against the idea of a single bloc is not any of these individually but the fact that communist states fought each other: ✅ Vietnam invaded Kampuchea in December 1978 and ✅ China attacked Vietnam in February 1979.

**The boundary discipline.** Four neighbouring topics own material this session touches constantly, and the handovers are stated in the lessons rather than hidden. The Korean War of 1950-3 and the Vietnam War of 1961-75 belong to the Cold War owner as superpower theatres; this session teaches the regimes on either side of those wars and the consequences of the wars for those regimes, and stops there. The end of the French and other European empires belongs to the decolonisation owner; this session uses the colonial background of Indochina only as the condition that produced its communist parties. The events of 1989-91 in Europe and the post-Cold-War order belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner; this session carries China only to the point where its own trajectory diverges from the European one, which is precisely June 1989. The Russian revolution and the Stalinist transformation belong to their own owner; Lesson 21 compares them with China deliberately and argues that the comparison is instructive mainly where it fails.

## Roadmap

Twenty-two lessons. The count is set by the number of distinct causal problems this topic contains, not by a template, and the lessons are of deliberately unequal size: Lesson 13 on the Great Leap Forward and Lesson 20 on Indochina are long because the evidence is contested and must be handled slowly, while Lesson 12 on the Hundred Flowers campaign is short because it teaches one mechanism cleanly.

| # | Stage | Lesson | What it settles |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Foundation | The crisis of capacity, 1839-1900 | Why the state, not the dynasty, was the thing that failed |
| 2 | Foundation | 1911 and the capture of the republic | Why removing an emperor solved nothing |
| 3 | Foundation | Warlords, humiliation and the intellectual awakening, 1916-28 | What fragmentation did to Chinese politics and thought |
| 4 | Foundation | Sun Yat-sen, the Nationalists and the Three Principles | The republican alternative and its structural weakness |
| 5 | Foundation | The Communist Party's foundation and Mao's formation, 1921-27 | Why a party of intellectuals allied with its future enemy |
| 6 | Core | The Northern March and the rupture of 1927 | How reunification and civil war arrived together |
| 7 | Core | The rural turn, the extermination campaigns and the Long March | Survival converted into strategy, and strategy into legend |
| 8 | Core | Why the peasants moved: the five recorded reasons | The comparative governance argument, in full |
| 9 | Core | Japan, the Sian incident and the Second United Front | How an external war reordered an internal contest |
| 10 | Core | The civil-war outcome, 1945-49 | Why the result was neither inevitable nor accidental |
| 11 | Core | State formation, land and collectivisation, 1949-56 | What the new state actually did, and what it cost |
| 12 | Core | The Hundred Flowers campaign and the Anti-Rightist turn, 1957 | The hinge between two political styles |
| 13 | Core | The Great Leap Forward, 1958-61, and the evidence problem | Ambition, catastrophe and how to write about mortality |
| 14 | Core | The Cultural Revolution and the Party's own verdict | Why it happened, what it destroyed, how it was judged |
| 15 | Advanced | Maoism as ideology and as practice | The doctrine, the debates and the balanced judgement |
| 16 | Advanced | The Sino-Soviet split and the opening to the United States | Why the communist world was never a single actor |
| 17 | Advanced | The Deng transition: reform without political reform | Market socialism inside a Leninist shell |
| 18 | Advanced | 1987, Tiananmen 1989 and the two sequencings | The moment China and the Soviet Union parted company |
| 19 | Advanced | Korea: a communist state made by division | The case that came first and copied nobody |
| 20 | Advanced | Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos | Three Indochinese communisms and one atrocity |
| 21 | Advanced | China and Russia compared, without false equivalence | Where the comparison teaches and where it misleads |
| 22 | Advanced | The verdict and the answer architecture | Converting a century into marks |

## Lesson 1 - The crisis of capacity: how the world's oldest continuous state stopped being able to govern, 1839-1900

Progress: 1/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the demographic and subsistence crisis of 1741-1841; the Opium Wars and the treaty-port system; the Taiping Rebellion and the loss of the monopoly of force; defeat by Japan in 1894-5; the leaseholds of 1898; the Boxer Rising and the indemnity; the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-5; and the returning students who carried revolutionary ideas home

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's summary for the chapter on China from 1900 to 1949, which records a long history of national unity under the Manchu or Ch'ing dynasty since the mid-seventeenth century, a country that moved during the 1840s into a troubled period of foreign interference, civil war and disintegration lasting until 1949, and its opening section on the background to the revolution of 1911
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four blows, and what each one removed

```text
   WHAT THE QING STATE HAD IN 1800          WHAT IT HAD LOST BY 1900
   ==============================          ========================

   [1] A subsistence margin                 GONE by the 1840s
       140m people (1741) -> 410m (1841)    -> food shortage
       Long peace, rising population        -> banditry as survival
                                            -> chaos that invited outsiders

   [2] Control of its own trade and coast    GONE 1839-42 and after
       Self-chosen separation from the       -> Opium Wars, defeat
       outside world                         -> Hong Kong handed over
                                            -> trade forced open at ports
                                            -> rights and concessions in
                                               about 80 ports and towns

   [3] A monopoly of armed force             GONE 1850-64
       Imperial armies                       -> Taiping Rebellion
                                            -> government troops INEFFECTIVE
                                            -> saved by NEW REGIONAL ARMIES
                                               the dynasty did not control
                                            -> provinces begin asserting
                                               independence of Beijing
                                            -> ends in the WARLORD ERA 1916-28

   [4] Standing as a great power             GONE 1895-1905
       Regional primacy                      -> defeated by Japan 1894-5,
                                                Formosa handed over
                                            -> Britain, Germany, France,
                                               Russia lease large areas
                                               by end of 1898
                                            -> Boxer Rising 1898-1900 crushed
                                               by an international army;
                                               massive compensation paid
                                            -> more territory lost to Japan
                                               after the Russo-Japanese War
                                               of 1904-5

   RESULT: a state that could not feed, defend, tax or command its own
   territory. The revolutionaries of 1911 inherited this, not an empire.
```

⚠️ Read the right-hand column downward and one thing becomes visible that a chronological narrative hides: each blow removed a different *capacity*, and the capacities were removed in an order that made recovery progressively harder. A state that has lost its subsistence margin can still repress; a state that has lost its monopoly of force cannot.

### 1. Start with the problem, not the ideology

Here is the question this whole topic is really about. In 1911 a dynasty that had ruled for more than two and a half centuries fell in a matter of weeks, with very little fighting. Thirty-eight years later a party founded in 1921 by a handful of intellectuals governed the entire mainland. In between, three other forces - a constitutional republic, a military dictator, and a Nationalist government backed by the United States - tried to rule China and failed.

The temptation is to explain this by asking which ideology was most attractive. Resist it. ⚠️ The better question, and the one that produces high-scoring answers, is: **which organisation could actually govern?** Governing means collecting revenue without theft, commanding soldiers who obey, reaching villages where most people live, and defending the frontier. ⚠️ For a century, nobody in China could do all four at once. The whole period from 1839 to 1949 is a competition to rebuild that capacity, and the ideological labels are best read as descriptions of *how* each competitor proposed to rebuild it.

### 2. The demographic squeeze, and why it mattered politically

✅ In the early part of the nineteenth century China kept itself very much separate from the rest of the world, and life went on quietly and peacefully with no great changes, as it had done since the Manchus took over in the 1640s. ✅ But the prolonged period of relative peace produced a rapid increase in population: between 1741 and 1841 the population rose from 140 million to 410 million. ✅ This made it difficult to produce enough food for subsistence, forcing many peasants to turn to robbery and banditry as a means of survival.

⚠️ Notice what this is and is not. It is not a moral failing of the dynasty, and it is not an argument that China was decadent. It is a resource problem produced by the dynasty's own success: peace raised population, population outran food, and food shortage produced disorder. ✅ The ensuing chaos encouraged foreigners, especially Europeans, to force their way into China to take advantage of trading possibilities. ⚠️ The causal order therefore runs *internal crisis first, foreign intrusion second* - which is the opposite of the version most students carry, in which a strong China is broken open from outside. Getting this order right is worth marks in any question on the origins of the Chinese revolution.

### 3. The Opium Wars and the treaty-port system

✅ The British were first on the scene, fighting and defeating the Chinese in the Opium Wars of 1839-42. ✅ They forced China to hand over Hong Kong and to allow them to trade at certain ports. ✅ Other western nations followed, and eventually these foreigners - whom the Chinese regarded as "barbarians" - had rights and concessions in about eighty ports and other towns.

The mechanism here deserves a sentence of its own. A concession is not a colony. ⚠️ It is a piece of a country's own territory in which that country's law does not fully run, and it is politically more corrosive than outright annexation, because it is a permanent visible demonstration that the government cannot exercise authority on its own soil. ✅ The core account preserves exactly this point in a striking form: there was a story in circulation that outside a British-run park in Shanghai there was a sign reading NO DOGS OR CHINESE; ✅ the sign never actually existed, but the story showed the outrage felt by ordinary Chinese people at the intrusive foreign presence in their country. ⚠️ That is a very useful piece of evidence and it must be used as the account uses it - as evidence about *feeling*, not about signage. An answer that reports the sign as a fact has misread the source.

### 4. The Taiping Rebellion: the blow that mattered most

✅ Next came the Taiping Rebellion of 1850-64, which spread all over southern China. ✅ It was partly a Christian religious movement and partly a political reform movement, which aimed to set up a "Heavenly Kingdom of Great Peace" (Taiping tianguo). ✅ The movement was eventually defeated - and here is the decisive detail - ✅ not by the Manchu government troops, which proved to be ineffective, but by newly-formed regional armies.

✅ The failure of the government forces was a serious blow to the authority of the Ch'ing dynasty. ✅ It left them dependent on regional armies that they did not control. ✅ This began the process in which provinces began to assert their independence from the central government in Beijing, culminating in the Warlord Era of 1916-28.

⚠️ Pause on that chain, because it is the single most important causal link in the first half of this topic. The dynasty survived the rebellion and lost the state. From 1864 onward, the organised military power in China sat in provincial hands. ⚠️ The Warlord Era, which looks in most accounts like a sudden collapse after 1916, is better understood as the visible surfacing of a decentralisation of force that had been completed more than fifty years earlier. ⚠️ This is also why the 1911 revolution could succeed so easily and achieve so little: it had only to remove a court that no longer commanded armies, and it could not create a centre that did.

### 5. Defeat by Japan, the leaseholds and the Boxer Rising

✅ China was defeated in a war with Japan in 1894-5 and forced to hand over territory, including the large island of Formosa. ✅ By the end of 1898, Britain, Germany, France and Russia had leased large areas of land from the Chinese government which they proceeded to treat as if they were no longer Chinese territory.

⚠️ The defeat by Japan was qualitatively worse than the defeats by European powers, and for a specific reason: Japan was an Asian state that had begun its own modernisation a generation earlier. Losing to Britain could be explained by industrial disparity; losing to Japan could not be explained away at all. ⚠️ It converted the argument inside China from "how do we resist the West?" into "why have we not reformed as Japan did?" - which is the argument that produced both the late Qing reforms and the revolutionaries who destroyed them.

✅ A Chinese uprising - the Boxer Rising - against foreign influence took place in 1898-1900, but it was defeated by an international army, and the Empress Tz'u-hsi was forced to pay massive compensation for damage done to foreign property in China. ✅ More territory was lost to Japan as a result of the Japanese victory in the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-5, and China was clearly in a sorry state.

⚠️ The Boxer episode completes the pattern in a particularly damaging way. An anti-foreign movement was suppressed by a foreign coalition operating on Chinese soil, and the Chinese government then paid for the damage. ⚠️ For a court whose legitimacy rested on the claim to embody the nation, this was close to a terminal demonstration of the opposite.

### 6. The returning students: where the revolution's ideas came from

✅ In the early years of the twentieth century thousands of young Chinese travelled abroad and were educated there. ✅ They returned with radical, revolutionary ideas of overthrowing the Manchu dynasty and westernizing China. ✅ Some revolutionaries, like Dr Sun Yat-sen, wanted a democratic state modelled on the USA.

⚠️ This is a small fact with large consequences, and it is worth stating why. The revolutionary movement of 1911 was not a peasant movement and did not arise from the villages where the subsistence crisis was worst. It arose among a small, mobile, foreign-educated elite. ⚠️ That social composition explains the central weakness of the republic they founded: it had ideas, prestige and international contacts, and almost no armed force or rural organisation. ⚠️ It also sets up, by contrast, the whole of Lessons 7 and 8, because the eventual communist solution was precisely to acquire what the 1911 revolutionaries lacked.

### Comparative note: why this is not simply "imperialism destroyed China"

⚠️ A very common answer treats the nineteenth-century crisis as externally caused. The evidence here does not support that as a complete explanation, and saying so is a discriminator. ✅ The population-subsistence crisis was internal and preceded the foreign intrusion. ✅ The Taiping Rebellion was an internal movement, and the blow it delivered to the dynasty was delivered by the *manner of its defeat*, not by any foreign power. ⚠️ Foreign pressure then interacted with these internal failures and made each of them worse - the indemnities drained revenue, the concessions advertised weakness, and the defeats destroyed the court's claim to protect the realm. ⚠️ The precise formulation, which is worth memorising, is that foreign pressure was an **accelerant and a delegitimiser** rather than the original cause; and that the original cause was the loss of the state's capacity to feed, tax and command.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The official General Studies Paper I syllabus covers world history from the eighteenth century onward, including colonization and decolonization and political philosophies such as communism, capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the precondition without which the Chinese half of that clause cannot be answered: the state whose collapse made a communist outcome possible.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This material is rarely examined on its own. It appears as the first paragraph of an answer on the causes of the Chinese revolution, on why the 1911 revolution failed, or on the reasons for the communist victory in 1949. ⚠️ The mark is earned by compressing it into three or four sentences that establish *capacity loss* rather than by narrating five wars.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the Opium Wars "opened China to the world" as though this were merely commercial. ✅ The examinable consequence is the concession system and the visible failure of sovereignty.
2. ⚠️ Treating the Taiping Rebellion as a failed peasant revolt and moving on. ✅ Its examinable consequence is the transfer of military power to regional armies.
3. ❌ Reporting the Shanghai park sign as historical fact. ✅ The account records that it never existed and that the story mattered.
4. ⚠️ Dating the fragmentation of China to 1916. ✅ The process began after 1864; 1916-28 is when it became the form of government.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Manchu or Ch'ing dynasty ruled from the mid-seventeenth century; the troubled period runs from the 1840s to 1949.
2. ✅ Population 140 million in 1741 to 410 million in 1841; subsistence failure; banditry; chaos invited foreign entry.
3. ✅ Opium Wars 1839-42; Hong Kong handed over; trade at certain ports; eventually rights and concessions in about eighty ports and towns.
4. ✅ The Shanghai park sign never existed; the story circulated and expressed genuine outrage.
5. ✅ Taiping Rebellion 1850-64; partly Christian religious, partly political reform; aimed at a "Heavenly Kingdom of Great Peace".
6. ✅ Defeated by new regional armies, not government troops - a serious blow to Ch'ing authority, leaving dependence on forces the dynasty did not control.
7. ✅ That dependence began provincial assertion against Beijing, culminating in the Warlord Era of 1916-28.
8. ✅ War with Japan 1894-5; Formosa handed over. By the end of 1898 Britain, Germany, France and Russia held leased areas treated as no longer Chinese.
9. ✅ Boxer Rising 1898-1900; defeated by an international army; Empress Tz'u-hsi forced to pay massive compensation.
10. ✅ Further territory lost to Japan after the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-5.
11. ✅ Thousands of young Chinese educated abroad returned with revolutionary and westernizing ideas; Sun Yat-sen wanted a democratic state modelled on the USA.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: internal subsistence and authority crisis first, foreign pressure as accelerant and delegitimiser second; the capacity lost was fiscal, military and administrative, and no successor regime recovered all three until 1949.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Taiping Rebellion was defeated. Explain why historians nevertheless treat it as one of the most damaging events in the dynasty's history, and connect your answer to a development that occurred more than fifty years later.

**Model answer:** The damage lay in the manner of the victory rather than in the rebellion itself. The Manchu government's own troops proved ineffective, and the rebellion was put down by newly formed regional armies that the dynasty did not control. The Ch'ing therefore survived at the price of becoming dependent on military power it could not command, and this began the process in which provinces asserted independence from the central government at Beijing. The development fifty years later is the Warlord Era of 1916-28, when provincial generals governed their own territories and fought one another: this was not a sudden post-imperial collapse but the visible surfacing of a decentralisation of armed force completed in the 1860s. The wider point is that the dynasty lost its monopoly of force long before it lost the throne, which is why 1911 required so little fighting and produced so little order.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the nineteenth-century crisis as something done to China entirely from outside. The subsistence crisis of 1741-1841 and the Taiping Rebellion were internal, and the account is explicit that the resulting chaos encouraged foreigners to force their way in - the intrusion followed the disorder. Foreign pressure then intensified and delegitimised the crisis, but an answer that begins with imperialism has inverted the causal sequence and will struggle to explain why the dynasty could not simply repress its opponents.

## Lesson 2 - 1911: how a dynasty fell in weeks and a republic was captured in months

Progress: 2/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the late Qing reforms and their unintended effect; the Wuchang rising of October 1911; the provincial declarations; the child emperor Puyi; the bargain with Yuan Shikai; the military dictatorship of 1912-15; Japan's seizure of the Shantung rights and the Twenty-One Demands of January 1915

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the 1911 revolution and the Twenty-One Demands of 1915, together with the chapter summary recording that the last emperor was overthrown in 1911, that a republic was proclaimed, and that the period 1916 to 1928 known as the Warlord Era was one of great chaos
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the reform that accelerated the collapse, and the bargain that voided the result

```text
   STEP 1  The government responds to radical ideas
           reforms + promise of democracy + ELECTED PROVINCIAL ASSEMBLIES
                          |
                          | unintended effect
                          v
   STEP 2  Provinces now have a legitimate institutional voice
           -> they distance themselves FURTHER from the centre
           -> the central government becomes extremely unpopular
                          |
                          v
   STEP 3  October 1911: rising among soldiers at WUCHANG
           -> most provinces quickly declare independence of Beijing
                          |
                          v
   STEP 4  The court's response: call in a general
           Emperor Puyi is FIVE YEARS OLD
           Government seeks help from YUAN SHIKAI (retired, early fifties,
           former commander of the Northern Army, great influence with
           the generals)
                          |
                          | THE PLAN BACKFIRES
                          v
   STEP 5  THE BARGAIN
           Revolutionaries get: abdication of Puyi + end of Manchu dynasty
           Yuan gets:          first PRESIDENT of the Chinese republic
                          |
                          v
   STEP 6  1912-1915  Yuan rules as a MILITARY DICTATOR, backed by the army
                          |
                          v
   RESULT  Dynasty removed. Republic proclaimed. Republican government
           never actually exercised. The revolution's stated goal and its
           institutional outcome came apart within months.
```

⚠️ The diagram is drawn as a single causal line because that is what it was. ⚠️ Each step is a rational response to the previous one, and the outcome was a dictatorship that nobody at Wuchang had fought for.

### 1. The reform trap

✅ The government tried to respond to the new radical ideas by introducing reforms, promising democracy and setting up elected provincial assemblies. ✅ However, this only encouraged the provinces to distance themselves still further from the central government, which was now extremely unpopular.

⚠️ This is a genuinely instructive mechanism and it is worth naming, because it recurs across this topic and across the syllabus. A weakened central authority that decentralises in order to buy support gives its opponents an institutional platform instead of a reason for loyalty. ⚠️ The provincial assemblies did not create provincial separatism - Lesson 1 showed that the regional armies had already done that - but they gave it a legitimate civilian voice and a meeting place. ⚠️ The comparison that examiners reward is with the Russian case in the same decade, where concessions extracted under pressure in 1905 also failed to stabilise the regime; the difference, which Lesson 21 develops, is that the Russian state retained its army and the Chinese state did not.

### 2. Wuchang, October 1911

✅ The revolution began among soldiers in Wuchang in October 1911, and most provinces quickly declared themselves independent of Beijing.

⚠️ Two features of that sentence should be underlined. First, the revolution began *among soldiers* - not among the foreign-educated revolutionaries who had supplied the ideas, and not in a capital. Second, what followed was not the conquest of China by a revolutionary army but a cascade of provincial declarations. ⚠️ The dynasty was not defeated; it was abandoned. That is why the collapse was so fast, and it is also why the republic inherited no instrument of national authority: the very speed of the collapse meant that no organisation had to be built in order to achieve it.

### 3. The child emperor and the general

✅ The government was ruling on behalf of the child emperor Puyi, who was only five years old. ✅ In desperation it sought help from a retired general, Yuan Shikai, who had been commander of the Chinese Northern Army and still had a lot of influence with the generals. ✅ However, the plan backfired: Yuan, who was still only in his early fifties, turned out to have ambitions of his own.

✅ He did a deal with the revolutionaries: they agreed to his becoming the first president of the Chinese republic in return for the abdication of Puyi and the end of the Manchu dynasty. ✅ With the support of the army, Yuan ruled as a military dictator from 1912 until 1915.

⚠️ Read the bargain as an exchange of assets and the logic becomes clear. The revolutionaries held legitimacy, provincial declarations and the demand for a republic; they did not hold an army. Yuan held the army and the loyalty of the generals; he did not hold legitimacy. Each side bought what it lacked, and the price the revolutionaries paid was the republic itself. ⚠️ This is the cleanest illustration in the whole topic of the capacity thesis from Lesson 1: in a country where organised force was the scarce resource, whoever held it could set the terms regardless of the political programme on offer.

### 4. What 1911 did and did not achieve

| Dimension | ✅ What actually happened | ⚠️ What it meant |
|---|---|---|
| The dynasty | The last emperor was overthrown in 1911 and a republic was proclaimed | A genuine and irreversible break: no restoration held |
| Central authority | Yuan ruled as a military dictator from 1912 to 1915 with army support | The republic existed in name; power rested on troops |
| The provinces | Most provinces declared themselves independent of Beijing in 1911 | Decentralisation was ratified, not reversed |
| Political programme | Reforms, promised democracy and elected provincial assemblies preceded the rising | The reform agenda was overtaken, not implemented |
| The revolutionaries | Supplied ideas and the demand for a republic; Sun Yat-sen wanted a democratic state modelled on the USA | They had no army, and therefore no leverage after the bargain |
| Consequence | The period 1916 to 1928 was one of great chaos as generals seized control of different provinces | The state-building problem was left entirely unsolved |

⚠️ The table is the answer to the standard question "what did the 1911 revolution achieve?". The honest response is: one large negative achievement, the permanent removal of dynastic rule, and no positive achievement at all. ❌ Do not write that 1911 solved China's political crisis; ✅ warlordism and civil conflict continued.

### 5. Japan moves: Shantung and the Twenty-One Demands

✅ Meanwhile the Japanese sought to take advantage of the upheaval in China and the outbreak of the First World War. ✅ A few days after the war began they demanded that Germany should hand over all its rights in the Chinese Shantung peninsula to Japan. ✅ This was followed up in January 1915 by Japan's Twenty-One Demands to China. ✅ These were divided into five groups; ✅ the first group sought Chinese approval of Japan's concessions in Shantung, seized from the Germans, including the right to build railways and to begin further development there.

⚠️ The extraction available in this session becomes damaged immediately after that clause, and the remaining four groups of demands are not legible in the held evidence. ⚠️ Rather than reconstruct them from memory, this session states what the source supports and records the rest as an open gap in the final ledger. ⚠️ What matters for the argument, and what the legible evidence fully supports, is the pattern: the collapse of Chinese central authority coincided with a European war that removed the other powers' attention, and Japan used both.

⚠️ The consequence is the beginning of the most important strand in the next twenty-five years. Japanese pressure after 1915 made nationalism the dominant political emotion in China, and it made *anti-Japanese credibility* the currency in which Chinese political movements would eventually be paid. ⚠️ Lessons 8 and 9 show the Communist Party earning that currency and the Nationalists losing it.

### Objection and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** If the republic was captured within months and the country fragmented within five years, was 1911 a revolution at all, or merely a coup with a constitutional cover?

⚠️ **The reply.** It was a revolution in the one respect that proved irreversible. The dynastic principle - rule by a heaven-mandated imperial house, in place since the mid-seventeenth century and in some form for two millennia - was destroyed and never successfully restored, and every subsequent contender for power in China, including Yuan himself, the warlords, the Nationalists and the Communists, had to justify itself in non-dynastic terms. ⚠️ What 1911 failed to do was build a state, and the reason is structural rather than moral: the revolutionaries possessed no army, because the army had passed to provincial commanders half a century earlier. ⚠️ The balanced verdict, and the one worth writing, is that 1911 completed a destruction that had been under way since 1864 and began a competition that lasted until 1949.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Typical stems ask why the 1911 revolution failed to stabilise China, or use 1911 as the opening move in a longer question about the communist victory. ⚠️ The demand is almost always causal rather than narrative: the examiner wants the distinction between removing a regime and constructing a state.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Open with the paradox in one sentence - a dynasty of two and a half centuries fell with very little fighting and nothing was solved. ⚠️ Then give the three-part explanation: the collapse was an abandonment rather than a conquest, so no national organisation was built in achieving it; the revolutionaries held legitimacy but not force, so the bargain with Yuan was unavoidable; and force had already been provincialised since the 1860s, so the republic had no instrument with which to govern. ⚠️ Close by naming the consequence - the Warlord Era - and the new question it posed: not which ideology, but who can build a state.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Writing that the republic was "founded" and leaving it there. ✅ It was proclaimed; ✅ Yuan then ruled as a military dictator from 1912 to 1915.
2. ⚠️ Attributing the revolution to Sun Yat-sen's leadership on the ground. ✅ The rising began among soldiers at Wuchang; ✅ Sun's contribution was programmatic, and Lesson 4 gives its content and its limits.
3. ⚠️ Treating the Twenty-One Demands as a wartime side-issue. ⚠️ They are the origin of the nationalist current that eventually decided the civil war.
4. ⚠️ Dating the Warlord Era from 1911. ✅ The core account dates it 1916-28, after Yuan.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Late Qing response to radical ideas: reforms, promised democracy, elected provincial assemblies - which pushed provinces further from an unpopular centre.
2. ✅ The revolution began among soldiers at Wuchang in October 1911; most provinces quickly declared independence of Beijing.
3. ✅ The government ruled on behalf of the child emperor Puyi, aged five.
4. ✅ It called on the retired general Yuan Shikai, former commander of the Northern Army, still influential with the generals, then in his early fifties.
5. ✅ The bargain: Yuan became first president of the republic; Puyi abdicated and the Manchu dynasty ended.
6. ✅ Yuan ruled as a military dictator with army support from 1912 until 1915.
7. ✅ The last emperor was overthrown in 1911 and a republic was proclaimed; ✅ the Warlord Era ran 1916-28.
8. ✅ Japan demanded Germany's Shantung rights days after the First World War began; ✅ the Twenty-One Demands followed in January 1915, in five groups, the first covering the Shantung concessions including railway rights.
9. ⚠️ Assets exchanged in the bargain: legitimacy for force. The scarce resource set the price.
10. ⚠️ Verdict formulation: 1911 was irreversibly successful against the dynastic principle and wholly unsuccessful at state-building; it converted the question from "which ideology?" to "who can govern?".

### Concept check

**Question:** Why was the bargain between the revolutionaries and Yuan Shikai almost inevitable, and what does its outcome reveal about the deeper problem facing every Chinese government between 1911 and 1949?

**Model answer:** It was close to inevitable because each side held exactly what the other lacked. The revolutionaries held political legitimacy, the provincial declarations of independence and the demand for a republic, but no armed force; Yuan held the loyalty of the generals as former commander of the Northern Army, but no claim to represent the revolution. The exchange - Yuan's presidency for Puyi's abdication and the end of the dynasty - was therefore a straightforward trade of legitimacy for force. The outcome reveals the deeper problem because Yuan then ruled as a military dictator from 1912 to 1915 with the support of the army, which shows that in China armed force was the scarce and decisive resource and that whoever held it could dictate terms irrespective of programme. That is the state-building problem in its purest form, and it explains why the period after Yuan was one of warlord chaos and why the eventual winner in 1949 was the movement that built both an army and an administration rather than the movement with the most attractive constitution.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the revolution failed because its leaders were naive or insufficiently committed to democracy. The constraint was structural. Armed force had been provincialised since the defeat of the Taiping Rebellion by regional armies in the 1860s, so no civilian revolutionary movement in 1911 could have enforced a republican settlement. Diagnosing the failure as a lack of will rather than a lack of instruments will also make the 1949 outcome unintelligible, because the Communist Party's decisive innovation was not superior conviction but the construction of an army and a rural administration.

## Lesson 3 - Warlords and the awakening: what fragmentation did to Chinese politics and Chinese thought, 1916-28

Progress: 3/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Warlord Era of 1916-28; provincial generals and the absence of a centre; the Nationalist Party trying to govern and control the generals; the May Fourth moment of 1919 and its intellectual consequences; why Marxism entered Chinese politics through universities rather than factories

Book context: queried - the core account's chapter summary on the Warlord Era as a period of great chaos in which a number of generals seized control of different provinces while the Kuomintang tried to govern China and control them; its record that the May the Fourth supporters and other modern progressive thinkers opposed a return to China's imperial past; and its statement that Beijing University was a centre of Marxist studies. The section of the extraction covering the intellectual awakening in detail is damaged and is recorded as an open gap in the final ledger

### Visual: two things happening at once, and why both matter

```text
   1916 ----------------------------------------------------------- 1928

   POLITICAL TRACK                     |   INTELLECTUAL TRACK
   ------------------------------------|--------------------------------
   Yuan's dictatorship ends            |   Accumulated humiliation:
            |                          |   concessions, indemnities,
            v                          |   defeats, Twenty-One Demands
   WARLORD ERA 1916-28                 |            |
   generals seize control of           |            v
   different provinces                 |   4 MAY 1919: Shandong rights
            |                          |   transferred to Japan at the
            v                          |   peace settlement, not returned
   The Kuomintang (KMT), the           |   to China -> student and
   Nationalists, try to govern         |   intellectual protest in Beijing
   China and control the generals,     |            |
   who are busy fighting each other    |            v
            |                          |   New Culture current: criticism
            v                          |   of inherited Confucian order,
   NO national tax base                |   demand for modernisation in
   NO national army                    |   politics and science, national
   NO national administration          |   self-strengthening
                                       |            |
                                       |            v
                                       |   Beijing University: a CENTRE OF
                                       |   MARXIST STUDIES. Mao Zedong works
                                       |   there as a library assistant.
                                       |   CCP founded 1921 (Lesson 5)

   THE JOIN: the political vacuum created the demand; the intellectual
   awakening supplied competing answers to it. Neither track alone
   explains what followed.
```

⚠️ The two columns are drawn in parallel because the commonest weak answer treats this decade as a gap between the revolution and the real story. It is not a gap. It is the decade in which the political problem became unmistakable and the ideological competitors to solve it were assembled.

### 1. What warlordism actually was

✅ The period 1916 to 1928, known as the Warlord Era, was one of great chaos, as a number of generals seized control of different provinces. ✅ A party known as the Kuomintang - the KMT, or Nationalists - was trying to govern China and control the generals, who were busy fighting each other.

⚠️ Take the definition seriously rather than as colour. A warlord is not a bandit and not a rebel; he is a regional military commander who performs the functions of a government - taxation, conscription, appointment, adjudication - within a territory, without deriving authority from a national centre. ⚠️ This is why the era is so damaging analytically: China in these years had governance without a government. Order existed in patches and was enforced by whoever held troops in that patch.

⚠️ Two consequences follow directly and both are examinable. First, any future national government would have to *defeat or absorb* these commanders, which is exactly what Lesson 6 describes. Second, any future national government that merely absorbed them would inherit their unreliability - and the Nationalist government that emerged in 1928 did precisely that, which is one of the quieter reasons for its later weakness.

### 2. Why the Nationalists could not simply take over

✅ The Kuomintang was trying to govern China and control the generals. ⚠️ The word "trying" carries the whole difficulty. A party with a programme and a base in the south faced perhaps a dozen commanders with armies, each of whom could be defeated only by a larger army. ⚠️ Lesson 4 shows Sun Yat-sen's response to this - building a party army with outside help - and the fact that this response was necessary is itself the best evidence for the capacity thesis of Lesson 1. In a country where force had been provincialised for fifty years, the entry ticket to national politics was an army, and a party that wanted power had to become in part a military organisation. ⚠️ Both parties in this topic did exactly that, and the fact that the Nationalists did it first and better between 1926 and 1928 explains the shape of the next decade.

### 3. The May Fourth moment and what it changed

⚠️ The account read for this session preserves the intellectual awakening of these years only in fragments, and one of them is decisive: ✅ when the Nationalist leadership later promoted a revival of traditional Confucian values, many May the Fourth supporters and other modern progressive thinkers protested that this was a backward step designed to return China to its oppressive imperial past. ⚠️ That single sentence tells us what the movement had come to stand for by the 1930s - modernity against inherited hierarchy, and a refusal to answer national weakness with cultural restoration.

The standard connecting facts, which the fragmentary extraction does not supply and which are needed here because their absence would leave a genuine hole in the syllabus, are these. ✅ The movement takes its name from student protests in Beijing on **4 May 1919**, triggered by the peace settlement's decision to transfer Germany's former rights in Shandong to Japan rather than return them to China - the very rights Japan had demanded at the outbreak of the war and consolidated through the Twenty-One Demands of January 1915. ✅ China did not sign the Versailles treaty. ✅ The protest broadened rapidly into a wider cultural and political current: anti-imperialist, critical of the inherited Confucian order, committed to modernisation in politics, science and language.

⚠️ The analytical point is the one that matters for an answer. May Fourth converted foreign humiliation from a grievance into a *political programme*. Before 1919, humiliation had produced episodic anti-foreign violence, as in the Boxer Rising, and elite reform proposals. After 1919, it produced organised, urban, ideologically articulate movements that identified the enemy as *both* foreign imperialism and the domestic political order that had failed to resist it. ⚠️ Every subsequent movement in this topic - Nationalist, Communist and the later student protests of 1978, 1986 and 1989 - inherits that double target.

### 4. Why Marxism arrived through the university

✅ Mao Zedong, after training as a teacher, moved northwards to Beijing where he worked as a library assistant at the university, a centre of Marxist studies. ⚠️ That short clause is worth more than it looks, because it identifies the route by which Marxism entered Chinese politics. It did not arrive through an industrial working class - China had very few industrial workers, concentrated in a handful of coastal cities - and it did not arrive through a peasant tradition. It arrived through universities, among students and teachers looking for an explanation of national weakness and a method of national recovery.

⚠️ Two consequences follow, and both are set up here to be collected later. First, the party founded in 1921 was, as Lesson 5 shows, a party of intellectuals with almost no military strength - which is why its early strategy was urban and why that strategy failed. Second, the eventual Maoist turn to the peasantry was therefore a *departure* from the social theory the party had imported, not an application of it. ⚠️ An answer that understands this can explain why Mao's strategy was innovative; an answer that treats communism as naturally peasant-facing cannot.

### Comparative note: fragmentation as opportunity

⚠️ Warlordism is usually written up as pure disaster, and for most Chinese it was. ⚠️ But it should also be read as the condition that made both later parties possible. A unified authoritarian state could have suppressed a party of two dozen intellectuals in 1921 without difficulty; a fragmented country could not. ⚠️ The same logic runs through the whole topic: the communist movement survived the 1930s in mountainous border areas between provinces, survived the 1940s in the interior while Japan held the coast and the Nationalists retreated west, and won in 1949 by converting territorial refuges into administered territory. ⚠️ Fragmentation was the medium in which the eventual winner grew.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This material almost never carries a question alone. It appears as the second paragraph of an answer on the causes of the communist victory, or as the context sentence in an answer on Chinese nationalism. ⚠️ Its function in an answer is to explain *why the competition of 1927-49 took the form it did* - two armed parties rather than two electoral parties.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ One sentence on governance without government; one sentence on the entry ticket being an army; one sentence on May Fourth converting humiliation into a programme with a double target; one sentence on Marxism entering through universities, which explains the early party's urban strategy and the later significance of Mao's departure from it.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating 1916-28 as an interlude. ⚠️ It is the period in which the form of Chinese politics for the next thirty years was set.
2. ❌ Assuming the Nationalist Party governed China during the Warlord Era. ✅ It was *trying* to govern and to control the generals.
3. ⚠️ Assuming Chinese communism began with a worker base. ✅ It began in a university milieu; ✅ the party at first consisted mostly of intellectuals.
4. ⚠️ Describing May Fourth as purely anti-Japanese. ⚠️ Its target was double - foreign encroachment and the domestic order that had permitted it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Warlord Era 1916-28: generals seized control of different provinces; great chaos.
2. ✅ The Kuomintang, or Nationalists, were trying to govern China and to control the generals, who were fighting each other.
3. ⚠️ Warlordism defined: regional military commanders performing governmental functions without national authorisation - governance without a government.
4. ⚠️ Entry ticket to national politics in such a system: an army. Both major parties therefore became part-military organisations.
5. ✅ May Fourth: named from student protests in Beijing on 4 May 1919, triggered by the transfer of Germany's Shandong rights to Japan at the peace settlement; China did not sign the treaty.
6. ✅ Its supporters were modern progressive thinkers who later protested against a revival of traditional values as a return to China's oppressive imperial past.
7. ⚠️ Effect: humiliation became a political programme with a double target - foreign imperialism and the domestic order that failed to resist it.
8. ✅ Beijing University was a centre of Marxist studies; Mao worked there as a library assistant after training as a teacher.
9. ⚠️ Consequence: Marxism entered through the universities, not through industry - which makes the later peasant strategy a departure from imported theory rather than an application of it.
10. ⚠️ Fragmentation was also opportunity: a unified state could have crushed a party of intellectuals in 1921; a fragmented one could not.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the route through which Marxism entered Chinese politics matters for assessing the originality of Mao's later strategy.

**Model answer:** Marxism entered through universities rather than through industry. Beijing University was a centre of Marxist studies, and it was there that Mao worked as a library assistant after training as a teacher; the party founded in 1921 consisted mostly of intellectuals and had very little military strength. The theory these intellectuals imported located revolutionary agency in an industrial proletariat, but China's industrial working class was tiny and confined to a few coastal cities, which is why the party's early urban insurrections failed against Nationalist strength. Mao's decision after the 1927 breach to concentrate on winning mass support among the peasants rather than trying to capture industrial towns was therefore a departure from the social theory the movement had adopted, adapted to the actual distribution of Chinese society. Its originality lies exactly there: it was a strategic response to a mismatch between imported theory and local structure, not an application of doctrine.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that communism is naturally a peasant ideology and that Mao was simply doing what communists do. The opposite is true of the theory the Chinese party imported, and treating the peasant turn as obvious removes the single most distinctive feature of Chinese communism and makes the contrast with the Russian case in Lesson 21 impossible to draw.
