### Visual: the Three Principles, each with its own built-in limit

```text
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  SUN YAT-SEN'S THREE PRINCIPLES  (his own summary of his aims) |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   [1] NATIONALISM
       Aim:   rid China of foreign influence; build the country into a
              strong and united power, respected abroad
       Limit: achievable by military reunification alone - and this is
              the ONLY one Chiang could later claim to have achieved

   [2] DEMOCRACY
       Aim:   China should be ruled not by warlords but by the people
              themselves - AFTER they had been educated to equip them
              for democratic self-government
       Limit: the education clause is a postponement clause. It permits
              an indefinite period of tutelage before any popular rule

   [3] LAND REFORM  ("the people's livelihood")
       Aim:   long-term economic development and redistribution of land
              to the peasants; in favour of rent restraint
       Limit: STATED BY THE SOURCE AS VAGUE. Sun was OPPOSED to the
              confiscation of landlords' property
              -> so redistribution without confiscation was left
                 unexplained, and the rural question stayed open

   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  WHY THIS MATTERS: principle 3 is the exact space into which    |
   |  the communists later moved (Lessons 7-8). The Nationalists     |
   |  left the largest social question in China unanswered, and      |
   |  their rivals answered it.                                      |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ The right-hand column is the teaching, not decoration. Each principle contains a qualification that determines what actually happened: nationalism was the achievable one, democracy was deferred by its own educational precondition, and livelihood was undefined at exactly the point where definition mattered.

### 1. The party that had to build itself twice

✅ The Nationalist Party developed its own party organisation along communist lines, as well as building up its own army. ⚠️ Both halves of that sentence are important and both are consequences of Lesson 3. A party facing provincial armies needs an army; a party trying to govern a fragmented country needs an organisational structure capable of discipline and penetration. ⚠️ The Soviet model supplied both, and it did so at a moment when no Western government was offering comparable assistance.

⚠️ This has a consequence that students routinely miss and examiners reward. The organisational similarity between the two Chinese parties is not a coincidence or a later convergence: the Nationalist Party was deliberately reorganised on the same structural model. ⚠️ When they fought after 1927, they fought as structurally similar organisations with opposite social bases - and that is exactly why the comparison between them in Lesson 8 is a comparison of *governance and social policy*, not of organisational form.

### 2. The Three Principles in Sun's own summary

✅ Sun himself summarised his aims as the Three Principles.

✅ **Nationalism** - to rid China of foreign influence and build the country into a strong and united power, respected abroad.

✅ **Democracy** - China should not be ruled by warlords, but by the people themselves, after they had been educated to equip them for democratic self-government.

✅ **Land reform**, sometimes known as "the people's livelihood" - ✅ this was vague: although Sun announced a long-term policy of economic development and redistribution of land to the peasants and was in favour of rent restraint, ✅ he was opposed to the confiscation of landlords' property.

⚠️ The third principle is where an examiner will probe, and the source itself supplies the verdict: it was vague. The vagueness is not a stylistic failing but a political position. Redistribution of land without confiscation of landlords' property is, in a country where most land was held by landlords, a programme without a mechanism. ⚠️ The Nationalist movement thereby preserved its coalition with propertied supporters at the cost of leaving the central rural question unanswered - and Lesson 8 shows precisely what its rivals did with the space that created.

### 3. Sun's limits and the reason for them

✅ Sun gained enormous respect as an intellectual statesman and revolutionary leader, ✅ but when he died in 1925 little progress had been made towards achieving the three principles, ✅ mainly because he was not himself a general. ✅ Until the Nationalist armies were built up, he had to rely on alliances with sympathetic warlords, ✅ and he had difficulty exercising any authority outside the south.

⚠️ The explanation offered is the capacity thesis again, in its sharpest form: the decisive deficiency of the most respected revolutionary figure in China was that he did not command troops. ⚠️ Note how the consequence follows in the next clause - reliance on alliances with sympathetic warlords. A movement without its own force must rent force, and rented force cannot be used against the people who own it. That is why the Nationalist programme could not be implemented in the provinces it nominally reached.

⚠️ It also produces the geographical fact that shapes the next lesson: ✅ Sun had difficulty exercising authority outside the south. The Nationalist movement was a southern movement with a national claim, and the Northern March of 1926 was the attempt to make the claim real.

### 4. Chiang Kai-shek: formation, Moscow and Whampoa

✅ General Chiang Kai-shek became leader of the Nationalists after Sun's death. ✅ He had received his military training in Japan before the First World War, and being a strong nationalist, joined the party.

✅ At this stage the new Russian Soviet government was providing help and guidance for the Nationalists, in the hope that Nationalist China would be friendly towards Russia. ✅ In 1923 Chiang spent some time in Moscow studying the organisation of the Communist Party and the Red Army. ✅ The following year he became head of the Whampoa Military Academy, near Canton, which was set up with the help of Russian cash, arms and advisers to train officers for the Nationalist army.

✅ However, in spite of his Russian contacts, Chiang was not a communist. ✅ In fact he was more right-wing than Sun Yat-sen and became increasingly anti-communist, his sympathies lying with businessmen and landowners. ✅ Soon after becoming party leader, he removed all left-wingers from leading positions in the party, though for the time being he continued the alliance with the communists.

⚠️ Four things are established here that the rest of the topic depends on. First, the Nationalist officer corps was created with Soviet assistance, which is why the 1927 rupture was a break with a patron as well as with an ally. Second, Soviet policy was instrumental rather than ideological: Moscow was backing the *Nationalists* in the hope of a friendly China, not backing the Chinese communists to win. Third, Chiang's anti-communism preceded the rupture and was acted on immediately - he removed left-wingers from leading positions as soon as he took over. Fourth, the alliance continued anyway, because it was still useful to both sides: the Nationalists needed the communists' capacity to organise workers and peasants, and the communists needed the protection of a larger party.

⚠️ The last point is the key to reading 1927 correctly. The alliance was never a partnership of belief. It was a temporary convergence of two organisations with a common enemy in the warlords, maintained by a leader who had already begun purging one partner from his own party.

### Comparative note: the two parties before the split

| Dimension | ✅ Nationalists under Sun and Chiang | ✅ Communists (Lesson 5 develops this) |
|---|---|---|
| Founding social base | Southern republicans, businessmen and landowners among Chiang's sympathies | Mostly intellectuals at first |
| Organisational model | Reorganised along communist party lines, with its own army | Party organisation with very little military strength |
| External patron | Soviet help and guidance; Russian cash, arms and advisers at Whampoa | Working with the Nationalists against the warlords |
| Rural programme | Redistribution and rent restraint, but no confiscation - vague | Land seizure and redistribution in areas it controlled (Lesson 8) |
| Decisive asset | An officer corps and a party army by 1924-6 | Organising capacity among workers and peasants |
| Decisive weakness | An unanswered land question | No independent armed force before 1927 |

⚠️ Read the last two rows together and the whole civil war is visible in advance. Each party's decisive asset was the other's decisive weakness, and the contest after 1927 was a race to acquire what one lacked. ⚠️ The communists acquired an army; the Nationalists never acquired a rural policy.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems on the Chinese revolution frequently invite the claim that the communist victory was the only available path. ⚠️ The existence of a serious republican alternative with a stated programme is the correction, and it earns marks precisely because it shows the candidate has not read history backwards.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Name the Three Principles with their content; identify the qualification inside each; show that nationalism was achievable by force alone, democracy was deferred by its own education clause, and livelihood was vague because redistribution was decoupled from confiscation; then connect the third to the communist opportunity.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Writing that Sun's programme included land confiscation. ✅ He was opposed to the confiscation of landlords' property.
2. ⚠️ Treating Soviet help as help to the communists. ✅ Soviet assistance at this stage went to the Nationalists, in the hope of a friendly China.
3. ⚠️ Dating Chiang's anti-communism to 1927. ✅ He was already more right-wing than Sun and removed left-wingers from leading positions soon after becoming leader.
4. ⚠️ Saying Sun failed because his ideas were impractical. ✅ The stated reason is that he was not himself a general and had to rely on alliances with sympathetic warlords.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Nationalist Party developed its own organisation along communist lines and built up its own army.
2. ✅ Three Principles, in Sun's own summary: nationalism, democracy, land reform or "the people's livelihood".
3. ✅ Nationalism: rid China of foreign influence; build a strong and united power, respected abroad.
4. ✅ Democracy: rule by the people rather than warlords, *after* they had been educated for democratic self-government.
5. ✅ Land reform: long-term economic development and redistribution to peasants, in favour of rent restraint, but opposed to confiscation of landlords' property - and described in the source as vague.
6. ✅ Sun died in 1925 with little progress made, mainly because he was not himself a general; he relied on alliances with sympathetic warlords and had difficulty exercising authority outside the south.
7. ✅ Chiang Kai-shek: military training in Japan before the First World War; strong nationalist; became leader after 1925.
8. ✅ Soviet government supplied help and guidance to the Nationalists, hoping Nationalist China would be friendly to Russia.
9. ✅ Chiang in Moscow in 1923 studying the Communist Party and the Red Army; head of the Whampoa Military Academy near Canton in 1924, set up with Russian cash, arms and advisers.
10. ✅ Chiang was not a communist; more right-wing than Sun; sympathies with businessmen and landowners; removed left-wingers from leading positions but continued the alliance for the time being.
11. ⚠️ Structural point: the two parties shared an organisational model and had opposite social bases; each one's decisive asset was the other's decisive weakness.

### Concept check

**Question:** Sun Yat-sen's third principle is described as vague. Identify the specific internal contradiction that makes it so, and explain its long-term political consequence.

**Model answer:** The contradiction is between ends and means. Sun announced a long-term policy of economic development and redistribution of land to the peasants and supported rent restraint, yet he was opposed to the confiscation of landlords' property. In a country where most cultivated land was held by landlords, redistribution without confiscation has no mechanism, so the principle stated a goal while excluding the only obvious route to it. The political reason for the contradiction is that the Nationalist movement depended on propertied support, and Chiang's own sympathies lay with businessmen and landowners. The long-term consequence is that the largest social question in China was left unanswered by the party that reunified the country: no moves were made towards land reform under the Nationalist government, while in the areas they controlled the communists seized the estates of rich landlords and redistributed them, and later restricted rents and ensured that even the poorest labourers got a small piece of land. The unanswered question became the opening through which the rival party built its rural base.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the three principles as a failed blueprint that simply was not implemented. Two of them were implementable and one of them was in fact implemented: Chiang could claim to have achieved nationalism through reunification. The problem is specific to the third principle, and locating it precisely - redistribution decoupled from confiscation - is what turns a descriptive paragraph into an analytical one.

## Lesson 5 - A party of intellectuals: the Communist Party's foundation and Mao's formation, 1921-27

Progress: 5/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the founding of the Chinese Communist Party in 1921 and its composition; why it was willing to work with the Nationalists; the First United Front and its rationale; Mao Zedong's background, training and organising experience; the failed urban insurrections and what they proved

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the early years of the Chinese Communist Party, recording its official foundation in 1921, its composition mostly of intellectuals with very little military strength, the explanation this gives for its willingness to work with the Nationalists, Mao's birth in Hunan in 1893 and his path through farm work, teacher training, the Beijing university library and trade union and peasant association organising, and the failure of communist insurrections in the industrial towns because of Nationalist strength
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a weak party's rational choice

```text
   1921: PARTY FOUNDED
         composition: MOSTLY INTELLECTUALS
         military strength: VERY LITTLE
                   |
                   | What can such a party do in a country
                   | governed by armies?
                   v
   +------------------------+     +-------------------------------+
   | OPTION A               |     | OPTION B                      |
   | Act alone              |     | Work with the Nationalists    |
   | - no army              |     | - they have Soviet backing    |
   | - no territory         |     | - they are building an army   |
   | - immediate suppression|     | - common enemy: the warlords  |
   +------------------------+     | - communists supply organising|
                                  |   capacity among workers and  |
                                  |   peasants                    |
                                  +-------------------------------+
                                                |
                                                v
                                  FIRST UNITED FRONT, 1924-1927
                                  Co-operation against the warlords
                                                |
                                  Tested in the Northern March:
                                  the capture of Shanghai was helped
                                  by a rising of industrial workers
                                  organised by Zhou En-lai - a member
                                  of the Nationalist Party AND a
                                  communist
                                                |
                                                v
                                  1927: the ally becomes the target
                                  (Lesson 6)

   THE LESSON THE PARTY DREW: co-operation without an independent army
   is survivable only while the partner wants it. After 1927 the party
   never again operated without its own armed force.
```

⚠️ The diagram is built as a decision rather than a narrative because that is the examinable content. The alliance was not naivety; it was the only available strategy for an organisation of that size, and its collapse taught the lesson that governed communist behaviour for the next twenty-two years.

### 1. What the party was in 1921

✅ The party had been officially founded in 1921; ✅ at first it consisted mostly of intellectuals and had very little military strength, ✅ which explains why it was willing to work with the Nationalists.

⚠️ That final clause is the whole of the early strategy in one line, and it should be quoted in substance in any answer on the united front. The co-operation was a function of weakness, not of doctrine. ⚠️ It also tells us what to expect: a party whose only assets are people who can write, teach, speak and organise will use those assets, which is precisely what happened in the cities.

✅ Mao Zedong was present at the founding meeting.

### 2. Mao's formation, and why each stage matters

✅ Mao Zedong was born in Hunan province in 1893 - in **south-central** China - the son of a prosperous peasant farmer. ✅ After spending some time working on the land, Mao trained as a teacher, and then moved northwards to Beijing where he worked as a library assistant at the university, a centre of Marxist studies. ✅ Later he moved back to Hunan and built up a reputation as a skilful trade union and peasant association organiser.

⚠️ Take the four stages in order, because each supplies something the later strategy required.

1. ⚠️ **Son of a prosperous peasant farmer, with time working on the land.** Direct familiarity with rural social structure - the distinctions between landlord, rich peasant, middle peasant and labourer that later land policy turned on.
2. ⚠️ **Trained as a teacher.** The capacity to explain a programme in language villagers could act on; the later Maoist emphasis on propaganda, study and mass education is continuous with this.
3. ⚠️ **Library assistant at a centre of Marxist studies.** Access to the theory, in the milieu where it circulated - and, equally important, a subordinate position within it. He was not a professor.
4. ⚠️ **Trade union and peasant association organiser in Hunan.** The combination that almost no other founding member had: he organised in both settings, and therefore had grounds for comparing them.

⚠️ The fourth stage is the one that produced the strategic departure. ✅ After the communist breach with the Nationalists, Mao was responsible for changing the party's strategy: they would concentrate on winning mass support among the peasants rather than trying to capture industrial towns, ✅ where several communist insurrections had already failed because of the strength of the Nationalists. ⚠️ That decision is taught in full in Lesson 7; what matters here is that it came from someone whose organising experience spanned both worlds.

### 3. The First United Front: what it was for

⚠️ The core account describes the co-operation and its collapse without giving the front a formal name or opening date. ✅ The standard designation and span, supplied here because a candidate needs to be able to name the arrangement the question refers to, are the **First United Front of 1924-1927**, the alliance between the Nationalists and the communists directed at ending warlordism and unifying the country, which collapsed in 1927 when Chiang turned on his partner.

⚠️ Why did each side want it?

- ⚠️ **The communists wanted survival and reach.** ✅ Very little military strength, mostly intellectuals - an organisation in that position either finds a protector or is destroyed.
- ⚠️ **The Nationalists wanted mobilisation.** ✅ The capture of Shanghai during the Northern March was helped by a rising of industrial workers organised by Zhou En-lai, ✅ who was a member of the Nationalist Party and also a communist. ✅ Much of Chiang's success sprang from massive local support among the peasants, who were attracted by communist promises of land. ⚠️ In other words, the communists supplied the social mobilisation that made a military campaign cheaper, and they did so in both city and countryside.

⚠️ That second point is the one most students miss and it is genuinely decisive: the Nationalist reunification of China was achieved in part on the strength of communist land promises. ⚠️ The alliance was therefore not charity in either direction; it was an exchange in which the junior partner's contribution was disproportionately large and therefore disproportionately threatening.

### 4. Why the urban strategy failed

✅ Several communist insurrections in the industrial towns had already failed because of the strength of the Nationalists.

⚠️ Two reasons stand behind that sentence and both should be understood.

⚠️ The first is arithmetic. China's industrial working class was small relative to its population and concentrated in a handful of coastal and riverine cities. A strategy based on it was a strategy based on a minority in a minority of places.

⚠️ The second is military geography. Cities are exactly where a state's coercive power is strongest and most easily concentrated: garrisons, police, communications and transport all favour the holder. An insurrection in a city must win quickly or be crushed. ⚠️ A rural base in a mountainous border region between provinces reverses every one of those advantages - which is the argument of Lesson 7.

### Objection and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** If the alliance benefited the Nationalists so much, and if Chiang's anti-communism was known from the start, was the communist decision to enter it simply a blunder that nearly destroyed the party?

⚠️ **The reply.** The alternative was worse, and the evidence supports this. ✅ The party had very little military strength; ✅ the insurrections it attempted on its own in the industrial towns failed because of Nationalist strength. ⚠️ A party in that condition operating independently in a country governed by armies would have been suppressed earlier and more completely. ⚠️ What the alliance bought was four years of legal existence, organisational growth, experience of mass mobilisation in both city and countryside, and - through figures such as Zhou En-lai, who held membership in both organisations - detailed knowledge of the rival. ⚠️ The honest verdict is that the united front was a correct decision with a catastrophic ending, and that the party's response to the catastrophe, not the decision itself, is what determined the outcome in 1949.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This material appears in questions on why the communists won, on the nature of the Chinese revolution, and on the relationship between nationalism and communism in Asia. ⚠️ Its specific value is that it prevents the common error of treating the two parties as permanent enemies from birth.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ State the party's composition and weakness; derive the alliance from it; note what each side gained, including the communist contribution to Nationalist military success; then use the failure of the urban insurrections to set up the rural turn as a solution to a demonstrated problem rather than as an ideological preference.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the two parties were enemies from 1921. ✅ The communists at first co-operated with the Nationalists against the warlords.
2. ⚠️ Presenting the alliance as Soviet-imposed. ✅ The account explains it by the party's own lack of military strength; ✅ Soviet help at this stage went to the Nationalists.
3. ❌ Describing Mao as a peasant. ✅ He was the son of a prosperous peasant farmer, trained as a teacher, and worked in a university library.
4. ⚠️ Saying the urban strategy was abandoned for doctrinal reasons. ✅ Insurrections in industrial towns had already failed because of Nationalist strength.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Party officially founded in 1921; at first mostly intellectuals; very little military strength - which explains willingness to work with the Nationalists.
2. ✅ Mao Zedong was present at the founding meeting.
3. ✅ Mao born Hunan 1893, in south-central China, son of a prosperous peasant farmer; worked on the land; trained as a teacher; library assistant at Beijing University, a centre of Marxist studies; returned to Hunan as a skilful trade union and peasant association organiser.
4. ✅ First United Front, 1924-1927: alliance against warlordism, collapsing in 1927.
5. ✅ Communist contribution to the Northern March: the capture of Shanghai was helped by a workers' rising organised by Zhou En-lai, a member of both parties; ✅ much of Chiang's success sprang from massive peasant support attracted by communist promises of land.
6. ✅ Several communist insurrections in industrial towns had already failed because of Nationalist strength.
7. ⚠️ Two reasons for that failure: the industrial working class was small and concentrated; and cities are where state coercive power is strongest.
8. ✅ After the breach, Mao was responsible for changing strategy to winning mass support among the peasants rather than capturing industrial towns.
9. ⚠️ Verdict on the alliance: a correct decision by a weak organisation, with a catastrophic ending; the response to the catastrophe decided the outcome.
10. ⚠️ The permanent lesson drawn by the party: never again operate without an independent armed force.

### Concept check

**Question:** The communists contributed materially to the Nationalists' military success during the Northern March. Explain how this contribution helps account for the rupture that followed.

**Model answer:** The contribution was social mobilisation. Much of Chiang's success sprang from massive local support among the peasants, who were attracted by communist promises of land, and the capture of Shanghai was helped by a rising of industrial workers organised by Zhou En-lai, a man who belonged to both parties. This made the alliance valuable but also made the junior partner visibly powerful in exactly the places where the senior partner's own supporters had interests: in areas where communists were strong, landlords were being attacked and land seized. Chiang, whose sympathies lay with businessmen and landowners and who had already removed left-wingers from leading party positions, decided during 1927 that the communists were becoming too powerful and that it was time to destroy an embarrassing ally. The rupture therefore followed from the alliance's success rather than its failure: the communists were purged because their method of assisting the campaign threatened the social coalition on which the Nationalist government rested.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the 1927 break as a simple ideological disagreement that was bound to occur sooner or later. The evidence identifies a specific trigger - the growth of communist strength and the seizure of land in areas they controlled - and a specific calculation about an ally that had become both powerful and embarrassing. Naming the trigger converts a vague claim about incompatible ideologies into an argument about social interests, which is what the question is actually testing.

## Lesson 6 - The Northern March and the rupture of 1927: reunification and civil war arriving together

Progress: 6/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Northern March from Canton in 1926; the captures of Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking by 1927 and of Beijing in 1928; the decision of 1927 and the purification movement; what the Nationalist government did and did not deliver; why 1928 is simultaneously the high point of the republic and the opening of the civil war

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record of the Northern March, the cities captured in 1927 and 1928, the sources of Nationalist success, the decision during 1927 that the communists were becoming too powerful, the purification movement and the estimate recorded for it, and the assessment of the Kuomintang government as a great disappointment for the majority of the Chinese people
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one campaign, two outcomes

```text
   1926  CANTON  ------ Northern March begins ------>
         purpose: destroy the warlords of central and northern China
         composition: Nationalists AND communists together
         fuel: massive local support among peasants, attracted by
               communist promises of land
                     |
                     v
   1927  HANKOW, SHANGHAI, NANKING captured
         Shanghai helped by a workers' rising organised by Zhou En-lai
         (member of the Nationalist Party and a communist)
                     |
         +-----------+-----------------------------+
         |                                         |
         v                                         v
   MILITARY OUTCOME                          POLITICAL OUTCOME
   1928  BEIJING taken                       During 1927 Chiang decides
         warlords under control              the communists are becoming
         Chiang is military and              TOO POWERFUL
         political leader of China           - in areas where communists
                                               are strong, landlords are
                                               being attacked and land seized
                                             - "time to destroy an
                                               embarrassing ally"
                                                     |
                                                     v
                                             ALL communists expelled
                                             from the Nationalist Party
                                                     |
                                                     v
                                             "PURIFICATION MOVEMENT"
                                             thousands of communists,
                                             trade union and peasant
                                             leaders massacred;
                                             SOME ESTIMATES put the total
                                             murdered as high as 250,000
                                                     |
                                                     v
                                             The alliance is over.
                                             The civil war has begun.

   THE PARADOX OF 1928: China is reunified and the conflict that will
   decide its future for twenty-two years has just started.
```

⚠️ Keep the two branches visually separate when you revise. The commonest structural error in answers on this period is to narrate the military campaign and then mention the split as an afterthought, when in fact they are the same event seen from two angles: the campaign's success is what made the junior partner both useful and intolerable.

### 1. The campaign

✅ In 1926 Chiang set out on the Northern March to destroy the warlords of central and northern China. ✅ Starting from Canton, the Nationalists and the communists had captured Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking by 1927. ✅ The capital, Beijing, was taken in 1928.

✅ Much of Chiang's success sprang from massive local support among the peasants, who were attracted by communist promises of land. ✅ The capture of Shanghai was helped by a rising of industrial workers organised by Zhou En-lai, a member of the Nationalist Party and also a communist.

⚠️ Notice what kind of campaign this was. It was not purely a matter of superior armies defeating inferior ones. It combined a trained officer corps produced at Whampoa with social mobilisation supplied by the junior partner, and the mobilisation mattered because warlord armies held territory they did not command politically. ⚠️ Where peasants were promised land and workers rose in the cities, the defenders' position collapsed from inside. ⚠️ This is worth stating precisely in an answer, because it demonstrates the mechanism that would later be turned against the Nationalists themselves.

### 2. The decision of 1927

✅ During 1927 Chiang decided that the communists were becoming too powerful. ✅ In areas where communists were strong, landlords were being attacked and land seized; ✅ it was time to destroy an embarrassing ally.

⚠️ Read those two sentences as cause and judgement. The cause is specific and social: land seizure in communist-controlled areas. The judgement - "an embarrassing ally" - is exact, because the embarrassment was political rather than moral. ⚠️ Chiang's sympathies lay with businessmen and landowners, and a campaign fuelled by promises to expropriate landowners could not continue once the campaign was won. ⚠️ The alliance's usefulness expired at precisely the moment its cost became payable.

### 3. The purification movement and how to write about it

✅ All communists were expelled from the Nationalist Party ✅ and a terrible "purification movement" was launched in which thousands of communists, trade union and peasant leaders were massacred; ✅ some estimates put the total murdered as high as 250,000. ✅ The communists had been checked, the warlords were under control and Chiang was the military and political leader of China.

⚠️ This is the first of four places in this session where a mass-death figure appears, and it establishes the attribution discipline. The source does not state that 250,000 people were murdered. It states that *some estimates put the total as high as* 250,000. ⚠️ Those are different claims, and an answer must reproduce the second rather than the first. ⚠️ The safe written form is: "the purification movement of 1927 massacred thousands of communists, trade union and peasant leaders, with some estimates putting the total as high as 250,000." ❌ Never write "250,000 were killed" as a flat assertion, and ❌ never combine this figure with any other figure in this topic.

⚠️ The analytical consequence of the purge is more important than its scale for most questions. It destroyed the communist presence in the cities - the trade union and peasant *leaders* were precisely the organisers - and thereby removed the urban option permanently. ⚠️ The rural turn of Lesson 7 was in part a forced migration.

### 4. What the Nationalist government delivered

✅ The Kuomintang government proved to be a great disappointment for the majority of the Chinese people. ✅ Chiang could claim to have achieved Sun's first principle, nationalism, ✅ but relying as he did on the support of wealthy landowners, no moves were made towards democracy or land reform, ✅ though there was some limited progress with the building of more schools and roads.

| Sun's principle | ✅ Nationalist record by the 1930s | ⚠️ Why |
|---|---|---|
| Nationalism | Achieved, in the sense of reunification and national leadership | Deliverable by military means alone |
| Democracy | No moves made | Sun's own education clause permitted indefinite postponement; and a regime built on a narrow social coalition had no incentive |
| Land reform / livelihood | No moves made | The government depended on the support of wealthy landowners - the principle's internal contradiction from Lesson 4, now operating |
| Other | Some limited progress with more schools and roads | Real but small; not the scale the country's crisis required |

⚠️ This table is the answer to a standard 10-mark demand and should be memorised in this shape. ⚠️ It also states the deepest reason the Nationalists eventually lost: a government that has achieved the easiest of its own three stated aims and abandoned the other two has told its population what it is.

### Comparative note: 1928 against 1911

⚠️ Compare what the two moments produced. ✅ 1911 removed a dynasty and produced no state. ✅ 1928 produced a state with a single recognised military and political leader, a capital, an army and an international presence - a genuine achievement measured against the Warlord Era - but a state whose social reach stopped at the edge of the cities and whose programme had contracted to one principle out of three.

⚠️ The formulation worth carrying into an answer is that the Nationalists solved the *first half* of the problem posed in 1911 and refused the second. They rebuilt a centre; they did not rebuild the relationship between that centre and the four-fifths of the population living in villages. ⚠️ Their rivals spent the next twenty-one years doing exactly that, and the outcome of 1949 followed.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems on the causes of the communist victory, on the failure of the Nationalist regime, or on the relationship between nationalism and social reform all route through this lesson. ⚠️ It is also the natural place to demonstrate that the candidate can separate a military outcome from a political one.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Give the campaign in one sentence with its two sources of success; give the rupture with its stated cause; give the purification movement with the estimate properly attributed; then give the four-row record table and close on the formulation that the regime achieved one of its own three principles and abandoned the other two.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Stating 250,000 deaths as fact. ✅ Some estimates put the total murdered as high as that figure.
2. ⚠️ Dating the split to the capture of Beijing in 1928. ✅ The decision and the purge belong to 1927; ✅ Beijing fell in 1928.
3. ⚠️ Saying the Nationalists achieved nothing. ✅ Nationalism was achieved and ✅ there was some limited progress with schools and roads.
4. ⚠️ Treating the split as the communists' choice. ✅ They were expelled and attacked; ✅ the decision was Chiang's.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Northern March began in 1926 from Canton, to destroy the warlords of central and northern China.
2. ✅ Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking captured by 1927; Beijing taken in 1928.
3. ✅ Success drew on massive peasant support attracted by communist land promises, and on a Shanghai workers' rising organised by Zhou En-lai, who belonged to both parties.
4. ✅ During 1927 Chiang decided the communists were becoming too powerful; in areas where they were strong, landlords were attacked and land seized; it was time to destroy an embarrassing ally.
5. ✅ All communists were expelled from the Nationalist Party; a "purification movement" massacred thousands of communists, trade union and peasant leaders; some estimates put the total murdered as high as 250,000.
6. ✅ Outcome: communists checked, warlords under control, Chiang the military and political leader of China.
7. ✅ The Kuomintang government proved a great disappointment: nationalism achieved; no moves towards democracy or land reform, because of reliance on wealthy landowners; some limited progress with schools and roads.
8. ⚠️ Attribution rule established here: report the estimate as an estimate and never merge figures across episodes.
9. ⚠️ Consequence of the purge: the urban organisers were destroyed, so the rural turn was partly forced.
10. ⚠️ Master formulation: 1928 rebuilt a centre without rebuilding its relationship to the villages - the half-solution that lost the civil war.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why 1928 can be described both as the high point of the Chinese republic and as the beginning of its defeat.

**Model answer:** It is the high point because reunification was genuinely achieved: starting from Canton in 1926 the Northern March took Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking by 1927 and Beijing in 1928, the warlords were brought under control, and Chiang emerged as the military and political leader of China - the first time since the 1911 revolution that the country had a recognised national centre. It is the beginning of defeat because the same campaign produced the rupture that created a permanent armed rival, and because the government that emerged abandoned two of its own three founding principles. Chiang could claim nationalism, but relying on the support of wealthy landowners he made no moves towards democracy or land reform, achieving only some limited progress with schools and roads, and the regime was a great disappointment to the majority of the population. A state had been rebuilt at the centre; its relationship with the villages where most Chinese lived had not been. That unfilled space is what the communists occupied over the next two decades.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the 1927 split as an ideological quarrel between two parties that had never really agreed. The recorded cause is concrete and social - in areas where communists were strong, landlords were being attacked and land seized - and the decision was taken by a leader whose sympathies lay with businessmen and landowners at the moment when the alliance's military usefulness had been exhausted. An answer that identifies the social interest at stake explains both the timing and the ferocity of the purge; one that speaks only of incompatible ideologies explains neither.

## Lesson 7 - The rural turn, the extermination campaigns and the Long March: survival converted into strategy

Progress: 7/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the strategic change after the breach; the Chinese Soviet Republic at Juichin in 1931 and the first All-China Congress of Soviets; the five extermination campaigns of 1930-34; the encirclement of 1934; the Long March of 1934-5 in its recorded dimensions; the survivors at Yenan; the historiographical dispute over what the march was; and the ten years of divergence that followed

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on Mao Zedong and the Chinese Communist Party, including the change of strategy after the breach, Mao's election as chairman of the Central Executive Committee in 1931, the proclamation of the Chinese Soviet Republic at Juichin and the first All-China Congress of Soviets on 7 November 1931, the five extermination campaigns, the breakthrough of October 1934, the recorded distance and duration of the march, the quoted description by the American journalist Edgar Snow, the refuge at Yenan, the 2005 reinterpretation by Jung Chang and Jon Halliday and the response of historians generally
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the march in the figures the source records

```text
   BEFORE
   ------
   1928-30  Strategy changed: mass support among the PEASANTS instead of
            capturing industrial towns, where insurrections had failed
   1930-34  FIVE "extermination campaigns" by Chiang against the base
   1931     Mao elected chairman of the Central Executive Committee
            Chinese Soviet Republic proclaimed at JUICHIN
            7 November 1931: first All-China Congress of Soviets,
            delegates from 15 soviet areas
            Base in the mountains between Hunan and Kiangsi;
            energies on SURVIVAL and on building the RED ARMY

   THE CRISIS
   ----------
   early 1934  Mao's base area SURROUNDED by Nationalist armies poised
               for the final destruction of Chinese communism
               Decision: break through and set up another power base

   THE MARCH
   ---------
   October 1934   breakthrough achieved
                  ALMOST 100,000 communists set out
                  about 6,000 miles in 368 days

                  In the words of the American journalist Edgar Snow,
                  they "crossed 18 mountain ranges, 5 of which were
                  snow-capped, and 24 rivers. They passed through 12
                  different provinces, occupied 62 cities, and broke
                  through enveloping armies of 10 different provincial
                  warlords, besides defeating, eluding, or out-manoeuvring
                  the various forces of government troops sent against them."

   ARRIVAL
   -------
   1935   20,000 SURVIVORS reach YENAN in Shensi province
          - the last surviving communist base in China
          - controlled by the guerrilla leader Kao Kang
          - the Shensi communists, NOT ENTIRELY WILLINGLY, accepted
            Mao as leader
          - a new base and a soviet were organised
          - Mao able to control the provinces of Shensi and Kansu

   AFTER
   -----
   1935-45  "During the ten years following the Long March the communists
            continued to gain support, while Chiang and the KMT steadily
            lost popularity."
```

⚠️ Two features of this visual are the teaching. First, the march begins and ends with *base areas*: it is a relocation, not a campaign, and its purpose was to exchange an untenable position for a tenable one. Second, the arrival was not triumphal - the existing communists at Yenan accepted Mao's leadership not entirely willingly, which tells you that his dominance was consolidated through the march rather than confirmed by it.

### 1. The strategic change, stated exactly

✅ After the communist breach with the Nationalists, Mao was responsible for changing the party's strategy: they would concentrate on winning mass support among the peasants rather than trying to capture industrial towns, where several communist insurrections had already failed because of the strength of the Nationalists.

⚠️ Three claims are packed into that sentence and each has an exam use.

1. ⚠️ **It was a change.** The party had a previous strategy and abandoned it. Say so; it demonstrates that you know Chinese communism did not begin as a peasant movement.
2. ⚠️ **Mao was responsible for it.** This is the basis of his later authority - not seniority, not theoretical output, but having been right about the central strategic question.
3. ⚠️ **The reason was demonstrated failure.** The urban insurrections had already failed because of Nationalist strength. ⚠️ The rural turn was empirical before it was doctrinal, and saying this protects an answer from the lazy claim that Mao adapted Marxism out of ideological creativity alone.

### 2. Institutional form: the Juichin republic

✅ In 1931 Mao was elected chairman of the Central Executive Committee of the party, and from then on he gradually consolidated his position as the real leader of Chinese communism. ✅ The Chinese Soviet Republic was proclaimed at Juichin in 1931, and on 7 November 1931 the first All-China Congress of Soviets was held there. ✅ It was attended by delegates from fifteen soviet areas.

⚠️ This detail is more significant than its brevity suggests, and candidates who use it stand out. The communists did not spend the early 1930s as a guerrilla band; they proclaimed a *republic*, held a *congress*, and had *fifteen* constituent soviet areas. ⚠️ In other words, they were already practising government - taxation, land policy, administration, military organisation - a full eighteen years before 1949. ⚠️ This is the empirical foundation of the thesis that the communists won by out-governing rather than merely out-fighting, and it is the reason the 1949 transition to rule was relatively rapid.

### 3. Encirclement and the decision to move

✅ Mao and his supporters spent most of their energies on survival as Chiang carried out five "extermination campaigns" against them between 1930 and 1934. ✅ They took to the mountains between Hunan and Kiangsi provinces and concentrated on building up the Red Army.

✅ However, early in 1934 Mao's base area was surrounded by Nationalist armies poised for the final destruction of Chinese communism. ✅ Mao decided that the only chance of survival was to break through Chiang's lines and set up another power base somewhere else. ✅ In October 1934 the breakthrough was achieved.

⚠️ Note the framing the source itself uses: *the only chance of survival*. The Long March was not a manoeuvre chosen from a menu; it was the alternative to annihilation. ⚠️ An answer that presents it as a bold strategic offensive has misread it, and will then be unable to explain why 80,000 of the roughly 100,000 who set out did not arrive.

### 4. The march, and how to handle its figures

✅ Almost 100,000 communists set out on the remarkable Long March, which was to become part of Chinese legend. ✅ They covered about 6,000 miles in 368 days. ✅ In the words of American journalist Edgar Snow, they "crossed 18 mountain ranges, 5 of which were snow-capped, and 24 rivers. They passed through 12 different provinces, occupied 62 cities, and broke through enveloping armies of 10 different provincial warlords, besides defeating, eluding, or out-manoeuvring the various forces of government troops sent against them."

✅ Eventually the 20,000 survivors found refuge at Yenan in Shensi province: ✅ this was the last surviving communist base in China and was controlled by the guerrilla leader Kao Kang. ✅ The Shensi communists, not entirely willingly, accepted Mao as leader, and a new base and a soviet were organised. ✅ Mao was able to control the provinces of Shensi and Kansu.

⚠️ Two controls apply to these numbers. First, the Snow passage is a **quotation from a journalist** and must be attributed to him in an answer, not presented as an archival finding - which is exactly the point the next section turns on. Second, the arithmetic of survival should be stated as the source states it: almost 100,000 set out and 20,000 survivors reached Yenan. ❌ Do not convert that into a casualty figure, because the difference includes those who died, those who deserted, those who were detached to other areas and those who stayed behind, and the source does not decompose it.

### 5. The historiographical dispute, and why it is examinable

✅ According to writers Jung Chang and Jon Halliday in their book *Mao: The Unknown Story*, published in 2005, the march was vastly exaggerated and was in fact nothing like as heroic as legend claimed. ✅ They even suggested that Mao's "breakout" in October 1934 was actually permitted by Chiang Kai-shek, because he preferred the communists to be in the north where he could box them in while he extended Nationalist control over the south-west. ✅ This interpretation was welcomed by Mao's critics, ✅ but historians generally gave a more balanced judgement: while agreeing that there had been some exaggeration in accounts of the march in order to show Mao and the communists in the best possible light, ✅ they rejected the Jung Chang and Halliday interpretation as "more fantasy than fact".

⚠️ This is the first genuine historiographical set-piece in the topic and it should be used, because it lets a candidate demonstrate the difference between a fact, an interpretation and a contested interpretation inside three sentences. ⚠️ The correct handling has three moves:

1. ⚠️ State the event with its recorded dimensions.
2. ⚠️ State the revisionist challenge accurately, including its most provocative element - that the breakout was permitted.
3. ⚠️ State the scholarly response accurately: partial concession on exaggeration, rejection of the interpretation as a whole.

⚠️ The balanced verdict to write is that the Long March was simultaneously a real military-political achievement and an object of deliberate mythmaking, and that recognising the second does not dissolve the first. ⚠️ The strongest version adds the functional point: whatever the march physically was, its *political* effect is undisputed and measurable - it preserved a leadership, settled a leadership contest and supplied a founding narrative that the party used for the rest of the century.

### 6. The ten years that followed

✅ During the ten years following the Long March the communists continued to gain support, while Chiang and the Nationalists steadily lost popularity.

⚠️ That single sentence is the bridge to Lesson 8 and it frames the question correctly: the decade from 1935 to 1945 was not a stalemate but a *divergence*, and the examinable task is to explain what drove the two curves apart. ⚠️ The next lesson gives the five recorded reasons.

### Objection and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** If almost 100,000 set out and 20,000 arrived, the Long March was a catastrophic defeat. Calling it a turning point in the communists' favour looks like reading the ending backwards.

⚠️ **The reply.** The objection is right about the military balance and wrong about the criterion. ⚠️ The measure of success for an organisation facing annihilation is not casualties but continuity, and on that measure the march succeeded completely: the leadership survived, an army survived, and the movement re-established itself in a defensible region beyond the reach of the forces that had encircled it. ⚠️ Three further outcomes were decisive. The march settled the leadership question, since Mao emerged as the dominant figure even though the Shensi communists accepted him not entirely willingly. It relocated the movement to the north-west - which, as Lesson 9 shows, placed it precisely where the Japanese invasion would make patriotic resistance the central political issue. And it produced the founding narrative. ⚠️ The balanced formulation is that the Long March was a military disaster that functioned as a political rescue, and that this is exactly why the mythmaking around it was both inevitable and, in part, justified.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems ask about the significance of the Long March, about Mao's rise, or about why the communists survived the 1930s. ⚠️ The word "significance" is the cue for the functional analysis above rather than for narration of the route.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Open by naming the situation - encirclement and the prospect of final destruction. ⚠️ Give the recorded dimensions in one sentence. ⚠️ Then give the four functions: survival of the leadership and army; settlement of the leadership question; relocation to a region that became strategically decisive after 1937; and the creation of a legitimating narrative. ⚠️ Close with the historiographical qualification, attributed.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Presenting the march as an offensive. ✅ It was the only chance of survival after encirclement.
2. ❌ Attributing the Snow description to an official record. ✅ It is a quoted passage from an American journalist.
3. ⚠️ Treating the 2005 revisionist reading as accepted. ✅ Historians generally rejected it as "more fantasy than fact" while conceding some exaggeration.
4. ⚠️ Saying Mao emerged as unchallenged leader at Yenan. ✅ The Shensi communists accepted him not entirely willingly.
5. ⚠️ Overlooking the Juichin republic. ✅ A Chinese Soviet Republic was proclaimed in 1931 with fifteen soviet areas represented - evidence of governing practice long before 1949.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Strategic change after the breach: mass support among peasants rather than capture of industrial towns, where insurrections had failed against Nationalist strength; Mao was responsible for it.
2. ✅ 1931: Mao elected chairman of the Central Executive Committee; Chinese Soviet Republic proclaimed at Juichin; first All-China Congress of Soviets on 7 November 1931 with delegates from fifteen soviet areas.
3. ✅ Five extermination campaigns, 1930-34; base in the mountains between Hunan and Kiangsi; energies on survival and on building the Red Army.
4. ✅ Early 1934: base area surrounded; Mao decided the only chance of survival was to break through and establish a new base.
5. ✅ October 1934 breakthrough; almost 100,000 set out; about 6,000 miles in 368 days.
6. ✅ Edgar Snow's description: 18 mountain ranges, 5 snow-capped, 24 rivers, 12 provinces, 62 cities, armies of 10 provincial warlords - quoted, and attributed to him.
7. ✅ 20,000 survivors reached Yenan in Shensi, the last surviving communist base, controlled by the guerrilla leader Kao Kang; the Shensi communists accepted Mao not entirely willingly; Mao controlled Shensi and Kansu.
8. ✅ Jung Chang and Jon Halliday, 2005: the march was vastly exaggerated and the breakout was permitted by Chiang; historians generally conceded some exaggeration but rejected the interpretation as "more fantasy than fact".
9. ✅ During the ten years following the march the communists continued to gain support while Chiang and the Nationalists steadily lost popularity.
10. ⚠️ Four functions of the march: survival, leadership settlement, strategic relocation to the north-west, and the founding narrative.
11. ⚠️ Verdict formulation: a military disaster that functioned as a political rescue.

### Concept check

**Question:** How should a candidate handle the Jung Chang and Halliday reinterpretation of the Long March in a 15-mark answer, and what does the correct handling demonstrate?

**Model answer:** It should be stated accurately, attributed and then placed against the scholarly response, in that order. Jung Chang and Jon Halliday argued in their 2005 book that the march was vastly exaggerated and nothing like as heroic as legend claimed, and even suggested that Mao's breakout in October 1934 was permitted by Chiang Kai-shek, who preferred the communists boxed in to the north while he extended control over the south-west. Historians generally gave a more balanced judgement: they agreed that accounts had been exaggerated in order to present Mao and the communists in the best light, but rejected the wider interpretation as more fantasy than fact. The correct handling therefore concedes the point about mythmaking, declines the point about collusion, and adds the functional argument that survives both - that whatever the march physically involved, it preserved the leadership and the army, settled the leadership question in Mao's favour, moved the movement to the north-west where the Japanese war would make patriotic resistance decisive, and created a legitimating narrative. Doing this demonstrates the ability to separate an event, an interpretation of it and the scholarly status of that interpretation, which is precisely what a historiographically aware answer is rewarded for.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a striking revisionist claim as the latest and therefore the best account. Recency is not authority. The recorded position is that historians generally rejected this reading while accepting the narrower point about exaggeration, and an answer that adopts the whole revisionist case will be marked down for misrepresenting the state of the scholarship - just as an answer that repeats the heroic legend uncritically will be marked down for ignoring it.
