## Lesson 8 - Why the peasants moved: the five recorded reasons, and the comparative governance argument in full

Progress: 8/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Nationalist inefficiency and corruption in government; unreformed factory conditions; unrelieved peasant poverty against a flexible communist land policy; the New Life Movement of 1934 and its reception; and the fifth and crucial reason, resistance to Japan, which opens the next lesson

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's numbered section on why Mao and the communists gained support, covering all five recorded reasons with their evidence, including the two-stage communist land policy and its political effect on smaller landowners, the New Life Movement of 1934 with the historian Rana Mitter's assessment of it, and the statement that the absence of effective Nationalist resistance to Japan was the crucial factor in communist success
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the five reasons, sorted by what kind of failure each one is

```text
   REASON                          TYPE OF FAILURE        WHO NOTICED
   ================================================================
   [1] Inefficiency and            ADMINISTRATIVE         everyone
       corruption of the KMT       + POLITICAL
       in government               (no reform to offer;
                                   too much time on
                                   industrialists, bankers
                                   and landowners; no
                                   effective attempt to
                                   organise mass support)

   [2] Little improvement in       REGULATORY             urban workers
       factory conditions          (laws existed, e.g.
                                   against child labour
                                   in textile mills;
                                   often NOT APPLIED;
                                   widespread bribery of
                                   inspectors; Chiang not
                                   prepared to offend his
                                   industrial supporters)

   [3] No improvement in           SOCIAL + DISTRIBUTIVE  the rural majority
       peasant poverty             (droughts and bad
                                   harvests, widespread
                                   rural famine in the
                                   early 1930s, while
                                   rice and wheat were
                                   HOARDED IN CITIES by
                                   profiteering merchants;
                                   high taxes; forced labour)
                                       vs
                                   COMMUNIST LAND POLICY,
                                   in two stages - see below

   [4] The New Life Movement       IDEOLOGICAL            intellectuals and
       (1934) was controversial    (a Confucian revival   modern progressive
                                   offered as the answer  opinion
                                   to national weakness)

   [5] The KMT put up NO           NATIONAL               the whole country
       EFFECTIVE RESISTANCE        <-- recorded as THE    -> Lesson 9
       TO THE JAPANESE                 CRUCIAL FACTOR
   ================================================================
   Read downward: the failures widen from administration, to one class,
   to the majority, to the educated, to the nation itself. The fifth
   is decisive because it is the only one that discredits the regime
   in every category at once.
```

⚠️ Sorting the five reasons by *type* rather than listing them is what turns a recall answer into an analytical one. The list is memorable; the ordering principle is what earns the higher band.

### 1. Inefficiency and corruption in government

✅ The Nationalists had little to offer in the way of reform, spent too much time looking after the interests of industrialists, bankers and landowners, and made no effective attempts to organise mass support. ✅ This provided the main opportunity for Mao and the communists to win support.

⚠️ The third clause is the one to underline, because it names a *capability* the regime never built. Organising mass support is not propaganda; it means having people in villages and workplaces who belong to the party, know the locality and can act on instructions. ⚠️ The Nationalist government held cities and armies; the communist movement built precisely that penetrating organisation, which is what its soviet areas, peasant associations and cadres were. ⚠️ The competition, in one line, was between a regime that administered from above and a movement that organised from below.

### 2. Factory conditions and the limits of law

✅ Poor industrial working conditions continued, in spite of laws designed to remove the worst abuses, such as child labour in textile mills. ✅ Often these laws were not applied: there was widespread bribery of inspectors, and Chiang himself was not prepared to offend his industrial supporters.

⚠️ Notice the structure of this failure, which is different from the first. The law existed. The failure was in enforcement, and the enforcement failure had two named causes: bribery at the inspection level and unwillingness at the top. ⚠️ This is a useful and transferable observation about regimes that depend on a narrow economic coalition: they can legislate but cannot enforce against their own base. ⚠️ It also has a direct consequence for the topic's central comparison - the communist claim to honest administration was credible partly because the Nationalist claim to lawful administration was visibly not.

### 3. Peasant poverty against a flexible land policy

✅ In the early 1930s there was a series of droughts and bad harvests which caused widespread famine in rural areas. ✅ At the same time there was usually plenty of rice and wheat being hoarded in the cities by profiteering merchants. ✅ In addition there were high taxes and forced labour.

⚠️ The juxtaposition in those two sentences is the politically explosive fact: rural famine coexisting with urban hoarding. Scarcity that is visibly distributional rather than absolute converts hunger into a political grievance with an identifiable villain, and the villain in this case was associated with the cities the Nationalist government represented.

✅ In contrast, the land policy followed in areas controlled by the communists was much more attractive. ✅ At first, in the south, they seized the estates of rich landlords and redistributed them among the peasants. ✅ After the temporary truce with the Nationalists during the war with Japan, the communists compromised, and confined themselves to a policy of restricting rents and making sure that even the poorest labourers got a small piece of land. ✅ This less drastic policy had the advantage of winning the support of the smaller landowners, as well as the peasants.

⚠️ This two-stage policy is the single most examinable piece of evidence in the entire topic, and most candidates know only the first stage. Set out what the second stage achieved:

| | ✅ Stage 1, in the south | ✅ Stage 2, after the truce |
|---|---|---|
| Content | Seizure of rich landlords' estates and redistribution to peasants | Rent restriction; a small piece of land even for the poorest labourers |
| Target group | Landless and poor peasants | Peasants **and** smaller landowners |
| Political effect | Maximum mobilisation, maximum enmity | Broader coalition at lower cost |
| ⚠️ Strategic reading | Revolutionary purity | A class alliance chosen for wartime conditions |

⚠️ The second stage is evidence of something that most accounts of Chinese communism obscure: tactical flexibility about class. A movement that can *narrow* its expropriation in order to widen its coalition is not behaving doctrinally; it is behaving like a government calculating support. ⚠️ Compare this deliberately with Lesson 4's finding that the Nationalists could not answer the land question at all because of their coalition, and the asymmetry is complete: one party adjusted its land policy to enlarge its coalition, the other adjusted its land policy to nothing because its coalition forbade it.

### 4. The New Life Movement and the ideological failure

✅ In the early 1930s Chiang began to advocate a return to the traditional values of Confucianism, the traditional Chinese religion. ✅ In 1934 he introduced the New Life Movement which, he claimed, was a unique secular, rational and modern Chinese version of Confucianism. ✅ It was meant to mobilise the population and to revive the country's "innate morality", thereby helping to create a healthy society and a strong and united country.

✅ However, in the words of historian Rana Mitter: "The movement was not ultimately successful, as its formal prescriptions, including not spitting in the street, and queuing up in an orderly fashion, came over as trivial in comparison with the much larger issues of national coherence which dogged twentieth-century China." ✅ Unfortunately many May the Fourth supporters and other modern progressive thinkers protested that this was another backward step designed to return China to its oppressive imperial past.

⚠️ Two distinct criticisms are recorded here and they should not be merged. Mitter's is a criticism of **proportion**: the prescriptions were trivial relative to the problem. The May Fourth criticism is a criticism of **direction**: cultural restoration was the wrong answer to national weakness. ⚠️ Together they explain why the movement failed with the one constituency the Nationalists most needed to retain - educated urban opinion, the milieu from which both parties recruited their cadres.

⚠️ There is also a comparative observation worth one sentence in a 20-mark answer. Both parties attempted moral mobilisation of the population. The Nationalist version asked people to behave better within an existing order; the communist version asked them to change the order and gave them land while doing so. ⚠️ Where a mobilising ideology offers a material stake, it competes with one that offers only conduct.

### 5. The crucial reason, stated as the source states it

✅ The Nationalists put up no effective resistance to the Japanese. ✅ This was the crucial factor in the communist success.

⚠️ That ranking is the source's own and should be reproduced. It also disciplines an answer's structure: the first four reasons explain a steady erosion of support, and the fifth explains why the erosion became irreversible. ⚠️ Lesson 9 takes it up in full.

### Objection and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** These five reasons are all criticisms of the Nationalists. Does that not make the communist victory merely the by-product of someone else's failure, with no positive explanation at all?

⚠️ **The reply.** Two of the five are genuinely comparative rather than merely negative, and they are the ones that carry the positive explanation. ✅ The third reason sets peasant poverty against an actively operated and deliberately adjusted communist land policy, and ✅ the recorded reasons for the 1949 triumph add discipline and honest, fair administration on the communist side. ⚠️ So the accurate formulation is neither "the communists won" nor "the Nationalists lost" but something more precise: the communists constructed, in the areas they controlled, a demonstrable alternative government - land policy, army discipline, honest administration - at exactly the time when the incumbent regime was demonstrating that it could not or would not supply any of the three. ⚠️ Voters, in a country that had no elections, were choosing between two observable administrations.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This is the heart of the most likely question on the topic: why the communists won in 1949. ⚠️ Stems may also come at it through the failure of the Nationalist regime, or through the broader Asian question of why peasant-based movements succeeded in some countries and failed in others.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Give the five reasons in the source's order, but sort them by type as the visual does. ⚠️ Spend disproportionate space on reason three, because it contains the two-stage land policy that most competitors will miss. ⚠️ Name reason five as the crucial factor and hand it to the war. ⚠️ Close on the comparative formulation: two observable administrations, one building capacity in the villages and one losing it in the cities.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Giving only the first stage of communist land policy. ✅ The second stage - rent restriction and a small plot for the poorest - won over smaller landowners as well.
2. ⚠️ Treating the famine of the early 1930s as a natural disaster only. ✅ Grain was being hoarded in the cities at the same time, alongside high taxes and forced labour.
3. ⚠️ Describing the New Life Movement as simply traditionalist. ✅ Chiang claimed it was a unique secular, rational and modern Chinese version of Confucianism; the criticisms were of triviality and of direction.
4. ⚠️ Ranking the reasons by your own judgement. ✅ The absence of effective resistance to Japan is recorded as the crucial factor.
5. ⚠️ Writing only about Nationalist failure. ⚠️ The comparative element - discipline, administration, land - is where the positive explanation lives.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Reason 1: Nationalist inefficiency and corruption; little reform on offer; too much time on industrialists, bankers and landowners; no effective attempt to organise mass support - the main opportunity for the communists.
2. ✅ Reason 2: factory conditions unimproved despite laws such as those against child labour in textile mills; laws often not applied; widespread bribery of inspectors; Chiang unwilling to offend industrial supporters.
3. ✅ Reason 3: early-1930s droughts and bad harvests, widespread rural famine, while rice and wheat were hoarded in cities by profiteering merchants; high taxes and forced labour.
4. ✅ Communist land policy stage 1, in the south: seizure of rich landlords' estates and redistribution to peasants.
5. ✅ Stage 2, after the truce during the Japanese war: rent restriction and a small piece of land even for the poorest labourers - winning smaller landowners as well as peasants.
6. ✅ Reason 4: the New Life Movement of 1934, claimed as a unique secular, rational and modern Chinese version of Confucianism, meant to revive "innate morality"; Rana Mitter records that its formal prescriptions, including not spitting in the street and queuing in an orderly fashion, seemed trivial against the much larger issues of national coherence; May Fourth supporters called it a backward step towards an oppressive imperial past.
7. ✅ Reason 5: no effective Nationalist resistance to the Japanese - the crucial factor.
8. ⚠️ Sorting principle: administrative, regulatory, social-distributive, ideological, national - widening at each step.
9. ⚠️ Master formulation: two observable administrations, one building capacity in the villages and one losing it in the cities.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the second stage of communist land policy more revealing about the party's character than the first?

**Model answer:** The first stage - seizing the estates of rich landlords in the south and redistributing them among the peasants - is what a revolutionary party would be expected to do, and it demonstrates only commitment. The second stage is revealing because it involves deliberate restraint for political gain. After the temporary truce with the Nationalists during the war with Japan, the communists compromised and confined themselves to restricting rents and ensuring that even the poorest labourers received a small piece of land, and this less drastic policy had the advantage of winning the support of smaller landowners as well as of peasants. That is a calculation about coalition size, not an application of doctrine: the party narrowed its expropriation in order to widen its base at the moment when national unity against an invader made a broad coalition valuable. It shows an organisation behaving like a prospective government rather than a sect, and it contrasts exactly with the Nationalists, whose dependence on wealthy landowners prevented them from having any operative land policy at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the five recorded reasons as a list of Nationalist sins. Two of them are explicitly comparative, and the explanatory weight sits in the comparison: peasants in contested regions were choosing between an administration that taxed heavily, permitted forced labour and presided over hoarding, and one that redistributed or restricted rents, maintained disciplined armies and administered honestly. An answer built only on Nationalist failure cannot explain why support went to this particular alternative rather than to any other.

---

## Lesson 9 - Japan, the Sian incident and the Second United Front: how an external war reordered an internal contest

Progress: 9/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the occupation of Manchuria in 1931 and Chiang's priorities; the Sian incident of 1936; the Second United Front and what it gave the communists; the full-scale war from 1937 and the geography it created; the growth of the base areas from 1937 to 1945; China's place in the wartime alliance; and the revisionist defence of Chiang Kai-shek

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's fifth recorded reason for communist success and its account of the Sian incident; its record of the advantages the new alliance brought the communists; the statement that when full-scale war broke out in 1937 Nationalist forces were quickly defeated and most of eastern China occupied while Chiang retreated westwards; the recorded growth of the base areas; the section on China and the Second World War including the renunciation of concessions in January 1943 and the promised Security Council seat; and the revisionist assessment of Chiang by his biographer Jay Taylor
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the geography the war created

```text
   1931  Japanese occupy MANCHURIA, and are obviously preparing to bring
         the neighbouring provinces of northern China under control
                        |
   1936  Chiang's priority: "it was more important to destroy the
         communists than to resist the Japanese"
         -> he moves into SOUTH SHENSI to attack Mao
                        |
                        v
         THE SIAN INCIDENT
         Chiang is taken prisoner BY HIS OWN TROOPS, mostly Manchurians,
         incensed at the Japanese invasion of their home region.
         They demand he turn against the Japanese. At first he is unwilling.
         Only after the prominent communist ZHOU EN-LAI comes to see him
         at SIAN does he agree to a fresh alliance and a NATIONAL FRONT.
                        |
                        v
   1937  FULL-SCALE WAR WITH JAPAN
         +-------------------------------------------------------------+
         |  EASTERN CHINA (coast, cities, industry, ports)              |
         |  -> Nationalist forces QUICKLY DEFEATED                      |
         |  -> most of eastern China OCCUPIED by Japan                  |
         |  -> Chiang RETREATS WESTWARDS                                |
         +-------------------------------------------------------------+
         |  THE NORTH (Shensi and beyond)                               |
         |  -> communists UNDEFEATED in Shensi                          |
         |  -> present themselves as PATRIOTIC NATIONALISTS             |
         |  -> lead an effective GUERRILLA campaign against the Japanese|
         |  -> massive support among peasants AND middle classes,       |
         |     appalled at Japanese arrogance and brutality             |
         +-------------------------------------------------------------+
                        |
                        v
   THE MEASURABLE RESULT
         1937:  5 base areas controlling  12 million people
         1945: 19 base areas controlling 100 million people
```

⚠️ The two-box middle of this diagram is the whole lesson. The war did not merely damage the Nationalists and help the communists in some general way; it *allocated different tasks to each party by geography*. One had to fight conventional battles against a superior army in the places it held and lose them; the other operated irregularly in territory the invader could not fully administer, and gained.

### 1. The priority that destroyed a reputation

✅ The Japanese occupied Manchuria in 1931 and were obviously preparing to bring the neighbouring provinces of northern China under their control. ✅ Chiang seemed to think it was more important to destroy the communists than to resist the Japanese, and moved into south Shensi to attack Mao in 1936.

⚠️ It is important to be fair about the reasoning before judging it. From Chiang's position, an internal armed rival that had already survived five campaigns was a permanent threat to the state he had just rebuilt, and fighting Japan with a hostile army in his rear was militarily unattractive. ⚠️ But the political cost of the choice was enormous and immediate, because it inverted the first of Sun's three principles. A party whose one achieved principle was nationalism was now visibly prioritising a civil war over national defence.

### 2. The Sian incident

✅ Here a remarkable incident took place: Chiang was taken prisoner by some of his own troops, mostly Manchurians, who were incensed at the Japanese invasion. ✅ They demanded that Chiang should turn against the Japanese, but at first he was unwilling. ✅ Only after the prominent communist Zhou En-lai came to see him at Sian did he agree to a fresh alliance with the Communist Party and a national front against the Japanese.

⚠️ Three things in this episode are examinable and are routinely missed.

1. ⚠️ **The captors were his own soldiers, and Manchurians.** The pressure to resist Japan came from inside the Nationalist army, from men whose home region was under occupation. That is direct evidence that the priority was unsustainable within the regime itself.
2. ⚠️ **He was at first unwilling even under arrest.** The commitment was extracted, not volunteered.
3. ⚠️ **It was a communist who negotiated his release into an alliance.** Zhou En-lai - the same man who had organised the Shanghai rising in 1927 and had belonged to both parties - now appears as the broker of a national front. ⚠️ The political dividend of that role was large and lasting.

### 3. What the Second United Front gave each side

⚠️ The core account records the alliance and its consequences without a formal name or span; ✅ the standard designation and dates, supplied here so that a candidate can name what a question refers to, are the **Second United Front of 1937-1945**, formed to resist the Japanese invasion and persisting, with deep mutual distrust, until the end of the war.

✅ The new alliance brought great advantages for the communists: the Nationalist extermination campaigns ceased for the time being, and consequently the Communist Party was secure in its Shensi base.

⚠️ Put that in the language of the topic's central thesis. The most valuable thing the alliance gave the communists was *time in secure territory*, and secure territory is the precondition for building administration. ⚠️ From 1937 the communists were no longer a movement surviving encirclement; they were a government of a defined region with an army, an external enemy that legitimised them and an internal rival that had promised not to attack them.

⚠️ What did the Nationalists get? A united national front and the political legitimacy of leading it - and the burden of conventional war. ⚠️ That asymmetry is the point.

### 4. The war and its consequences for each party

✅ When full-scale war broke out with Japan in 1937, the Nationalist forces were quickly defeated and most of eastern China was occupied by the Japanese as Chiang retreated westwards. ✅ This enabled the communists, undefeated in Shensi, to present themselves as patriotic nationalists, leading an effective guerrilla campaign against the Japanese in the north. ✅ This won them massive support among the peasants and middle classes, who were appalled at Japanese arrogance and brutality.

✅ Whereas in 1937 the Communist Party had five base areas controlling 12 million people, by 1945 this had grown to nineteen base areas controlling 100 million people.

⚠️ Those two figures are the most useful quantitative evidence in the topic, and they should be quoted in this exact form. ⚠️ What they measure is not popularity but *administered population*: the communists were governing a hundred million people before the civil war resumed. ⚠️ That is why the transition to national government in 1949 was faster and more competent than anybody outside China expected: the apparatus already existed at provincial scale.

⚠️ Note also the social breadth. ✅ Support came from peasants **and middle classes**. A movement that had begun among intellectuals, turned to peasants for survival, and now attracted middle-class support on patriotic grounds had assembled an unusually wide coalition for a communist party - and this too matters for Lesson 21's comparison with Russia.

### 5. China in the wartime alliance

✅ When the war began, Chiang Kai-shek was in a dilemma: China had already been in a state of undeclared war with Japan since 1937, yet he had great admiration for Japan's ally Germany, and for the German military tradition. ✅ It was only after the German defeat at Stalingrad in 1942-3 that he decided to commit China to the Allied side.

✅ Relations between China and the USSR were strained because of Chiang's campaigns against the communists, so that Stalin refused to take part in any meeting at which Chiang was present.

✅ As an encouragement, in January 1943 the USA, Britain and several other states renounced their territorial rights and concessions in China - though Britain insisted on keeping Hong Kong - and promised that Manchuria and Formosa would be returned to China after the war. ✅ The irony was that most of these territories were occupied by the Japanese at the time: unless Japan could be defeated, none of it would happen. ✅ Nevertheless the agreements were important because they showed that at last China was being treated as an equal among the great powers, and was promised a permanent seat on the Security Council of the United Nations.

⚠️ This paragraph closes the circle opened in Lesson 1. ✅ The concession system that began with the Opium Wars was formally renounced in January 1943 - and the state that received the renunciation was not the state that had suffered the concessions, and would itself be replaced six years later. ⚠️ The promised Security Council seat is the sharpest illustration: it was granted to the Republic of China, and the question of who occupied it became one of the enduring disputes of the Cold War, a matter belonging to the United Nations and Cold War owners rather than to this session.

✅ The Japanese reaction was to launch an offensive by troops moved from Manchuria; striking southwards from the Yangtse Valley they eventually reached the frontier with Indochina, cutting off the south-east coast from the interior. ✅ The Nationalist forces were disorganised and ineffective, and their sporadic attempts to repel the advance were swept aside. ✅ In August 1945 the atomic bombs were dropped on Hiroshima and Nagasaki, and within a few days Japan surrendered. ✅ The Chinese contribution to the defeat of Japan had been to keep hundreds of thousands of Japanese troops bogged down in what was, for them, only a sideshow.

### 6. The revisionist defence of Chiang

✅ A recent biographer of Chiang Kai-shek, Jay Taylor, has suggested that he deserves more credit than the Americans and British have given him. ✅ The American General Stilwell used to refer to him as "Peanut", while the British Field-Marshal Lord Alanbrooke described him as "a cross between a pine-marten and a ferret". ✅ Without trying to ignore Chiang's brutality and his mistakes, Taylor argues that, given the enormity of the problems facing him, he governed the country with reasonable skill and certainly understood the challenges facing him far better than his American advisers did.

⚠️ This is the second historiographical set-piece of the topic, and it should be handled with the same three-move discipline used for the Long March: state the orthodox judgement, state the revision with its author named, and give the balance. ⚠️ The balance here is genuinely open, and saying so is not fence-sitting. ✅ The record of corruption, inflation, terror and tactical error in Lesson 10 is substantial and sourced; ✅ so is the observation that Chiang faced an invasion, a civil war, a fragmented state and an allied relationship in which his own allies mocked him. ⚠️ The defensible formulation is that Chiang's failure was real but was not simply a failure of individual competence: he inherited a state that had lost its capacity in the 1860s and attempted to rebuild it while fighting two wars, and the parts of that task he actually completed - reunification, national leadership, great-power recognition - are not trivial.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems ask how far the Japanese invasion explains the communist victory, or ask about the relationship between nationalism and communism in Asia - a framing under which this lesson supplies the Chinese case and Lesson 20 supplies the Vietnamese one.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Establish the priority problem (communists before Japan) and the Sian incident that broke it. ⚠️ Give the alliance's asymmetric effect: the Nationalists gained legitimacy and the burden of conventional war; the communists gained secure territory and freedom from attack. ⚠️ Give the geographical allocation of tasks and the base-area figures. ⚠️ Close on the formulation that the war converted an ideological civil war into a competition over national defence, and that the communists won that competition before they won the war.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying the Japanese invasion caused the communist victory. ⚠️ It accelerated a shift already under way; ✅ the communists had been gaining support since 1935.
2. ⚠️ Forgetting that the Sian captors were Chiang's own troops. ✅ They were, and mostly Manchurians.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the base-area figures. ✅ Five areas and 12 million in 1937; nineteen areas and 100 million in 1945.
4. ⚠️ Assuming China joined the Allies in 1937 or 1939. ✅ Chiang committed to the Allied side only after Stalingrad in 1942-3.
5. ⚠️ Treating the January 1943 renunciation as complete. ✅ Britain insisted on keeping Hong Kong.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Japan occupied Manchuria in 1931 and was preparing to take neighbouring northern provinces.
2. ✅ Chiang seemed to think destroying the communists more important than resisting Japan, and moved into south Shensi to attack Mao in 1936.
3. ✅ Sian incident: Chiang taken prisoner by his own troops, mostly Manchurians incensed at the Japanese invasion; at first unwilling; agreed to a fresh alliance and national front only after Zhou En-lai came to see him.
4. ✅ Second United Front, 1937-1945. Advantages to the communists: extermination campaigns ceased and the party was secure in its Shensi base.
5. ✅ 1937 full-scale war: Nationalist forces quickly defeated, most of eastern China occupied, Chiang retreating westwards.
6. ✅ Communists undefeated in Shensi presented themselves as patriotic nationalists leading effective guerrilla warfare in the north, winning massive support among peasants and middle classes appalled at Japanese arrogance and brutality.
7. ✅ Base areas: five controlling 12 million people in 1937; nineteen controlling 100 million by 1945.
8. ✅ Chiang admired Germany and the German military tradition; committed China to the Allied side only after Stalingrad, 1942-3; Stalin refused to attend any meeting at which Chiang was present.
9. ✅ January 1943: USA, Britain and several other states renounced territorial rights and concessions in China, Britain keeping Hong Kong; Manchuria and Formosa promised back; China treated as an equal great power and promised a permanent Security Council seat.
10. ✅ Japanese offensive from Manchuria struck south from the Yangtse Valley to the Indochina frontier, cutting the south-east coast from the interior; Nationalist forces disorganised and ineffective.
11. ✅ August 1945 atomic bombs; Japan surrendered within days; the Chinese contribution had been to keep hundreds of thousands of Japanese troops bogged down in a sideshow.
12. ✅ Jay Taylor's revision: given the enormity of his problems Chiang governed with reasonable skill and understood the challenges better than his American advisers; Stilwell called him "Peanut" and Alanbrooke "a cross between a pine-marten and a ferret".

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the mechanism by which the Japanese invasion of 1937 benefited the communists, and state what the base-area figures actually measure.

**Model answer:** The mechanism was a geographical division of military tasks. The Nationalists held eastern China - the coast, the cities, the industry and the ports - and were therefore obliged to fight conventional battles there against a superior army; they were quickly defeated, most of eastern China was occupied, and Chiang retreated westwards. The communists, undefeated in Shensi and protected from Nationalist attack by the Second United Front, operated in the north where the occupier could not fully administer the countryside, and led an effective guerrilla campaign. This allowed them to present themselves as patriotic nationalists and won massive support among peasants and middle classes appalled at Japanese conduct. The base-area figures measure administered population rather than sympathy: five base areas controlling 12 million people in 1937 became nineteen areas controlling 100 million by 1945. That is the decisive point, because it means the communists were already governing at provincial scale before the civil war resumed, which explains both their rapid conversion to conventional warfare in 1948 and the speed with which they established control after 1949.

**Misconception to avoid:** Saying the Japanese invasion caused the communist victory. It accelerated and consolidated a shift that was already under way - the communists had been gaining support and the Nationalists losing popularity throughout the ten years after the Long March - and it did so by allocating an impossible military task to one party and a favourable one to the other. An answer that treats the invasion as the sole cause cannot explain why the communists were in a position to take advantage of it, and will also miss the recorded ranking, in which the absence of effective resistance is the crucial factor among five, not the only one.

## Lesson 10 - The civil-war outcome, 1945-49: why the result was neither inevitable nor accidental

Progress: 10/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recorded judgement that communist victory was still not inevitable in 1945; American assistance and the Manchurian complication; the conversion from guerrilla to conventional war in 1948; the collapse of the Nationalist armies; Beijing in January 1949, Taiwan and the proclamation of the People's Republic; and the full recorded balance-sheet of reasons for the communist triumph

### Visual: the balance-sheet, both columns, as recorded

```text
   | advantage of KMT weaknesses     |  | and DESERTED to the communists  |
   | and completely dedicated        |  |                                 |
   |                                 |  | Attempts to TERRORIZE local     |
   | Generals LIN BIAO, CHU TEH and  |  | populations into submission     |
   | CH'EN YI had prepared their     |  | only ALIENATED MORE AREAS       |
   | armies carefully and were more  |  |                                 |
   | competent tactically than their |  | Chiang's TACTICAL BLUNDERS:     |
   | KMT counterparts                |  | like Hitler he could not bear   |
   |                                 |  | to order retreats, so scattered |
   |                                 |  | armies were surrounded and      |
   |                                 |  | often, as at Beijing and        |
   |                                 |  | Shanghai, SURRENDERED WITHOUT   |
   |                                 |  | RESISTANCE, totally             |
   |                                 |  | disillusioned                   |
   +---------------------------------+  +---------------------------------+

   THE SEQUENCE
   1945  Japan defeated. Americans help the KMT take over areas formerly
         occupied by Japan - EXCEPT MANCHURIA, captured by the Russians a
         few days before the war ended, where the Russians obstruct the
         KMT and allow CCP guerrillas to move in.
   1948  The apparent strength of the KMT is DECEPTIVE. Ever-growing
         communist armies are large enough to ABANDON GUERRILLA WARFARE
         and challenge Chiang's armies directly. Under direct pressure
         the KMT armies begin to DISINTEGRATE.
   Jan 1949  Communists take BEIJING.
   1949  Chiang and what remains of his forces flee to TAIWAN.
   Oct 1949  Standing at TIANANMEN, the Gate of Heavenly Peace, in
         Beijing, Mao proclaims the People's Republic of China, with
         himself as both Chairman of the CCP and president of the
         republic. (1 October 1949.)
```

⚠️ Set the two columns side by side when you revise, because the examinable judgement is about their relative weight, and you cannot weigh what you have not laid out.

### 1. The claim that matters most: it was not inevitable

✅ The core account states it as a heading: victory for the communists was still not inevitable. ✅ When the Japanese were defeated in 1945, the Nationalists and the communists became locked in the final struggle for power. ✅ Many observers, especially in the USA, hoped and expected that Chiang would be victorious. ✅ The Americans helped the Nationalists to take over all areas previously occupied by the Japanese, except Manchuria, which had been captured by the Russians a few days before the war ended. ✅ Here the Russians obstructed the Nationalists and allowed communist guerrillas to move in.

⚠️ Three lessons follow and the first is methodological. History written backwards from 1949 makes the communist victory look like the working-out of deep social forces; the contemporary evidence is that informed observers expected the opposite. ⚠️ An answer that opens by registering this - that in 1945 the Nationalists held the cities, the international recognition, the American supply line and the larger conventional army - is immediately stronger than one that narrates an inevitable rise.

⚠️ The second is about Manchuria and is worth a full sentence in any answer touching on external influence. ✅ The single most significant external intervention in favour of the communists was Soviet obstruction in Manchuria, which allowed communist guerrillas into the most industrialised region of China. ⚠️ But this must be stated with its limit, and the limit is recorded: ❌ the communist victory in China cannot be explained only by Soviet help, because domestic Chinese conditions were decisive. ⚠️ American aid to the Nationalists was, in absolute terms, far larger - and part of it ended in officials' pockets, which is itself part of the explanation of the outcome.

⚠️ The third is that both superpowers backed the losing horse in their own way: Soviet policy from the 1920s had been to cultivate the Nationalists, and Soviet obstruction in Manchuria was opportunism at the margin, while American assistance was substantial and ineffective. ⚠️ This is a good closing observation for a question about external influence on the Chinese revolution.

### 2. 1948: the conversion

✅ In fact the apparent strength of the Nationalists was deceptive: in 1948 the ever-growing communist armies were large enough to abandon their guerrilla campaign and challenge Chiang's armies directly. ✅ As soon as they came under direct pressure, the Nationalist armies began to disintegrate.

⚠️ This is the moment where the whole preceding twenty years pays off, and it deserves explicit treatment because most answers skip it. Guerrilla warfare is what a weaker force does; conventional warfare is what a stronger one does. ⚠️ The transition in 1948 is therefore a public statement about the balance of forces, and the source explains where the growth came from - the base areas of Lesson 9, nineteen of them, administering a hundred million people, which is a recruitment and supply base rather than a refuge.

⚠️ Equally important is what happened on the other side: the Nationalist armies *disintegrated under direct pressure* rather than being destroyed in battle. ⚠️ Disintegration is a political phenomenon. It is what happens to armies that are poorly paid, allowed to loot, exposed to enemy propaganda, commanded by a leader who forbids retreat, and fighting for a government whose currency has collapsed.

### 3. The end

✅ In January 1949 the communists took Beijing, and later in the year Chiang and what remained of his forces fled to the island of Taiwan, leaving Mao Zedong in command of mainland China. ✅ In October 1949, standing at Tiananmen - the Gate of Heavenly Peace - in Beijing, Mao proclaimed the new People's Republic of China with himself as both Chairman of the Communist Party and president of the republic. ✅ The advanced owner records the date as 1 October 1949.

⚠️ Two small points of precision that examiners notice. First, Mao held both offices - party chairman and head of state - and the distinction becomes important in Lesson 13, when he gives up the second and keeps the first. Second, the Nationalist government did not dissolve; it relocated. ⚠️ The consequences of that relocation - the Taiwan question, the disputed China seat at the United Nations, the American security commitment - belong to the Cold War and United Nations owners and are not pursued here.

### 4. The reasons for the triumph, in full

✅ The communists continued to win popular support by their restrained land policy, which varied according to the needs of particular areas: some or all of a landlord's estate might be confiscated and redistributed among the peasants, or there might simply be rent restriction. ✅ Communist armies were well disciplined and communist administration was honest and fair.

✅ On the other hand the Nationalist administration was inefficient and corrupt, much of its American aid finding its way into the pockets of officials. ✅ Its policy of paying for the wars by printing extra money resulted in galloping inflation, which caused hardship for the masses and ruined many of the middle class. ✅ Its armies were poorly paid and were allowed to loot the countryside; subjected to communist propaganda, the troops gradually became disillusioned with Chiang and began to desert to the communists. ✅ The Nationalists tried to terrorise the local populations into submission, but this only alienated more areas. ✅ Chiang also made some tactical blunders: like Hitler, he could not bear to order retreats, and consequently his scattered armies were surrounded and often, as happened at Beijing and Shanghai, surrendered without resistance, totally disillusioned.

✅ Finally the communist leaders, Mao Zedong and Zhou En-lai, were shrewd enough to take advantage of Nationalist weaknesses and were completely dedicated. ✅ The communist generals, Lin Biao, Chu Teh and Ch'en Yi, had prepared their armies carefully and were more competent tactically than their Nationalist counterparts.

⚠️ Two features of this list are worth naming explicitly because they discriminate between a good and an average answer.

⚠️ First, **inflation is the most underrated item on it.** Printing money to pay for war destroyed the savings of exactly the urban middle class on which the Nationalist regime depended - the officials, teachers, clerks, merchants and soldiers' families in the cities. A regime can survive peasant hostility for a long time; it cannot survive the impoverishment of its own administrative class. ⚠️ Lesson 8 showed the Nationalists losing the villages; inflation is how they lost the cities too.

⚠️ Second, **the list names individuals on both sides**, and does so asymmetrically: three competent communist generals, a shrewd and dedicated leadership pair, and on the other side a commander whose refusal to authorise retreat produced encirclements. ⚠️ Agency belongs in the answer. The structural explanation is primary, but an answer that mentions Lin Biao, Chu Teh and Ch'en Yi and the Hitler comparison on retreats will read as informed rather than generic.

### Comparative note: out-governing, not only out-fighting

⚠️ Assemble the six mechanisms from the discipline list in the opening and check them against the record:

| Mechanism | ✅ Evidence in 1945-49 |
|---|---|
| Organisational survival | The Long March and the Shensi base, preserved further by the truce of 1937 |
| Social-base construction | The restrained, locally varied land policy; rent restriction; plots for the poorest |
| Patriotic legitimacy | The guerrilla campaign in the north while eastern China was occupied |
| Administrative contrast | Honest and fair communist administration against corruption, looting and aid diversion |
| Military conversion | 1948: abandonment of guerrilla warfare for direct challenge |
| Adversary collapse | Desertion, disintegration under pressure, surrender without resistance at Beijing and Shanghai |

⚠️ All six are present, and that is the real finding. ⚠️ The thesis worth writing is that the communists won because in a country where the central question since 1911 had been who could actually govern, they demonstrated in the villages and in the anti-Japanese war that they could, and the Nationalists demonstrated in the cities that they could not.

### Objection and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** If the outcome depended so heavily on Nationalist failure - corruption, inflation, looting, desertion, blunders - then the communist victory tells us little about communism and much about the weakness of its opponent. Any disciplined organisation might have won.

⚠️ **The reply.** The objection is half right and the reply must concede the half. ⚠️ Yes: the record shows the Nationalists losing at least as clearly as it shows the communists winning, and an answer that ignores this is unbalanced. ⚠️ But "any disciplined organisation might have won" is false for a specific reason: no other disciplined organisation existed, and the reason none existed is that building one required exactly what the communists had built over twenty years - an army, a rural administration, a land policy and a patriotic record. ⚠️ The third-party alternatives had no armed force, and armed force was the entry ticket. ⚠️ The precise verdict is therefore that the communist victory was not a validation of Marxist theory but a validation of a particular organisational practice, which is why the ideological content of Chinese communism matters far less to the explanation of 1949 than its administrative content does.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The standard demand is "discuss the causes of the communist victory in China in 1949" or "assess the reasons for the communist triumph". ⚠️ The directive - discuss, assess, examine - asks for weighing, not listing.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Open with the non-inevitability point, which signals that you are not reading backwards. ⚠️ Give the comparative balance-sheet in two columns rather than as a single list, and weigh it: land policy and administration on one side, inflation and disintegration on the other. ⚠️ Add the external dimension with its limit - Soviet obstruction in Manchuria mattered, American aid mattered more in volume and less in effect, and domestic conditions were decisive. ⚠️ Close on the out-governing thesis.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Saying the Nationalists lost only because of foreign interference. ✅ Their own corruption and weak governance mattered greatly.
2. ❌ Saying the communists won because of Soviet help. ✅ Domestic Chinese conditions were decisive.
3. ⚠️ Omitting inflation. ✅ Printing money produced galloping inflation that ruined many of the middle class.
4. ⚠️ Treating 1948 as just another year of fighting. ✅ It is the year the communists abandoned guerrilla warfare for direct challenge.
5. ⚠️ Writing that the Nationalist government ended in 1949. ✅ Chiang and his remaining forces relocated to Taiwan.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Victory for the communists was still not inevitable in 1945; many observers, especially in the USA, expected Chiang to win.
2. ✅ Americans helped the Nationalists take over areas formerly occupied by Japan, except Manchuria, captured by the Russians days before the war ended, where the Russians obstructed the Nationalists and let communist guerrillas in.
3. ✅ 1948: communist armies large enough to abandon guerrilla warfare and challenge directly; Nationalist armies began to disintegrate under direct pressure.
4. ✅ January 1949: communists took Beijing. Later in 1949 Chiang and his remaining forces fled to Taiwan.
5. ✅ October 1949, at Tiananmen: Mao proclaimed the People's Republic with himself as both party Chairman and president; ✅ the date is 1 October 1949.
6. ✅ Communist strengths: restrained land policy varying by area - confiscation and redistribution, or simply rent restriction; well-disciplined armies; honest and fair administration; shrewd, dedicated leadership in Mao and Zhou En-lai; carefully prepared and tactically superior generals Lin Biao, Chu Teh and Ch'en Yi.
7. ✅ Nationalist weaknesses: inefficient and corrupt administration with American aid reaching officials' pockets; galloping inflation from printing money, ruining many of the middle class; poorly paid armies allowed to loot; troops disillusioned by communist propaganda and deserting; attempts at terror alienating more areas; Chiang's refusal to order retreats, leading to encirclement and surrender without resistance at Beijing and Shanghai.
8. ⚠️ All six victory mechanisms are present in the record - survival, social base, patriotic legitimacy, administrative contrast, military conversion, adversary collapse.
9. ⚠️ Thesis: in a country whose central question since 1911 was who could govern, one party demonstrated it could in the villages and in the war, and the other demonstrated it could not in the cities.
10. ⚠️ External balance: Soviet obstruction in Manchuria helped at the margin, American aid was larger and partly stolen, domestic conditions were decisive.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is inflation a more revealing item on the list of Nationalist weaknesses than corruption, and how should the two be related in an answer?

**Model answer:** Corruption alienated the population at large and wasted American aid, much of which found its way into the pockets of officials, but corruption is compatible with regime survival where the ruling coalition continues to benefit from it. Inflation is more revealing because it attacked the regime's own coalition. Paying for the wars by printing extra money produced galloping inflation which caused hardship for the masses and ruined many of the middle class - that is, the urban officials, clerks, teachers, merchants and army families who were the Nationalist government's social base and its administrative machinery. A regime that has already made no moves towards land reform and therefore has little rural support can survive only if the cities hold; inflation destroyed the material position of the people in the cities who had most reason to prefer it to the alternative. The two should therefore be related as cause and amplifier: corruption meant that the resources entering the system leaked away, which forced greater reliance on monetary financing, which destroyed the savings of the class whose loyalty the corruption had already strained.

**Misconception to avoid:** Listing the recorded weaknesses without weighting them. The directive in questions on 1949 is almost always to discuss or assess, which requires a judgement about relative importance. A list of eight Nationalist failings and five communist strengths, given equal weight, reads as recall; identifying inflation as the mechanism that broke the regime's own base, and the two-stage land policy as the mechanism that built its rival's, reads as analysis.

---

## Lesson 11 - State formation, land and collectivisation, 1949-56: what the new state did, and what it cost

Progress: 11/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the condition of the country in 1949; the transformation of agriculture from private farms to co-operatives between 1950 and 1956; the sharply conflicting accounts of the violence of land reform; collectivisation reaching about 95 per cent of surviving peasants by 1956; nationalisation and the Five Year Plan of 1953; and the first appearance of the pattern that recurs for the next quarter-century

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's chapter on China since 1949, including the recorded problems facing the People's Republic in 1949, the section on agricultural changes with the conflicting assessments by Jack Gray writing in 1970 and Professor Frank Dikotter working in archives declassified around the 2008 Beijing Olympics, the quoted Hebei archival document, the collectivisation figure for 1956, and the section on industrial changes including nationalisation, the Five Year Plan of 1953 and Russian assistance. The portion of the extraction covering the political changes of the early 1950s is damaged and is recorded as an open gap in the final ledger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two programmes, one state, and a contested evidence base

```text
   THE INHERITANCE, 1949
   ---------------------
   Country devastated after the long civil war and the war with Japan.
   Railways, roads, canals and dykes destroyed.
   Chronic food shortages.

   PROGRAMME A: AGRICULTURE, 1950-56
   ---------------------------------
   Transformed China from a country of small, inefficient PRIVATE FARMS
   into one of large CO-OPERATIVE FARMS like those in Russia.

   Stage 1: land taken from large landowners and redistributed among
            the peasants - "no doubt with violence in places"
   Stage 2: by 1956, about 95 PER CENT of all surviving peasants were in
            COLLECTIVE FARMS with joint ownership of the farm and its
            equipment

   THE EVIDENCE PROBLEM ON STAGE 1
   -------------------------------
   Source A: "some sources mention as many as two million people killed"
   Source B: Jack Gray, writing in 1970 while Mao was alive, claimed the
             redistribution "was carried out with a remarkable degree of
             attention to legality and the minimum of physical violence
             against landlords"
   Source C: Professor Frank Dikotter, using archives declassified around
             the 2008 Beijing Olympics, has shown that in some areas there
             were very few wealthy landowners because land was already
             fairly equally divided; what actually happened was that
             peasants' land was taken and redistributed to communist party
             activists, with considerable violence, torturing and execution
   -> The accounts are INCOMPATIBLE. Report them as competing accounts.

   PROGRAMME B: INDUSTRY, from 1949
   --------------------------------
   Government NATIONALIZED most businesses.
   1953: FIVE YEAR PLAN concentrating on HEAVY INDUSTRY
         (iron, steel, chemicals, coal)
   The Russians helped with cash, equipment and advisers.
   The plan had SOME SUCCESS.
   Recovery claimed: full communications restored, inflation under
   control, the economy looking much healthier.
   BUT: before it was complete Mao began to have grave doubts as to
   whether China was SUITED to this sort of heavy industrialization
   -> the doubt that produces the Great Leap Forward (Lesson 13)
```

⚠️ The right-hand side of Programme B is the hinge of the whole post-1949 story. The First Five Year Plan worked reasonably well and Mao decided that working reasonably well on a Soviet model was the wrong objective. Everything in Lessons 12 to 14 follows from that judgement.

### 1. The inheritance

✅ The problems facing the People's Republic in 1949 were complex, to say the least. ✅ The country was devastated after the long civil war and the war with Japan: railways, roads, canals and dykes had been destroyed and there were chronic food shortages.

⚠️ Hold that list in mind when you assess the regime later, because it is the baseline against which the achievements of the early 1950s must be measured, and it is also the reason those achievements produced such confidence. ⚠️ A government that restores communications, controls inflation and feeds the country within four years, after a century in which no government had managed any of the three, will draw a conclusion about its own capacities. ⚠️ Lesson 13 shows what that conclusion cost.

⚠️ The detailed account of the political changes of the early 1950s - the structure of the new party-state, the mass campaigns of those years and the consolidation of one-party control - is damaged in the extraction available for this session. ⚠️ What the canonical owners record is retained: ✅ early consolidation combined land reform and reconstruction with tighter one-party control; ✅ the old landlord power was weakened as a social force and ✅ the party-state expanded rapidly into every sphere. ⚠️ Beyond that, this session records an open gap rather than filling it from memory.

### 2. Agriculture: what was done

✅ The agricultural changes transformed China from a country of small, inefficient private farms into one of large co-operative farms like those in Russia, between 1950 and 1956. ✅ In the first stage, land was taken from large landowners and redistributed among the peasants, ✅ no doubt with violence in places. ✅ By 1956, whatever the methods used, about 95 per cent of all surviving peasants were in collective farms with joint ownership of the farm and its equipment.

⚠️ The two stages must be kept apart because they are different operations with different politics. **Redistribution** gives land to individual peasant households and is overwhelmingly popular among those who receive it; it is also the fulfilment of the promise that won the civil war. **Collectivisation** takes that land back into joint ownership and is, in every country where it has been attempted, resisted. ⚠️ The fact that the same regime did both within six years, and that the second reversed the social meaning of the first, is one of the sharpest things a candidate can say about the early People's Republic.

### 3. The evidence problem, handled properly

This is the first place in the topic where three accounts of the same events are irreconcilable, and the correct handling is a skill, not a fact.

✅ **Account one, an unattributed range:** some sources mention as many as two million people killed.

✅ **Account two, Jack Gray, writing in 1970 when Mao was still alive:** the redistribution of China's land "was carried out with a remarkable degree of attention to legality and the minimum of physical violence against landlords".

✅ **Account three, Professor Frank Dikotter of the University of Hong Kong, using archives declassified during the atmosphere of goodwill and openness surrounding the 2008 Beijing Olympics:** in some areas there were very few wealthy landowners, since the land was already fairly equally divided between the peasants; what actually happened was that their land was taken away from them and redistributed to communist party activists, with considerable violence, torturing and execution. ✅ One document from the Hebei archives reported: "When it comes to the ways in which people are killed, some are buried alive, some are executed, some are cut to pieces, and among those who are strangled or mangled to death, some of the bodies are hung from trees or doors."

⚠️ How to write about this, in four moves.

1. ⚠️ **Report the policy as policy.** Land was taken from large landowners and redistributed; by 1956 about 95 per cent of surviving peasants were in collective farms.
2. ⚠️ **Report each account with its author and its date.** The date matters: Gray wrote in 1970 while Mao was alive and while archives were closed; Dikötter worked in archives declassified around 2008. ⚠️ Naming the evidentiary conditions under which each judgement was formed is not a rhetorical trick; it is the substance of the historiographical point.
3. ⚠️ **Do not average them.** ❌ There is no defensible middle figure between "minimum of physical violence" and "considerable violence, torturing and execution", and an answer that invents one has fabricated a finding.
4. ⚠️ **Say what the archival evidence changes.** ✅ The declassified material shows that official accounts of a number of events and policies do not tell the whole truth: achievements were exaggerated and unpleasant events were either toned down or not reported at all. ⚠️ That is a general finding about the evidence base for the whole Maoist period, and it applies again in Lesson 13.

⚠️ There is also a substantive point inside Dikötter's account that is easy to miss and is worth marks: in some areas there were **very few wealthy landowners** because land was already fairly equally divided. ⚠️ If that is right, then in those areas the campaign was not redistribution from a landlord class at all but a transfer to party activists, which changes the character of the policy from social reform to political consolidation. ⚠️ That is a serious claim about what land reform *was*, not merely about how violent it was.

### 4. Industry, and the doubt that changed everything

✅ The industrial changes began with the government nationalising most businesses. ✅ In 1953 it embarked on a Five Year Plan concentrating on the development of heavy industry - iron, steel, chemicals and coal. ✅ The Russians helped with cash, equipment and advisers, and the plan had some success.

✅ Before it was complete, however, Mao began to have grave doubts as to whether China was suited to this sort of heavy industrialisation. ✅ On the other hand he could claim that under his leadership the country had recovered from the ravages of the wars: full communications had been restored, inflation was under control and the economy was looking much healthier.

⚠️ Two observations, both examinable.

⚠️ First, the recovery claim is substantial and should be granted. ✅ Inflation under control is a direct reversal of the failure that destroyed the Nationalist regime, and it is the clearest single demonstration that the new government possessed a capacity its predecessor lacked.

⚠️ Second, Mao's doubt is the origin of everything that follows. The Soviet model implied heavy industry, urban concentration, technical expertise, managerial hierarchy and central planning - and each of those implied a class of specialists whose authority would rest on knowledge rather than on political commitment. ⚠️ Lesson 12 shows the cadres recognising that threat; Lesson 13 shows Mao's alternative; Lesson 14 shows the quarrel becoming a mass campaign. ⚠️ The through-line from 1953 to 1976 is a single argument about whether a poor agrarian country should industrialise by importing a model or by mobilising its own population, and whether expertise or political commitment should govern.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems on the impact of Maoism on Chinese society and state formation, or on how far Mao dealt successfully with China's problems, open here. ⚠️ Questions on land reform in Asia may also route here for the Chinese case.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Give the 1949 inheritance in one line. ⚠️ Separate redistribution from collectivisation and note that the second reversed the social meaning of the first within six years. ⚠️ Handle the violence with attributed competing accounts and refuse to average. ⚠️ Give the industrial record with its success acknowledged, and end on Mao's doubt as the hinge to the rest of the period.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Stating a death toll for land reform as fact. ✅ There are competing accounts: an unattributed figure of up to two million killed, Gray's minimum-violence judgement of 1970 and Dikötter's archival findings.
2. ⚠️ Merging redistribution and collectivisation. ✅ They are separate stages between 1950 and 1956 with opposite implications for peasant property.
3. ⚠️ Saying the First Five Year Plan failed. ✅ It had some success; the doubts were Mao's, about suitability rather than performance.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting the inflation reversal. ✅ Inflation was brought under control - the direct contrast with the Nationalist collapse.
5. ⚠️ Overlooking the general archival finding. ✅ Official accounts exaggerated achievements and toned down or omitted unpleasant events.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1949 inheritance: devastation after the civil war and the Japanese war; railways, roads, canals and dykes destroyed; chronic food shortages.
2. ✅ Agriculture 1950-56: from small, inefficient private farms to large co-operative farms like those in Russia.
3. ✅ Stage 1: land taken from large landowners and redistributed among peasants, no doubt with violence in places.
4. ✅ Stage 2: by 1956 about 95 per cent of all surviving peasants were in collective farms with joint ownership of farm and equipment.
5. ✅ Competing accounts of the violence: some sources mention as many as two million killed; Jack Gray in 1970 described a remarkable degree of attention to legality and minimum physical violence; Frank Dikötter, using archives declassified around the 2008 Olympics, describes land taken from ordinary peasants and given to party activists with considerable violence, torturing and execution, quoting a Hebei document.
6. ✅ The declassified archives show that official accounts exaggerated achievements and toned down or omitted unpleasant events.
7. ✅ Industry: most businesses nationalised; Five Year Plan from 1953 on heavy industry - iron, steel, chemicals, coal; Russian cash, equipment and advisers; some success.
8. ✅ Recovery claim: full communications restored, inflation under control, economy much healthier.
9. ✅ Before the plan was complete, Mao had grave doubts whether China was suited to that sort of heavy industrialisation.
10. ⚠️ The through-line from 1953 to 1976: import a model and accept an expert class, or mobilise the population and keep politics in command.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks you to assess land reform in China between 1950 and 1956. How do you handle the fact that your sources contradict each other about its violence, and what substantive claim of Dikötter's goes beyond the question of violence?

**Model answer:** You report the policy, then report each account with its author and the evidentiary conditions under which it was formed, and you refuse to produce a compromise figure. The policy is that land was taken from large landowners and redistributed among the peasants and that by 1956 about 95 per cent of surviving peasants were in collective farms. The accounts are that some sources mention as many as two million killed; that Jack Gray, writing in 1970 while Mao was alive and the archives closed, judged that redistribution was carried out with remarkable attention to legality and minimum physical violence against landlords; and that Frank Dikötter, working in archives declassified around the 2008 Beijing Olympics, found considerable violence, torturing and execution. No middle figure between these is defensible, so none should be offered. The substantive claim that goes beyond violence is Dikötter's finding that in some areas there were very few wealthy landowners because land was already fairly equally divided, and that what happened there was the transfer of ordinary peasants' land to communist party activists. If correct, that changes the character of the campaign in those areas from social redistribution to political consolidation, which is a claim about what land reform was rather than about how it was conducted.

**Misconception to avoid:** Trying to be even-handed by splitting the difference between the accounts. Averaging incompatible testimony produces a number that no source supports and that is therefore fabricated. Even-handedness in a contested evidence base means reporting each position with its provenance and stating what would have to be true for each to be right - here, that Gray was writing without access to materials that were closed until decades later.
