## Lesson 12 - The Hundred Flowers campaign and the Anti-Rightist turn, 1957: the hinge between two political styles

Progress: 12/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the new class of technicians and engineers produced by industrialisation; the cadres and their fear of the experts; the invitation to constructive criticism; the three criticisms actually made; the clampdown; and the conclusion Mao drew from it

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Hundred Flowers campaign of 1957, including its origin in industrialisation and the tension between party cadres and the new class of experts, the definition of cadres it supplies, Mao's invitation to constructive criticism, the three categories of criticism that resulted, the abrupt ending of the campaign, and the statement that it convinced Mao a drive was needed to consolidate the advance of socialism, leading directly to the call for the Great Leap Forward in 1958
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a four-step mechanism that repeats three more times in this topic

```text
   STEP 1  A STRUCTURAL TENSION EXISTS
           Industrialization produced a vast new class of TECHNICIANS
           and ENGINEERS.
           The party CADRES - the groups who organized the masses
           politically and economically; collectivization of the farms,
           for example, was carried out by the cadres - believed that
           this new class of experts would THREATEN THEIR AUTHORITY.
                          |
   STEP 2  THE LEADERSHIP INVITES CRITICISM, feeling pleased with its
           progress so far, in the hope that open discussion of the
           problems might improve relations between cadres and experts
           or intellectuals.
           "Let a hundred flowers bloom and a hundred schools of thought
           contend", said Mao, calling for CONSTRUCTIVE criticism.
                          |
   STEP 3  MORE CRITICISM ARRIVES THAN WAS ANTICIPATED
           Critics attacked:
             - the CADRES, for incompetence and over-enthusiasm
             - the GOVERNMENT, for over-centralization
             - the COMMUNIST PARTY, for being undemocratic; some
               suggested that OPPOSITION PARTIES should be allowed
                          |
   STEP 4  THE CAMPAIGN IS CLOSED DOWN
           Mao hurriedly called off the campaign and clamped down on his
           critics, insisting that his policies were right.
                          |
                          v
   THE CONCLUSION DRAWN
           The campaign showed how much opposition there still was to
           communism and to the uneducated cadres, and it CONVINCED MAO
           THAT A DRIVE WAS NEEDED TO CONSOLIDATE THE ADVANCE OF
           SOCIALISM - so in 1958 he called for the GREAT LEAP FORWARD.

   THE SAME FOUR STEPS RECUR:
      1978-79  Democracy Wall approved, then abolished (Lesson 17)
      1986-87  student demonstrations tolerated, then dispersed (Lesson 18)
      1989     protests allowed to run, then crushed (Lesson 18)
```

⚠️ This is the shortest lesson in the session and the most transferable. Learn the four steps as a pattern, because once you have it, three later episodes become instances of a known mechanism rather than separate stories to memorise.

### 1. Where the campaign came from

✅ The campaign seems to some extent to have developed out of industrialisation, which produced a vast new class of technicians and engineers. ✅ The party cadres - groups who organised the masses politically and economically; the collectivisation of the farms, for example, was carried out by the cadres - believed that this new class of experts would threaten their authority.

⚠️ Learn the definition of "cadre" from that clause, because the word appears repeatedly in this topic and in Lesson 20 on Vietnam and Cambodia, and candidates frequently use it as a synonym for "official" or "soldier". ✅ A cadre is an organiser of the masses, politically and economically, and the collectivisation of the farms is the recorded example of what cadres actually did.

⚠️ The tension the campaign was meant to resolve is therefore a real structural problem, not a personality clash: a revolutionary state that industrialises creates a body of people whose authority derives from technical knowledge, and that authority competes with the authority of people whose standing derives from political commitment and organisational work. ⚠️ Every revolutionary regime that industrialises faces this, and the Chinese answer - repeatedly, from 1957 to 1976 - was to subordinate expertise to politics. ⚠️ The Deng period reversed the answer, and that is the cleanest way to state what the reform era changed.

### 2. The invitation and the response

✅ The government, feeling pleased with its progress so far, decided that open discussion of the problems might improve relations between cadres and experts or intellectuals. ✅ "Let a hundred flowers bloom and a hundred schools of thought contend", said Mao, calling for constructive criticism. ✅ Unfortunately he got more than he had anticipated as critics attacked the cadres for incompetence and over-enthusiasm; the government for over-centralisation; and the Communist Party for being undemocratic, with some suggesting that opposition parties should be allowed.

⚠️ Read the three criticisms as an escalating sequence, because the escalation is the reason the campaign was stopped. The first is a personnel complaint, entirely permissible. The second is a policy complaint, uncomfortable but arguable. The third is a systemic complaint that questions the single-party order itself - and the suggestion of opposition parties crosses the line beyond which no criticism was ever going to be tolerated.

⚠️ This also supplies the honest answer to the long-running question of whether the campaign was a trap set to expose critics or a genuine invitation that went wrong. ⚠️ The recorded account supports the second reading - the government felt pleased with its progress and expected constructive criticism; Mao got more than he had anticipated and *hurriedly* called the campaign off. ⚠️ That sequence describes miscalculation rather than entrapment, and an answer should say so while noting that the consequences for the critics were the same either way.

### 3. The conclusion, and why it is the hinge

✅ Mao hurriedly called off the campaign and clamped down on his critics, insisting that his policies were right. ✅ The campaign showed how much opposition there still was to communism and to the uneducated cadres, ✅ and it convinced Mao that a drive was needed to consolidate the advance of socialism; ✅ so in 1958 he called for the Great Leap Forward.

⚠️ That causal link, stated in the source itself, is the single most valuable sentence in this lesson. It means the Great Leap Forward was not simply an economic strategy: it was a response to evidence of political disaffection. ⚠️ An answer that presents the Leap purely as a development plan has missed half its motivation. ⚠️ The logic was that if socialism was insufficiently secure after eight years, the remedy was to advance faster and to mobilise the population in a way that would make the advance irreversible.

⚠️ Note the structure of the inference, because it recurs: opposition is read as evidence that the revolution is incomplete rather than as evidence that the policy is wrong. ⚠️ A regime that interprets criticism this way will always respond to failure with intensification, and Lessons 13 and 14 are the two great instances.

### Comparative note: the same mechanism, four times

| Episode | ✅ Opening | ✅ Escalation | ✅ Closure | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hundred Flowers, 1957 | Criticism invited; "let a hundred flowers bloom" | Attacks on cadres, over-centralisation, party's lack of democracy; opposition parties suggested | Campaign hurriedly called off; clampdown on critics | Convinced Mao a drive was needed; Great Leap Forward called for in 1958 |
| Democracy Wall, 1978-9 | Poster campaign approved by Deng, who welcomed attacks on the Gang of Four | Posters attacked Chairman Mao and demanded open criticism of government, representation for non-communist parties, freedom to travel, abolition of the communes | Deng attacked leading dissidents; several arrested with sentences of up to fifteen years; the Wall abolished in November 1979 | Law, order and party discipline restored; the pattern of reform without political opening confirmed |
| Student demonstrations, December 1986 | Demonstrations supporting Deng and the Four Modernizations | Demands for a quicker pace and, ominously, more democracy; bans on posters and notice requirements ignored | Demonstrators dispersed; hardliners forced the resignation of Hu Yaobang | Reformers weakened; Li Peng became prime minister demanding a clampdown |
| Tiananmen, 1989 | Protests tolerated; Zhao Ziyang said the students' demands were just and allowed the press to report them | Occupation of the square through Gorbachev's visit and into June | Martial law declared on 20 May; the army attacked on 3-4 June | Party control reasserted; economic reform continued without political reform |

⚠️ This table is one of the most useful things in the session for a 20-mark answer, because it demonstrates continuity across the Mao and Deng eras. ⚠️ The reform era changed the economy and did not change this mechanism, which is exactly the point Lessons 17 and 18 are built to establish.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The campaign rarely carries its own question, but it is the best available evidence for two recurring demands: the limits of dissent in the People's Republic, and the motivation of the Great Leap Forward.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ State the structural tension between cadres and experts; state the invitation; give the three criticisms in escalating order; state the closure; and then state the recorded causal link to 1958. ⚠️ If the question is broader, add the four-episode table to show the mechanism repeating.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the campaign as purely cultural or literary. ✅ It grew out of industrialisation and the tension between cadres and the new class of experts.
2. ⚠️ Asserting confidently that it was a deliberate trap. ⚠️ The recorded sequence indicates Mao got more than he had anticipated and hurriedly called it off.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the link to the Great Leap Forward. ✅ It convinced Mao that a drive was needed to consolidate the advance of socialism.
4. ⚠️ Using "cadre" loosely. ✅ Cadres organised the masses politically and economically; collectivisation was carried out by them.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Origin: industrialisation produced a vast new class of technicians and engineers; cadres believed these experts would threaten their authority.
2. ✅ Definition of cadres: groups who organised the masses politically and economically - collectivisation of the farms was carried out by them.
3. ✅ The government, pleased with progress, invited open discussion to improve relations between cadres and experts or intellectuals.
4. ✅ Mao: "Let a hundred flowers bloom and a hundred schools of thought contend", calling for constructive criticism.
5. ✅ He got more than anticipated: attacks on cadres for incompetence and over-enthusiasm; on the government for over-centralisation; on the party for being undemocratic, with some suggesting opposition parties.
6. ✅ Mao hurriedly called off the campaign and clamped down, insisting his policies were right.
7. ✅ The campaign showed how much opposition remained to communism and to the uneducated cadres, and convinced Mao a drive was needed to consolidate socialism - hence the Great Leap Forward of 1958.
8. ⚠️ The four-step mechanism: structural tension - invitation - escalation beyond the permitted line - closure and repression.
9. ⚠️ The mechanism recurs in 1978-9, 1986-7 and 1989, spanning both the Mao and Deng eras.
10. ⚠️ The inference pattern: opposition read as incomplete revolution rather than as mistaken policy, producing intensification rather than correction.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Hundred Flowers campaign is often treated as a minor episode. Give the reason it deserves more attention, using the recorded causal link.

**Model answer:** It deserves attention because the account records it as the direct cause of the Great Leap Forward. The campaign showed how much opposition there still was to communism and to the uneducated cadres, and it convinced Mao that a drive was needed to consolidate the advance of socialism, so in 1958 he called for the Leap. That makes the Leap a political response to evidence of disaffection as well as an economic strategy, and an answer that treats it only as a development plan will misstate its motivation. The episode also displays a repeatable mechanism: a structural tension - here between party cadres and the new class of technicians and engineers created by industrialisation - leads the leadership to invite criticism, the criticism escalates past the permitted line when some critics question the party's monopoly and suggest opposition parties, and the campaign is abruptly closed with repression of the critics. The same four steps recur at the Democracy Wall in 1978-9, in the student demonstrations of December 1986 and at Tiananmen in 1989, which is why the episode is worth more than its length.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the campaign was designed from the outset to flush out critics. The recorded sequence is that the government felt pleased with its progress, expected constructive criticism, received more than it had anticipated, and then hurriedly called the campaign off - a description of miscalculation. You may note that the outcome for the critics was identical either way, but asserting entrapment as established fact goes beyond what the evidence supports.

## Lesson 13 - The Great Leap Forward, 1958-61: ambition, catastrophe and the evidence problem

Progress: 13/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what the Leap was for; the communes and their design; the change of emphasis in industry; the harvest failures and the withdrawal of Soviet aid; the mortality evidence in its competing forms; the political consequence for Mao; the recorded counter-ledger of long-term effects; and the discipline for writing about all of it. Taught in three Parts

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Great Leap Forward, including the reasons Mao believed something new and different was needed, the two most important features with their recorded detail and the illustrative commune of 1965, the difficulties of 1959-63, the figure that emerged later for premature deaths, Mao's resignation as Chairman of the People's Congress and his retention of the party chairmanship, the quoted findings of Professor Frank Dikotter with their stated bracket, the long-term assessment recorded from the official account including the infant-mortality series and the position of women, and Jonathan Fenby's 2012 counter-assessment

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the design, the failure and the ledger

```text
   PART A - THE DESIGN
   ===================
   WHY: Mao felt something new and different was needed to meet China's
        special problems - something NOT BASED ON RUSSIAN EXPERIENCE.
   AIM: increase output (agriculture in particular was not providing the
        required food) and adapt industry to Chinese conditions.

   FEATURE 1: COMMUNES
     units LARGER than collective farms, containing up to 75,000 people
     divided into BRIGADES and WORK TEAMS with an ELECTED COUNCIL
     they ran their own collective farms and factories
     carried out most of the functions of LOCAL GOVERNMENT
     undertook special local projects
     One typical commune in 1965 contained 30,000 people:
        one third children at school or in creches
        one third housewives or elderly
        the rest the workforce - including a science team of
        32 graduates and 43 technicians
     Each family received a share of the profits and also had a
        SMALL PRIVATE PLOT of land

   FEATURE 2: A COMPLETE CHANGE OF EMPHASIS IN INDUSTRY
     instead of large-scale works of the type seen in the USSR and the
     West, much SMALLER FACTORIES were set up in the COUNTRYSIDE to
     provide machinery for agriculture
     Mao talked of 600,000 "BACKYARD STEEL FURNACES" organized and
     managed by the communes, which also undertook to build roads,
     canals, dams, reservoirs and irrigation channels

   PART B - THE FAILURE
   ====================
     some opposition to the communes
     a series of BAD HARVESTS (1959-61)
     the WITHDRAWAL OF ALL RUSSIAN AID following the breach between
        Russia and China
     lack of experience among the cadres
        -> HARDSHIP in the years 1959-63
        -> "statistics which emerged later suggested that SOME 20 MILLION
            PEOPLE MAY HAVE DIED PREMATURELY as a result of hardships,
            especially the disastrous FAMINE OF 1959-60, caused by the
            Great Leap"
        -> Even Mao's prestige suffered: he was FORCED TO RESIGN as
            Chairman of the People's Congress (succeeded by LIU SHAOQI),
            though he REMAINED CHAIRMAN OF THE COMMUNIST PARTY

   PART C - THE LEDGER
   ===================
   RECORDED ON THE OTHER SIDE (from the official account):
     by the early 1970s both agricultural and industrial production had
        increased substantially
     China was at least managing to feed its massive population without
        further famine (which had rarely happened under the KMT)
     the communes proved a successful innovation - more than collective
        farms: an efficient unit of local government enabling the central
        government to keep in touch with local opinion
     the crucial decision: China would remain predominantly agricultural
        with small-scale industry scattered around the countryside; the
        economy would be LABOUR-INTENSIVE
     spread of education and welfare services
     INFANT MORTALITY fell from 203 per thousand births in 1949 to 84 by
        the end of the 1960s
     a definite improvement in the POSITION OF WOMEN in society

   AND THE COUNTER-ASSESSMENT:
     in 2012 Jonathan Fenby, using the latest research, claimed that Mao
     "had brought the country to its knees" and that China was virtually
     bankrupt in 1976 when Mao died
```

⚠️ The three-Part structure is the lesson. Most answers give Part B only. An answer that gives all three, and states clearly which parts rest on which evidence, is doing the historian's job rather than the pamphleteer's.

### Part A - What the Leap was and why it was attempted

✅ Mao felt that something new and different was needed to meet China's special problems - something not based on Russian experience. ✅ The Great Leap Forward involved further important developments in both industry and agriculture, in order to increase output - agriculture in particular was not providing the required food - and to adapt industry to Chinese conditions.

⚠️ Begin by taking the rationale seriously, because a question asking you to assess the Leap cannot be answered well by an author who thinks it was obviously absurd. The diagnosis contained three propositions, and two of them were correct. ⚠️ First, agriculture was not producing enough food - true, and the reason the whole exercise was undertaken. ⚠️ Second, the Soviet model was designed for a different country - defensible, since Soviet heavy industrialisation presupposed a capital-intensive path that a country with China's labour surplus and capital scarcity had reason to question. ⚠️ Third, the remedy was to mobilise labour at enormous scale through new institutions - and this is where the proposition failed, for reasons Part B gives.

**The communes.** ✅ These were units larger than collective farms, containing up to 75,000 people, divided into brigades and work teams with an elected council. ✅ They ran their own collective farms and factories, carried out most of the functions of local government within the commune, and undertook special local projects. ✅ One typical commune in 1965 contained 30,000 people, of which a third were children at school or in creches, a third were housewives or elderly, and the rest were the workforce - including a science team of 32 graduates and 43 technicians. ✅ Each family received a share of the profits and also had a small private plot of land.

⚠️ Read that description carefully and notice what a commune actually was. It was not a big farm. It was a *unit of government* that also farmed, manufactured, educated, provided childcare and undertook public works. ⚠️ That is why the assessment in Part C can call it a successful innovation even while the campaign that created it is judged a catastrophe: the institution and the campaign are separable. ⚠️ Notice also the small private plot, which survived inside the collective structure - a detail worth knowing because it is precisely what the reform era later expanded into the household responsibility system.

**The industrial change.** ✅ Instead of aiming for large-scale works of the type seen in the USSR and the West, much smaller factories were set up in the countryside to provide machinery for agriculture. ✅ Mao talked of 600,000 "backyard steel furnaces" springing up, organised and managed by the communes, which also undertook to build roads, canals, dams, reservoirs and irrigation channels.

⚠️ Note the precision required here: ✅ Mao *talked of* 600,000 backyard furnaces. ❌ Do not write that 600,000 were built and produced a stated quantity of steel; the source supports the target and the concept, not an output figure.

### Part B - The failure, and the mortality evidence

✅ At first it looked as though the Great Leap might be a failure: there was some opposition to the communes, a series of bad harvests in 1959-61, and the withdrawal of all Russian aid following the breach between Russia and China. ✅ All this, coupled with the lack of experience among the cadres, caused hardship in the years 1959-63.

⚠️ Four causes, and they should be distinguished in an answer because they carry different degrees of responsibility. Opposition to the communes and cadre inexperience are internal to the policy; the harvests are environmental; the Soviet withdrawal is external and is itself a consequence of the split taught in Lesson 16. ⚠️ Note the interaction that makes the episode so lethal: a policy that reorganised food production on an unprecedented scale, executed by inexperienced organisers, coincided with bad harvests and the loss of external technical support.

**The mortality evidence.** This session reports two figures, each with its own author and its own bracket, and does not combine them.

✅ **Figure one:** statistics which emerged later suggested that *some 20 million people may have died prematurely* as a result of hardships, especially the disastrous famine of 1959-60, caused by the Great Leap.

✅ **Figure two:** Professor Frank Dikötter's research in the newly opened archives reveals that the situation was much worse than the official account shows. ✅ Towards the end of the Great Leap Forward, special teams were sent out to discover the extent of the disaster around the country; ✅ their findings included reports on peasant resistance during the collectivisation campaign, reports about mass murders, confessions of leaders responsible for millions of deaths, and reports about working conditions. ✅ In Dikötter's words: "What comes out of this massive and detailed dossier is a tale of horror in which Chairman Mao emerges as one of the greatest mass murderers in human history, responsible for the premature deaths of *at least 45 million people between 1958 and 1962*. It is not merely the extent of the catastrophe that dwarfs earlier estimates, but also the manner in which many people died: *between two and three million victims were tortured to death or summarily killed*, often for the slightest infraction." ✅ He records an instance: when a boy stole a handful of grain in a Hunan village, the local boss Xiong Dechang forced his father to bury him alive, and the father died of grief a few days later. ✅ He also records that the killing of slackers, weaklings, those too ill to work, or otherwise unproductive elements increased the overall food supply for those who contributed to the regime through their labour, and that at one meeting Mao announced: "It is better to let half the people die so that the other half can eat their fill".

⚠️ **The writing discipline, stated as rules.**

1. ⚠️ Each figure travels with its author and its period. The first is "statistics which emerged later suggested some 20 million may have died prematurely, especially in the famine of 1959-60". The second is "Frank Dikötter, working in the newly opened archives, puts the premature deaths at at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962".
2. ❌ Never present either figure as the settled toll, and ❌ never write a range such as "20 to 45 million" as though the two were endpoints of one estimate. They are estimates by different people, over different periods, using different evidence.
3. ⚠️ The qualifier "may have died prematurely" in the first figure is doing real work and must be retained. Premature death from hardship is a different measurement from killing, and the sources do not use one term for both.
4. ⚠️ The two-to-three million tortured to death or summarily killed is a separate category within Dikötter's account and should be reported as such, because it distinguishes deliberate killing from famine mortality.
5. ⚠️ The quotation attributed to Mao at a meeting should be reported as recorded by Dikötter from the archives, not as a saying in general circulation.

⚠️ Why the discipline matters beyond honesty: an examiner reading an answer that says "the Great Leap killed 45 million" cannot tell whether the candidate knows anything about the evidence. An answer that says "estimates diverge sharply, from statistics emerging later suggesting some 20 million premature deaths concentrated in the 1959-60 famine, to Dikötter's archival figure of at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962, of whom he estimates two to three million were tortured to death or summarily killed" demonstrates command of a contested historiography in one sentence.

**The political consequence.** ✅ Even Mao's prestige suffered and he was forced to resign as Chairman of the People's Congress, to be succeeded by Liu Shaoqi, ✅ though he remained Chairman of the Communist Party.

⚠️ This is the most consequential institutional fact in the post-1949 half of the topic and it is routinely missed. Mao lost the *state* office and kept the *party* office. ⚠️ In a party-state, the party office is the one that matters, and Lesson 14 shows exactly what he did with it: ✅ he used his position as Chairman of the Party to rouse the young people, and the man who replaced him as head of state became the principal target. ⚠️ Any answer on the origins of the Cultural Revolution that does not mention this division of offices has missed the mechanism.

### Part C - The ledger, and how to weigh it

✅ In the long term the importance of the Great Leap became clear. ✅ According to the official account, by the early 1970s both agricultural and industrial production had increased substantially, and China was at least managing to feed its massive population without any further famine, which had rarely happened under the Nationalists. ✅ The communes proved to be a successful innovation: they were much more than merely collective farms - they were an efficient unit of local government and they enabled the central government in Beijing to keep in touch with local opinion. ✅ They seemed to be the ideal solution to the problem of running a vast country while avoiding the over-centralisation that stifles initiative.

✅ The crucial decision had been taken that China would remain predominantly an agricultural country with small-scale industry scattered around the countryside, and that the economy would be labour-intensive, relying on massive numbers of workers instead of labour-saving machines. ✅ Given the country's enormous population, this was arguably the best way of making sure that everybody had a job, and it enabled China to avoid the growing unemployment problems of the highly industrialised western nations.

✅ Other genuine benefits were the spread of education and welfare services and a reduction in infant mortality, which fell from 203 per thousand births in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s. ✅ There was also a definite improvement in the position of women in society.

✅ Again, however, the true picture may well not be quite so rosy as it appears: ✅ in 2012 Jonathan Fenby, an expert in Chinese affairs making use of the latest research, claimed that Mao "had brought the country to its knees" and that China was virtually bankrupt in 1976 when Mao died.

⚠️ **How to handle Part C without either whitewashing or dismissing it.** Three rules.

1. ⚠️ **Mark the provenance of the positive ledger.** ✅ The production and food-security claims are explicitly "according to the official account", and ✅ the same archives that produced Dikötter's figures also showed that official accounts exaggerated achievements. ⚠️ So the positive production claims carry an evidentiary discount that the infant-mortality series and the position of women do not carry in the same way.
2. ⚠️ **Do not let the ledger become a justification.** ⚠️ Improvements in infant mortality and in the position of women are consequences of the regime across a quarter-century; they are not payment for a famine. ⚠️ The appropriate relation between the two sides is simultaneity, not offsetting.
3. ⚠️ **State the strongest form of each side.** ⚠️ The strongest form of the positive case is the labour-intensive strategic choice and the commune as a unit of local government - real institutional innovations with defensible reasoning behind them. ⚠️ The strongest form of the negative case is Dikötter's archival finding combined with Fenby's economic verdict: the human cost was far greater than earlier estimates, and the economy Mao left was, on the latest research, close to bankruptcy.

⚠️ The formulation worth writing is that the Great Leap Forward attempted a genuine strategic alternative to the Soviet model, produced one durable institution, and imposed a human cost whose scale is disputed but whose character is not; and that a regime which responds to the failure of a mobilisation by mobilising harder - which is what Lesson 14 shows happening - has converted a policy error into a systemic one.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The Leap appears in almost every question on Maoism, on the assessment of Mao, and on the costs of forced modernisation. ⚠️ The core account's own end-of-chapter question puts it sharply: how far would you agree that the policies of Mao and the party between 1949 and 1960 were "a total and unmitigated disaster"?

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Give the rationale first and grant its two defensible premises. ⚠️ Describe the communes precisely, as units of government rather than large farms. ⚠️ Give the four causes of failure and their interaction. ⚠️ Give both mortality estimates with authors and brackets. ⚠️ Give the political consequence - state office lost, party office retained. ⚠️ Give the ledger with its provenance marked. ⚠️ Conclude with the formulation above, which answers the "total and unmitigated" phrasing directly: not total, because of the commune and the strategic choice; not mitigated either, because the ledger does not offset the cost.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Giving a single death toll. ✅ Two estimates with different authors and periods exist and must be distinguished.
2. ❌ Writing that 600,000 backyard furnaces were built. ✅ Mao talked of 600,000; the figure is a target and a concept.
3. ⚠️ Saying Mao lost power after the Leap. ✅ He resigned as Chairman of the People's Congress and remained Chairman of the party.
4. ⚠️ Treating communes as merely large collective farms. ✅ They carried out most local government functions, had elected councils, brigades and work teams, ran factories, and allowed family profit shares and small private plots.
5. ⚠️ Repeating the official production claims without marking them. ✅ They are attributed to the official account, and the archives showed such accounts exaggerated achievements.
6. ⚠️ Dating the famine loosely. ✅ Bad harvests 1959-61; hardship 1959-63; the disastrous famine named as 1959-60; Dikötter's bracket 1958-62.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Rationale: something new and different, not based on Russian experience; increase output, since agriculture was not providing the required food; adapt industry to Chinese conditions.
2. ✅ Communes: larger than collective farms, up to 75,000 people, brigades and work teams with an elected council; ran collective farms and factories; carried out most local government functions; undertook special local projects.
3. ✅ Illustrative commune, 1965: 30,000 people - one third children at school or in creches, one third housewives or elderly, the rest the workforce, including a science team of 32 graduates and 43 technicians; family profit shares and small private plots.
4. ✅ Industry: small rural factories making machinery for agriculture instead of large-scale works; Mao talked of 600,000 backyard steel furnaces; communes also built roads, canals, dams, reservoirs and irrigation channels.
5. ✅ Failure: opposition to the communes; bad harvests 1959-61; withdrawal of all Russian aid after the breach; cadre inexperience; hardship 1959-63.
6. ✅ Estimate one: statistics emerging later suggested some 20 million may have died prematurely, especially in the disastrous famine of 1959-60.
7. ✅ Estimate two: Frank Dikötter, from the newly opened archives, at least 45 million premature deaths between 1958 and 1962, of whom between two and three million were tortured to death or summarily killed.
8. ✅ Mao forced to resign as Chairman of the People's Congress, succeeded by Liu Shaoqi; remained Chairman of the Communist Party.
9. ✅ Ledger, from the official account: substantial production increases by the early 1970s; population fed without further famine; communes a successful innovation and an efficient unit of local government; the labour-intensive strategic choice; spread of education and welfare; infant mortality 203 per thousand in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s; definite improvement in the position of women.
10. ✅ Counter-assessment: Jonathan Fenby in 2012 claimed Mao had brought the country to its knees and that China was virtually bankrupt in 1976.
11. ⚠️ Writing rules: figures travel with author and period; never merge estimates into a range; retain "may have died prematurely"; report deliberate killing separately from famine mortality.
12. ⚠️ Verdict formulation: a genuine strategic alternative, one durable institution, a human cost disputed in scale but not in character; and a regime that answered failure with intensification.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Mao's resignation as Chairman of the People's Congress is the key institutional fact linking the Great Leap Forward to the Cultural Revolution.

**Model answer:** Because it separated two offices whose separation determined what happened next. Mao's prestige suffered after the Leap and he was forced to resign as Chairman of the People's Congress, being succeeded by Liu Shaoqi, but he remained Chairman of the Communist Party. In a party-state the party office is the one that controls mobilisation, appointment and the political line, so Mao retained the instrument of power while losing the formal headship of state to the man who, with Deng Xiaoping, led the argument for incentives, wage differentials, larger private plots and an expert managerial class. When Mao launched the Cultural Revolution he used his position as Chairman of the party to rouse the young people, and the target was an over-bureaucratic party leadership under his deputy Liu Shaoqi. The Cultural Revolution is therefore the party chairman using the party against the state apparatus headed by his own successor - a conflict made possible precisely by the division of offices that the Leap's failure produced.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Leap's failure as a straightforward loss of power for Mao. He lost a formal office and kept the decisive one, and the interval between 1959 and 1966 was not a period of retirement but one in which a public debate between the rightists, including Liu Shaoqi and Deng Xiaoping, and the Maoists took place over which course China should follow. Reading the period as Mao's eclipse makes the Cultural Revolution look like an inexplicable return rather than the outcome of a continuing contest.

## Lesson 14 - The Cultural Revolution: why it happened, what it destroyed, and how the Party itself judged it

Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Cultural Revolution, including its purpose, the rightist arguments for incentives and an expert managerial class, Deng Xiaoping's own doubts about the Great Leap, the public debate of 1963 to 1966, the use of the party chairmanship to rouse the young, the Red Guards and their Little Red Books, and the closure of schools and later factories; and the later section recording the trial of the Gang of Four, the Party's Resolution condemning the Cultural Revolution, the praise retained for the suppression of the Lin Biao clique and the historian Steve Smith's assessment of what the Resolution was for. The portion of the extraction covering the closing phase of the campaign is damaged and is recorded as an open gap in the final ledger; the canonical owners supply the recorded damage and the longer dating
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: an argument that became a campaign

```text
   THE ARGUMENT, 1963-1966
   =======================
   RIGHTISTS (including LIU SHAOQI and DENG XIAOPING)
     - INCENTIVES are necessary if the communes are to function
       efficiently: piecework, greater wage differentials, larger
       private plots (which had been creeping in in some areas)
     - there should be an EXPERT MANAGERIAL CLASS to push forward with
       industrialization on the Russian model, instead of relying on
       the cadres
     - even Deng Xiaoping, one of Mao's most loyal supporters, had
       grave doubts about the wisdom of the Great Leap
                              vs
   MAOISTS
     - these ideas are TOTALLY UNACCEPTABLE: they are exactly what Mao
       was condemning among the Russians, whom he dismissed as
       "REVISIONISTS" taking the "CAPITALIST ROAD"
     - the Party must avoid the emergence of a PRIVILEGED CLASS who
       would exploit the workers
     - it is vital to KEEP IN TOUCH WITH THE MASSES

   THE CONVERSION OF ARGUMENT INTO CAMPAIGN
   ========================================
   Mao's purpose: keep the revolution and the Great Leap on a pure
   Marxist-Leninist course, and hit back at what he considered an
   OVER-BUREAUCRATIC PARTY LEADERSHIP under his deputy Liu Shaoqi
                              |
   Instrument: his position as CHAIRMAN OF THE PARTY, used to ROUSE THE
   YOUNG PEOPLE (the office he kept after 1959 - see Lesson 13)
                              |
   "GREAT PROLETARIAN CULTURAL REVOLUTION"
     Mao appealed to the masses
     RED GUARDS, mostly students, toured the country arguing Mao's case,
     carrying their LITTLE RED BOOKS containing the thoughts of
     Chairman Mao
     In some areas SCHOOLS, and later FACTORIES, were closed down

   THE DATES - BOTH ARE DEFENSIBLE
   ===============================
     1966-1969 : the period of mass upheaval (core account's dating)
     1966-1976 : the whole campaign to Mao's death (canonical owners)
   State which phenomenon you are dating and you cannot be wrong.

   THE VERDICT, DELIVERED BY THE PARTY ITSELF
   ==========================================
     The GANG OF FOUR put on trial for "evil, monstrous and unpardonable
     crimes" committed during the Cultural Revolution; all found guilty
     and sentenced to LIFE IMPRISONMENT
     The Central Committee issued a "RESOLUTION" condemning the Cultural
     Revolution as a grave "LEFT" ERROR for which MAO HIMSELF WAS
     CHIEFLY RESPONSIBLE
     - but Mao was PRAISED for his successful efforts to "smash the
       counter-revolutionary LIN BIAO clique"
     Steve Smith: "By pinning the blame on one man in this fashion, the
     Resolution sought to exculpate the 'overwhelming majority' of CCP
     leaders who were said to have been on the right side in the
     struggle. The Resolution thus underwrote a shift of authority
     within the CCP from a single leader to a COLLECTIVE LEADERSHIP."
```

⚠️ The last box is the most sophisticated thing in this lesson. The Party's condemnation of its own founder was not simply an act of honesty; it performed a political function, and identifying that function is what a strong answer does.

### 1. The quarrel that produced the campaign

✅ The Cultural Revolution was Mao's attempt to keep the revolution and the Great Leap on a pure Marxist-Leninist course, and to hit back at what he considered to be an over-bureaucratic party leadership under his deputy, Liu Shaoqi.

✅ In the early 1960s, when the success of the Great Leap was by no means certain, opposition to Mao grew. ✅ Right-wing members of the party believed that incentives - piecework, greater wage differentials and larger private plots, which had been creeping in in some areas - were necessary if the communes were to function efficiently. ✅ They also felt that there should be an expert managerial class to push forward with industrialisation on the Russian model, instead of relying on the cadres. ✅ Even Deng Xiaoping, one of Mao's most loyal supporters, had grave doubts about the wisdom of the Great Leap. ✅ But to the Maoists, these ideas were totally unacceptable; it was exactly what Mao was condemning among the Russians, whom he dismissed as "revisionists" taking the "capitalist road". ✅ The party must avoid the emergence of a privileged class who would exploit the workers; it was vital to keep in touch with the masses.

✅ Between 1963 and 1966 there was a great public debate between the rightists, including Liu Shaoqi and Deng Xiaoping, and the Maoists about which course to follow.

⚠️ Three observations that raise an answer above narrative.

⚠️ First, **the rightist position is the reform programme of 1978 in embryo.** Incentives, wage differentials, larger private plots, managerial expertise - every element of Deng's later policy is present in the argument he was losing in 1963. ⚠️ This is the single most useful continuity in the post-1949 half of the topic: the Cultural Revolution was the defeat of a programme that was implemented twelve years after it was defeated, by the man who had proposed it.

⚠️ Second, **the domestic and international quarrels are the same quarrel.** Mao condemned the Russians as revisionists on the capitalist road, and he condemned his own deputy in the same terms. ⚠️ Lesson 16 shows the international side; the point to carry across is that the Sino-Soviet split and the Cultural Revolution are not two separate stories but two expressions of one argument about whether socialism could be built through expertise, incentives and coexistence, or only through mobilisation and struggle.

⚠️ Third, **the fear was of class formation inside the revolution.** The Maoist objection was not to prosperity but to the emergence of a privileged class who would exploit the workers. ⚠️ That is a coherent worry for a revolutionary movement and it should be stated as such before it is judged, because a question asking whether the Cultural Revolution was a genuine battle of ideas or a bid for personal power cannot be answered by someone who thinks there were no ideas in it.

### 2. The campaign

✅ Mao, using his position as Chairman of the party to rouse the young people, launched a desperate campaign to "save" the revolution. ✅ In this Great Proletarian Cultural Revolution, as he called it, Mao appealed to the masses. ✅ His supporters, the Red Guards - mostly students - toured the country arguing Mao's case, and carrying their Little Red Books containing the thoughts of Chairman Mao. ✅ In some areas schools, and later factories, were closed down.

⚠️ Note the instrument and the constituency. The instrument was the party chairmanship - the office retained in 1959 - and the constituency was the young, who had no stake in the bureaucratic order being attacked and every reason to welcome a licence to attack their seniors. ⚠️ Note also what "closing schools" means in a campaign whose stated purpose was to prevent the emergence of a privileged expert class: the institution that produces experts was shut. ⚠️ The canonical owners record the consequence: ✅ education, administration and social stability were badly damaged, and ⚠️ the assault on education produced what is fairly described as a lost cohort - a generation whose schooling was interrupted or ended.

⚠️ The extraction available for this session is damaged at the point where the campaign's closing phase is described, and this session therefore does not narrate the suppression of the Red Guards, the role of the army, or the sequence leading to the Lin Biao episode. ⚠️ What is recorded and used is what the legible evidence supports: the origins, the instruments, the closures, the two datings, the Lin Biao clique as named in the Party's own Resolution, the trial of the Gang of Four and the Resolution's verdict. ⚠️ The remainder is recorded as an open gap in the final ledger rather than reconstructed.

### 3. The two datings, handled openly

✅ The core account dates the campaign **1966-9**. ✅ Both canonical owners date it **1966-76**.

⚠️ Neither is an error, and a candidate who understands why will never lose a mark on it. ⚠️ The shorter dating covers the period of mass upheaval - Red Guards, closures, the most intense phase of struggle. ⚠️ The longer dating covers the whole campaign as a political era, ending only with Mao's death in 1976, which is also how the Party's own retrospective treats it. ⚠️ The safe written form is: "the Cultural Revolution, launched in 1966, whose phase of mass upheaval is usually dated to 1969 and whose political era is conventionally closed at Mao's death in 1976".

⚠️ The related control from the canonical owners is that ✅ the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution are two distinct Mao-era campaigns with distinct failures and ❌ must never be merged into one event.

### 4. The Party's own verdict, and what it was for

✅ As a gesture of open criticism of Mao and his policies, the Gang of Four were put on trial for "evil, monstrous and unpardonable crimes" committed during the Cultural Revolution. ✅ They were all found guilty and sentenced to life imprisonment. ✅ The Central Committee of the party issued a "Resolution" condemning the Cultural Revolution as a grave "Left" error for which Mao himself was chiefly responsible. ✅ However, Mao was praised for his successful efforts to "smash the counter-revolutionary Lin Biao clique".

✅ As historian Steve Smith explained: "By pinning the blame on one man in this fashion, the Resolution sought to exculpate the 'overwhelming majority' of CCP leaders who were said to have been on the right side in the struggle. The Resolution thus underwrote a shift of authority within the CCP from a single leader to a collective leadership."

⚠️ This is the most analytically rich passage in the post-Mao material and it repays close reading. The Resolution did three things at once.

1. ⚠️ **It told the truth about the campaign.** A grave "Left" error for which Mao was chiefly responsible is a severe and accurate judgement, and the fact that a ruling party delivered it about its own founder within five years of his death is remarkable.
2. ⚠️ **It protected the institution.** By concentrating responsibility on one man, it implied that the overwhelming majority of leaders had been on the right side - which was convenient, since many of them had served throughout.
3. ⚠️ **It changed the constitutional principle.** The move from a single leader to a collective leadership is the durable consequence, and it is the framework within which every subsequent succession has been managed.

⚠️ The examinable formulation is that the Resolution was simultaneously an act of historical judgement and an act of institutional self-defence, and that these are not in contradiction: it was by blaming Mao that the party could both acknowledge the catastrophe and survive the acknowledgement.

### Objection and reply

⚠️ **The objection.** The core account's own end-of-chapter question puts it directly: was the Cultural Revolution an attempt by Mao to protect his own power and position rather than a genuine battle of ideas?

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** ⚠️ First, concede the power dimension, because the evidence supports it: Mao had been forced out of the state chairmanship after the Great Leap, the campaign was directed at an over-bureaucratic party leadership under the very deputy who had replaced him, and he used his remaining office to mobilise a constituency against his colleagues. ⚠️ Second, insist on the ideas, because the evidence supports those too: there was a great public debate between 1963 and 1966 about incentives, wage differentials, private plots and whether an expert managerial class should replace the cadres, and Mao's objection - that a privileged class would emerge and exploit the workers - is a recognisable position within Marxist argument, held consistently and applied to the Soviet Union as well as to his own party. ⚠️ Third, resolve them: the two explanations reinforce rather than exclude each other, because in a party-state a defeat on policy *is* a defeat on power, and a leader who believes his policy is the revolution will experience the defence of his position as the defence of the revolution. ⚠️ The balanced verdict is that the Cultural Revolution was a genuine battle of ideas fought by a leader whose position depended on winning it, and that the means chosen - mobilising students against institutions - reveal how little confidence he had of winning it by any other route.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The core account's questions supply two framings directly: whether Mao's policies from 1949 to 1960 were a total and unmitigated disaster, and whether the Cultural Revolution was a bid for power rather than a battle of ideas. ⚠️ Broader stems on Maoism's impact on Chinese society and state formation route here for the destructive half of the ledger.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Open with the 1963-66 debate and name both sides, including Deng among the doubters. ⚠️ Give the purpose in Mao's own terms - purity of course and the over-bureaucratic leadership. ⚠️ Give the instruments: the party chairmanship, the Red Guards, the Little Red Books, the closures. ⚠️ Use the correct dating formula. ⚠️ Give the Party's Resolution and Smith's reading of its function. ⚠️ Close with the objection-and-reply resolution above.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Merging the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution. ✅ They are distinct campaigns with distinct failures.
2. ⚠️ Using only one dating. ✅ Say which phenomenon you are dating - upheaval to 1969, era to 1976.
3. ⚠️ Presenting it as purely a power struggle. ✅ There was a great public debate on identifiable economic and organisational questions between 1963 and 1966.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting that Deng had doubts about the Great Leap. ✅ Even Deng Xiaoping, one of Mao's most loyal supporters, had grave doubts.
5. ⚠️ Treating the Party's Resolution as simple honesty. ⚠️ Smith records its function in exculpating the leadership and shifting authority to collective leadership.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Purpose: keep the revolution and the Great Leap on a pure Marxist-Leninist course and hit back at an over-bureaucratic party leadership under Liu Shaoqi.
2. ✅ Rightist case, 1963-66: incentives - piecework, greater wage differentials, larger private plots; an expert managerial class on the Russian model instead of reliance on cadres; Liu Shaoqi and Deng Xiaoping among them; Deng had grave doubts about the Great Leap.
3. ✅ Maoist case: these ideas were what Mao condemned among the Russians as "revisionists" on the "capitalist road"; the party must avoid a privileged class exploiting the workers; keeping in touch with the masses was vital.
4. ✅ Instrument: Mao used his position as Chairman of the party to rouse the young people.
5. ✅ Great Proletarian Cultural Revolution: appeal to the masses; Red Guards, mostly students, touring the country with Little Red Books of the thoughts of Chairman Mao; in some areas schools, and later factories, closed down.
6. ✅ Dating: 1966-9 in the core account for the mass upheaval; 1966-76 in the canonical owners for the era to Mao's death.
7. ✅ Recorded damage: education, administration and social stability badly damaged; ⚠️ a cohort lost to interrupted schooling.
8. ✅ Gang of Four tried for "evil, monstrous and unpardonable crimes", found guilty, life imprisonment.
9. ✅ Central Committee Resolution: the Cultural Revolution a grave "Left" error for which Mao was chiefly responsible; Mao praised for smashing the "counter-revolutionary Lin Biao clique".
10. ✅ Steve Smith: the Resolution exculpated the "overwhelming majority" of leaders and underwrote a shift from a single leader to a collective leadership.
11. ⚠️ Continuity point: the rightist programme of 1963-66 is the reform programme of 1978, implemented by one of the men who lost the argument.
12. ⚠️ Verdict: a genuine battle of ideas fought by a leader whose position depended on winning it.

### Concept check

**Question:** What political work did the Party's Resolution on the Cultural Revolution perform, beyond stating a historical judgement?

**Model answer:** It performed two further functions. By condemning the Cultural Revolution as a grave "Left" error for which Mao himself was chiefly responsible, it concentrated blame on a single individual, and as Steve Smith explains, pinning the blame on one man in this fashion sought to exculpate the overwhelming majority of party leaders who were said to have been on the right side of the struggle - many of whom had in fact served through the period. That protected the institution while condemning the campaign. Second, the Resolution underwrote a shift of authority within the party from a single leader to a collective leadership, which converted a historical verdict into a constitutional principle and became the framework for managing succession thereafter. The accompanying trial of the Gang of Four for "evil, monstrous and unpardonable crimes" supplied identifiable culprits, and the retained praise for Mao's suppression of the counter-revolutionary Lin Biao clique showed that the repudiation was calibrated rather than total. The Resolution was therefore simultaneously an act of historical judgement and an act of institutional self-preservation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the Resolution as either pure honesty or pure cynicism. Both readings lose the interesting point. The judgement it delivered was severe and substantially accurate, and it was delivered in a form that allowed the surviving leadership to continue governing and to change the rule about how power would be held. Recognising that a truthful verdict can also be a political instrument is exactly the kind of observation that distinguishes a high-scoring answer on this topic.

---

## Lesson 15 - Maoism as ideology and as practice: the doctrine, the debates and the balanced judgement

Progress: 15/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: what was distinctive in Maoist practice; the campaign as an instrument of government; the relation between Maoism and the communism owned elsewhere in the syllabus; the achievement side of the ledger; the catastrophe side; the historiography from Gray to Dikötter to Fenby; and the formulation that holds both without evasion

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the advanced owner's balance-sheet on Mao's legacy with its achievement and cost rows and its summarising judgement, together with the core account's records across the post-1949 chapter: the rejection of Russian experience, the reliance on cadres rather than experts, the repeated recourse to mass campaigns, the Hundred Flowers sequence, the commune design, and the assessments by Jack Gray, Frank Dikotter, Jonathan Fenby, Steve Smith and Robert Service
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five distinctive features, each with its characteristic failure

| ⚠️ Feature of Maoist practice | ✅ Where it is visible in the record | ⚠️ Its characteristic failure mode |
|---|---|---|
| **The peasantry as revolutionary base** | The strategic change after 1927 to winning mass support among the peasants rather than capturing industrial towns; the locally varied land policy | Works for insurgency and land reform; supplies no method for industrial or technical development |
| **Politics in command of expertise** | Reliance on cadres rather than an expert managerial class; the cadres' fear of the new technicians in 1957; the rejection of the rightist case in 1963-66 | Policies executed by organisers without technical competence - recorded as the lack of experience among the cadres during the Great Leap |
| **Rejection of the imported model** | Mao's grave doubts whether China was suited to Soviet-style heavy industrialisation; something new and different, not based on Russian experience | Correct as a diagnosis; the alternative adopted was untested at the scale at which it was attempted |
| **Government by mass campaign** | The Hundred Flowers campaign; the Great Leap Forward; the Great Proletarian Cultural Revolution | Campaigns mobilise but cannot be calibrated, reversed quickly or audited; failure is read as insufficient commitment |
| **Continuous struggle against internal privilege** | The party must avoid the emergence of a privileged class who would exploit the workers; the charge of revisionism against the Soviet Union and against Liu Shaoqi alike | Makes every policy dispute a loyalty question, so criticism, disloyalty and class war become blurred |

⚠️ The third column is the teaching. Each of the five features has a real rationale and a predictable pathology, and an assessment of Maoism that names both is far stronger than one that either celebrates or denounces.

### 1. What is owned here and what is not

⚠️ A definition first, and a boundary. What communism *is* as a political philosophy - its claims about property, class, the state and history, and its relation to socialism and to social democracy - is owned by the doctrine owner in this subject's allocation and is not restated here. ⚠️ This session owns the historical record of what a communist regime in China did.

⚠️ The practical consequence is a rule for answers. If the question asks about communism as a political philosophy and its forms and effects on society, open with the doctrine as owned elsewhere and use China as the case study. ⚠️ If the question asks about the Chinese revolution, Mao's legacy or communism in Asia, stay here and use doctrinal vocabulary only where the record supplies it - which it does, precisely, in the words Mao himself used: revisionism, the capitalist road, the masses, the cadres, the privileged class.

### 2. The achievement side, stated at its strongest

✅ China was politically unified, foreign domination was rolled back and the party-state established real authority across the country. ✅ Land reform and mass mobilisation changed rural power structures and created a new political order.

⚠️ Expand each of those into the form an examiner can credit.

⚠️ **Unification and the end of the century of humiliation.** Measured against Lesson 1, this is not a small claim. A state that had lost control of its coast, its tariffs, its legal jurisdiction in eighty ports and towns, and its monopoly of armed force recovered all of them. ✅ The concessions had already been formally renounced in January 1943, but it was after 1949 that a government existed capable of making the renunciation real.

⚠️ **Administrative capacity.** ✅ Full communications were restored and inflation was brought under control by the early 1950s - the two specific failures that had destroyed the previous regime.

⚠️ **Social transformation.** ⚠️ Land reform destroyed the landlord class as a social force; communes and later decollectivisation twice restructured rural life within a single generation; ✅ education and welfare services spread; ✅ infant mortality fell from 203 per thousand births in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s; ✅ there was a definite improvement in the position of women in society. ⚠️ Keep all of the qualitative claims qualitative: ❌ no output, literacy or growth figures are asserted here beyond the sourced infant-mortality series.

### 3. The catastrophe side, stated at its strongest

✅ The Great Leap Forward showed the danger of ideological overreach in economic policy. ✅ The Cultural Revolution shattered educational, administrative and social stability. ✅ Maoist politics repeatedly blurred criticism, disloyalty and class war.

⚠️ The third of those is the one to develop, because it is the mechanism behind the other two. ⚠️ A political system in which criticism of a policy can be recategorised as disloyalty, and disloyalty as class enmity, has disabled its own error-correction. ⚠️ Follow it through the record: ✅ in 1957 constructive criticism was invited and the critics were clamped down on; ✅ in 1959 the failure of the Leap was followed by the resignation of a state chairman rather than by policy reversal; ✅ between 1963 and 1966 an economic argument about incentives was recategorised as revisionism and the capitalist road; ✅ in 1966 the response was mass mobilisation against the party's own leadership. ⚠️ At each stage information about failure reached the leadership and was converted into evidence of insufficient revolutionary commitment.

⚠️ That is why the strongest single sentence available on Mao's legacy is not a moral verdict but a structural one: **the capacity that unified China was the capacity that made the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution possible.** A weaker state could not have collectivised 95 per cent of the peasantry in six years; it also could not have imposed a famine of the recorded scale or closed the schools of a continent-sized country.

### 4. The historiography, arranged as a sequence

| ✅ Assessment | ✅ Position | ⚠️ Evidentiary conditions |
|---|---|---|
| Jack Gray, writing in 1970 | Land redistribution carried out with remarkable attention to legality and minimum physical violence against landlords | Written while Mao was alive and the archives were closed |
| The official account, as recorded | Substantial production increases by the early 1970s; the population fed without further famine; the communes a successful innovation | ⚠️ The same archives later showed that official accounts exaggerated achievements and toned down or omitted unpleasant events |
| Frank Dikötter, using archives declassified around 2008 | Mao among the greatest mass murderers in history; at least 45 million premature deaths between 1958 and 1962; two to three million tortured to death or summarily killed; land reform in some areas a transfer to party activists rather than redistribution from landlords | Archival, and the basis for the sharpest revision of the record |
| Steve Smith | The Party's 1981 Resolution exculpated the leadership and shifted authority to collective leadership | Institutional analysis of the regime's own verdict |
| Jonathan Fenby, 2012 | Mao "had brought the country to its knees"; China virtually bankrupt in 1976 | Uses the latest research; an economic rather than a demographic judgement |
| The advanced owner's summarising judgement | Mao gave China unity, independence and revolutionary purpose, but at a huge human and institutional cost when campaigns displaced administrative rationality | ⚠️ A synthesis, and the formulation most useful for an answer's conclusion |

⚠️ Read the column of dates and a pattern appears that is itself examinable: assessments of Mao have moved steadily in one direction as archival access has widened. ⚠️ That is a statement about evidence, not about fashion, and it is the correct way to explain to an examiner why an older sympathetic judgement should not simply be dismissed as propaganda nor accepted as equally weighted.

### 5. Maoism outside China

⚠️ This is developed in Lessons 19 and 20, but the framing belongs here. ⚠️ Maoist practice offered other Asian movements a template that Soviet communism did not: a route to power for a movement without an industrial base, through rural organisation, land policy, guerrilla warfare and patriotic legitimacy. ⚠️ The record shows the template travelling unevenly. ✅ Vietnam's communism fused with anti-colonial nationalism more strongly than in China. ✅ North Korea's regime was produced by external division rather than internal revolution and predates the Chinese one. ✅ Cambodia's Khmer Rouge attempted total collectivisation almost overnight - ✅ and the account is explicit that even Mao had taken years to reach that stage in China. ⚠️ So the Chinese model was known, invoked and in Cambodia's case radically exceeded, but it was not exported as a package, and treating Asian communism as Chinese communism repeated is the error Lesson 20 exists to prevent.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The standard demands are "assess the impact of Maoism on Chinese society and state formation" and the quotation-style prompt that Mao made modern China but also deeply damaged it. ⚠️ Both require a single integrated judgement rather than two lists.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Define what is distinctive in Maoist practice using the five-feature table, not by reciting doctrine. ⚠️ Give the achievement side with its specific content, including the measures the previous regime had failed at. ⚠️ Give the catastrophe side and locate the mechanism in the blurring of criticism, disloyalty and class war. ⚠️ Deploy the historiographical sequence to show that the evidence base has shifted. ⚠️ Conclude with the structural formulation - the same capacity did both - rather than with a moral summary.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing a doctrine essay. ⚠️ The philosophy of communism is owned elsewhere; here the record is the evidence.
2. ❌ Producing an unowned death toll. ✅ Every figure carries an author and a period.
3. ⚠️ Treating achievement and catastrophe as offsetting. ⚠️ They are simultaneous, and the relation between them is structural.
4. ⚠️ Dismissing older sympathetic assessments. ⚠️ State the evidentiary conditions under which they were formed.
5. ⚠️ Asserting that Maoism was exported to Asia. ⚠️ It was invoked, adapted and in one case exceeded; it was not transplanted.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Five distinctive features: peasant base; politics in command of expertise; rejection of the imported model; government by mass campaign; continuous struggle against internal privilege.
2. ⚠️ Each has a characteristic failure mode - notably cadre inexperience in execution, and the impossibility of calibrating or auditing a campaign.
3. ✅ Achievements: political unification; foreign domination rolled back; a party-state with real authority; land reform and mass mobilisation transforming rural power structures; restored communications; inflation controlled.
4. ✅ Social change: education and welfare spread; infant mortality 203 per thousand in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s; a definite improvement in the position of women; ⚠️ the landlord class destroyed as a social force; ⚠️ rural life restructured twice in a generation.
5. ✅ Costs: ideological overreach in economic policy in the Great Leap; educational, administrative and social stability shattered by the Cultural Revolution; criticism, disloyalty and class war repeatedly blurred.
6. ⚠️ Error-correction mechanism disabled: failure read as insufficient commitment, producing intensification.
7. ✅ Historiography: Gray in 1970 (closed archives); the official account (later shown to exaggerate); Dikötter from 2008 archives; Smith on the 1981 Resolution; Fenby in 2012 on bankruptcy.
8. ⚠️ Summarising judgement: unity, independence and revolutionary purpose at huge human and institutional cost when campaigns displaced administrative rationality.
9. ⚠️ The structural sentence: the capacity that unified China was the capacity that made the two catastrophes possible.
10. ⚠️ Maoism abroad: invoked and adapted, not transplanted; exceeded in Cambodia, where total collectivisation was attempted almost overnight although even Mao had taken years.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it better to conclude an assessment of Mao with a structural statement than with a moral balance?

**Model answer:** Because a moral balance requires weighing incommensurable things - unification, literacy and infant mortality against famine mortality and destroyed institutions - and any weighting will look arbitrary. A structural statement explains instead of scoring. The structural point is that the same capacity produced both outcomes: a state able to collectivise 95 per cent of the peasantry within six years, restore communications, control inflation and mobilise the whole population is also a state able to impose the Great Leap Forward and to close the schools of a country of hundreds of millions. The mechanism that converted that capacity into catastrophe is identifiable in the record: Maoist politics repeatedly blurred criticism, disloyalty and class war, so evidence of failure was read as evidence of insufficient revolutionary commitment and produced intensification rather than correction. Concluding this way also matches the advanced owner's summarising judgement - unity, independence and revolutionary purpose at huge human and institutional cost when campaigns displaced administrative rationality - and answers the "made modern China but deeply damaged it" prompt without treating the two halves as separate verdicts.

**Misconception to avoid:** Trying to reach a verdict by adding up benefits and harms. The achievements and the catastrophes were produced by the same institutional machinery within the same quarter-century, so they cannot be netted against one another. Treating them as a ledger to be balanced also tends to smuggle in a justification, which the attribution discipline of this topic exists to prevent.

### Visual: a quarrel that escalated through four registers, and then closed
