```text
                                improved.
```

⚠️ Read the four registers in order and the analytical point writes itself: the quarrel began as theology, became strategy, became money, and ended as a nineteenth-century territorial claim - a grievance that predated both regimes and outlived the ideology that was supposed to have superseded it.

### 1. Why this lesson exists

⚠️ The single most common analytical error in Cold War answers is to treat "international communism" as one actor. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the decisive refutation, and it is empirical rather than theoretical: ✅ two communist states went to war with each other in February 1979, one of them in defence of a third communist regime that a fourth communist state had just overthrown. ⚠️ No argument about doctrinal unity survives that sentence.

⚠️ The formulation worth memorising is that the communist world behaved like a states system rather than like a church: China and the Soviet Union quarrelled over doctrine, then over strategy, then over territory, and finally fought each other's allies.

### 2. The alliance, and the date the deterioration begins

✅ Relations between the USSR and China deteriorated steadily after 1956. ✅ They had earlier signed a treaty of mutual assistance and friendship in 1950.

⚠️ Two controls apply immediately, and both are recorded by the canonical owner. ⚠️ First, because there was a treaty, the split must be described as a *breakdown* of an alliance, not as a permanent condition of hostility. ⚠️ Second, the date is 1956 and the deterioration is *steady*. ❌ Do not attribute the breach to a specific 1964 event or to Khrushchev's fall; ✅ the evidence held here dates the deterioration to "steadily after 1956" and supplies no 1964 breach.

⚠️ Why 1956 matters: it is the year of de-Stalinisation in the Soviet Union. The Chinese objection was to the political line that came with it - coexistence with the West and the possibility of non-revolutionary routes to communism - which is why the split is inseparable from the argument inside China described in Lessons 13 to 15.

### 3. The doctrinal and strategic charges

✅ The Chinese did not approve of Khrushchev's policies, particularly his belief in "peaceful coexistence", and his claim that it was possible to achieve communism by methods other than violent revolution. ✅ This went against the ideas of Lenin, leader of the 1917 Russian communist revolution, and so the Chinese accused the Russians of "revisionism" - revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs. ✅ They were angry at Khrushchev's "soft" line towards the USA. ✅ In retaliation the Russians reduced their economic aid to China.

⚠️ Three points to carry into an answer.

⚠️ First, **the definition of revisionism is supplied by the source and should be used**: revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit one's own needs. ⚠️ Candidates use the word constantly without defining it; defining it costs one clause and reads as precision.

⚠️ Second, **this is the same charge Mao brought against his own colleagues.** Lesson 14 recorded the rightist case on incentives and managerial expertise being dismissed as exactly what Mao condemned among the Russians, whom he called revisionists on the capitalist road. ⚠️ The international and domestic quarrels are therefore one quarrel with two theatres, and saying so is a genuinely high-level observation.

⚠️ Third, **the retaliation was material and it had consequences inside China.** ✅ The reduction and then withdrawal of Russian aid is one of the four recorded causes of the Great Leap Forward's failure. ⚠️ So the split is not only a Cold War fact; it is part of the explanation of a domestic catastrophe.

### 4. The territorial claim

✅ The ideological argument was not the only source of trouble: there was also a frontier dispute. ✅ During the nineteenth century Russia had taken over large areas of Chinese territory north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang province, which the Chinese were now demanding back, so far without success. ✅ Now that China herself was following a "softer" policy towards the USA, it seemed that the territorial problem was the main bone of contention.

⚠️ This is the strongest single observation available for a 20-mark answer on the split, and it should be stated in full. The territories were taken during the nineteenth century - that is, during exactly the period covered in Lesson 1, by one of the same powers that had extracted leaseholds and concessions. ⚠️ A revolutionary state that had overthrown its own imperial order and allied with the successor of the Russian empire was pressing an imperial-era grievance against it. ⚠️ And as soon as the doctrinal quarrel softened, the territorial one emerged as the principal dispute - which is the clearest possible evidence that state interest, not ideology, was the durable substrate.

⚠️ The boundary must be observed: ❌ this session does not narrate any border-clash episode, troop numbers or aid and trade figures, because none is supported by the evidence held here.

### 5. The competition, the war and the itemised grievances

✅ At the end of the 1970s both Russia and China were vying for American support, against each other, for the leadership of world communism.

⚠️ Pause on the absurdity, because it is the point. The principal adversary of both communist powers had become the arbiter between them.

✅ To complicate matters further, Vietnam now supported Russia. ✅ When the Chinese attacked Vietnam in February 1979, relations reached rock bottom. ✅ The Chinese attack was partly in retaliation for Vietnam's invasion of Kampuchea in December 1978, which overthrew the Khmer Rouge government of Pol Pot, a protégé of China, and partly because of a frontier dispute. ✅ They withdrew after three weeks, having, as Beijing put it, "taught the Vietnamese a lesson".

✅ In 1984 the Chinese set out their grievances against the USSR: the presence of Russian troops in Afghanistan; Soviet backing of the Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea; and the Soviet troop build-up along the Chinese frontiers of Mongolia and Manchuria.

⚠️ The 1984 list is worth memorising because it is checkable and precise, and because all three items are about *state security and spheres of influence*. Not one of them is doctrinal. ⚠️ By 1984 the quarrel that had begun over peaceful coexistence had become a conventional great-power grievance list.

### 6. The closure

✅ Mikhail Gorbachev was determined to begin a new era in Sino-Russian relations. ✅ Five-year agreements on trade and economic co-operation were signed in July 1985 and regular contact took place between the two governments. ✅ A formal reconciliation took place in May 1989 when Gorbachev visited Beijing. ✅ Also in 1989 Vietnam withdrew its troops from Kampuchea, and so its relations with China improved.

⚠️ The bookend is what makes this a complete argument rather than a list of episodes: the split opened with de-Stalinisation in 1956 and closed with the last Soviet reformer in 1989 - the same year the bloc itself began to dissolve, and the same visit that coincided with the crisis in Tiananmen Square. ⚠️ Lesson 18 collects that coincidence, which is not a coincidence at all: the demonstrations continued throughout Gorbachev's visit, and the presence of the world's press for the reconciliation is part of why the crisis unfolded as it did.

### 7. The parallel track: the opening to the United States

✅ China and the USA had been extremely hostile towards each other since the Korean War and seemed likely to remain so while the Americans backed Chiang Kai-shek and the Nationalists in Taiwan, and while the Chinese backed Ho Chi Minh. ✅ However, in 1971 the Chinese unexpectedly invited an American table-tennis team to visit China. ✅ Following the success of that visit, the USA responded by calling off its veto of Chinese entry into the UN, and communist China was therefore allowed to become a member of the United Nations in October 1971.

✅ President Nixon decided he would visit China himself; Chairman Mao agreed to receive him and the visit took place in February 1972. ✅ Though Mao was reported to have told Zhou Enlai that the USA was "like an ape moving towards becoming a human being", the meeting reopened contact between two governments that had been hostile since the Korean War. ⚠️ It did **not** restore formal diplomatic relations: the visit produced rapprochement and working contact, and recognition came only at the end of the decade. ✅ President Ford also paid a successful visit to Beijing in 1975. ✅ There was still the problem of Taiwan to sour the relationship: though Chiang himself died in 1975, his supporters still occupied the island. ✅ Relations improved further in 1978 when President Carter decided to withdraw recognition of Nationalist China, which caused a row in the USA. ✅ The climax of détente came early in 1979 when Carter gave formal recognition to the People's Republic of China and ambassadors were exchanged; ✅ formal diplomatic relations took effect on **1 January 1979**, seven years after the Nixon visit. ✅ Good relations were maintained during the 1980s; ✅ the Chinese were anxious that détente with the USA should continue because of their conflict with Vietnam, Russia's ally, which had begun in 1979; ✅ and in 1985 an agreement was signed on nuclear co-operation.

⚠️ **The boundary.** The Korean War of 1950-3, the Vietnam War of 1961-75 and the structure of superpower détente belong to the Cold War owner. ⚠️ What is owned here is the effect of the opening on *China's* position: a state that had been isolated from the United States since the Korean War obtained the United Nations seat, diplomatic recognition and a strategic partner against the Soviet Union - and did so while still governed by the leadership that was simultaneously conducting the Cultural Revolution at home.

⚠️ That last observation is the one to use. ⚠️ The 1971-72 opening occurred during the Cultural Revolution era, which demonstrates that Maoist China's foreign policy was governed by calculations of state interest even at the height of its ideological radicalism. ⚠️ It is the strongest available evidence against the idea that revolutionary regimes conduct foreign policy by doctrine.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems ask whether international communism was a single bloc, about the Sino-Soviet split's causes and significance, or about China's changing Cold War alignment. ⚠️ The material is also required as a corrective paragraph in almost any broad Cold War answer.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Open with the 1950 treaty, so the split is framed as a breakdown. ⚠️ Give the four registers in order with the revisionism definition supplied. ⚠️ Give the frontier claim and the observation that it emerged as the main issue once doctrine softened. ⚠️ Give the 1979 war as the decisive empirical refutation of the bloc thesis, and the 1984 list as the itemised grievance. ⚠️ Close with the 1985 agreements and the May 1989 reconciliation, noting the bookend, and add the opening to the United States as the parallel demonstration that state interest governed.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Dating the breach to 1964 or attributing it to Khrushchev's fall. ✅ Deterioration ran steadily after 1956.
2. ❌ Adding border-clash narratives, congress dates, polemical texts, aid or trade figures or troop numbers. ⚠️ None is supported by the evidence held here.
3. ⚠️ Describing the communist world as monolithic. ✅ Use February 1979 as the decisive counter-evidence.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the territorial dimension. ✅ It became the main bone of contention once the doctrinal quarrel softened.
5. ⚠️ Treating the American opening as a post-Mao development. ✅ The table-tennis invitation, the UN seat and the Nixon visit all fall in 1971-72, with Mao receiving Nixon personally.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting that the aid cut fed the Great Leap's failure. ✅ Withdrawal of all Russian aid is one of the recorded causes.
7. ⚠️ Writing that the Nixon visit restored diplomatic relations. ✅ February 1972 reopened contact and produced rapprochement; ✅ formal recognition came from Carter early in 1979, with diplomatic relations effective 1 January 1979.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Treaty of mutual assistance and friendship, 1950; relations deteriorated steadily after 1956.
2. ✅ Doctrinal charge: disapproval of Khrushchev's "peaceful coexistence" and of achieving communism by methods other than violent revolution, against Lenin's ideas; hence the accusation of "revisionism" - revising or reinterpreting Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs.
3. ✅ Strategic charge: anger at Khrushchev's "soft" line towards the USA.
4. ✅ Material retaliation: the Russians reduced their economic aid to China.
5. ✅ Frontier dispute: nineteenth-century Russian acquisitions north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang, demanded back without success; the main bone of contention once China softened towards the USA.
6. ✅ End of the 1970s: both vying for American support against each other for the leadership of world communism.
7. ✅ February 1979: China attacked Vietnam - partly retaliation for Vietnam's December 1978 invasion of Kampuchea overthrowing Pol Pot, a protégé of China, partly frontier; withdrew after three weeks having "taught the Vietnamese a lesson".
8. ✅ 1984 grievances: Russian troops in Afghanistan; Soviet backing of Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea; Soviet build-up along the Mongolian and Manchurian frontiers.
9. ✅ July 1985: five-year trade and economic co-operation agreements; regular governmental contact. ✅ May 1989: formal reconciliation on Gorbachev's visit to Beijing; Vietnam withdrew from Kampuchea in the same year.
10. ✅ United States track: hostility since the Korean War; 1971 table-tennis invitation; UN membership October 1971; Nixon's visit February 1972, which reopened contact and produced rapprochement but did not restore formal diplomatic relations; Ford in 1975; Chiang died 1975; Carter withdrew recognition of Nationalist China in 1978; formal recognition of the People's Republic early in 1979, with diplomatic relations effective 1 January 1979 and ambassadors exchanged; nuclear co-operation agreement 1985.
11. ⚠️ Master formulation: the communist world behaved like a states system, not like a church.
12. ⚠️ Cross-link: the same charge of revisionism was used internationally and domestically - one quarrel, two theatres.

### Concept check

**Question:** Which single fact most decisively disproves the claim that international communism operated as one bloc, and why is the frontier dispute the most analytically revealing part of the split?

**Model answer:** The decisive fact is that communist states fought each other: in February 1979 China attacked Vietnam, partly in retaliation for Vietnam's invasion of Kampuchea in December 1978, which had overthrown the Khmer Rouge government of Pol Pot, a protégé of China, and partly because of a frontier dispute; the Chinese withdrew after three weeks claiming to have taught the Vietnamese a lesson. Four communist states were involved on opposing sides, with the Soviet Union backing Vietnam. The frontier dispute is the most revealing element because it shows the durable substrate beneath the doctrine. Russia had taken large areas of Chinese territory north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang during the nineteenth century, and China was demanding them back without success; and once China itself adopted a softer line towards the United States, the territorial problem appeared to be the main bone of contention. A grievance inherited from the imperial era therefore outlasted the ideological quarrel that was supposed to have superseded it, which is why the split is best explained as great-power politics conducted in doctrinal vocabulary rather than as a theological disagreement with geopolitical side-effects.

**Misconception to avoid:** Dating the breach to a single dramatic event, and in particular attributing it to 1964 or to Khrushchev's fall. The record states that relations deteriorated steadily after 1956, following de-Stalinisation, and supplies no 1964 rupture. Inventing a precise breaking point also obscures the structure of the quarrel, which escalated through doctrine, strategy, aid and territory over more than two decades before closing with the agreements of July 1985 and the reconciliation of May 1989.

---

## Lesson 17 - The Deng transition: reform without political reform

Progress: 17/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the succession struggle and Hua Guofeng's fall; the policy changes from June 1978; the reversals of Cultural Revolution measures; foreign loans, contracts and entry to the IMF and World Bank in 1980; the breaking up of the communes into household plots; the Democracy Wall of 1978-9; the procurement change of December 1984; the trade and inflation problems; Deng's own statement of his aims in November 1986; and the boundary where this topic hands over

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on China after Mao, including the removal of Hua Guofeng and the Politburo's stated reason, Robert Service's characterisation of Deng, the trial of the Gang of Four and the Party's Resolution, the dramatic policy changes from June 1978 with their two numbered strands, the Democracy Wall episode and its abolition in November 1979, modernisation and its problems including Zhao Ziyang's provincial record and the breaking up of the communes, the December 1984 procurement announcement, the 1984 trade deficit and import duties, the inflation figure for 1986 and Deng's magazine article of November 1986
CA search: "China Communist Party Fifteenth Five-Year Plan 2026 adopted National People's Congress official announcement"
CA found: Government of China official release, 24 October 2025 - the fourth plenary session of the 20th Communist Party Central Committee deliberated and adopted recommendations for formulating the 15th Five-Year Plan, setting development priorities for the 2026-2030 period
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what changed, what did not, and where the line was drawn

```text
   THE SUCCESSION
   ==============
   1976  Mao dies.
   Blame for economic failures falls on HUA GUOFENG:
     insufficient exports produced the biggest trade gap since the
     mid-1950s; inflation cancelled out wage increases
   1980  The Politburo decides Hua "lacks the political and organizational
         ability to be the Chairman of the Party". Hua is forced to resign.
   JUNE 1981  the 73-year-old DENG XIAOPING is undisputed leader.
   Robert Service: "Deng was as hard as teak. He endured as many demotions
   as promotions at Mao's hand since the 1950s. His son was crippled from
   the waist down after leaping from a window to escape physical
   maltreatment in the Cultural Revolution. Deng was forthright about the
   need for change ... and he knew he had no time to waste if he wanted to
   make the changes he wanted."

   WHAT CHANGED, FROM JUNE 1978
   ============================
   POLITICAL-ADMINISTRATIVE REVERSALS
     revolutionary committees running local government ABOLISHED, replaced
       by more democratically elected groups
     property confiscated from former capitalists RETURNED to survivors
     more RELIGIOUS FREEDOM
     greater freedom for INTELLECTUALS to express themselves in literature
       and the arts
   ECONOMIC OPENING (Deng and his protege HU YAOBANG)
     technical and financial help from the West to modernize industry,
       agriculture, science and technology - the FOUR MODERNIZATIONS
       (agriculture, industry, science and defence)
     LOANS accepted from foreign governments and banks
     CONTRACTS signed with foreign companies for modern equipment
     1980  China JOINS THE IMF AND THE WORLD BANK
     permission for PRIVATE INDUSTRIAL COMPANIES
     state farms given more control over planning, financing and profits
     BONUSES, PIECE-RATES and PROFIT-SHARING encouraged
     the state paid HIGHER PRICES to the communes and REDUCED TAXES
     result: grain output reached a RECORD LEVEL IN 1979; many peasants
       became prosperous
   THE RURAL REVERSAL (Zhao Ziyang)
     experiments, later extended nationwide, to BREAK UP THE COMMUNES and
       give peasants control of individual plots
     land still officially OWNED BY THE STATE, but divided and allocated
       to INDIVIDUAL PEASANT HOUSEHOLDS, allowed to keep most of the
       profits
     successful in raising agricultural production; living standards
       improved for many
     DECEMBER 1984  compulsory state purchase of crops ABANDONED; the
       state would continue to buy staple products in much smaller
       quantities; prices of surplus grain, pork, cotton and vegetables
       allowed to FLUCTUATE ON THE OPEN MARKET

   WHAT DID NOT CHANGE
   ===================
   NOVEMBER 1978  poster campaign in Beijing; DEMOCRACY WALL, approved by
     Deng because most posters were criticizing the Gang of Four
   1979  the posters become progressively more daring, attacking Chairman
     Mao and demanding:
       the right to criticize the government openly
       representation for non-communist parties in the National People's
         Congress
       freedom to change jobs and to travel abroad
       abolition of the communes
   Deng, infuriated, attacks the leading dissidents for trying to destroy
     the socialist system. Several ARRESTED with sentences of UP TO 15
     YEARS. NOVEMBER 1979: the Democracy Wall is ABOLISHED altogether.
   Deng: "Without the party, China will retrogress into divisions and
     confusions."

   THE COSTS THAT APPEAR
   =====================
   1984  exports up 10 per cent, imports up 38 per cent; record trade
         deficit of $1100 million; sharp fall in foreign exchange reserves
         -> heavy duties on imports except vital raw materials and
            microchip equipment (80 per cent on cars, 70 per cent on
            colour televisions and video players)
   1986  annual inflation reaches 22 PER CENT
```

⚠️ The structure of this visual is the argument of the lesson. Two of the three boxes are about change and one is about a limit; the limit was enforced in November 1979 and again in 1987 and 1989, and it is what the phrase "reform without political reform" actually names.

### 1. The succession

✅ Insufficient exports resulted in the biggest trade gap since the mid-1950s, and inflation cancelled out wage increases. ✅ Hua Guofeng was blamed for the failures and Deng seized the chance to get rid of him. ✅ In 1980 the Politburo decided that Hua "lacks the political and organisational ability to be the Chairman of the Party". ✅ Hua was forced to resign, leaving the 73-year-old Deng as undisputed leader in June 1981.

⚠️ Note the method. Deng did not remove a rival by purge or by mass campaign; a Politburo decision recorded a judgement about administrative competence. ⚠️ That is the collective-leadership principle of Lesson 14 already operating, and it is the first evidence that something in the political system had in fact changed even though the monopoly of the party had not.

✅ Robert Service's characterisation should be quoted in an answer on Deng, because it explains the urgency: "Deng was as hard as teak. He endured as many demotions as promotions at Mao's hand since the 1950s. His son was crippled from the waist down after leaping from a window to escape physical maltreatment in the Cultural Revolution. Deng was forthright about the need for change ... and he knew he had no time to waste if he wanted to make the changes he wanted."

⚠️ The biographical detail is not decoration. ⚠️ The man who dismantled Maoist economics had personally suffered under Maoist politics, and he was seventy-three when he obtained the authority to act.

### 2. What changed

✅ This new phase began in June 1978 as Deng gained the ascendancy. ✅ Deng somehow succeeded in persuading the Politburo that changes were vital, after all the upheavals and crises caused by the Great Leap Forward and then the Cultural Revolution.

✅ Many changes introduced during the Cultural Revolution were reversed: the revolutionary committees set up to run local government were abolished and replaced by more democratically elected groups; property confiscated from former capitalists was returned to survivors; and there was more religious freedom and greater freedom for intellectuals to express themselves in literature and the arts.

✅ In economic matters Deng and his protégé Hu Yaobang wanted technical and financial help from the West in order to modernise industry, agriculture, science and technology. ✅ Loans were accepted from foreign governments and banks, and contracts were signed with foreign companies for the supply of modern equipment. ✅ In 1980 China joined the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank. ✅ On the home front, permission was given for the setting up of private industrial companies. ✅ State farms were given more control over planning, financing and profits; bonuses, piece-rates and profit-sharing schemes were encouraged; the state paid higher prices to the communes for their produce and reduced taxes in order to stimulate efficiency and output. ✅ These measures had some success: grain output reached a record level in 1979, and many peasants became prosperous.

⚠️ Look at the list of incentives - bonuses, piece-rates, profit-sharing, larger household control - and compare it with the rightist programme of 1963-66 recorded in Lesson 14: incentives, piecework, greater wage differentials, larger private plots, and an expert managerial class. ⚠️ They are the same list. ⚠️ The reform era was the implementation of the case that had lost the argument that caused the Cultural Revolution, by one of the men who had lost it.

✅ Zhao Ziyang had won a reputation as a brilliant administrator in Sichuan province, where he was responsible for an 80 per cent increase in industrial production in 1979. ✅ He also began experiments, later extended to the whole country, to break up the communes so as to give peasants control of individual plots. ✅ The land, although still officially owned by the state, was divided up and allocated to individual peasant households, which would be allowed to keep most of the profits. ✅ This was successful in raising agricultural production, and the standard of living for many people improved. ✅ In December 1984 Zhao announced that compulsory state purchase of crops was to be abandoned: the state would continue to buy staple products, but in much smaller quantities than before, and prices of surplus grain, pork, cotton and vegetables would be allowed to fluctuate on the open market.

⚠️ This is decollectivisation, and it completes a striking pattern that should be stated explicitly in any answer on rural China: ⚠️ within roughly thirty-five years, rural land arrangements were restructured three times - redistribution to households in the early 1950s, collectivisation and then communes by 1958, and allocation back to households from the early 1980s with state ownership retained. ⚠️ Peasant households ended the period holding use rights over plots they had held as owners in 1953, under a state that had taken them away in between.

### 3. What did not change, and the moment it was enforced

✅ In November 1978 there was a poster campaign in Beijing and other cities, often in support of Deng Xiaoping. ✅ Soon there were massive demonstrations demanding more drastic changes, and the government felt obliged to ban marches and poster campaigns. ✅ However, there still remained what was called the "Democracy Wall" in Beijing, where the public could express itself with huge wall posters. ✅ During 1979 the posters displayed there became progressively more daring, attacking Chairman Mao and demanding a wide range of human rights: the right to criticise the government openly; representation for non-communist parties in the National People's Congress; freedom to change jobs and to travel abroad; and abolition of the communes.

✅ This infuriated Deng, who had approved the Democracy Wall in the first place only because most of the posters were criticising the Gang of Four. ✅ Now he launched a fierce attack on the leading dissidents, accusing them of trying to destroy the socialist system. ✅ Several were arrested and given prison sentences of up to fifteen years. ✅ In November 1979 the Democracy Wall was abolished altogether. ✅ Law and order and party discipline were restored. ✅ "Without the party", Deng remarked, "China will retrogress into divisions and confusions."

⚠️ This is the four-step mechanism of Lesson 12 running for the second time, and the parallel should be drawn explicitly in an answer. ⚠️ Note one detail that makes it sharper than the 1957 case: one of the four demands on the wall - abolition of the communes - was a demand Deng's own government proceeded to implement. ⚠️ The objection was therefore not to the content of the demand but to the *channel* through which it was made. ⚠️ That is the precise meaning of reform without political reform: policies could change without limit; the monopoly on deciding them could not be contested.

### 4. The costs, and Deng's own statement

✅ By this time modernisation, and what Deng called the move to "market socialism", were having some unfortunate side effects. ✅ Although exports increased by 10 per cent during 1984, imports increased by 38 per cent, leaving a record trade deficit of $1100 million and causing a sharp fall in China's foreign exchange reserves. ✅ The government tried with some success to control imports by placing heavy duties on all imported goods except vital raw materials and microchip equipment - 80 per cent on cars and 70 per cent on colour televisions and video players. ✅ Another unwelcome development was that the annual rate of inflation began to rise, reaching 22 per cent in 1986.

✅ Apparently not unduly worried by these trends, the 82-year-old Deng explained his ideas for the future in a magazine article of November 1986. ✅ His main aim was to enable his people to get richer: by the year 2000, if all went well, the average annual income per head should have risen from the equivalent of £280 to somewhere near £700, and China's production should have doubled. ✅ "To get rich is not a crime", he added. ✅ He was happy with the way agricultural reform was going, but emphasised that in industry, sweeping decentralisation was still needed: the party must withdraw from administrative tasks, issue fewer instructions and allow more initiative at the lower levels. ✅ Only capitalist investment could create the conditions in which China could become a prosperous, modernised state. ✅ His other main theme was China's international role: to lead a peace alliance of the rest of the world against the dangerous ambitions of the USA and the USSR. ✅ Nothing, he said, could possibly alter the course he had set for his country.

⚠️ The projection figures are recorded as Deng's stated aspiration in a 1986 article and must be reported that way. ❌ They are not an outcome and this session does not state whether they were met.

### 5. The current linkage, used once

📰 The Government of China's own release of **24 October 2025** records that the fourth plenary session of the 20th Communist Party Central Committee deliberated and adopted a document of recommendations for formulating the **15th Five-Year Plan**, setting out development priorities for the **2026-2030** period, with the stated emphases including a modernised industrial system, greater self-reliance in science and technology and the development of what it terms new quality productive forces.

⚠️ This is the only current item in this session and it is limited here in three ways. ⚠️ First, it is an official institutional statement by the government concerned, which makes it reliable evidence of *stated doctrine and procedure* and not independent evidence that any target has been achieved. ⚠️ Second, its historical use is narrow: it shows that the institutional form established in this lesson - a party plenum setting the recommendations that a five-year plan then implements - remains the operative mechanism, which is the continuity a history answer can legitimately note in one sentence. ⚠️ Third, ❌ no current Chinese growth rate, output figure, leadership arrangement or diplomatic position is asserted anywhere in this session.

### 6. The boundary

⚠️ This topic owns China's revolutionary and reform trajectory. ⚠️ The end of the Cold War in Europe, the revolutions of 1989 in Eastern Europe, German reunification, the dissolution of the Soviet Union and the post-1991 order belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner. ⚠️ Lesson 18 carries China to June 1989 and to the comparison with Gorbachev's sequencing, and stops there. ⚠️ India's relations with the People's Republic - Panchsheel, the 1962 border war and subsequent strategic caution - belong to the Indian foreign-policy and international-relations owners and are cross-linked rather than narrated here.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The standard demand is on Deng's paradox: why economic reform did not become political liberalisation. ⚠️ The core account's own questions add two sharper framings - whether Deng could be considered a liberal in either his economic or his political outlook, and why his policies led to a crisis in 1987-9.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Give the succession briefly, noting the collective-leadership method. ⚠️ Set out the reversals and the economic opening, and make the point that this was the 1963-66 rightist programme implemented. ⚠️ Give decollectivisation and the three restructurings of rural land in thirty-five years. ⚠️ Give the Democracy Wall with the commune-abolition detail, which proves the objection was to the channel and not the content. ⚠️ Give the costs. ⚠️ Conclude that reform was a deliberate separation of economic from political liberalisation.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Saying Deng democratised China. ✅ He reformed the economy; the political system remained one-party.
2. ⚠️ Dating Deng's leadership to 1976 or 1978. ✅ The policy phase began in June 1978; ✅ he became undisputed leader in June 1981 after Hua's forced resignation.
3. ⚠️ Treating the Four Modernizations as Deng's coinage alone. ✅ They are recorded as agriculture, industry, science and defence, and students demonstrated in support of them in December 1986.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the Democracy Wall. ✅ It is the clearest single demonstration of the limit, and it was abolished in November 1979.
5. ⚠️ Reporting Deng's 1986 projections as outcomes. ✅ They are stated aims in a magazine article.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Hua Guofeng blamed for the trade gap and inflation; Politburo decided in 1980 that he lacked the political and organisational ability to be Chairman; Deng undisputed leader in June 1981 at seventy-three.
2. ✅ Robert Service on Deng: as hard as teak; as many demotions as promotions; his son crippled escaping maltreatment in the Cultural Revolution; no time to waste.
3. ✅ From June 1978: revolutionary committees abolished and replaced by more democratically elected groups; confiscated property returned to surviving former capitalists; more religious freedom; greater freedom for intellectuals in literature and the arts.
4. ✅ Economic opening: Western technical and financial help; foreign loans and contracts; IMF and World Bank membership in 1980; private industrial companies permitted; state farms given control over planning, financing and profits; bonuses, piece-rates and profit-sharing; higher state prices to communes and reduced taxes; record grain output in 1979.
5. ✅ Zhao Ziyang: 80 per cent rise in Sichuan industrial production in 1979; experiments to break up the communes, extended nationwide; land still owned by the state but allocated to individual households keeping most of the profits; December 1984 abandonment of compulsory state purchase, with surplus grain, pork, cotton and vegetable prices free to fluctuate.
6. ✅ Democracy Wall: poster campaign from November 1978; four demands - open criticism of government, representation for non-communist parties, freedom to change jobs and travel abroad, abolition of the communes; dissidents arrested with sentences up to fifteen years; the Wall abolished in November 1979; Deng: "Without the party, China will retrogress into divisions and confusions."
7. ✅ Costs: 1984 exports up 10 per cent, imports up 38 per cent, record trade deficit of $1100 million; import duties of 80 per cent on cars and 70 per cent on colour televisions and video players; inflation at 22 per cent in 1986.
8. ✅ Deng, November 1986: aim to enable people to get richer, income per head from about £280 to near £700 by 2000 and production doubled; "To get rich is not a crime"; sweeping industrial decentralisation still needed; only capitalist investment could create a prosperous modernised state; internationally, to lead a peace alliance against the ambitions of the USA and the USSR.
9. ⚠️ Rural land restructured three times in about thirty-five years: household redistribution, collectivisation and communes, household allocation with state ownership retained.
10. ⚠️ The Democracy Wall proves the objection was to the channel rather than the content, since one of its four demands was implemented by Deng's own government.
11. 📰 Official release of 24 October 2025: the 20th Central Committee's fourth plenary session adopted recommendations for the 15th Five-Year Plan covering 2026-2030 - the single current item in this session, used only to show institutional continuity.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Democracy Wall demanded the abolition of the communes, and Deng's government proceeded to break up the communes. What does that combination establish about the nature of the limit he enforced?

**Model answer:** It establishes that the limit was procedural rather than substantive. The posters at the Democracy Wall demanded the right to criticise the government openly, representation for non-communist parties in the National People's Congress, freedom to change jobs and travel abroad, and abolition of the communes; Deng attacked the leading dissidents for trying to destroy the socialist system, several were imprisoned for up to fifteen years, and the Wall was abolished in November 1979. Yet Zhao Ziyang's experiments to break up the communes and allocate land to individual households were extended nationwide and became the centrepiece of agricultural reform. So the government adopted one of the demands while punishing those who made it. What was being defended was therefore not the policy but the monopoly on deciding policy - as Deng's own remark makes explicit, that without the party China would retrogress into divisions and confusions. That is the exact content of reform without political reform: the range of permissible economic change was very wide, and the range of permissible political participation was not widened at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Describing Deng as a cautious or gradual reformer who simply had not yet reached political reform. The evidence shows a deliberate and enforced separation, applied within a year of the reform phase beginning and repeated in 1987 and 1989. Treating political reform as merely deferred also makes the crackdown of June 1989 look like an aberration, when the record presents it as the same limit enforced a third time.

## Lesson 18 - 1987, Tiananmen 1989 and the two sequencings: the moment China and the Soviet Union parted company

Progress: 18/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Deng's balancing act between reformers and hardliners; the student demonstrations of December 1986 and the fall of Hu Yaobang; the dilemma stated in January 1987; the inflation of 1988-9; the protests of April to June 1989 and Gorbachev's visit; martial law and the crackdown of 3-4 June; the divergent casualty records and how to write about them; the leadership consequences; and the comparison with Soviet sequencing that the leadership itself drew

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on Tiananmen Square and the crisis of communism, including the crisis of 1987 with Deng's balancing act, the December 1986 demonstrations, the forced resignation of Hu Yaobang and the promotion of Zhao Ziyang and Li Peng, Zhao's statement of continued economic reform, the dilemma about choice in buying and selling against choice in politics, and the 1989 sequence. The portion of the extraction covering the square itself in April to June 1989 is heavily damaged; the legible content and the separate account in the Cold War chapter are used, and the damage is disclosed here and recorded in the final ledger
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two reform sequences, and the conclusion each leadership drew

```text
   THE SAME PROBLEM, TWO SOLUTIONS
   ===============================

   SOVIET SEQUENCE (as the Chinese leadership read it)
     economic reform AND political reform ATTEMPTED AT THE SAME TIME
       -> the Communist Party lost control
       -> the economic reforms were a disaster
       -> the USSR broke up into 15 separate states

   CHINESE SEQUENCE
     economic reform FIRST and ALONE; political monopoly retained and
     enforced whenever contested
       1979  Democracy Wall abolished
       1987  student demonstrations dispersed; Hu Yaobang forced out
       1989  martial law; the army sent into the square
       -> "the Chinese leadership could congratulate itself on avoiding
           Gorbachev's mistakes and preserving communism in China at a
           time when it was being swept away in eastern Europe"

   THE DILEMMA, AS STATED AT THE TIME (January 1987)
   =================================================
     "was it possible to offer people a choice in buying and selling and
      yet deny them any choice in other areas such as policies and
      political parties?"
     Many western observers thought it was impossible to have one
     without the other - AND SO DID GORBACHEV.
     On the other hand, if the economic reforms proved successful, Deng
     and Zhao could turn out to be right.

   THE 1989 SEQUENCE
   =================
     APRIL 1989   student protests begin in Tiananmen Square, continuing
                  into June; encouraged by Gorbachev's glasnost policy;
                  demanding DEMOCRACY and AN END TO CORRUPTION in the
                  Communist Party
     4 MAY        Zhao Ziyang treats the students' demands as just and
                  allows the press to report them - which outrages Deng
     MID-MAY      GORBACHEV'S VISIT, marking the formal Sino-Soviet
                  reconciliation (Lesson 16). The demonstrations continue
                  throughout it, with the world's press in the city
     20 MAY       DENG DECLARES MARTIAL LAW; Li Peng sent to negotiate
     3-4 JUNE     negotiations having failed, the army moves in and
                  attacks
     AFTER        protest leaders sentenced to long terms in labour camps;
                  Zhao Ziyang removed as party chief, replaced by JIANG
                  ZEMIN, a more "middle of the road" politician;
                  the hardliners triumphant and LI PENG the leading figure;
                  worldwide condemnation
     ECONOMICS    reform continued; political liberalisation did not
```

⚠️ The value of this visual is that it states the Chinese leadership's own justification in its own terms. ⚠️ An answer that can reproduce that justification before assessing it is doing something more demanding, and more useful, than an answer that only condemns.

### 1. The balancing act, and why 1987 came first

✅ In spite of his radical words, Deng always had to keep an eye on the traditional, conservative or Maoist members of the Politburo, who were still powerful and might be able to get rid of him if his economic reforms failed or if party control seemed to be slipping. ✅ Deng was doing a clever balancing act between the reformers like Zhao Ziyang and Hu Yaobang on the one hand, and the hardliners like Li Peng on the other. ✅ Deng's tactics were to encourage criticism from students and intellectuals, but only up to a point: enough to enable him to drop some of the oldest and most inefficient party bureaucrats. ✅ If the criticism looked like getting out of hand, it had to be stopped, as had happened in 1979, for fear of antagonising the hardliners.

⚠️ This is the four-step mechanism of Lesson 12 described from the inside, with the motive made explicit. ⚠️ Criticism was a *tool of factional management*: it let a reforming leader remove entrenched bureaucrats whom he could not remove by other means. ⚠️ That is why the tolerance always ended at the same place - the moment the criticism threatened party control, it became a liability with the hardliners whose support Deng needed.

✅ In December 1986 there was a series of student demonstrations supporting Deng Xiaoping and the "Four Modernizations" - agriculture, industry, science and defence - but urging a much quicker pace and, ominously, more democracy. ✅ After the students ignored a new ban on wall posters and a new rule requiring five days' notice for demonstrations, Deng decided that this challenge to party control and discipline had gone far enough, and the demonstrators were dispersed.

✅ However, it had been enough to alarm the hardliners, who forced the resignation of the reformer Hu Yaobang as party general secretary. ✅ He was accused of being too liberal in his political outlook, encouraging intellectuals to demand greater democracy and even some sort of opposition party. ✅ Although this was a serious blow to Deng, it was not a complete disaster since his place was taken by Zhao Ziyang, another economic reformer, but one who had so far kept clear of controversial political ideas; ✅ however, Li Peng, a hardline supporter of order and authority, took Zhao's place as prime minister, and he demanded a clampdown on all further protests.

⚠️ Note what the December 1986 demonstrations were *for*: they supported Deng and the Four Modernizations. ⚠️ A reforming leader was weakened by demonstrations in his own favour, because their additional demand crossed the line. ⚠️ Note too the personnel consequence, which matters in 1989: Hu Yaobang was removed, Zhao moved up, and the hardliner Li Peng moved into the premiership. ⚠️ The configuration of the 1989 crisis - Zhao on one side, Li Peng on the other, Deng deciding - was set in place two years earlier.

### 2. The dilemma, stated in 1987

✅ Zhao soon announced that the government had no intention of abandoning its economic reform programme, and promised new measures to speed up financial reform, and at the same time a clampdown on "bourgeois intellectuals" who threatened party control. ✅ This highlighted the dilemma facing Deng and Zhao: was it possible to offer people a choice in buying and selling and yet deny them any choice in other areas such as policies and political parties? ✅ Many western observers thought it was impossible to have one without the other - and so did Gorbachev in the USSR - and by the end of January 1987 there were signs that they could be right. ✅ On the other hand, if the economic reforms proved successful, Deng and Zhao could turn out to be right.

⚠️ This is the intellectual centre of the whole reform era and it is worth writing out as a question rather than a conclusion, because that is how it stood at the time. ⚠️ The proposition that economic and political liberalisation are inseparable was the standard Western expectation and was also Gorbachev's working assumption. ⚠️ The Chinese leadership made the opposite bet. ⚠️ Lesson 22 returns to how a candidate should frame the outcome without asserting anything about the present.

### 3. 1989

✅ Unfortunately for Deng and Zhao, the economic reforms ran into problems during 1988 and 1989. ✅ Inflation went up to 30 per cent, and wages, especially of state employees, were affected.

⚠️ The extraction of the pages describing the square itself is heavily damaged, and this session uses only what is legible there together with the separate and clean account in the Cold War chapter. ⚠️ The following is what the evidence supports.

✅ Encouraged by Gorbachev's glasnost policy in the USSR, student protests began in Tiananmen Square in Beijing in April 1989, continuing through into June. ✅ They demanded democracy and an end to corruption in the Communist Party. ✅ On 4 May Zhao Ziyang treated the students' demands as just and allowed the press to report them, which outraged Deng. ✅ The demonstrations continued throughout Gorbachev's visit in mid-May, which marked the formal reconciliation between China and the USSR, and on into June, with the square and surrounding streets occupied. ⚠️ The visit's exact dates are not legible in the damaged page and are not asserted here. ✅ Behind the scenes a power struggle was under way between Zhao Ziyang and the hardline prime minister Li Peng; ✅ Li Peng, with the support of Deng Xiaoping, eventually won. ✅ On 20 May Deng declared martial law and sent Li Peng to negotiate with the protesters. ✅ When negotiations failed, thousands of troops were brought in, and on 3-4 June the army, using paratroopers, tanks and infantry, attacked. ✅ Tiananmen Square was under government control again, and demonstrations in other large cities were also suppressed, though with less bloodshed. ✅ The protest leaders were sentenced to long terms in labour camps. ✅ Zhao Ziyang was removed from his position as party chief and replaced by Jiang Zemin, a more "middle of the road" politician. ✅ The hardliners were triumphant and Li Peng became the leading figure.

⚠️ The coincidence with Gorbachev's visit deserves a full sentence in an answer, because it is causally relevant rather than merely ironic. ⚠️ The visit that closed the Sino-Soviet split brought the international press corps to Beijing in mid-May, which meant that the occupation of the square was reported worldwide as it happened; and the visitor was the author of the reform programme whose example the students invoked. ⚠️ Two of this topic's major threads - the split of Lesson 16 and the reform limit of Lesson 17 - intersect in a single week.

### 4. The casualty record, and the discipline it requires

⚠️ This is the fourth and final place in this session where mass death is recorded, and the evidence held here does not agree with itself. ⚠️ The correct procedure is to report the divergence rather than to select from it.

| ✅ Record | ✅ What it states | ⚠️ Status |
|---|---|---|
| The chapter summary of the core account | On 3-4 June the army moved in, attacked the students, "killing hundreds", and restored order | Legible and clean |
| The Cold War chapter of the same account | "At least a thousand students were killed and many later executed", bringing worldwide condemnation | Legible and clean |
| The main narrative of the China chapter | Read from a heavily damaged page as a figure of between 1,500 and 3,000 | ⚠️ Read from a damaged extraction; reported here with that limitation stated, and not used as a primary figure |
| The canonical owners | The protest movement was crushed by force; no figure is given | ⚠️ Deliberately unquantified |

⚠️ **The writing rule.** ⚠️ Write "estimates of those killed on 3-4 June 1989 diverge sharply even within a single account, which records the army killing hundreds in one place and at least a thousand students killed with many later executed in another". ❌ Do not select the highest figure because it is the most striking, ❌ do not select the lowest because it is the most cautious, and ❌ do not present any figure as established. ⚠️ Where the underlying page is damaged, say so rather than quoting confidently from an unreliable reading.

⚠️ This is not pedantry. A question on Tiananmen is, among other things, a test of whether a candidate can write about a contested and politically charged event with evidentiary discipline, and the divergence is itself a finding worth one sentence.

### 5. The leadership's own justification

✅ There was worldwide condemnation of the massacres, but Deng and the hardliners were convinced that they had taken the right decision. ✅ They felt that to have given way to the students' demands for democracy would have caused too much disruption, and that one-party control was needed to supervise the transition to a "socialist market economy". ✅ Further, events in the USSR seemed to prove them right: when Mikhail Gorbachev tried to introduce economic and political reforms both at the same time he failed; the Communist Party lost control, the economic reforms were a disaster, and the USSR broke up into fifteen separate states. ✅ Whatever the rest of the world thought about the Tiananmen Square massacres, the Chinese leadership could congratulate itself on avoiding Gorbachev's mistakes and preserving communism in China at a time when it was being swept away in eastern Europe.

⚠️ Three things must be said about this passage in an answer, and in this order.

1. ⚠️ **It is the leadership's reasoning, reported.** It explains the decision; it does not justify it, and an answer should mark the difference explicitly.
2. ⚠️ **The comparative claim is about sequencing, not about content.** The proposition is not that political reform is undesirable but that attempting it simultaneously with economic reform destroys the capacity to conduct either. ⚠️ That is an empirical claim with exactly one large case on each side, which is far too small a sample to settle anything - and saying so is the correct analytical move.
3. ⚠️ **The detailed Soviet and Eastern European story belongs to another owner.** ⚠️ This session states the comparison the Chinese leadership drew and hands the substantive account of Gorbachev's reforms, the revolutions of 1989 and the dissolution of the Soviet Union to the end-of-Cold-War owner.

⚠️ There is a further observation available and it is the sharpest thing in the lesson. ⚠️ The two governments reached opposite conclusions from the *same* diagnosis. Both leaderships agreed that a command economy was failing and had to be reformed. Gorbachev concluded that reform required political opening to break bureaucratic resistance; Deng concluded that reform required political closure to contain the disruption it caused. ⚠️ Each leadership then cited the other's outcome as proof of its own correctness.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems ask why economic reform in China did not produce political liberalisation, whether Deng can be considered a liberal, or why his policies produced a crisis in 1987-9 and how successfully he dealt with it.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Give the balancing act and the factional use of criticism. ⚠️ Give December 1986 with the detail that the demonstrations supported Deng, and the personnel consequences. ⚠️ State the 1987 dilemma as a question. ⚠️ Give the 1989 sequence with the Gorbachev-visit intersection. ⚠️ Handle the casualty record with the divergence stated. ⚠️ Report the leadership's justification as its reasoning, and add the observation about opposite conclusions from the same diagnosis.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Saying Tiananmen ended economic reform. ✅ Repression and reform proceeded together.
2. ❌ Asserting a single death toll. ✅ The records diverge within a single account.
3. ⚠️ Treating 1989 as the first confrontation. ✅ It is the third, after 1979 and 1986-7.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting Hu Yaobang. ✅ His forced resignation in 1987 set the 1989 configuration in place.
5. ⚠️ Narrating the Soviet collapse. ⚠️ That belongs to the end-of-Cold-War owner; only the comparison drawn by the Chinese leadership is used here.
6. ⚠️ Missing the Gorbachev visit. ✅ The demonstrations continued throughout it in mid-May, and it marked the formal Sino-Soviet reconciliation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Deng balanced reformers Zhao Ziyang and Hu Yaobang against hardliners such as Li Peng; he encouraged criticism only up to a point, to drop inefficient bureaucrats, and stopped it when it threatened party control, as in 1979.
2. ✅ December 1986: student demonstrations supporting Deng and the Four Modernizations - agriculture, industry, science and defence - but urging a quicker pace and more democracy; bans on posters and a five-days' notice rule ignored; demonstrators dispersed.
3. ✅ Hardliners forced Hu Yaobang's resignation as general secretary for being too liberal; Zhao Ziyang replaced him; Li Peng became prime minister and demanded a clampdown.
4. ✅ The dilemma of January 1987: could people be given a choice in buying and selling while denied any choice in policies and political parties? Western observers and Gorbachev thought not; Deng and Zhao bet otherwise.
5. ✅ 1988-9: reforms ran into problems; inflation reached 30 per cent, affecting state employees' wages especially.
6. ✅ April 1989: protests began in Tiananmen Square, encouraged by glasnost, demanding democracy and an end to party corruption; on 4 May Zhao treated the demands as just and allowed press reporting, outraging Deng.
7. ✅ Mid-May: Gorbachev's visit to Beijing marking the formal Sino-Soviet reconciliation; demonstrations continued throughout and into June. ⚠️ The visit's exact dates are not legible in the damaged page.
8. ✅ 20 May: Deng declared martial law and sent Li Peng to negotiate; negotiations failed; on 3-4 June the army attacked with paratroopers, tanks and infantry.
9. ✅ Casualty record diverges: "killing hundreds" in one place; "at least a thousand students were killed and many later executed" in another; the canonical owners give no figure.
10. ✅ Aftermath: control restored; other cities suppressed with less bloodshed; protest leaders sentenced to long terms in labour camps; Zhao removed and replaced by Jiang Zemin; hardliners triumphant with Li Peng the leading figure; worldwide condemnation.
11. ✅ The leadership's justification: giving way would have caused too much disruption; one-party control was needed to supervise the transition to a "socialist market economy"; Gorbachev's simultaneous reforms failed and the USSR broke into fifteen states; the leadership congratulated itself on avoiding his mistakes.
12. ⚠️ The sharpest observation: the same diagnosis produced opposite prescriptions, and each leadership cited the other's outcome as proof of its own.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the 1987 dilemma in its original form and explain why an answer should present it as an open question rather than as a settled matter.

**Model answer:** The dilemma as stated at the time was whether it was possible to offer people a choice in buying and selling and yet deny them any choice in other areas such as policies and political parties. Many western observers thought it impossible to have one without the other, and Gorbachev agreed with them; Deng and Zhao bet the other way, and by the end of January 1987 there were signs the sceptics might be right, though the record also notes that if the economic reforms proved successful Deng and Zhao could turn out to be right. It should be presented as an open question for two reasons. First, the evidentiary base is tiny: one large state attempted simultaneous reform and disintegrated, and one attempted sequenced reform and retained party control, and two cases cannot settle a general proposition about the relation between markets and pluralism. Second, a history answer that pronounces on the outcome must make claims about the present political trajectory of a state, which this topic does not own and which no historical source can supply. Framing it as the bet that two leaderships made in opposite directions, and reporting the justification each gave, is both more accurate and more examinable than announcing a winner.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the crackdown as the end of the reform programme. Economic reform continued, the leadership explicitly justified the repression as necessary to supervise the transition to a socialist market economy, and the hardliners' triumph changed the personnel rather than the economic direction. An answer that presents June 1989 as a reversal of reform misstates the relationship that the whole period exists to demonstrate: political closure was treated as the condition of economic opening, not as its abandonment.
