## Lesson 19 - Korea: a communist state made by division, not by revolution

Progress: 19/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Japanese rule from 1905 and the Cairo promise of 1943; the Soviet entry of 8 August 1945 and the American proposal of the 38th parallel; Kim Il-sung and Syngman Rhee; the proclamation of 1948 and the recognition of 1949; the boundary at the Korean War; the effect of the Sino-Soviet split on a small client; the economy and the succession; the famine years and the reported indicators; and the nuclear confrontation to 2012

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's chapter on communism in Korea and south-east Asia, section by section: the establishment of the communist regime, the question of one state or two, the recorded record of the North's policy towards unification, the economic difficulties and their causes including the Sino-Soviet rift, life under Kim Jong-il with the reported indicators of the 1990s and after, and the nuclear confrontation with the United States through to 2012. The pages covering the Korean War itself are missing from the extraction, which is consistent with the boundary observed here since that war belongs to the Cold War owner
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 17 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a state created from outside, in eight steps

```text
   1905   Korea under JAPANESE occupation and rule, following the
          Japanese victory in the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-5.
          A strong Korean NATIONALIST movement exists.
                    |
   1943   CAIRO conference: the USA, the UK and CHINA promise that when
          the war is over, a UNITED, INDEPENDENT KOREA will be created.
                    |
   8 AUG 1945  The USSR declares war on Japan - THREE WEEKS BEFORE the
          Japanese surrender. Russian troops in Manchuria are CLOSEST to
          Korea and move into the north even before Japan officially
          surrenders on 2 September.
                    |
   1945   Soviet forces work closely with Korean communists and
          nationalists; the Japanese occupying armies are quickly
          disarmed; the Korean People's Republic is proclaimed;
          KIM IL-SUNG, trained in the USSR, emerges as the dominant
          political figure and begins to introduce his own version of
          Marxism-Leninism, supported by Soviet troops.
                    |
   1945   The AMERICANS, worried that the entire peninsula was about to
          be taken over by the Russians, hastily send troops to occupy
          the south. IT WAS THE AMERICANS WHO PROPOSED the division
          along the 38TH PARALLEL.
          In the south, DR SYNGMAN RHEE emerges - strongly nationalist,
          anti-communist, determined to bring about a united Korea free
          of communism.
                    |
   1948   Stalin pours massive Russian aid into the north, transforming
          it into a powerful military state well able to defend itself.
          Stalin WITHDRAWS Soviet troops; the DEMOCRATIC PEOPLE'S
          REPUBLIC OF KOREA is proclaimed with Kim Il-sung as premier.
          -> NORTH KOREA THEREFORE HAD AN INDEPENDENT COMMUNIST
             GOVERNMENT **BEFORE** THE COMMUNIST VICTORY IN CHINA.
                    |
   1949   After Mao becomes Chinese leader, independent North Korea is
          given official diplomatic recognition by China, the USSR and
          the communist states of eastern Europe.
                    |
   THE DEADLOCK  The Americans wanted a united, anti-communist and
          pro-Western Korea; the Russians, and after 1949 the Chinese,
          wanted a unified COMMUNIST Korea. Neither the USA nor the USSR
          wanted to become closely involved, and given the entrenched
          positions of Kim and Rhee the dilemma seemed INSOLUBLE.
          The problem was handed to the UNITED NATIONS, which undertook
          to organize elections for the whole country as a first step
          towards unifying the peninsula.
```

⚠️ The boxed line in the middle of that diagram is the reason this lesson exists. ⚠️ A communist state existed on the Korean peninsula before there was one in China, and it was produced by the arrival of a foreign army rather than by a revolution. ⚠️ Every generalisation about Asian communism as a Chinese export dies on that fact.

### 1. What Korea proves

⚠️ Three propositions, and each refutes something a weak answer would assert.

1. ⚠️ **Communism in Asia was not necessarily revolutionary in origin.** ✅ Soviet forces entered the north, disarmed the Japanese, worked with local communists and nationalists, and a Soviet-trained leader emerged with Soviet troops supporting him. ⚠️ There was no civil war, no peasant base built over twenty years, no Long March.
2. ⚠️ **It was not Chinese in origin or in timing.** ✅ The Democratic People's Republic of Korea was proclaimed in 1948, before the communist victory in China, and was recognised by China only afterwards, in 1949.
3. ⚠️ **The division was proposed by the United States.** ✅ It was the Americans who proposed the division along the 38th parallel, out of fear that the whole peninsula was about to be taken over. ⚠️ An answer that treats the partition as a communist imposition has the agency backwards.

⚠️ There is also a sharp irony to note: ✅ the Cairo conference of 1943 had promised a united, independent Korea, and the promise was made by the USA, the UK and China. ⚠️ The division that followed was therefore a breach of an Allied commitment, produced by the speed of the Soviet entry into the war on 8 August 1945 and by the geography of where troops happened to be.

### 2. The boundary at the Korean War

⚠️ ✅ The problem of unification was handed to the United Nations, which undertook to organise elections for the whole country as a first step. ⚠️ What followed - the failure of that process, the war of 1950-3 and its conduct as the first armed test of collective security - belongs to the Cold War owner and, for the institutional dimension, to the United Nations owner. ⚠️ This session records only that ✅ after the war of 1950-3 the two states remained strictly separate, and that ✅ North Korea has remained one of the most secretive and little-known states in the world.

⚠️ The pages of the account that narrate the war itself are missing from the extraction available for this session, and this is recorded in the final ledger. ⚠️ Since the war is not owned here, the gap does not affect coverage of this topic's scope; it is disclosed for completeness.

### 3. A small client caught between two patrons

✅ During the second half of the 1960s North Korea's economy ran into difficulties for a number of reasons. ✅ The rift between the USSR and China, which gradually widened from 1956 onwards, placed Kim in a difficult position: which side should he support? ✅ At first he stayed pro-Soviet, then he switched his allegiance to China, and finally tried to be independent of both. ✅ When he moved away from Moscow at the end of the 1950s the USSR sharply reduced its aid; ✅ in 1966, at the beginning of Mao's Cultural Revolution, the Chinese cut off their aid. ✅ After that, none of Kim's development plans reached their targets.

⚠️ This is the most analytically useful passage in the lesson, because it shows the Sino-Soviet split of Lesson 16 operating on a third party. ⚠️ A small state in a divided bloc cannot be neutral cheaply: alignment with either patron costs the other's assistance, and attempted independence costs both. ⚠️ The sequence - Soviet aid sharply reduced at the end of the 1950s, Chinese aid cut off in 1966 - is the mechanism behind the failure of every subsequent development plan.

✅ Another serious weakness was the excessive expenditure on heavy industry and armaments, with consumer goods and luxuries considered of secondary importance. ✅ There was a rapid population increase, which put a strain on agriculture and the food industries generally. ✅ Living standards fell; life for most people was hard and conditions basic. ✅ During the 1980s the economy recovered, but in the early 1990s, as aid from Russia disappeared, there were more difficulties.

⚠️ Compare this with China's trajectory over the same decades and the contrast is instructive. ⚠️ Both states pursued heavy industry with Soviet help; both lost that help; China responded eventually by opening to foreign capital and decollectivising agriculture, while the North Korean response was continued military-industrial priority. ⚠️ The divergence discipline of this topic is visible in a single comparison.

### 4. Unification policy, and the dynastic succession

✅ The North's policy on unification was erratic: sometimes Kim suspended all discussions; ✅ in 1980 he proposed a federal state in which both North and South would have equal representation; ✅ in 1983 several leading South Koreans were killed in a bomb explosion; ✅ in 1987 a South Korean airliner was destroyed by a time bomb; ✅ then in 1991 high-level talks were held which led to the announcement of a joint renunciation of violence and nuclear weapons. ✅ However, it seemed as though no genuine progress could be made while Kim was still in charge.

✅ In 1980 Kim Il-sung, the "Great Leader", made it clear that he intended his son Kim Jong-il - soon to be known as the "Dear Leader" - who had been acting as Party Secretary, to be his successor. ✅ The younger Kim gradually took over more of the day-to-day work of government until his father died of a heart attack in 1994 at the age of 82.

⚠️ The hereditary succession is the feature that most sharply distinguishes this regime from the others in this topic, and it should be named as such. ⚠️ A communist party that transmits leadership from father to son has adopted a dynastic principle that no Marxist theory supplies. ⚠️ In the Chinese case the Party's 1981 Resolution moved authority from a single leader to a collective leadership; in the Korean case authority moved to the leader's son.

### 5. The crisis of the 1990s, reported as reported

✅ By 1994 North Korea was facing crisis: the economy had deteriorated further during the previous ten years, the population had increased threefold since 1954, and the country was on the verge of famine. ✅ Yet enormous amounts of cash had been spent developing nuclear weapons and long-range missiles. ✅ With the collapse of the USSR, North Korea had lost one of the few states which might be expected to show some sympathy with its plight.

✅ In April 2001 it was reported that, following the severe winter, there were serious food shortages, with most people surviving on 200 grams of rice a day; ✅ Germany immediately promised to send 30,000 tonnes of beef. ✅ In May the deputy foreign minister presented a report to a UNICEF conference on conditions in his country: between 1993 and 2000, mortality rates for children under five had risen from 27 to 48 per thousand; per capita Gross National Product had fallen from $991 per year to $457; the percentage of children being vaccinated against diseases such as polio and measles fell from 90 to 50 per cent; and the percentage of the population with access to safe water fell from 86 to 53. ✅ In 2001 North Korea received almost $300 million-worth of food aid from the European Union, the USA, Japan and even from South Korea.

⚠️ Two controls. ⚠️ First, these indicators are reported as the **deputy foreign minister's own report to a United Nations agency conference**, which makes them an official self-description and therefore reliable evidence of what the government itself acknowledged. ⚠️ Second, ❌ this session states no famine mortality figure for North Korea, because none is supplied by the evidence held here; ✅ the record is of a country on the verge of famine in 1994 and of the indicators above.

✅ In July 2002 a programme of limited economic reform was introduced: the currency was devalued and food prices were allowed to rise in the hope of encouraging agricultural production; food rationing was to be phased out and a family-unit farming system was introduced for the first time since collectivisation. ✅ At the end of 2003 reports indicated that living conditions were showing signs of improvement. ✅ However, by the summer of 2005 there were soldiers in the paddy fields to make sure that every grain of rice was handed over to the state procurement agency, and there was even a ban on private selling of produce from kitchen gardens. ✅ At the same time there was disturbing information about the existence of large numbers of labour camps in the north of the country containing thousands of political prisoners, where torture and execution were common - a situation reminiscent of Stalin's gulag system in the USSR.

⚠️ The 2002 reform and the 2005 reversal are worth pairing in an answer. ⚠️ A family-unit farming system introduced for the first time since collectivisation is the same instrument China adopted in the early 1980s; its reversal within three years is the difference between the two regimes stated in a single policy.

### 6. The nuclear confrontation, in outline

✅ Relations with the USA worsened when George W. Bush came to power in January 2001; after the 11 September atrocities he issued threats against what he called "the axis of evil", by which he meant Iraq, Iran and North Korea. ✅ The confrontation developed over whether North Korea possessed nuclear weapons; the Americans suspected it did, and the North Koreans claimed their reactor plants were for electricity. ✅ The dispute was still ongoing in 2012.

✅ The problem arose from the lack of progress with the KEDO project agreed in 1994, under which Kim Jong-il had agreed to shut down plutonium-producing reactor plants in return for two light-water reactors supplied by an international consortium involving the USA, South Korea and Japan. ✅ Work was not even started on the promised reactors; the Americans accused Kim of not completing the shutdown, while the North Koreans insisted work on the new reactors must start first. ✅ In August 2002 work actually began on the first light-water reactor; the Americans then demanded International Atomic Energy Agency inspection, which was refused; technology sanctions followed. ✅ North Korea then announced it would restart its plant at Yongbyon, and the USA declared KEDO null and void. ✅ In December 2002 North Korea announced its withdrawal from the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty signed in 1970, insisting it had no plans to make nuclear weapons and wanting a non-aggression pact, which the Americans refused. ✅ In January 2003 Bush offered to resume food and fuel aid if the weapons programme were dismantled; ✅ in April 2003 a North Korean foreign ministry spokesman claimed they already had nuclear weapons and would shortly have enough plutonium for eight more warheads; ✅ in August 2003 the Americans accepted a step-by-step approach. ✅ In February 2005 the government announced that it now had nuclear weapons, and in October 2006 it claimed to have successfully exploded a nuclear device underground.

✅ In 2009 relations with South Korea became strained after further nuclear tests, and more so in 2010 when a new uranium enriching plant was revealed. ✅ In March 2010 a South Korean corvette, the Cheonan, was sunk by a torpedo fired from a North Korean submarine with the loss of 46 lives; ✅ in November 2010 the South Korean island of Yeonpyeong was bombarded, with considerable damage and four people killed. ✅ The North Koreans claimed the south had fired first; ✅ the incident took place during the annual joint South Korean-American military and naval exercise off the west coast, which the North Korean government regards as preparation for an eventual invasion.

✅ In December 2011 Kim Jong-il died of a heart attack and his third son, Kim Jong-un, was named as the next Supreme Leader. ✅ His administration began disappointingly when, in April 2012, a rocket intended to place an observational satellite in orbit broke up shortly after lift-off.

⚠️ ❌ This session carries the record no further than the evidence held here, which ends in 2012, and asserts nothing about any later development. ⚠️ The contemporary Korean peninsula belongs to the international-relations owners.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Korea appears in comparative questions on Asian communism, in questions about whether communism was a single movement, and as a contrast case in questions about China.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Lead with the origin - external division rather than internal revolution - and the date that proves priority over China. ⚠️ Note that the Americans proposed the 38th parallel and that Cairo had promised unity. ⚠️ Use the Sino-Soviet split's effect on Kim as the best small-state illustration of bloc fragmentation. ⚠️ Name the dynastic succession as the distinguishing feature. ⚠️ Contrast the 2002 family-farming experiment and its 2005 reversal with the Chinese reform to make the divergence concrete.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Assuming North Korea followed the Chinese model. ✅ It preceded the Chinese regime and was Soviet-sponsored.
2. ⚠️ Attributing the partition to the communists. ✅ The Americans proposed the 38th parallel.
3. ⚠️ Narrating the Korean War. ⚠️ It belongs to the Cold War owner; this session stops at the referral to the United Nations.
4. ❌ Asserting a famine death toll. ✅ Only the recorded indicators and the statement that the country was on the verge of famine are supported.
5. ⚠️ Overlooking the 1966 Chinese aid cut. ✅ It followed Kim's realignment and, with the earlier Soviet reduction, wrecked his development plans.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Korea under Japanese occupation and rule since 1905 after the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-5; a strong nationalist movement existed.
2. ✅ Cairo 1943: the USA, UK and China promised a united, independent Korea after the war.
3. ✅ The USSR declared war on Japan on 8 August 1945, three weeks before the surrender; Russian troops from Manchuria entered the north before the official surrender on 2 September.
4. ✅ Soviet forces worked with Korean communists and nationalists; the Korean People's Republic was proclaimed; Kim Il-sung, trained in the USSR and backed by Soviet troops, emerged dominant.
5. ✅ The Americans, fearing a Soviet takeover of the whole peninsula, sent troops to the south and proposed the 38th parallel; Syngman Rhee emerged in the south as a strongly nationalist anti-communist.
6. ✅ Stalin poured massive aid into the north and withdrew Soviet troops in 1948; the Democratic People's Republic of Korea was proclaimed with Kim as premier - before the communist victory in China; recognition by China, the USSR and eastern Europe followed in 1949.
7. ✅ The deadlock over one state or two was handed to the United Nations, which undertook to organise elections; the war of 1950-3 and its aftermath belong to the Cold War owner.
8. ✅ The Sino-Soviet rift from 1956 placed Kim in difficulty: pro-Soviet, then pro-Chinese, then independent of both; Soviet aid sharply reduced at the end of the 1950s and Chinese aid cut off in 1966; no development plan then reached its targets.
9. ✅ Excessive expenditure on heavy industry and armaments; consumer goods secondary; rapid population growth straining food supply; falling living standards; recovery in the 1980s; renewed difficulty in the early 1990s as Russian aid disappeared.
10. ✅ Unification policy erratic: suspension of talks; a federal proposal in 1980; the bomb that killed leading South Koreans in 1983; the airliner destroyed in 1987; the joint renunciation of violence and nuclear weapons in 1991.
11. ✅ Hereditary succession announced in 1980; Kim Il-sung died in 1994 aged 82; Kim Jong-il died in December 2011; Kim Jong-un named Supreme Leader.
12. ✅ 1994 crisis: economy deteriorated, population trebled since 1954, country on the verge of famine, heavy spending on nuclear weapons and missiles, loss of the USSR.
13. ✅ Indicators reported by the deputy foreign minister to a UNICEF conference in May 2001, for 1993-2000: under-five mortality 27 to 48 per thousand; per capita GNP $991 to $457; vaccination 90 to 50 per cent; access to safe water 86 to 53 per cent.
14. ✅ July 2002 limited reform including family-unit farming for the first time since collectivisation; reversed by summer 2005 with soldiers enforcing procurement and a ban on private sale from kitchen gardens; labour camps with thousands of political prisoners, torture and execution common.
15. ✅ Nuclear confrontation: KEDO of 1994; "axis of evil" from January 2001; NPT withdrawal announced December 2002; claims of weapons in April 2003 and February 2005; claimed underground test October 2006; further tests 2009; uranium plant revealed 2010; Cheonan sunk March 2010 with 46 lives lost; Yeonpyeong shelled November 2010 with four killed; the record here ends in 2012.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the date of the Democratic People's Republic's proclamation is the most efficient single fact for refuting the claim that Asian communism was a Chinese export.

**Model answer:** Because it establishes priority and independent origin in one stroke. The Democratic People's Republic of Korea was proclaimed in 1948, with Kim Il-sung as premier, after Stalin had poured massive aid into the north and then withdrawn Soviet troops - and therefore before the communist victory in China in 1949. Recognition by China came only the following year, after Mao had become Chinese leader. So a communist state existed on the Korean peninsula before there was one in China, and its sponsor was the Soviet Union, whose troops had entered the north after the declaration of war on Japan on 8 August 1945 and had worked with local communists and nationalists to disarm the Japanese. Its leader had been trained in the USSR and introduced his own version of Marxism-Leninism under the protection of Soviet forces. The regime was therefore produced by external division and foreign sponsorship rather than by internal revolution, and the division itself was proposed by the Americans along the 38th parallel. A single date, correctly used, disposes of both the export thesis and the assumption that Asian communist states arose from peasant revolutions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating North Korea as an extreme version of the Chinese model. The two regimes differ in origin, in sponsor, in timing and in their constitutional principle: China's Party moved after 1981 from a single leader to a collective leadership, while the Korean regime moved to hereditary succession announced in 1980 and executed twice. Korea also demonstrates the opposite economic choice, introducing family-unit farming in July 2002 and reversing it by the summer of 2005, where China made the equivalent change permanent twenty years earlier.

---

## Lesson 20 - Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos: three Indochinese communisms and one atrocity

Progress: 20/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the Indochinese Union of 1887 and its social consequences; the unified communist party Ho Chi Minh helped to found in February 1930 and the Vietminh; the proclamation of 1945 and its suppression; Dien Bien Phu, Geneva and the elections that never happened; reunification in 1976 and the Doi Moi turn of December 1986; Sihanouk's Cambodia, the coup of March 1970 and the bombing; the Khmer Rouge years and the competing estimates; the Vietnamese intervention and the international response; the settlement of 1991-3; and Laos, the quiet case with the sharpest definitional question. Taught in three Parts

A. S. Grenville and Anthony Parsons, and the return of Sihanouk; and the Laos material with its concluding definitional question. ### Visual: one colony, three outcomes

⚠️ One colonial structure, three outcomes: a reunified party-state that adopted market reform, a monarchy restored after an atrocity and a United Nations transition, and a one-party state that abandoned socialist economics so thoroughly that its classification became a genuine question. ⚠️ That is the divergence discipline in a single diagram.

### Part A - Vietnam

**The colonial condition.** ✅ Vietnam, together with Laos and Cambodia, was part of the French Empire in south-east Asia, known as the Indochinese Union, which was established in 1887. ✅ In many ways the French were good colonial administrators: they built roads and railways, schools and hospitals, and even a university in Hanoi, in the north of Vietnam. ✅ But there was very little industrialisation; most of the people were poor peasants for whom life was a struggle. ✅ During the 1930s protest movements began to emerge, but these were unceremoniously suppressed by the French authorities. ✅ The French attitude encouraged nationalist and revolutionary feelings and brought a rush of support for the new Vietnamese Communist Party. ✅ That party was the unified Vietnamese communist organisation which **Ho Chi Minh helped to found in February 1930**, and which was renamed the **Indochinese Communist Party** later in the same year.

⚠️ **February 1930, not 1929, is the date to write**, and the reason is more than pedantry: the event was the *unification* of rival Vietnamese communist groups into a single party, and Ho's role in it was as principal organiser rather than sole founder. ⚠️ An answer that says "Ho Chi Minh founded the party in 1929" has misdated the event and misdescribed his role in it.

⚠️ Note the causal claim, which is the single most important thing in the lesson for a comparative question: the suppression of protest *produced* support for the communist party. ⚠️ Where a colonial regime criminalises moderate nationalism, it leaves the field to the organisation best equipped to operate illegally - and in Indochina that was the communist party.

✅ Ho Chi Minh had spent time in France, China and the USSR; he had always been a committed nationalist, but after his travels abroad he became a committed communist as well. ✅ His dream was a united Vietnam under communist rule.

⚠️ The ordering in that sentence is decisive and should be quoted in substance. Nationalist first, communist second. ⚠️ This is the recorded basis of the canonical judgement that ✅ nationalism and communism fused more strongly in Vietnam than in China, and it is why a Vietnamese communist movement could claim to be the national movement in a way the Chinese party had to earn through the anti-Japanese war.

**1940-45.** ✅ The French defeat in Europe in June 1940 raised hopes of Vietnamese independence, but these were soon dashed when Japanese forces moved into Indochina. ✅ When the nationalists and communists launched a full-scale uprising in the south of Vietnam, the French - now under orders from the Vichy government and therefore technically on the same side as Germany and Japan - and the Japanese worked together, and the rising was brutally crushed. ✅ With the communist movement almost wiped out in the south, Ho Chi Minh moved to the north and organised the communist and nationalist resistance movement, the League for the Independence of Vietnam, known as the Vietminh.

⚠️ The geographical division of the movement dates from here, not from Geneva: the southern organisation was destroyed in 1940 and the movement reconstituted itself in the north. ⚠️ The later north-south asymmetry has a wartime origin.

✅ In the summer of 1945, with the Japanese defeat imminent - they surrendered on 14 August - Ho Chi Minh prepared to seize the initiative before the French returned. ✅ Vietminh forces and supporters took over Hanoi, Saigon and most of the large towns, and in September 1945 the Democratic Republic of Vietnam was proclaimed with Ho Chi Minh as president.

**The episode that decided the next thirty years.** ✅ The declaration proved premature. ✅ It had been agreed among the Allies that when the war ended, the southern half of Vietnam should come under British and French administration. ✅ When British forces moved in, it was decided that French control should be restored as soon as possible. ✅ The British used Japanese troops who were still in Vietnam after their government had surrendered, and who had still not been disarmed, to suppress the Vietminh in the south. ✅ The British were anxious not to deprive their ally of its colonies, since this might encourage a general trend towards decolonisation, in which Britain might also lose its empire. ✅ By the end of the year order had been restored and some 50,000 French troops had arrived to take control. ✅ At this time, before the Cold War developed, the Americans were appalled at what had happened, since they had promised to liberate the people of Indo-China.

✅ As J. A. S. Grenville points out, this was "one of the most extraordinary episodes of the post-war period. If the south had been permitted to follow the north and the independence of the whole of Indo-China had been accepted by the British, the trauma of the longest war in Asia, which led to at least 2.5 million deaths and untold misery, might have been avoided."

⚠️ Three observations. ⚠️ First, the British motive is stated explicitly and is about **empire preservation in general**, not about Vietnam: allowing decolonisation in one place might encourage it elsewhere. ⚠️ That is the clearest recorded statement in this topic of colonial powers acting as a system. ⚠️ Second, the American position in 1945 was anti-colonial, and the reversal that followed is a Cold War development owned elsewhere. ⚠️ Third, the Grenville counterfactual carries a figure - at least 2.5 million deaths in the longest war in Asia - and it must be attributed to him and to that war, ❌ never merged with the Cambodian estimates below.

**To Dien Bien Phu and Geneva.** ✅ At first the French seemed prepared to compromise: they controlled the south but recognised the independence of the Vietnamese Republic in the north, provided it remained within the French Union. ✅ However, during the summer of 1946 it became increasingly clear that the French had no intention of allowing the north genuine independence. ✅ Ho Chi Minh therefore demanded complete independence for the whole of Vietnam; the French rejected this, and hostilities began when they shelled the northern port of Haiphong, killing thousands of Vietnamese civilians. ✅ After eight years of bitter struggle, the French were finally defeated at Dien Bien Phu in 1954. ✅ The Geneva Agreements recognised the independence of Ho Chi Minh's North Vietnam, but for the time being the area south of the 17th parallel of latitude was to be controlled by an international commission of Canadians, Poles and Indians. ✅ The commission was to organise elections for the whole country in July 1956, after which Vietnam would be united.

⚠️ Note the composition of the commission - Canadians, Poles and Indians, one from each side of the Cold War divide and one non-aligned state. ⚠️ India's presence is worth a clause in an answer that touches on non-alignment, though India's foreign policy itself belongs to its own owners.

**The elections that never happened.** ✅ All the indications were that the Vietminh would win the national elections, but once again their hopes were dashed: the elections never took place. ✅ With the Cold War in full swing, the Americans were determined to prevent Vietnam becoming united under a government with strong communist connections. ✅ They backed Ngo Dinh Diem, a nationalist and anti-communist, for the leadership of the south, and in 1955 he proclaimed the Vietnam Republic with himself as president of a strongly anti-communist regime; ✅ elections had disappeared from the agenda. ✅ By this time both Vietnams were in a sorry state, devastated by almost a decade of fighting. ✅ Ho Chi Minh's government in Hanoi received aid from the USSR and China and began to introduce socialist policies of industrialisation and the collectivisation of agriculture. ✅ President Ngo Dinh Diem's government in Saigon became increasingly unpopular, causing more people to join the communists or Vietcong, who were enthusiastically backed by the north.

⚠️ **The boundary.** The Vietnam War of 1961-75 and the American intervention belong to the Cold War owner. ⚠️ This session teaches the regimes on either side of it and the consequences of the war for the communist state that emerged, and stops there.

**The isolated republic, 1976-86.** ✅ The Socialist Republic of Vietnam was officially proclaimed in July 1976 with its capital at Hanoi. ✅ The country had hardly known peace for over thirty years; large parts of the north had been devastated by American bombings; throughout the country millions of people were homeless; and their inspirational leader, Ho Chi Minh, had died in 1969.

✅ The government began to extend its centralised command-economy policies to the south, abolishing capitalism and collectivising farm land. ✅ This aroused serious opposition, especially in the great business and commercial centre of Saigon, which was renamed Ho Chi Minh City: many people refused to co-operate and did their utmost to sabotage the new socialist measures; the cadres whose job was to organise collectivisation were often unwilling and incompetent; and this, together with the corruption which was rife among party officials, turned the whole process into a disaster.

✅ There were serious divisions among the top party leaders over how long pure Marxist-Leninist policies should be continued: some wanted to follow China's example and experiment with elements of capitalism, but the hardliners condemned these ideas as sacrilegious. ✅ In the late 1970s the country suffered major floods and drought which, together with collectivisation problems and rapid population increase, caused serious food shortages. ✅ Hundreds of thousands of people fled the country, some on foot to Thailand and Malaya, and others by sea - the "boat people".

⚠️ Notice the repetition of a pattern already established: ⚠️ collectivisation executed by unwilling and incompetent cadres, in a region hostile to it, during a period of natural disaster, produces food shortage and mass flight. ⚠️ That is the Chinese sequence of 1958-61 in miniature, with an added variable - it was being imposed on a territory that had just been conquered.

**Isolation and the reversal.** ✅ Vietnamese foreign policy was expensive and brought the country into conflict with its neighbours. ✅ The regime aimed to form alliances with the new left-wing governments in Laos and Cambodia; when Pol Pot's Khmer Rouge government refused the offer of a close relationship and persisted with provocative border raids, Vietnam invaded and occupied most of the country in December 1978. ✅ The Khmer Rouge were driven out and replaced by a pro-Vietnamese government; ✅ but they were not finished, and began a guerrilla war against the new regime, forcing the Vietnamese to send some 200,000 troops to maintain their ally in power. ✅ Pol Pot was a protégé of the Chinese, who were furious at Vietnam's intervention; in February 1979 they launched an invasion of northern Vietnam, inflicting considerable damage in the frontier area, although they did not escape unscathed as the Vietnamese mounted a spirited defence. ✅ The Chinese withdrew after three weeks, claiming to have taught the Vietnamese a sharp lesson. ✅ After that the Chinese supported the Khmer Rouge guerrillas, and the USA, Japan and most of the states of Western Europe imposed a trade embargo on Vietnam. ✅ It was a bizarre situation in which the USA and its allies continued to support Pol Pot, one of the most grotesque and brutal dictators the world had ever seen.

✅ By the mid-1980s Vietnam was almost completely isolated: its ASEAN neighbours were all hostile and supported the resistance movement in Cambodia, and even the USSR, which had consistently backed Vietnam against China, was drastically reducing its aid.

✅ In 1986 Vietnam was in serious crisis: internationally isolated, with a vast permanent army of around one million that was cripplingly expensive to maintain, and still without a viable socialist economy in the south. ✅ With the deaths of the older party leaders, younger members were able to convince the party of the need for drastic policy changes, and in particular the need to extricate themselves from Cambodia. ✅ At the party's national congress of **December 1986**, a leading economic reformer, Nguyen Van Linh, was appointed general secretary. ✅ He introduced a new doctrine known as **Doi Moi**, which meant renewing the economy, as the Chinese had already begun to do, by moving towards the free market, in an attempt to raise living standards to the level enjoyed by Vietnam's neighbours.

✅ Agreement was at last reached over Cambodia: Vietnamese troops were withdrawn in September 1989 and the task of finding a permanent settlement was handed to the United Nations. ✅ This freed vast sums of revenue for investment in the economy. ✅ Even so, economic progress was slow, and it was several years before the population felt much benefit; ✅ one problem was the rapidly growing population, which reached almost 80 million at the end of the century, against around 17 million in 1950. ✅ Signs of progress were more obvious in the early years of the new century: in July 2000 the country's first stock exchange opened in Ho Chi Minh City, a trade agreement was signed with the USA allowing American goods into Vietnam in exchange for lower duties on Vietnamese goods entering the USA, and in November President Clinton visited Vietnam to encourage closer business and cultural ties.

⚠️ The Doi Moi sentence contains its own comparison - "as the Chinese had already begun to do" - and an answer should use it. ⚠️ Vietnam followed the Chinese sequence: market reform under continued party control, adopted after the failure of command economics and under acute fiscal pressure from a foreign military commitment. ⚠️ The difference is that the trigger was external isolation rather than internal campaign failure.

### Part B - Cambodia

**Sihanouk's neutrality.** ⚠️ The opening of this section is missing from the extraction available for this session and is recorded as a gap. ✅ The legible record begins with Cambodia under Japanese occupation until 1945, and with Sihanouk proclaiming Cambodia an independent state in March 1945 as Japanese defeat became inevitable; ✅ French troops soon returned and he had to accept a reversion to the pre-war position. ✅ Sihanouk was a shrewd politician: he believed French rule would not survive long and was prepared to bide his time rather than use force. ✅ While the struggle for independence raged in neighbouring Vietnam, Cambodia was relatively peaceful; he placed himself at the head of the nationalist movement, avoided involvement in any political party, and won respect and popularity with a wide cross-section of Cambodian society.

✅ In 1954, after the French defeat in Vietnam, the Geneva Conference recognised the independence of Cambodia and Sihanouk's government as the rightful authority. ✅ Although immensely popular as the architect of peace and independence, many of the intelligentsia resented his growing authoritarianism; the opposition included pro-democracy groups and the Communist Party, formed in 1951, which eventually became known as the Communist Party of Kampuchea. ✅ Sihanouk founded his own party, the People's Socialist Community, and in March 1955 took the remarkable step of abdicating in favour of his father, Norodom Suramarit, so that he himself could play a full part in politics as plain Mr Sihanouk. ✅ His party took every seat in the National Assembly; he became prime minister, and when his father died in 1960 he became head of state without taking the title of king. ✅ The opposition, especially the communists now calling themselves the Khmer Rouge, made very little headway, and Sihanouk remained in power for the next fifteen years. ✅ His rule succeeded in being authoritarian and benign at the same time, and the country enjoyed a period of peace and reasonable prosperity while, for much of this time, Vietnam was torn by civil war.

⚠️ This is the comparison the whole Cambodian tragedy turns on. ⚠️ Independence without a war of independence produced a government with genuine popular legitimacy and a weak communist opposition - the opposite of the Vietnamese pattern. ⚠️ What destroyed it came from outside.

**The destruction of neutrality.** ✅ Sihanouk's foreign policy antagonised the USA: he distrusted American motives and suspected that Thailand and South Vietnam, both American allies, had designs on Cambodia. ✅ He tried to remain neutral, avoided accepting American aid and was encouraged in this by President de Gaulle of France, whom he admired. ✅ As the war in Vietnam escalated, Sihanouk realised the Vietnamese communists were likely to win in the end, and agreed to allow them to use bases in Cambodia, as well as the Ho Chi Minh trail through Cambodian territory. ✅ Since he was powerless to prevent this anyway, it seemed the most sensible policy. ✅ However, the Americans started to bomb Cambodian villages near the border with Vietnam, and in May 1965 Sihanouk broke off relations with the USA, at the same time moving towards a closer relationship with China.

✅ In the late 1960s his popularity waned: right-wingers resented his anti-American stance and his collaboration with the Vietnamese communists, while the left and the communists opposed his authoritarian methods. ✅ The communists, under Saloth Sar - who later called himself Pol Pot, and who had been a teacher in Phnom Penh before leaving to organise the party - were becoming stronger. ✅ In 1967 they provoked an uprising among peasants in the north, which frightened Sihanouk into thinking a communist revolution was imminent; he overreacted, using troops to quell it, and villages were burned and suspected troublemakers murdered or imprisoned without trial. ✅ He further discredited himself with the left by reopening diplomatic relations with the USA, and clashes between Khmer Rouge guerrillas and his army became almost daily events.

✅ Worse still, the new American president, Richard Nixon, and his security adviser Henry Kissinger began large-scale bombings of Vietnamese bases in Cambodia; as the communists moved deeper inside the country, the bombers followed and Cambodian civilian casualties mounted. ✅ By 1970 the leading anti-communists decided that drastic action was needed, and in March 1970, while Sihanouk was visiting Moscow, General Lon Nol and his supporters, backed by the Americans, staged a coup. ✅ Sihanouk was overthrown, took refuge in Beijing, and Lon Nol became head of the government.

✅ Lon Nol's period in power, 1970-5, was a disaster for Cambodia. ✅ He had rashly promised to drive Vietcong forces out of the country, but this drew Cambodia into the thick of the Vietnam War: almost immediately American and South Vietnamese troops invaded eastern Cambodia, while over the next three years heavy American bombing pounded the countryside, destroying hundreds of villages. ✅ The Americans failed to destroy either the Vietcong or Pol Pot's Khmer Rouge, both of which continued to harass American forces; ✅ even Sihanouk's supporters joined the struggle against the invaders.

✅ In January 1973 peace came to Vietnam, but the Americans continued a massive aerial bombardment of Cambodia in a final attempt to prevent the Khmer Rouge from coming to power. ✅ During March, April and May 1973 the tonnage of bombs dropped on Cambodia was more than double that of the whole of the previous year. ✅ Yet the USA and Cambodia were not at war, and no American troops were being threatened by Cambodians. ✅ Cambodia's infrastructure, such as it was, and its traditional economy were all but destroyed. ✅ After the Americans called off the bombings the civil war continued for a further two years, as the Khmer Rouge gradually closed in on Lon Nol's government in Phnom Penh. ✅ In April 1975 Lon Nol's regime collapsed, the Khmer Rouge entered the capital, and Pol Pot became the ruler of Cambodia.

⚠️ The causal claim here is explicit in the sources and should be stated carefully rather than either suppressed or exaggerated. ✅ Grenville's judgement is that "if the Americans had not turned against Sihanouk, one of the cleverest and wiliest of south-east Asian leaders, Cambodia might have been spared the almost unbelievable horrors that followed". ⚠️ That is a counterfactual by a named historian, and it should be attributed as such. ⚠️ What is not counterfactual is the sequence: a neutral state was drawn into a neighbouring war, its neutral ruler was removed in an American-backed coup, it was then subjected to bombing on a scale that destroyed its infrastructure and traditional economy while the two states were not at war, and the movement that had made very little headway under Sihanouk took power five years later.

**The Khmer Rouge years.** ✅ The new government called the country "Democratic Kampuchea", a completely inappropriate term in view of what happened over the next four years. ✅ Prince Sihanouk, who had worked with the Khmer Rouge during the previous five years, returned home from Beijing expecting to be well received; instead he was placed under house arrest and forced to watch helplessly as Pol Pot exercised total power. ✅ The Khmer Rouge caused even more misery by trying to introduce doctrinaire Marxist-Leninist principles almost overnight without adequate preparation.

✅ In the words of Michael Leifer: "Under the leadership of the fearsome Pol Pot, a gruesome social experiment was inaugurated. Cambodia was transformed into a primitive agricultural work camp combining the worst excesses of Stalin and Mao in which around a million people died from execution, starvation and disease."

✅ The communists ordered the population of Phnom Penh and other cities to move out, live in the countryside and wear peasant working clothes. ✅ Within a short time the urban centres were virtually empty, and thousands of people were dying in what amounted to forced marches. ✅ The aim was to collectivise the entire country immediately, in order to double the rice harvest. ✅ **Even Mao had taken years to get to this stage in China.** ✅ But the party cadres whose job it was to organise the transformation were inexperienced and incompetent, and most city dwellers were helpless in rural settings; the whole operation was a disaster and conditions became unbearable. ✅ At the same time money, private property, shops and markets were abolished, and schools, hospitals and monasteries were closed. ✅ Pol Pot's next move was to launch a campaign of genocide against all educated Cambodians and against anybody he thought might be capable of leading opposition; ✅ the result was that an entire generation of educated people was either killed or driven into exile.

⚠️ The sentence about Mao is the comparison the syllabus most needs here, and it is in the source itself. ⚠️ The Khmer Rouge did not apply the Chinese model; they attempted in months what the Chinese had approached over years, and they abolished money, markets, cities and schooling outright, which the Chinese did not. ⚠️ Cambodia is therefore not an extreme point on a Chinese scale; it is a different project that used the same vocabulary.

**The competing explanations.** ✅ In his controversial 2005 biography of Pol Pot, Philip Short argues that these atrocities were not the product of either a sociopathic dictator or his Marxist ideology, but of a Cambodian popular culture which had a long history of violent extremism: ✅ during Pol Pot's own schooldays in the 1950s naughty children were severely beaten and their wounds exposed to red ants, and previous royalist and republican governments had regularly tortured, raped and murdered on a huge scale. ✅ The reviewer Tim Stanley records that Short is correct that there is something so uniquely insane about the Khmer genocide that national character presents itself as an explanation. ⚠️ The extraction of the remainder of that review is damaged and this session does not reproduce a conclusion it cannot read.

✅ Against that reading, the account itself records the decisive comparative objection: **neighbouring Vietnam and Laos experienced war and terror at the same time but never attempted such a radical social solution.**

⚠️ That objection is devastating to the cultural explanation and should be used as such. ⚠️ If regional culture or shared wartime trauma explained the Khmer Rouge, the same outcome should have appeared in the neighbouring states that had the same colonial ruler, the same war and comparable devastation. ⚠️ It did not. ⚠️ The balanced verdict to write is that the cultural argument identifies a real background of political violence but cannot explain the specific and unique programme - immediate total collectivisation, the emptying of the cities, the abolition of money and markets, and the targeting of the educated - and that a full explanation must combine the leadership's doctrine, the absence of institutional restraint and the destruction of the country's infrastructure and traditional economy in the preceding five years.

**The estimates.** ✅ Some estimates put the total of those who died in the notorious "killing fields" as high as two million; ✅ just over a third of the total population of 7.5 million disappeared. ✅ Michael Leifer's figure, quoted above, is around a million dead from execution, starvation and disease.

⚠️ **The rule, once more.** ⚠️ Write "estimates of the death toll differ: Michael Leifer gives around a million from execution, starvation and disease, while some estimates put the total who died in the killing fields as high as two million, with just over a third of a population of 7.5 million recorded as having disappeared." ❌ Do not select one figure and present it as the toll; ❌ do not merge these with the Grenville figure for the Vietnam war, which measures a different thing.

**The intervention and the world's response.** ✅ Pol Pot contributed to his own downfall: he tried to cover up the failings of his economic policies by adopting a brash nationalistic foreign policy, which caused unnecessary tensions with Vietnam, whose government was anxious for a close relationship with its communist neighbour. ✅ After a number of border incidents and provocations by the Khmer Rouge, the Vietnamese army invaded Cambodia and drove out the Pol Pot regime in January 1979. ✅ They installed a puppet government in Phnom Penh in which Hun Sen - a former Khmer Rouge military commander who had fled to Vietnam and organised an anti-Pol Pot army of Cambodian exiles - was a leading figure. ✅ Most of the country was occupied by Vietnamese troops until 1989, while Pol Pot and a large army of Khmer Rouge guerrillas retreated into the mountains of the south-west.

✅ The new regime was a great improvement on Pol Pot's murderous government, but it was not recognised by the USA and most other countries. ✅ According to Anthony Parsons, the United Kingdom's permanent representative at the United Nations, "instead of receiving a public vote of thanks from the UN for ridding Cambodia of a latter-day combination of Hitler and Stalin, and saving the lives of countless Cambodians, the Vietnamese found themselves on the receiving end of draft resolutions in January and March 1979 calling for a cease-fire and the withdrawal of 'foreign forces'." ✅ The USSR supported Vietnam and vetoed the resolutions, so no further action was taken. ✅ The reason for the anti-Vietnam stance was that the USA and the non-communist states of south-east Asia were more afraid of a powerful Vietnam than they were of the Khmer Rouge; ✅ for the sake of their own interests they would have preferred to see Pol Pot's regime continue in power.

⚠️ This is the most uncomfortable passage in the topic and it is examinable precisely for that reason. ⚠️ It shows Cold War alignment overriding every other consideration, including the character of the regime being defended, and it shows a communist state receiving international censure for removing a communist regime that had killed a large fraction of its own population. ⚠️ Any answer claiming that the Cold War was a contest between moral systems has to deal with this case.

**The settlement.** ✅ The new Phnom Penh government consisted mainly of moderate communists who had deserted Pol Pot. ✅ Uncertainty caused perhaps half a million Cambodians, including former communists and members of the intelligentsia, to take refuge in Thailand. ✅ Although kept in power by Vietnamese troops, the government could claim considerable success over the next ten years: the extreme Khmer Rouge policies were abandoned, people were allowed to return to the towns and cities, schools and hospitals reopened, and Buddhists were allowed to practise their religion; later, money and private property were restored, the economy settled down and trade started up again.

✅ Its main problem was opposition from three resistance groups operating from over the border in Thailand: the Khmer Rouge, still a formidable force of some 35,000; Prince Sihanouk and his armed supporters, numbering about 18,000; and the non-communist National Liberation Front led by Son Sann, with around 8,000 troops. ✅ In 1982 the three formed a joint government-in-exile with Sihanouk as president and Son Sann as prime minister; ✅ the United Nations officially recognised them as the rightful government, but they received very little support from ordinary Cambodians, who seemed happy with the existing regime in Phnom Penh. ✅ Hun Sen became prime minister in 1985 and the opposition made no headway.

✅ The situation changed towards the end of the 1980s as it became clear that Vietnam could no longer afford to keep a large military force in Cambodia. ✅ For a time there was the frightful possibility that the Khmer Rouge might seize power again when the Vietnamese withdrew, but the other two opposition groups, as well as Hun Sen, were determined not to let this happen, and all agreed to take part in talks organised by the United Nations. ✅ The ending of the Cold War made it easier to reach a settlement, and agreement was reached in October 1991: there was to be a transitional government known as the Supreme National Council, consisting of representatives of all four factions including the Khmer Rouge, and United Nations troops and administrators were to help prepare the country for democratic elections in 1993. ✅ The Supreme National Council elected Prince Sihanouk as president, and a large United Nations team of 16,000 troops and 6,000 civilians arrived to demobilise the rival armies and make arrangements for the elections. ✅ Progress was far from easy, mainly because the Khmer Rouge, seeing its chances of regaining power slipping away, refused to co-operate or take part.

✅ The elections went ahead in June 1993: the royalist party led by Prince Ranariddh, Sihanouk's son, emerged as the largest group, with Hun Sen's Cambodian People's Party second. ✅ Hun Sen refused to give up power, and the United Nations found a solution by setting up a coalition with Ranariddh as first prime minister and Hun Sen as second prime minister. ✅ One of the first acts of the new National Assembly was to vote to restore the monarchy, and Prince Sihanouk became king and head of state once again. ✅ From this point the political history of Cambodia consisted largely of a feud between the royalists and the supporters of Hun Sen: in July 1997 Hun Sen removed Ranariddh in a violent coup, and the prince was tried and found guilty in absentia of attempting to overthrow the government, apparently having tried to enlist help from what was left of the Khmer Rouge; he was pardoned by his father the king and was able to take part in the 1998 elections.

⚠️ The extraction of the pages covering the outcome of those elections and the subsequent period is missing, and this session carries the Cambodian record no further. ⚠️ The gap is recorded in the final ledger.

⚠️ The closing observation for an answer: ✅ the man restored as king in 1993 was the man overthrown in March 1970, and the country arrived, after an atrocity and a decade of occupation, at something like democratic rule under a restored monarchy - an outcome no ideological account of the region would have predicted in 1975.

### Part C - Laos

⚠️ The opening of the Laos section is missing from the extraction and is recorded as a gap. ✅ The chapter summary supplies the essentials: Laos was drawn into the Vietnam War in spite of itself and had to endure indiscriminate American bombing; ✅ at the end of 1975 a communist organisation took power and remained in control.

✅ Before they came to power, the Lao communist leaders had worked in close co-operation with their allies in Vietnam, and it was only to be expected that the two governments would follow similar paths. ✅ In Laos the communists introduced farming collectives and brought trade, and what little industry there was, under government control. ✅ They also imprisoned several thousand political opponents in what were called re-education camps. ✅ The country and the economy were slow to recover from the ravages of the previous fifteen years, and thousands of people - some estimates put the total at around 10 per cent of the population - left the country to live in Thailand.

✅ Fortunately, the government was prepared to compromise its strict Marxist principles: in the mid-1980s, following the example of China and Vietnam, the collectivisation programme was abandoned and replaced by groups of family-run farms. ✅ State control over business and industry was relaxed, market incentives were introduced, and private investment was invited and encouraged. ✅ United Nations statistics suggested that by 1989 the economy of Laos was performing better than those of Vietnam and Cambodia in terms of Gross National Product per head. ✅ The party still kept full political control, but after the introduction of a new constitution in 1991 people were allowed more freedom of movement.

✅ **The definitional question, stated by the account itself:** the fact that the government, like those of China and Vietnam, had abandoned its communist or socialist economic policies raised the interesting question of whether or not it still was a communist regime. ✅ The leaders still seemed to think of themselves and describe themselves as having communist political systems, and yet their economic restructuring had left them with very few specifically socialist attributes. ✅ They could just as well be called simply "one-party states".

⚠️ This is the sharpest conceptual moment in the second half of the topic and it should be used in any question about the forms and effects of communism. ⚠️ If a regime retains the party monopoly and discards the economic content, what remains of the classification? ⚠️ Three positions are defensible and a candidate should pick one and defend it: that communism was always primarily a form of political organisation and therefore survives intact; that it was primarily an economic programme and therefore has been abandoned; or that the category has simply ceased to do useful work and "one-party state" is now the more accurate description. ⚠️ The third is the account's own suggestion and is the easiest to argue, but the point is to argue, not to assert.

✅ At the end of the century Laos was still a one-party state with a mixed economy performing disappointingly. ✅ In March 2001 President Khamtai Siphandon admitted that the government had so far failed to bring about the hoped-for increase in prosperity, and outlined a twenty-year programme of economic growth and improved education, health and living standards; ✅ impartial analysts pointed out that the economy was precarious, that foreign aid to Laos had doubled over the previous fifteen years, and that the International Monetary Fund had just approved a loan of $40 million to help balance the budget. ✅ None of this made any difference to the National Assembly elections of February 2002: there were 166 candidates for 109 seats, but all except one were members of the ruling party, and the state-run media reported a 100 per cent turnout. ✅ Dissatisfaction nevertheless began to cause unrest: in July 2003 an organisation called the Lao Citizens' Movement for Democracy held demonstrations and mini-uprisings in ten provinces, and in October another group, calling itself the Free Democratic People's Government of Laos, exploded a bomb in Vientiane and claimed responsibility for fourteen other explosions since 2000. ✅ In 2006 Choummaly Sayasone was chosen as Communist Party general secretary and president of Laos.

### Comparative synthesis

| | ✅ China | ✅ North Korea | ✅ Vietnam | ✅ Cambodia | ✅ Laos |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Route to power** | Peasant-based revolution and civil-war victory, 1949 | External division; Soviet sponsorship; proclaimed 1948 | Anti-colonial war, division, then victory and reunification 1976 | Civil war after an American-backed coup and heavy bombing; power April 1975 | Communist organisation took power at the end of 1975 |
| **Relation to nationalism** | Earned through the anti-Japanese war | Supplied by division and by resistance to the south | Fused from the start; Ho was nationalist before communist | Weak before 1970; the movement had made very little headway under Sihanouk | Allied to Vietnam's movement |
| **Distinctive feature** | Rural strategy; later market reform without democratisation | Dynastic succession; military-industrial priority | Doi Moi from December 1986, following the Chinese example | Immediate total collectivisation, emptied cities, abolition of money and markets, genocide against the educated | Quiet, adaptive; abandoned socialist economics while keeping the party |
| **Conflict with other communist states** | Split with the USSR; attacked Vietnam February 1979 | Lost Soviet aid at the end of the 1950s and Chinese aid in 1966 | Invaded Kampuchea December 1978; attacked by China February 1979 | Overthrown by Vietnam; backed thereafter by China | Aligned with Vietnam |
| **What it proves** | Communism could win by out-governing | Communism could be installed rather than won | Anti-colonial legitimacy could be the primary asset | Ideological radicalism without institutional restraint produced mass atrocity | The category itself can become uncertain |

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Stems ask candidates to compare the spread of communism in China with one South-East Asian case, to compare Chinese communism with Vietnamese or North Korean communism, or to assess whether communism in Asia formed a single bloc.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Begin with the shared colonial starting point and the recorded causal claim that suppression of protest produced support for the communist party. ⚠️ Take one case in depth rather than three in outline, unless the question demands a survey. ⚠️ Use the Doi Moi sentence's own comparison with China. ⚠️ Use the "even Mao had taken years to get to this stage" sentence to prevent Cambodia being treated as Chinese communism intensified. ⚠️ Close on the 1978-79 wars as proof that these were separate states with separate interests.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating Cambodia as an extreme version of China. ✅ Even Mao had taken years to reach the stage the Khmer Rouge attempted at once, and the abolition of money, markets and schooling has no Chinese parallel.
2. ❌ Selecting one Cambodian death estimate as the figure. ✅ Leifer gives around a million; some estimates reach two million; just over a third of a population of 7.5 million is recorded as having disappeared.
3. ⚠️ Attributing the Khmer Rouge to Cambodian national character without the objection. ✅ Vietnam and Laos experienced war and terror at the same time and never attempted such a radical social solution.
4. ⚠️ Narrating the Vietnam War. ⚠️ It belongs to the Cold War owner.
5. ⚠️ Omitting the international response to the 1979 intervention. ✅ The United Nations recognised the exile coalition and drafted resolutions against Vietnam, because the USA and the non-communist states of the region feared a powerful Vietnam more than the Khmer Rouge.
6. ⚠️ Missing the Laos definitional question. ✅ The account itself asks whether these were still communist regimes or simply one-party states.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Indochinese Union established 1887; French built roads, railways, schools, hospitals and a university in Hanoi, but there was very little industrialisation and most people were poor peasants.
2. ✅ 1930s protest movements unceremoniously suppressed; the French attitude brought a rush of support for the new Vietnamese Communist Party - the unified Vietnamese communist organisation Ho Chi Minh helped to found in February 1930, renamed the Indochinese Communist Party later that year; Ho was a committed nationalist before he became a committed communist.
3. ✅ 1940: Japanese entry; a southern rising crushed by the Vichy French and the Japanese together; Ho moved north and organised the Vietminh.
4. ✅ Japan surrendered 14 August 1945; the Vietminh took Hanoi, Saigon and most large towns; the Democratic Republic of Vietnam was proclaimed in September 1945.
5. ✅ The British, using undisarmed Japanese troops, suppressed the Vietminh in the south to preserve their ally's colonies and avoid encouraging decolonisation; about 50,000 French troops arrived by the end of the year; the Americans were appalled. ✅ Grenville: had the south been allowed to follow the north, "the trauma of the longest war in Asia, which led to at least 2.5 million deaths and untold misery, might have been avoided".
6. ✅ Hostilities began with the shelling of Haiphong; after eight years the French were defeated at Dien Bien Phu in 1954; Geneva recognised North Vietnam's independence with the area south of the 17th parallel under a commission of Canadians, Poles and Indians, to hold all-country elections in July 1956.
7. ✅ The elections never took place; the Americans backed Ngo Dinh Diem, who proclaimed the Vietnam Republic in 1955; Hanoi received Soviet and Chinese aid and began industrialisation and collectivisation; Saigon's unpopularity pushed people towards the Vietcong.
8. ✅ Socialist Republic proclaimed July 1976; command economics extended to the south was a disaster, with sabotage, unwilling and incompetent cadres and corruption; floods, drought and population growth caused food shortages; hundreds of thousands fled, including the boat people.
9. ✅ December 1978: Vietnam invaded Kampuchea; some 200,000 troops needed against Khmer Rouge guerrillas; February 1979: China invaded northern Vietnam and withdrew after three weeks; trade embargo by the USA, Japan and most of Western Europe; by the mid-1980s Vietnam was almost completely isolated even from Soviet aid.
10. ✅ December 1986: Nguyen Van Linh appointed general secretary and introduced Doi Moi, moving towards the free market as the Chinese had already begun to do; troops withdrawn from Cambodia September 1989; population almost 80 million by the end of the century against around 17 million in 1950; first stock exchange July 2000; trade agreement and Clinton's visit in November 2000.
11. ✅ Cambodia: Sihanouk proclaimed independence March 1945, accepted reversion, bided his time; Geneva 1954 recognised independence; Communist Party formed 1951; abdication March 1955; landslide; head of state from 1960; fifteen years of authoritarian and benign rule with peace and reasonable prosperity.
12. ✅ Neutrality destroyed: American bombing of border villages; relations broken off May 1965; the 1967 peasant uprising and his overreaction; Nixon and Kissinger's large-scale bombings; the American-backed coup of March 1970 while he was in Moscow; Lon Nol 1970-5; bombing tonnage in March-May 1973 more than double the whole previous year, with the two states not at war; Khmer Rouge took Phnom Penh April 1975.
13. ✅ Democratic Kampuchea: cities emptied, peasant clothing, deaths on forced marches, immediate total collectivisation to double the rice harvest - even Mao had taken years to reach that stage; money, private property, shops and markets abolished; schools, hospitals and monasteries closed; genocide against the educated. ✅ Leifer: around a million died from execution, starvation and disease.
14. ✅ Estimates: some put the killing-fields total as high as two million; just over a third of a population of 7.5 million disappeared. ✅ Philip Short's 2005 cultural explanation, and the recorded objection that Vietnam and Laos experienced war and terror at the same time and never attempted such a radical social solution.
15. ✅ January 1979: Vietnam drove out Pol Pot and installed a government including Hun Sen; occupation until 1989; the new regime unrecognised; Parsons on the United Nations resolutions of January and March 1979; the USSR vetoed them; the USA and non-communist south-east Asia feared a powerful Vietnam more than the Khmer Rouge.
16. ✅ Three resistance groups from Thailand - Khmer Rouge about 35,000, Sihanouk about 18,000, Son Sann's front about 8,000 - formed a government-in-exile in 1982 recognised by the United Nations; Hun Sen prime minister from 1985; October 1991 agreement; Supreme National Council with Sihanouk as president; 16,000 United Nations troops and 6,000 civilians; June 1993 elections; Ranariddh first and Hun Sen second prime minister; monarchy restored; July 1997 coup against Ranariddh.
17. ✅ Laos: drawn into the Vietnam War and subjected to indiscriminate American bombing; communist power at the end of 1975; collectives, state control of trade and industry, several thousand in re-education camps; around 10 per cent of the population left for Thailand by some estimates; collectivisation abandoned in the mid-1980s for family-run farms following China and Vietnam; better GNP per head than Vietnam or Cambodia by 1989 on United Nations statistics; new constitution 1991; February 2002 elections with 166 candidates for 109 seats and a reported 100 per cent turnout; unrest in 2003; Choummaly Sayasone from 2006.
18. ✅ The definitional question: with socialist economics abandoned and the party monopoly retained, these governments could just as well be called simply one-party states.

### Concept check

**Question:** Philip Short explains the Khmer Rouge atrocity by Cambodian popular culture. State the single strongest objection recorded against that explanation, and give the balanced conclusion.

**Model answer:** The strongest objection is comparative and is stated in the account itself: neighbouring Vietnam and Laos experienced war and terror at the same time but never attempted such a radical social solution. If a regional culture of violent extremism were the explanation, the same outcome should have appeared in states that shared the same colonial ruler, the same war and comparable devastation, and it did not. The balanced conclusion is that Short identifies a genuine background - previous royalist and republican governments had regularly tortured, raped and murdered on a huge scale - but that a background cannot account for a specific and unprecedented programme: the immediate collectivisation of the whole country to double the rice harvest, the emptying of Phnom Penh and other cities, the abolition of money, private property, shops and markets, the closure of schools, hospitals and monasteries, and a campaign of genocide against educated Cambodians. A full explanation has to combine the leadership's doctrinaire attempt to apply Marxist-Leninist principles almost overnight without adequate preparation, the complete absence of institutional restraint on Pol Pot's power, and the destruction of the country's infrastructure and traditional economy during five years of heavy bombing and civil war that preceded the regime.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Cambodia as Chinese communism taken further. The account draws the line explicitly - even Mao had taken years to reach the stage the Khmer Rouge attempted at once - and the abolition of money, markets and schooling has no Chinese counterpart. Cambodia used the shared vocabulary of Marxism-Leninism for a project its communist neighbours neither attempted nor tolerated, and the clearest proof is that it was a communist state, Vietnam, that overthrew it.

## Lesson 21 - China and Russia compared, without false equivalence

Progress: 21/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: what the two revolutions share and what they do not; seizure against protracted war; city against countryside; the timing of the civil war; the role of an external war in each; collectivisation and famine compared with the attribution discipline intact; the divergence after 1956; and the point at which the comparison stops teaching
