━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a comparison built on differences, not similarities

```text
                        RUSSIA                     CHINA
   ==================================================================
   FORM OF SEIZURE      An organised SEIZURE in    A PROTRACTED WAR of
                        October 1917, on the       22 years, 1927-49,
                        night of 25-26 October,    ending in the capture
                        after the broad overthrow  of a country by an
                        of February                army that already
                                                   governed 100 million
                                                   people
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   SOCIAL BASE          Urban, with a peasant      RURAL from 1927
                        land settlement            onwards, by strategic
                        ratified afterwards        decision after urban
                                                   insurrections FAILED
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   SEQUENCE             SEIZE POWER (1917), then   BUILD ADMINISTRATION
                        fight a CIVIL WAR          (1927-49), then take
                        (1918-20) to keep it       power (1949)
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   EXTERNAL WAR         The First World War        The Japanese war
                        DESTROYED the old order    (1937-45) DAMAGED THE
                        and created the opening    RIVAL and legitimised
                                                   the challenger
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   PARTY AT THE MOMENT  A party with a theory of   A party whose theory
   OF DECISION          urban revolution, acting   of urban revolution
                        in an urban setting        had already failed,
                                                   acting in villages
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   AFTER 1956           De-Stalinisation; PEACEFUL Rejection of both;
                        COEXISTENCE; communism by  the charge of
                        methods other than violent REVISIONISM
                        revolution
   ==================================================================

   THE ONE-LINE FORMULATION
   The Bolsheviks took a state and then had to learn to govern it;
   the Chinese communists learned to govern and then took a state.
```

⚠️ The last line is the thesis of this lesson. ⚠️ Everything else is evidence for it, and the differences carry the argument.

### 1. The boundary, stated before the comparison begins

⚠️ The Russian revolution and the Stalinist transformation are owned by their own topic, which holds the 1905 settlement, the collapse of tsarism, February and October 1917 as distinct revolutions, the failure of the Provisional Government, Bolshevik consolidation, the Civil War, War Communism and the New Economic Policy, Stalin's rise, industrialisation, collectivisation, famine, the purges, the war and post-war repression. ⚠️ This session uses only the dated stages and stated mechanisms recorded by that owner, and it narrates nothing.

⚠️ Why the discipline matters: a comparative question tempts a candidate into writing half an essay on Russia inside an answer about China. ⚠️ The correct proportion is that the Russian material appears as the term of comparison, always in one clause, and the analytical weight stays on the Chinese side.

### 2. The four differences that carry the argument

**Difference one: seizure against protracted war.** ✅ The Bolshevik seizure took place on the night of 25-26 October 1917, after the abdication of 2 March had already removed the old order in a broad overthrow. ✅ The Chinese communists fought from the rupture of 1927 until 1949, and by 1945 already administered nineteen base areas containing a hundred million people. ⚠️ A seizure produces a government that must immediately improvise administration; a protracted war produces a government that has been administering for two decades before it arrives. ⚠️ This is why the Chinese transition after 1949 was rapid - ✅ communications restored and inflation controlled within a few years - and why the Bolsheviks passed almost immediately into a civil war fought to retain what they had taken.

**Difference two: the city and the countryside.** ⚠️ The Russian revolution was made in cities by a party whose theory placed revolutionary agency in an industrial working class, in a setting where that class, though small, was concentrated in the political centres. ✅ The Chinese party began with the same theory and found that several communist insurrections in the industrial towns had already failed because of the strength of the Nationalists, ✅ whereupon Mao changed the strategy to winning mass support among the peasants. ⚠️ The Chinese rural turn is therefore a *correction* of the model the Russian case had established, and it is the single most important thing Chinese communism contributed to the history of the movement.

**Difference three: the order of civil war and power.** ✅ In Russia the sequence was seizure in 1917 followed by civil war from 1918 to 1920; ✅ in China it was civil war from 1927 followed by power in 1949. ⚠️ The consequence is about what each regime learned. ⚠️ A party that wins a civil war *after* taking power learns coercion as the instrument of survival; ⚠️ a party that wins a civil war *in order to* take power has spent twenty years persuading, taxing, redistributing and administering in contested territory, and arrives with a repertoire that includes those skills as well.

**Difference four: what the external war did.** ⚠️ In the Russian case the First World War converted structural weakness into collapse: the war destroyed the existing order and created the opening. ✅ In the Chinese case the Japanese war did something different - it damaged the incumbent, who was obliged to fight conventionally in the territory he held and lost it, while legitimising the challenger, who fought irregularly in the north and grew from five base areas to nineteen. ⚠️ One war made a revolution possible by destroying a state; the other decided a contest between two existing contenders.

### 3. Where the comparison is genuinely close, and where it is not

| Dimension | ⚠️ Assessment |
|---|---|
| A single party monopolising political power | ⚠️ Genuinely parallel, and the parallel is structural rather than accidental: the Chinese party was reorganised on the same model, and so, earlier, was the Nationalist Party |
| Collectivisation of agriculture followed by famine | ⚠️ Superficially parallel and analytically dangerous. ✅ Both states collectivised and both experienced famine, but the evidence bases, the mortality estimates, the chronologies and the local mechanisms are different, and this session states only the Chinese figures with their authors and periods. ❌ No Russian figure is asserted here, and ❌ the two must never be totalled or treated as one phenomenon |
| Terror against internal enemies | ⚠️ The Russian owner records the purges of 1936-38 as its own material. ⚠️ On the Chinese side this session records the purification movement of 1927 by the Nationalists, the Anti-Rightist turn after 1957, and the Cultural Revolution. ⚠️ These are different in form, in target and in duration, and calling them all "terror" removes the information |
| Industrialisation strategy | ⚠️ The clearest divergence. ✅ China adopted the Soviet heavy-industry model in 1953 with Russian cash, equipment and advisers, ✅ and then Mao doubted whether China was suited to it and turned to a labour-intensive, rural, small-factory alternative. ⚠️ The Great Leap Forward is precisely the rejection of the Russian model |
| Doctrinal position after 1956 | ⚠️ Not parallel at all: the two states took opposite sides. ✅ The Soviet leadership adopted peaceful coexistence and the possibility of communism by methods other than violent revolution; ✅ the Chinese called this revisionism |
| Reform sequencing in the 1980s | ⚠️ Opposite, and each cited the other. ✅ The Chinese leadership concluded that Gorbachev's simultaneous economic and political reform had failed, and justified its own course accordingly |

⚠️ Read that table downward and the shape of the relationship appears: the two regimes were structurally similar in political form, divergent in social base and strategy, and eventually opposed in doctrine, in foreign policy and in reform method. ⚠️ Treating them as two instances of one thing loses every one of those findings.

### 4. The false equivalences to avoid, named

⚠️ Four specific errors, each of which appears regularly and each of which is refutable from material already taught.

1. ⚠️ **"Both were peasant revolutions."** ✅ The Chinese was, by strategic decision after urban failure. ⚠️ The Russian was not, and the Chinese party's own founding generation did not expect its revolution to be one either.
2. ⚠️ **"China simply followed the Soviet model."** ✅ It adopted the Soviet heavy-industry model in 1953 and then repudiated it; ✅ it accused the Soviet leadership of revisionism from the later 1950s; ✅ it lost all Russian aid during the Great Leap; ✅ it fought a Soviet ally in February 1979. ⚠️ Soviet assistance in the 1920s had in any case gone to the Nationalists.
3. ⚠️ **"Both revolutions were exported."** ✅ Soviet backing produced a communist state in northern Korea; ✅ Chinese influence did not produce the regimes in Vietnam, Cambodia or Laos, which had their own colonial origins, and ✅ China and Vietnam ended up at war.
4. ⚠️ **"Both ended the same way in 1989-91."** ⚠️ They ended in opposite ways, and the divergence is the most examinable single fact of the comparison: one state dissolved into fifteen and its party lost control; the other retained party control, continued economic reform and enforced the limit by force in June 1989.

### 5. What the comparison finally teaches

⚠️ Three things, and they are the reason to make the comparison at all.

⚠️ First, that a shared doctrine does not produce a shared history. Two movements using the same vocabulary produced different social bases, different strategies, different economic models, different foreign policies and opposite endings.

⚠️ Second, that the decisive variables in both cases were local. ⚠️ In Russia, the recorded mechanism is that war converted structural weakness into collapse and that February and October were different kinds of event. ⚠️ In China, the recorded mechanisms are Nationalist governance failure, communist land policy and discipline, patriotic legitimacy from the Japanese war and the conversion to conventional warfare in 1948. ⚠️ Neither set of mechanisms is derivable from Marxist theory; both are derivable from the condition of the state in question.

⚠️ Third, that the comparison stops teaching at exactly the point where it is most often pushed - the human cost. ⚠️ Both regimes produced famine after collectivisation, and both produced political repression. ⚠️ But the evidence bases differ in accessibility, in date of release and in reliability; the estimates differ in authorship, bracket and method; and an answer that merges them has made a statement that no source supports about either country.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Comparative stems on communism's forms and its effects on society, on revolutionary ideologies mobilising peasants, or on whether communism was one movement all route here.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Open with the one-line formulation. ⚠️ Give the four differences with a clause of Russian evidence each and a sentence of Chinese evidence each. ⚠️ Use the table's divergence rows to show that the two states ended opposed in doctrine and in reform method. ⚠️ Name at least one false equivalence explicitly and refute it. ⚠️ Close on the finding that shared doctrine did not produce shared history.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing half an essay on Russia. ⚠️ Russian material appears as the term of comparison only.
2. ❌ Merging famine mortality across the two countries. ⚠️ Different evidence bases, estimates and mechanisms.
3. ⚠️ Saying China followed the Soviet model. ✅ It adopted, repudiated, quarrelled with and eventually fought the allies of that model.
4. ⚠️ Treating 1989-91 as a common ending. ⚠️ The outcomes were opposite and each leadership used the other's outcome as evidence.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Formulation: the Bolsheviks took a state and then had to learn to govern it; the Chinese communists learned to govern and then took a state.
2. ✅ Russian stages used here as boundary material only: the abdication of 2 March 1917; the seizure on the night of 25-26 October 1917; the Civil War of 1918-20; the New Economic Policy of 1921; Stalin's consolidation of 1928-29; the purges of 1936-38.
3. ⚠️ Difference one: seizure against a twenty-two-year protracted war ending with an army that already administered a hundred million people.
4. ⚠️ Difference two: urban theory applied in an urban setting against urban theory that failed and was corrected into a rural strategy.
5. ⚠️ Difference three: civil war after power against civil war for power - and the different repertoires each produces.
6. ⚠️ Difference four: a world war that destroyed a state against a war that damaged an incumbent and legitimised a challenger.
7. ⚠️ Genuine parallel: single-party monopoly and the organisational model, which the Nationalist Party also adopted.
8. ⚠️ Dangerous parallel: collectivisation and famine - different evidence, estimates and mechanisms; state only the Chinese figures with their authors and periods.
9. ✅ Clearest divergence: the Soviet heavy-industry model adopted in 1953 and then repudiated, with the Great Leap Forward as the explicit alternative.
10. ✅ Doctrinal opposition after 1956: peaceful coexistence and non-violent routes against the charge of revisionism.
11. ⚠️ Opposite endings: one state dissolved into fifteen with its party losing control; the other retained control, continued reform and enforced the limit by force.
12. ⚠️ Four false equivalences to refute: both peasant revolutions; China followed the Soviet model; both revolutions were exported; both ended the same way.

### Concept check

**Question:** Give the single formulation that best captures the difference between the Russian and Chinese routes to power, and justify it with two pieces of evidence from each case.

**Model answer:** The formulation is that the Bolsheviks took a state and then had to learn to govern it, while the Chinese communists learned to govern and then took a state. On the Russian side, the seizure took place on the night of 25-26 October 1917 after the abdication of 2 March had already removed the old order, and the Civil War of 1918-20 followed the seizure rather than preceding it - so the party acquired power first and then fought to retain it. On the Chinese side, the Chinese Soviet Republic was proclaimed at Juichin in 1931 with delegates from fifteen soviet areas attending the first All-China Congress of Soviets, and by 1945 the party administered nineteen base areas containing a hundred million people; power at national level came only in 1949, after twenty-two years of governing contested territory. The formulation explains two things that a simple ideological comparison cannot: why the Chinese transition after 1949 was rapid enough to restore communications and bring inflation under control within a few years, and why the Chinese party's decisive assets in the civil war were a land policy, disciplined armies and honest administration rather than doctrine.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the two revolutions as the same event in different countries because both produced single-party states that collectivised agriculture. The social bases differed, the strategies differed and were explicitly corrected in the Chinese case, the industrialisation models diverged from 1958, the doctrinal positions were opposed from the later 1950s, the two states fought each other's allies in 1979, and their reform sequences ended in opposite outcomes. A comparison that begins from similarity throws away every one of those findings.

## Lesson 22 - The verdict and the answer architecture: converting a century into marks

Progress: 22/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the seven judgements this topic requires; the five master theses; the mark-scaled structures for 10, 15 and 20 marks; the directive and demand map; the complete factual-risk control list; and the closing statement of what has and has not been established

Book context: queried - the canonical owner's answer-architecture sections, including its directive and demand map, its qualified thesis templates, its mark-scaled structures, its verdict scaffolds and its complete factual-risk control list; together with the advanced owner's analytical prompt table, its four-part Mains frameworks and its recorded probable questions; and the core account's own end-of-chapter questions for the two China chapters

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the directive and demand map

| If the question says | ✅ It is really testing | ❌ Do NOT write |
|---|---|---|
| *Why did the communists win in 1949?* | A comparative organisational and social explanation, not merely Nationalist failure | A civil-war narrative |
| *Assess Maoism's impact on society and the state* | Achievement **and** catastrophe in one balanced answer | Either one alone |
| *Reform without democracy* | The distinction between economic and political liberalisation | "Deng modernised China" |
| *Compare communism in China with one other Asian case* | Real divergence in route, social base and outcome | "Communism spread across Asia" |
| *Peasant-based revolution* | Why a rural strategy worked where an urban one had failed | A restatement of Marxist theory |
| *1911 and its consequences* | Why removing a dynasty did not create a state | "The republic was founded" |
| *Was international communism a single bloc?* | The Sino-Soviet record and the wars of 1978-79 | "The communist bloc was united against the West" |
| *A total and unmitigated disaster?* | A weighted judgement with the evidence base declared | An uncritical denunciation or defence |

⚠️ Read the middle column before writing anything. ⚠️ Most marks lost on this topic are lost by answering a question the examiner did not ask.

### 1. The five master theses

⚠️ These are the load-bearing sentences of the topic. Each answers one of the recurring demands and each is supported by evidence taught above.

1. ⚠️ **On 1949.** "The communists won because they built, in the countryside and during a war of national defence, the two things the Nationalists lost in the cities - administrative discipline and popular legitimacy."
2. ⚠️ **On the Mao era.** "Mao's regime achieved what the republic had failed to achieve for forty years - a unified, sovereign, centrally governed China - and then inflicted on that achievement two self-generated catastrophes; the capacity that did the first is what made the second possible."
3. ⚠️ **On Deng.** "Deng's reform was a deliberate separation of economic from political liberalisation, and the Democracy Wall in 1979, the dispersals of 1987 and Tiananmen in 1989 were the three occasions on which that separation was enforced."
4. ⚠️ **On Asian communism.** "Asian communism was not exported from China but locally produced: it was installed by division in Korea, fused with anti-colonial nationalism in Vietnam, produced an atrocity without Chinese precedent in Cambodia, and became so economically un-socialist in Laos that its own classification became a question."
5. ⚠️ **On the bloc.** "The communist world behaved like a states system, not like a church: China and the Soviet Union quarrelled over doctrine, then over strategy, then over territory, and finally fought each other's allies - which is why treating international communism as a single actor is the commonest analytical error in Cold War answers."

### 2. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | ⚠️ Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis → **two** reasons for the communist victory, **or** two Mao-era policies with their outcomes → one qualification → verdict. Nothing else fits in 150 words |
| **15** | Thesis → the 1911-49 chain in three moves → why the communists won, comparatively → the Mao-era balance-sheet with the evidence base declared → verdict |
| **20** | All of the above → **plus** the reform transition with its enforced limit **and** a genuine comparison with one non-Chinese Asian case → verdict |

⚠️ One warning about the 20-mark structure. ⚠️ The temptation is to add a fifth and sixth element - the Sino-Soviet split, the Russian comparison, the Cambodian atrocity - and produce a survey. ⚠️ Resist it. ⚠️ Two well-developed additions beat four mentioned ones, and the examiner is marking depth of explanation rather than breadth of reference.

### 3. The verdict scaffolds

- ⚠️ **"Why did the communists win?"** → "Because in a country where the central question since 1911 had been who could actually govern, the communists demonstrated in the villages and in the anti-Japanese war that they could - and the Nationalists demonstrated in the cities that they could not."
- ⚠️ **"Mao's legacy?"** → "State-building achievement and social catastrophe are not competing verdicts but the same verdict: the capacity that unified China was the capacity that made the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution possible."
- ⚠️ **"Deng's paradox?"** → "China's reform separated economic liberalisation from political liberalisation deliberately, and enforced that separation three times; the separation, not the growth, is the historically distinctive fact."
- ⚠️ **"Asian comparison?"** → "There was no single Asian communism; there were national revolutions and, in one case, a national partition, that adopted a common vocabulary for very different problems."
- ⚠️ **"A single bloc?"** → "No - a quarrel that began over doctrine in 1956 hardened into a territorial claim, drew both parties into competing for American favour, and ended with communist states at war with each other in 1979, reconciled only in May 1989."
- ⚠️ **"Total and unmitigated disaster?"** → "Neither total nor mitigated: the commune as a unit of local government and the labour-intensive strategic choice were real innovations with defensible reasoning, and the human cost, whose scale is disputed and whose character is not, is not offset by them."

### 4. The complete factual-risk control list

⚠️ Every item here is a place where a confident sentence goes wrong. ⚠️ Read the list before any answer on this topic.

1. ❌ Do not say 1911 solved China's political crisis. ✅ Warlordism and civil conflict continued.
2. ❌ Do not say the Nationalists lost only because of foreign interference. ✅ Their own corruption and weak governance mattered greatly, and ✅ Soviet obstruction in Manchuria was marginal beside them.
3. ❌ Do not say the communists won because of Soviet help. ✅ Domestic Chinese conditions were decisive, and ✅ American aid to the Nationalists was larger and partly stolen.
4. ❌ Do not say Deng democratised China. ✅ He reformed the economy; the political system remained one-party.
5. ❌ Do not say Tiananmen ended economic reform. ✅ Repression and reform proceeded together.
6. ❌ Do not give an unowned famine, casualty, growth or output figure. ✅ Every figure in this topic travels with its author and its period.
7. ❌ Do not merge the two Great Leap mortality estimates into a single range. ✅ They are by different authors over different periods.
8. ❌ Do not state a single Tiananmen death toll. ✅ The records diverge even within one account.
9. ❌ Do not treat North Korea, Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos as Chinese copies. ✅ All diverged sharply, and ✅ Korea's regime preceded China's.
10. ❌ Do not date the Party's founding, the Long March, the People's Republic, the Great Leap, the Cultural Revolution or Tiananmen loosely. ✅ 1921, 1934-35, 1949, 1958-61, 1966-76 or 1966-9 with the phenomenon named, and 1989.
11. ❌ Do not describe the communist bloc as monolithic. ✅ Use February 1979 as the decisive evidence.
12. ❌ Do not attribute the Sino-Soviet breach to a 1964 event or to Khrushchev's fall. ✅ Deterioration ran steadily after 1956.
13. ❌ Do not add Chinese or Soviet internal party history, congress dates, named polemical texts, aid or trade figures, troop numbers or a border-clash narrative to the Sino-Soviet account. ⚠️ None is supported by the evidence held here.
14. ❌ Do not report the Shanghai park sign as fact. ✅ It never existed; the story circulated and expressed real outrage.
15. ❌ Do not say 600,000 backyard furnaces were built. ✅ Mao talked of that number.
16. ❌ Do not assert any current Chinese economic, demographic, military or diplomatic fact. ⚠️ The single current item in this session is dated and limited to Lesson 17.
17. ❌ Do not merge the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution. ✅ Two distinct campaigns with distinct failures.
18. ❌ Do not narrate the Korean War, the Vietnam War, European decolonisation or the post-1991 order. ⚠️ Each belongs to another owner.

### 5. What the seven judgements are

⚠️ A complete command of this topic means being able to deliver seven judgements on demand, each in three or four sentences. ⚠️ They are, in order: why the Qing state failed; why 1911 achieved so little; why the communists rather than the Nationalists won; what the Mao era achieved and cost; why economic reform did not become political reform; why Asian communism was plural; and whether the communist world was ever one actor. ⚠️ Every lesson in this session exists to supply one or part of one of them.

### 6. Where the topic ends

⚠️ This session stops at three boundaries, and saying so in an answer is a strength rather than an admission.

⚠️ It stops at **1989** for the comparative question about reform sequencing, because what happened afterwards in Europe belongs to the end-of-Cold-War owner and what has happened since in China is not a matter a history answer needs to assert.

⚠️ That stopping point produces an asymmetry worth naming, because a reader will notice it: the Chinese narrative closes in June 1989 while Korea, Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos are carried into the 2000s. ⚠️ The asymmetry is deliberate. ⚠️ For the four Asian cases, what happened after 1989 is part of the comparison itself - the point of Vietnam and Laos is that they reformed their economies while keeping the party, the point of Cambodia is that an atrocity was followed by an international transition and a restored monarchy, and the point of North Korea is that its model endured when almost every other communist regime changed. ⚠️ Remove their later decades and the comparison collapses. ⚠️ For China the opposite is true: the argument this topic makes about the post-Mao period - that economic liberalisation was separated from political liberalisation and that the separation was enforced - is complete by June 1989, and everything after it illustrates the thesis rather than testing it. ⚠️ Adding three more decades of Chinese economic and leadership detail would therefore lengthen the session without strengthening a single judgement, while importing material that other owners hold and that a history answer should not assert.

⚠️ It stops at the **edge of the wars** - Korea 1950-3 and Vietnam 1961-75 - because those are Cold War theatres owned elsewhere; this session teaches the regimes on either side of them.

⚠️ It stops at the **doctrine boundary**: what communism *is* as a political philosophy is owned by the topic that holds political philosophies, and what communism *did* in China and Asia is owned here.

⚠️ And there is a fourth, honest limit that is not a boundary but a gap. ⚠️ The account read for this session has damaged pages at several points - the later groups of the Twenty-One Demands, the political changes of the early 1950s, the closing phase of the Cultural Revolution, the Korean War chapter, the opening of the Cambodian and Lao sections and the Cambodian elections after 1998. ⚠️ Where those gaps fall inside this topic's scope, the canonical owners have supplied the substance and the gap is recorded rather than filled; where they fall outside it, they are noted for completeness. ⚠️ Every one is listed in the final ledger.

### Examination use

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Any of the eight framings in the demand map above, and the core account's own end-of-chapter questions: whether Mao's policies from 1949 to 1960 were a total and unmitigated disaster; whether the Cultural Revolution was a bid for personal power rather than a battle of ideas; whether Deng could be considered a liberal in either his economic or his political outlook; and why his policies produced a crisis in 1987-9 and how successfully he dealt with it.

**Answer route.** ⚠️ Identify the demand from the middle column of the map. ⚠️ Select the master thesis that answers it. ⚠️ Build the mark-scaled structure. ⚠️ Run the factual-risk list mentally before writing any figure or date.

**Traps.** ⚠️ The three that cost the most marks on this topic, in order: an unowned mortality figure; a survey answer that mentions six things and explains none; and a verdict that treats achievement and catastrophe as an arithmetic balance rather than as products of the same institutional capacity.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Eight recurring demands with their real tests are given in the map; read the middle column before writing.
2. ⚠️ Five master theses: 1949; the Mao era; Deng; Asian plurality; the bloc.
3. ⚠️ Mark-scaled structures: 10 marks - thesis, two reasons or two policies, one qualification, verdict; 15 - plus the 1911-49 chain and the balance-sheet; 20 - plus the reform transition and one genuine Asian comparison.
4. ⚠️ Six verdict scaffolds, one for each recurring demand plus the "total and unmitigated" framing.
5. ⚠️ Eighteen factual-risk controls, covering dates, figures, attributions, boundaries and prohibited additions.
6. ⚠️ Seven judgements constitute command of the topic.
7. ⚠️ Three owner boundaries and one honest evidentiary gap list.
8. ⚠️ The three costliest errors: unowned figures, survey answers, arithmetic verdicts.

### Concept check

**Question:** A 20-mark question asks you to assess Maoism's impact on Chinese society and state formation. Give the architecture of your answer in six moves, and name the one thing you would deliberately leave out.

**Model answer:** Move one: a thesis stating that Mao's regime achieved what the republic had failed to achieve for forty years - a unified, sovereign, centrally governed China - and then inflicted on that achievement two self-generated catastrophes. Move two: the state-formation achievement with its specific content - political unification, the rolling back of foreign domination, restored communications, inflation brought under control, and a party-state with real authority - measured against the failures of the regime it replaced. Move three: the social transformation, kept qualitative except for the sourced series - the landlord class destroyed as a social force, collectivisation reaching about 95 per cent of surviving peasants by 1956, the spread of education and welfare, infant mortality falling from 203 per thousand births in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s, and a definite improvement in the position of women. Move four: the catastrophes as distinct campaigns - the Great Leap Forward of 1958-61 with both mortality estimates given with their authors and periods, and the Cultural Revolution with its purpose, its instruments and the Party's own 1981 verdict. Move five: the mechanism that links them, namely that Maoist politics repeatedly blurred criticism, disloyalty and class war, so that evidence of failure produced intensification rather than correction. Move six: the structural verdict that the capacity which unified China is the capacity that made both catastrophes possible. The thing to leave out is the Sino-Soviet split, except for the single clause needed to explain the withdrawal of Russian aid during the Great Leap: it is a strong topic in its own right and including it properly would cost the depth that moves three to five need.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a 20-mark answer rewards coverage. It rewards explanation. A script that mentions 1911, the Long March, the Japanese war, 1949, the Great Leap, the Cultural Revolution, the split, Deng, Tiananmen and three Asian cases in 250 words has said nothing about any of them; a script that develops six moves with named evidence and a stated mechanism has made an argument.

## Probable framings recorded by the owners and by the core account

⚠️ Since no question is owned, the best available guide to how this topic would be examined is the set of framings recorded by the two canonical owners and by the account itself. ⚠️ All eleven are covered by the original Mains practice below.

✅ **From the canonical owner:** discuss the causes of the communist victory in China in 1949; assess the impact of Maoism on Chinese society and state formation; compare the spread of communism in China with one South-East Asian case.

✅ **From the advanced owner:** assess the reasons for the communist triumph in China in 1949; comment on the proposition that Mao made modern China but also deeply damaged it; compare Chinese communism with Vietnamese or North Korean communism; and its four analytical prompts - why the communists defeated the Nationalists, how Mao should be judged, why reform did not become democratisation, and whether Asian communism formed one bloc.

✅ **From the core account's own end-of-chapter questions for the two China chapters:** how far would you agree that the policies of Mao Zedong and the Chinese Communist Party during 1949-60 were "a total and unmitigated disaster"; to what extent is it a fair verdict that "the Cultural Revolution of 1966-9 was an attempt by Mao Zedong to protect his own power and position rather than a genuine battle of ideas"; how far would you agree that "neither in his economic nor in his political outlook could Deng Xiaoping be considered to be a liberal"; and assess the reasons why the policies of Deng Xiaoping led to a period of crisis in 1987-9 and how successfully he dealt with it.

## What a topic with no owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a nil return over nine years should change preparation rather than reduce it, and three things follow.

1. ⚠️ **The topic is examined through other papers and other owners.** ✅ Every question in the span that concerns China is routed to the international-relations or geography owners. ⚠️ The material taught here is therefore the historical foundation of questions that are asked in another paper, and a candidate who treats a world-history nil return as permission to skip the topic will meet China elsewhere without the depth that makes an answer good.
2. ⚠️ **The Paper I syllabus clause nevertheless covers it directly.** ✅ The syllabus names political philosophies such as communism, capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ The Chinese revolution, the Maoist state and the Asian communist regimes are the principal non-European material available for that clause, and a stem on the forms and social effects of communism in the twentieth century would route here for at least half its content.
3. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** ⚠️ Lesson 22 lists the five master theses and the seven judgements. ⚠️ The two that transfer fastest to an unanticipated stem are the capacity thesis - who could actually govern - and the divergence thesis - that Asian communism was locally produced rather than exported.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - the collapse and the contest, Lessons 1 to 10

### Cumulative check A1 - one question, asked five times

**Question:** State the organising question of the period 1839-1949 and show it being asked and answered differently at five points in the chronology.

**Model answer:** The question is not which ideology deserved to win but which organisation could actually govern - collect revenue honestly, command obedient soldiers, reach the villages and defend the frontier. In the 1860s it is answered against the dynasty: the Taiping Rebellion was defeated not by ineffective government troops but by new regional armies the Ch'ing did not control, so the state lost its monopoly of force and the provinces began asserting independence of Beijing. In 1911-12 it is answered against the revolutionaries: they held legitimacy and provincial declarations but no army, so they traded the republic to Yuan Shikai, who ruled as a military dictator with army support until 1915. Between 1916 and 1928 it is answered by nobody: generals seized control of different provinces while the Nationalists tried to govern and control them. In 1928 it is answered half-way: reunification was achieved and Chiang could claim Sun's first principle, nationalism, but relying on wealthy landowners he made no moves towards democracy or land reform. By 1945 it is answered decisively in the communists' favour: they administered nineteen base areas containing a hundred million people, against five areas and 12 million in 1937, while the Nationalist regime was losing the cities to corruption and to inflation produced by printing money. The 1949 outcome is the answer to that question, not to a question about doctrine.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the period as a competition between ideologies in which the more attractive one won. Ideology mattered chiefly as a description of how each contender proposed to rebuild state capacity, and the winner is the contender that demonstrated the capacity in practice - through land policy, army discipline, honest administration and patriotic credibility - rather than the one with the most persuasive theory.

### Cumulative check A2 - what the Japanese war did, precisely

**Question:** A candidate writes that the Japanese invasion caused the communist victory. Correct the claim in a way that keeps everything true in it.

**Model answer:** The correction is that the invasion accelerated and made irreversible a shift already under way, and that it did so through a specific mechanism rather than by general effect. The shift was already under way: during the ten years following the Long March the communists continued to gain support while Chiang and the Nationalists steadily lost popularity, for the five recorded reasons - Nationalist inefficiency and corruption, unreformed factory conditions, unrelieved peasant poverty against an actively operated communist land policy, the contested New Life Movement of 1934, and the absence of effective resistance to Japan. The mechanism was geographical allocation of military tasks: the Nationalists held eastern China and were obliged to fight conventionally there, were quickly defeated and retreated westwards, while the communists, undefeated in Shensi and protected by the Second United Front, fought an effective guerrilla campaign in the north, presented themselves as patriotic nationalists and won support among peasants and middle classes. The measurable result is the growth from five base areas controlling 12 million people in 1937 to nineteen controlling 100 million by 1945. The claim is therefore true in ranking - the absence of effective resistance is recorded as the crucial factor - and false in exclusivity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Replacing one single-cause explanation with another. The invasion is one of five recorded reasons, and it is identified as the crucial one; an answer that drops the other four in order to emphasise it will be unable to explain why the communists were positioned to exploit it, or why the Nationalist regime was already a great disappointment to the majority of the Chinese people before 1937.

## Block B - the People's Republic, Lessons 11 to 18

### Cumulative check B1 - the recurring four-step mechanism

**Question:** Identify the political mechanism that recurs four times between 1957 and 1989, give its four steps, and name the four episodes with their outcomes.

**Model answer:** The mechanism is: a structural tension exists; the leadership invites or tolerates criticism for its own purposes; the criticism escalates past the point at which the party's monopoly is questioned; the campaign is closed and the critics are repressed. In 1957 the tension was between party cadres and the new class of technicians and engineers; Mao called for constructive criticism with the hundred flowers formula; critics attacked the cadres for incompetence, the government for over-centralisation and the party for being undemocratic, with some suggesting opposition parties; Mao hurriedly called the campaign off and clamped down, and it convinced him that a drive was needed to consolidate socialism, producing the Great Leap Forward of 1958. In 1978-9 Deng approved the Democracy Wall because the posters criticised the Gang of Four; the posters became more daring, attacking Mao and demanding open criticism, non-communist representation, freedom to travel and abolition of the communes; Deng attacked the dissidents, several received sentences of up to fifteen years and the Wall was abolished in November 1979. In December 1986 students demonstrated in support of Deng and the Four Modernizations but demanded a quicker pace and more democracy; they were dispersed, and the hardliners forced Hu Yaobang's resignation while Li Peng became prime minister. In 1989 protests demanding democracy and an end to party corruption were initially treated as just by Zhao Ziyang; martial law was declared on 20 May and the army attacked on 3-4 June, after which Zhao was removed and replaced by Jiang Zemin. The mechanism therefore spans both the Mao and Deng eras, which is why the reform period is correctly described as economic rather than political.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 1989 as an aberration produced by exceptional circumstances. It is the fourth operation of a mechanism that had run three times already, and the Democracy Wall case proves the limit was procedural rather than substantive, since one of the demands made there - abolition of the communes - was subsequently implemented by the very government that jailed the people who made it.

### Cumulative check B2 - writing about mass death in this topic

**Question:** Set out the rules this topic requires for writing about mortality, and apply them to the four episodes in which mass death is recorded.

**Model answer:** The rules are four. Every figure travels with the name of whoever produced it and the period it covers; no figure is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated or combined across sources; qualifiers such as "may have died prematurely" are retained because they name a different measurement from killing; and responsibility is attributed to named leaders, organs, armies and officials rather than to nations or peoples. Applied to the four episodes: for the purification movement of 1927, thousands of communists, trade union and peasant leaders were massacred, with some estimates putting the total murdered as high as 250,000. For land reform in the early 1950s the accounts are irreconcilable - some sources mention as many as two million killed, Jack Gray writing in 1970 while the archives were closed described a remarkable degree of attention to legality and minimum physical violence against landlords, and Frank Dikötter, using archives declassified around the 2008 Beijing Olympics, found considerable violence, torturing and execution. For the Great Leap Forward there are two estimates with different periods: statistics emerging later suggested some 20 million may have died prematurely, especially in the disastrous famine of 1959-60, while Dikötter puts the premature deaths at at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962, of whom he estimates between two and three million were tortured to death or summarily killed. For Tiananmen the records diverge even within a single account, which states in one place that the army killed hundreds and in another that at least a thousand students were killed and many later executed. In every case the divergence is itself reported, because it is evidence about the state of the historical record.

**Misconception to avoid:** Producing a compromise figure in the interest of balance. Averaging incompatible testimony creates a number no source supports, which is fabrication rather than moderation. The honest form of balance is to report each estimate with its author, its period and the evidentiary conditions under which it was formed.

### Cumulative check B3 - the one argument that runs from 1953 to 1989

**Question:** Trace the single continuing argument that connects the First Five Year Plan of 1953 to the crackdown of June 1989.

**Model answer:** The argument is about whether a poor agrarian country should develop by importing a model that requires technical expertise and managerial hierarchy, or by mobilising its own population under political direction - and, relatedly, whether authority should rest on knowledge or on political commitment. In 1953 China adopted the Soviet heavy-industry model with Russian cash, equipment and advisers, and Mao began to doubt whether China was suited to it. In 1957 the same tension appeared institutionally when cadres feared that the new class of technicians and engineers would threaten their authority, and the Hundred Flowers campaign was an attempt to manage it that ended in repression. In 1958 the Great Leap Forward supplied Mao's alternative - communes, small rural factories, labour-intensive development, something not based on Russian experience. Between 1963 and 1966 the rightists, including Liu Shaoqi and Deng Xiaoping, argued for incentives, wage differentials, larger private plots and an expert managerial class on the Russian model, and the Maoists rejected this as the revisionism they condemned among the Russians. The Cultural Revolution was the defeat of that argument by mobilisation. From June 1978 Deng implemented it: bonuses, piece-rates, profit-sharing, foreign loans, private companies, household plots and membership of the IMF and World Bank. The Democracy Wall of 1979, the dispersals of 1987 and June 1989 then settled the remaining question by force - expertise and incentives were admitted, and political pluralism was not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading 1978 as a break with everything that preceded it. The reform programme is the position that had been stated and defeated inside the party fifteen years earlier, and one of its principal authors, Deng Xiaoping, had been among the doubters about the Great Leap Forward and a target of the Cultural Revolution. Continuity of argument, not discontinuity of policy, is what the period demonstrates.

## Block C - the Asian comparison, Lessons 19 to 22

### Cumulative check C1 - five regimes, five refutations

**Question:** For each of the five communist regimes taught in this session, state the generalisation it refutes.

**Model answer:** China refutes the claim that communism could win only by force of arms: it won by out-governing, through a restrained and locally varied land policy, well-disciplined armies and honest and fair administration, against an incumbent ruined by corruption and inflation. North Korea refutes the claim that Asian communism was Chinese in origin or timing: the Democratic People's Republic was proclaimed in 1948, before the communist victory in China, by a Soviet-trained leader installed under Soviet protection after a division proposed by the Americans along the 38th parallel. Vietnam refutes the claim that communism competed with nationalism: Ho Chi Minh was a committed nationalist before he became a committed communist, and the suppression of protest by the French produced a rush of support for the unified Vietnamese communist party he helped to found in February 1930. Cambodia refutes the claim that the Chinese model was simply intensified elsewhere: the Khmer Rouge attempted immediate total collectivisation where even Mao had taken years, and abolished money, private property, shops, markets, schools, hospitals and monasteries, which China did not. Laos refutes the claim that the category itself is stable: having abandoned socialist economics in the mid-1980s in favour of family-run farms, market incentives and private investment while retaining full party control, the government could just as well be called simply a one-party state. Together they establish that Asian communism was locally produced rather than exported.

**Misconception to avoid:** Arranging the five on a single spectrum from moderate to extreme. They differ in origin, sponsor, timing, social base, economic policy and constitutional principle, and a spectrum implies one variable when there are at least six. The decisive evidence against a single Asian communism is not any regime's position on a scale but the fact that these states fought each other in December 1978 and February 1979.

### Cumulative check C2 - the boundary test

**Question:** A stem asks you to examine the spread and effects of communism in Asia after 1945. List what you would include from this topic, what you would hand to other owners, and why.

**Model answer:** Include: the Chinese revolution and the reasons for the communist victory in 1949; the transformation of Chinese society through land reform, collectivisation and the communes; the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution as the two distinct Mao-era campaigns; the reform turn after 1978 and the limit enforced at the Democracy Wall and in 1989; the establishment of the North Korean regime by division and Soviet sponsorship in 1948; the Vietnamese fusion of communism with anti-colonial nationalism from February 1930 to reunification in 1976 and the Doi Moi turn of December 1986; the Khmer Rouge period and the Vietnamese intervention; the Lao case and its definitional question; and the Sino-Soviet split with the wars of 1978-79. Hand over: the Korean War of 1950-3 and the Vietnam War of 1961-75, because those are Cold War theatres owned by the Cold War topic, which holds containment, alliances, deterrence and détente; the end of the European empires, because the causes and routes of decolonisation are owned by the decolonisation topic and this session uses the Indochinese colonial condition only as the circumstance that produced its communist parties; the events of 1989-91 in Europe and the post-1991 order, owned by the end-of-Cold-War topic; and the definition of communism as a political philosophy, owned by the topic that holds political philosophies. The reason for each handover is that the other owner holds the causal analysis, and duplicating it costs words without adding argument, while the regimes on either side of those wars and empires are owned here.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that observing a boundary means losing marks for omission. The boundary is about causal ownership, not about prohibition of mention: an answer may and should say that the Korean and Vietnam wars occurred and what they did to the regimes concerned, while declining to narrate their conduct. Mentioning the handover explicitly - that the war's own causes and course belong to the Cold War analysis - reads as control rather than as gap.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions are original. ⚠️ Because this topic owns no verified previous-year question, they are constructed to cover the eleven probable framings recorded by the two canonical owners and by the core account. ⚠️ Each model answer is written to the stated ceiling and should be read as a demonstration of structure and evidence density, not as a passage to be memorised.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Examine why the revolution of 1911 failed to produce a stable political order in China.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Examine* - identify the components of the failure and inspect their causes; a narrative of events will not satisfy it.

**Model answer (150-word ceiling).** The 1911 revolution removed a dynasty without creating a state. Armed force had been provincialised since the Taiping Rebellion, when ineffective government troops were replaced by regional armies the Ch'ing did not control, and provinces had asserted independence of Beijing ever since. The rising among soldiers at Wuchang in October 1911 therefore produced abandonment rather than conquest: most provinces simply declared independence, so no national organisation was built in the act of revolution. The revolutionaries held legitimacy but no army, and exchanged the republic for Yuan Shikai's presidency in return for Puyi's abdication; Yuan then ruled as a military dictator from 1912 to 1915. Fragmentation followed in the Warlord Era of 1916-28, while Japan exploited the vacuum through the Twenty-One Demands of January 1915. The revolution was irreversibly successful against the dynastic principle and wholly unsuccessful at state-building, converting China's question from which ideology to who could govern.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the mark here is earned by the sentence about armed force being provincialised in the 1860s. Most scripts begin in 1911; a script that establishes the pre-condition explains why the collapse was fast and the reconstruction impossible, and that single causal move separates the top band from the middle.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"The Nationalist government achieved only one of Sun Yat-sen's three principles." Discuss.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Discuss* - state the claim's truth, its limits and the reason for the pattern; a verdict without the reason will be incomplete.

**Model answer (150-word ceiling).** The claim is broadly correct and its reason is structural. Sun summarised his aims as nationalism, democracy, and land reform or the people's livelihood. Chiang could claim nationalism: the Northern March from Canton in 1926 took Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking by 1927 and Beijing in 1928, bringing the warlords under control. Democracy was not attempted, and Sun's own formulation permitted delay, since the people were to rule only after being educated for self-government. Land reform was not attempted either, because the government relied on the support of wealthy landowners, and the principle was in any case vague: Sun sought redistribution and rent restraint while opposing confiscation of landlords' property. The qualification is that there was some limited progress with schools and roads. The pattern is explained by coalition: the achievable principle required only force, and the two abandoned principles would have required action against the regime's own supporters.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the discriminator is the sentence explaining *why* the pattern occurred rather than merely recording it. Note also the deliberate qualification about schools and roads: a 10-mark answer that concedes a small point correctly reads as controlled, while one that asserts total failure reads as rhetorical.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Discuss the causes of the communist victory in China in 1949.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Discuss* - weigh competing causes rather than listing them, and reach a supported judgement.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** Victory was not inevitable: in 1945 many observers, especially in the United States, expected Chiang to win, and the Americans helped the Nationalists occupy territories Japan had held. The outcome is best explained comparatively, as two administrations.

The communists built capacity. After urban insurrections failed against Nationalist strength, Mao changed strategy to winning mass support among the peasants. The Long March of 1934-5 preserved the leadership, and the Second United Front after the Sian incident left the party secure in Shensi while Nationalist forces were quickly defeated in eastern China. Base areas grew from five controlling 12 million people in 1937 to nineteen controlling 100 million by 1945. Land policy varied by locality - confiscation and redistribution, or simply rent restriction - and armies were well disciplined and administration honest and fair.

The Nationalists lost capacity. Administration was inefficient and corrupt, with much American aid reaching officials' pockets; paying for war by printing money produced galloping inflation that ruined many of the middle class; armies were poorly paid and allowed to loot, and troops deserted; attempts at terror alienated more areas; and Chiang's refusal to order retreats left scattered armies surrounded, surrendering without resistance at Beijing and Shanghai.

External factors were secondary: Soviet obstruction in Manchuria let communist guerrillas in, yet domestic conditions were decisive.

In 1948 the communists abandoned guerrilla warfare and challenged directly, and the Nationalist armies disintegrated. In a country whose central question since 1911 was who could govern, one side demonstrated that it could.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** two features carry the marks. First, the opening sentence on non-inevitability, which signals that the candidate is not reading backwards from the result. Second, inflation, which is the single most underrated item in the standard list because it destroyed the regime's own administrative class rather than its opponents.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Assess the Great Leap Forward of 1958-61, indicating how the evidence for its human cost should be handled.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Assess* - deliver a weighted judgement; the second clause makes historiographical method part of the demand, so an answer without it is incomplete.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The Leap had a defensible diagnosis and an indefensible method. Mao judged that agriculture was not providing the required food and that China was unsuited to Soviet-style heavy industrialisation, and sought something not based on Russian experience. Its instruments were communes of up to 75,000 people, divided into brigades and work teams with elected councils, running farms and factories and performing most local government functions; and a shift to small rural factories, with Mao speaking of 600,000 backyard furnaces.

It failed through interacting causes: opposition to the communes, bad harvests in 1959-61, the withdrawal of all Russian aid after the breach with Moscow, and cadre inexperience, producing hardship in 1959-63.

The evidence must be handled by attribution. Statistics emerging later suggested some 20 million may have died prematurely, especially in the famine of 1959-60. Frank Dikötter, using archives declassified around 2008, puts premature deaths at at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962, of whom he estimates two to three million were tortured to death or summarily killed. These are different authors, periods and methods; they must not be averaged or merged into a range.

A ledger exists on the other side, but its provenance matters: the production and food-security claims are official, and the same archives showed such accounts exaggerated achievements. The infant-mortality fall from 203 per thousand in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s, and improvement in women's position, stand better. Jonathan Fenby judged in 2012 that Mao had brought the country to its knees.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** this is the one question on the topic where method is explicitly examined, and the marks sit in the refusal to average. Note also that the ledger is introduced with its provenance discounted rather than either suppressed or accepted, which demonstrates that the candidate can handle a source that is simultaneously useful and interested.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Mao made modern China, but he also deeply damaged it." Critically examine.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Critically examine* - test both halves of the proposition against evidence and state the relation between them; two lists side by side will not satisfy "critically".

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** Both halves are true, and the interesting question is how they are related.

He made modern China. A state that by 1900 had lost its subsistence margin, its coastal jurisdiction, its monopoly of force and its standing was unified, sovereign and centrally governed. Communications were restored and inflation brought under control within a few years - the two failures that had destroyed the previous regime. Land reform destroyed the landlord class as a social force; by 1956 about 95 per cent of surviving peasants were in collective farms; education and welfare spread; infant mortality fell from 203 per thousand births in 1949 to 84 by the end of the 1960s; and the position of women definitely improved.

He damaged it. The Great Leap Forward showed the danger of ideological overreach, with mortality estimates ranging from some 20 million premature deaths in statistics emerging later to Dikötter's at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962. The Cultural Revolution shattered educational, administrative and social stability, and the Party's own 1981 Resolution called it a grave "Left" error for which Mao was chiefly responsible.

The relation is structural, not arithmetical. The same capacity did both: only a state able to collectivise a continent in six years could impose a famine of that scale or close a nation's schools. The mechanism that converted capacity into catastrophe is identifiable - Maoist politics repeatedly blurred criticism, disloyalty and class war, so failure was read as insufficient commitment and produced intensification rather than correction.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the closing paragraph is the whole difference between a 20-mark answer and an extended 15-mark one. Stating the mechanism that links achievement to catastrophe - the disabling of error correction - converts two descriptive lists into a single causal argument, and it is the move that examiners reward in "critically examine".

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Communism in Asia was locally produced rather than exported from China." Evaluate with reference to at least three cases.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Evaluate* - judge the proposition, including what is right in the opposing view; three cases is a floor, and a survey of five will lose depth.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The proposition is well supported, with one genuine qualification.

Korea refutes export decisively by date and sponsor. The Democratic People's Republic was proclaimed in 1948, before the communist victory in China, after Soviet troops entered the north following the declaration of war on 8 August 1945 and a Soviet-trained Kim Il-sung emerged under their protection; the division at the 38th parallel was proposed by the Americans, and Chinese recognition came only in 1949.

Vietnam refutes it by origin. Ho Chi Minh helped found the unified Vietnamese communist party in February 1930 after French suppression of protest brought a rush of support, and he was a nationalist before becoming a communist. The movement's legitimacy came from war against France, culminating at Dien Bien Phu in 1954, not from Chinese tutelage.

Cambodia refutes it by content. The Khmer Rouge emptied the cities, abolished money, private property, markets, schools, hospitals and monasteries, and attempted immediate total collectivisation - where even Mao had taken years - alongside a campaign against the educated. Leifer records around a million dead from execution, starvation and disease; some estimates reach two million.

The qualification is that Chinese influence was real: China backed Pol Pot, and both Vietnam and Laos adopted market reform explicitly following the Chinese example, Vietnam through Doi Moi in December 1986.

But influence is not export, and the decisive evidence is that these states fought each other: Vietnam invaded Kampuchea in December 1978 and China attacked Vietnam in February 1979.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the qualification paragraph is what makes this an evaluation rather than an assertion. Conceding that Chinese influence was real, and then distinguishing influence from export before closing on the 1978-79 wars, demonstrates the capacity to hold a position against its strongest counter-evidence - which is precisely what "evaluate" demands and what most scripts on this question omit.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ These are the error families that recur in scripts on this topic. ⚠️ Each entry names the error, gives the correction from evidence taught above, and states the diagnostic that prevents recurrence.

## Error family A - dates and their phenomena

1. ⚠️ **Error:** dating the fragmentation of China to 1911. ✅ **Correction:** the Warlord Era is 1916-28; the process began after the Taiping Rebellion of 1850-64, when the dynasty became dependent on regional armies it did not control. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask what each date dates - a collapse, a process or an era.
2. ⚠️ **Error:** giving one date for the Cultural Revolution and treating the other as wrong. ✅ **Correction:** 1966-9 dates the mass upheaval and 1966-76 dates the political era to Mao's death; both are used by reliable sources. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** name the phenomenon in the same clause as the date.
3. ⚠️ **Error:** dating the Sino-Soviet breach to a single dramatic event. ✅ **Correction:** relations deteriorated steadily after 1956, following de-Stalinisation; no 1964 rupture is supported. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** where a source says "steadily after", do not convert it into a point.
4. ⚠️ **Error:** dating Deng's leadership from 1976. ✅ **Correction:** the policy phase began in June 1978; Hua Guofeng was forced out in 1980 and Deng became undisputed leader in June 1981. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** separate the death of a predecessor from the consolidation of a successor.
5. ⚠️ **Error:** placing the founding of the North Korean state after the Chinese revolution. ✅ **Correction:** the Democratic People's Republic was proclaimed in 1948, before the communist victory in China, and was recognised by China in 1949.

## Error family B - figures and attribution

6. ⚠️ **Error:** "the Great Leap Forward killed 45 million". ✅ **Correction:** Dikötter, working in archives declassified around 2008, puts premature deaths at at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962; statistics emerging earlier suggested some 20 million may have died prematurely, especially in the 1959-60 famine. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** if the sentence has no author in it, it is not yet finished.
7. ⚠️ **Error:** writing "20 to 45 million" as a range. ✅ **Correction:** these are separate estimates over different periods by different authors; a range implies a single estimate with bounds. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** never join two authors with a dash.
8. ⚠️ **Error:** asserting a single Tiananmen death toll. ✅ **Correction:** the records diverge within a single account - the army killing hundreds in one place, at least a thousand students killed and many later executed in another. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** report the divergence; it is itself a finding.
9. ⚠️ **Error:** "250,000 communists were murdered in 1927". ✅ **Correction:** thousands of communists, trade union and peasant leaders were massacred, with some estimates putting the total as high as 250,000. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** retain the phrase "some estimates".
10. ⚠️ **Error:** merging the Cambodian and Vietnamese mortality figures. ✅ **Correction:** Leifer's around one million and the killing-fields estimates of up to two million concern Khmer Rouge Cambodia; Grenville's at least 2.5 million concerns the longest war in Asia. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask what each figure counts before using it.
11. ⚠️ **Error:** "600,000 backyard furnaces produced useless steel". ✅ **Correction:** Mao talked of 600,000 backyard furnaces; no output figure is supported here. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** distinguish a stated target from a recorded outcome.

## Error family C - mechanisms and causation

12. ⚠️ **Error:** "imperialism destroyed China". ✅ **Correction:** the subsistence crisis of 1741-1841 and the Taiping Rebellion were internal, and the resulting chaos encouraged foreigners to force their way in; foreign pressure was an accelerant and delegitimiser. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** check which came first in the source.
13. ⚠️ **Error:** "the Japanese invasion caused the communist victory". ✅ **Correction:** it is recorded as the crucial factor among five, and it worked by allocating a losing conventional task to the Nationalists and a favourable irregular one to the communists. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** name the mechanism, not just the factor.
14. ⚠️ **Error:** "the communists won because they were more popular". ✅ **Correction:** six distinct mechanisms operated - organisational survival, social-base construction, patriotic legitimacy, administrative contrast, military conversion in 1948 and adversary collapse. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** popularity is an outcome; name its producers.
15. ⚠️ **Error:** "the Cultural Revolution was just a power struggle". ✅ **Correction:** there was a great public debate between 1963 and 1966 about incentives, wage differentials, private plots and an expert managerial class; the power dimension and the argument reinforced each other. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** in a party-state, a defeat on policy is a defeat on power.
16. ⚠️ **Error:** "Tiananmen ended the reform era". ✅ **Correction:** repression and economic reform proceeded together, and the leadership justified the former as a condition of the latter. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** check whether the policy changed or only the personnel.

## Error family D - categories and comparisons

17. ⚠️ **Error:** treating "the communist bloc" as one actor. ✅ **Correction:** Vietnam invaded Kampuchea in December 1978 and China attacked Vietnam in February 1979, while the Soviet Union backed Vietnam and China backed the Khmer Rouge. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask whether the members ever fought each other.
18. ⚠️ **Error:** placing the five Asian regimes on a single moderate-to-extreme spectrum. ✅ **Correction:** they differ in origin, sponsor, timing, social base, economic policy and constitutional principle. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** a spectrum implies one variable.
19. ⚠️ **Error:** equating Chinese and Soviet collectivisation and famine. ✅ **Correction:** the evidence bases, estimates, chronologies and mechanisms differ, and only the Chinese figures with their authors are stated here. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** similar policy does not license merged evidence.
20. ⚠️ **Error:** describing the Khmer Rouge as Maoism intensified. ✅ **Correction:** even Mao had taken years to reach the stage they attempted at once, and the abolition of money, markets and schooling has no Chinese parallel. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** compare programmes, not adjectives.
21. ⚠️ **Error:** using "cadre" as a synonym for official or soldier. ✅ **Correction:** cadres organised the masses politically and economically; collectivisation of the farms was carried out by them. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** define the term on first use.

## Error family E - boundaries and scope

22. ⚠️ **Error:** narrating the Korean War or the Vietnam War inside an answer on Asian communism. ✅ **Correction:** both are Cold War theatres owned elsewhere; this session teaches the regimes on either side and the consequences for them. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask whether the question needs the war's causes or its results.
23. ⚠️ **Error:** defining communism as a political philosophy in an answer on the Chinese revolution. ✅ **Correction:** the doctrine is owned by the political-philosophies topic; the historical record of communist regimes in China and Asia is owned here. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** is the demand "what is it?" or "what did it do?".
24. ⚠️ **Error:** extending a history answer into current Chinese capabilities, growth or intentions. ✅ **Correction:** the single current item in this session is dated and limited, and no contemporary indicator is asserted anywhere. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** if a claim would change next year, a history answer does not need it.
25. ⚠️ **Error:** narrating the Soviet collapse in the reform-sequencing comparison. ✅ **Correction:** only the comparison drawn by the Chinese leadership itself is used; the European events belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** cite the comparison, not the narrative.

## Map 3 - The argument map on Mao's legacy

```text
CLAIM: "Mao made modern China but deeply damaged it."

SUPPORT FOR "MADE"                    SUPPORT FOR "DAMAGED"
- unification and sovereignty         - Great Leap: estimates of some 20m
- communications restored               premature deaths (statistics emerging
- inflation controlled                  later) and at least 45m 1958-62
- landlord class destroyed as a          (Dikotter), incl. 2-3m tortured to
  social force                           death or summarily killed
- ~95% of surviving peasants in       - Cultural Revolution: education,
  collective farms by 1956              administration and social stability
- education and welfare spread          shattered; a cohort lost
- infant mortality 203 -> 84 per      - Party's own 1981 Resolution: a grave
  thousand (1949 -> late 1960s)         "Left" error, Mao chiefly responsible
- position of women improved          - Fenby (2012): brought the country to
                                        its knees; virtually bankrupt in 1976

OBJECTION TO THE "MADE" COLUMN: the production and food-security claims are
the official account's, and the same archives showed official accounts
exaggerated achievements.
REPLY: the infant-mortality series and the social changes stand on a
different footing from the production claims and should be used, with the
production claims marked as attributed.

OBJECTION TO BALANCING: the two columns cannot be netted.
REPLY: they are not competing verdicts but the same verdict. THE SAME
CAPACITY DID BOTH. The converting mechanism is that Maoist politics blurred
criticism, disloyalty and class war, so failure produced intensification
rather than correction.

QUALIFIED CONCLUSION: unity, independence and revolutionary purpose at huge
human and institutional cost when campaigns displaced administrative
rationality.
```

     |      Feb 1979 China attacks Vietnam; withdraws after three weeks

     |
