## Map 5 - Five Asian communisms, sorted by six variables

| Variable | ✅ China | ✅ North Korea | ✅ Vietnam | ✅ Cambodia | ✅ Laos |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Origin | Internal revolution | External division and Soviet sponsorship | Anti-colonial war | Civil war after coup and bombing | Alliance with the Vietnamese movement |
| Date of power | 1949 | 1948 | 1945 north; 1976 reunified | April 1975 | End of 1975 |
| Social base | Peasantry, by strategic decision | Soviet-backed party and army | Nationalist-communist fusion from February 1930 | Rural cadres; urban population expelled | Party allied to Vietnam |
| Economic path | Command, then reform from June 1978 | Heavy industry and armaments; a 2002 reform reversed by 2005 | Command extended to the south; Doi Moi December 1986 | Immediate total collectivisation; money and markets abolished | Collectives, then family farms from the mid-1980s |
| Constitutional principle | Collective leadership after 1981 | Hereditary succession from 1980 | Party congress leadership | Personal rule by Pol Pot | Party monopoly with a 1991 constitution |
| Conflict with communist neighbours | Attacked Vietnam February 1979 | Lost Soviet aid at the end of the 1950s and Chinese aid in 1966 | Invaded Kampuchea December 1978; attacked by China | Overthrown by Vietnam; then backed by China | Aligned throughout |

## Map 6 - Retrieval drills

⚠️ Use these as timed recall exercises rather than as reading. ⚠️ Each should be answerable in under a minute once the session has been worked through.

**Drill 1 - the date ladder.** Recite in order with the phenomenon named: 1839-42, 1850-64, 1894-5, 1898-1900, 1904-5, October 1911, 1912-15, 1916-28, 4 May 1919, 1921, 1924-27, 1926-28, 1927, 1931, 1934-35, 1936, 1937, 1945, 1948, 1 October 1949.

**Drill 2 - the five reasons.** Name the five recorded reasons why the communists gained support, in order, and identify which is recorded as crucial.

**Drill 3 - the six mechanisms.** Name the six mechanisms of the 1949 victory and give one piece of evidence for each.

**Drill 4 - the two-stage land policy.** State both stages, the target group of each and the political effect of the second.

**Drill 5 - the four-step political mechanism.** Give the four steps and the four episodes, with the outcome of each.

**Drill 6 - the mortality table.** For each of the four episodes of mass death, state the figure or figures with author and period, without merging any of them.

**Drill 7 - the escalation ladder.** Recite the four registers of the Sino-Soviet quarrel, then the proxy war, the 1984 list and the two closure dates.

**Drill 8 - the five refutations.** For each of the five Asian regimes, state the generalisation it refutes in one sentence.

**Drill 9 - the boundary list.** Name the four owner boundaries this topic observes and the material each holds.

**Drill 10 - the five master theses.** Recite them from memory; each should take about fifteen seconds.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The crisis of capacity, 1839-1900

✅ Manchu or Ch'ing rule from the mid-seventeenth century; troubled period from the 1840s to 1949. ✅ Population 140 million (1741) to 410 million (1841); subsistence failure; banditry; disorder invites foreign entry. ✅ Opium Wars 1839-42; Hong Kong ceded; trade at certain ports; eventually rights and concessions in about eighty ports and towns. ✅ The Shanghai park sign never existed; the story expressed real outrage. ✅ Taiping Rebellion 1850-64, partly Christian religious and partly political reform, aiming at a "Heavenly Kingdom of Great Peace"; defeated by new regional armies, not by ineffective government troops - the decisive blow, leaving dependence on forces the dynasty did not control and beginning provincial assertion that culminated in the Warlord Era. ✅ War with Japan 1894-5; Formosa ceded. ✅ By end-1898 Britain, Germany, France and Russia hold leased areas treated as no longer Chinese. ✅ Boxer Rising 1898-1900 crushed by an international army; Empress Tz'u-hsi pays massive compensation. ✅ Further losses to Japan after 1904-5. ✅ Students educated abroad return with revolutionary and westernizing ideas; Sun Yat-sen wants a democratic state modelled on the USA. ⚠️ Causal order: internal crisis first, foreign pressure as accelerant and delegitimiser.

## 2. 1911 and the capture of the republic

✅ Late Qing reforms, promised democracy and elected provincial assemblies push provinces further from an unpopular centre. ✅ Rising among soldiers at Wuchang, October 1911; most provinces declare independence of Beijing. ✅ Child emperor Puyi, aged five; the court calls on retired general Yuan Shikai, former Northern Army commander, still influential with the generals. ✅ Bargain: Yuan becomes first president; Puyi abdicates; the Manchu dynasty ends. ✅ Yuan rules as a military dictator with army support, 1912-15. ✅ Japan demands Germany's Shantung rights at the outbreak of the war; Twenty-One Demands, January 1915, in five groups, the first covering Shantung concessions including railway rights. ⚠️ Assets exchanged: legitimacy for force. ⚠️ Verdict: irreversible against the dynastic principle, wholly unsuccessful at state-building.

## 3. Warlordism and the intellectual awakening, 1916-28

✅ Warlord Era 1916-28: generals seize provinces; the Kuomintang tries to govern China and control them while they fight each other. ⚠️ Definition: governance without a government; the entry ticket to national politics is an army, which is why both parties became part-military organisations. ✅ May Fourth: named from student protests in Beijing on 4 May 1919, triggered by the transfer of Germany's Shandong rights to Japan at the peace settlement; China did not sign the treaty. ✅ Its supporters were modern progressive thinkers who later opposed a revival of traditional values as a return to an oppressive imperial past. ⚠️ Effect: humiliation became a programme with a double target - foreign encroachment and the domestic order that permitted it. ✅ Beijing University a centre of Marxist studies; Mao a library assistant there. ⚠️ Marxism entered through universities, not industry, so the later peasant turn was a departure from imported theory.

## 4. The Nationalists and the Three Principles

✅ The party was reorganised along communist lines and built its own army. ✅ Sun's Three Principles: nationalism - rid China of foreign influence, build a strong united power respected abroad; democracy - rule by the people rather than warlords, after education for self-government; land reform or "the people's livelihood" - long-term development, redistribution to peasants and rent restraint, but opposed to confiscation of landlords' property, and described in the source as vague. ✅ Sun died 1925 with little progress, mainly because he was not himself a general; he relied on alliances with sympathetic warlords and could not exercise authority outside the south. ✅ Chiang Kai-shek: military training in Japan before the First World War; Moscow 1923 studying the Communist Party and the Red Army; head of the Whampoa Military Academy near Canton in 1924, set up with Russian cash, arms and advisers; not a communist, more right-wing than Sun, sympathies with businessmen and landowners; removed left-wingers early but continued the alliance for the time being. ⚠️ Each party's decisive asset was the other's decisive weakness.

## 5. The Communist Party's foundation and Mao's formation

✅ Founded 1921; at first mostly intellectuals with very little military strength, which explains willingness to work with the Nationalists; Mao present at the founding meeting. ✅ Mao born Hunan 1893, in south-central China, son of a prosperous peasant farmer; farm work; teacher training; library assistant in Beijing; skilful trade union and peasant association organiser back in Hunan. ✅ First United Front 1924-1927 against warlordism. ✅ Communist contribution: peasant support drawn by land promises, and the Shanghai workers' rising organised by Zhou En-lai, a member of both parties. ✅ Several communist insurrections in industrial towns had already failed because of Nationalist strength. ⚠️ Two reasons: a small concentrated industrial class, and cities being where state coercion is strongest. ⚠️ The alliance was a correct decision by a weak organisation with a catastrophic ending.

## 6. Reunification and rupture, 1926-28

✅ Northern March from Canton 1926; Hankow, Shanghai and Nanking by 1927; Beijing 1928. ✅ Success drew on massive peasant support attracted by communist land promises. ✅ During 1927 Chiang decided the communists were too powerful - in areas where they were strong, landlords were attacked and land seized; time to destroy an embarrassing ally. ✅ All communists expelled; "purification movement" massacred thousands of communists, trade union and peasant leaders; some estimates put the total murdered as high as 250,000. ✅ Warlords under control; Chiang the military and political leader of China. ✅ The Kuomintang government proved a great disappointment: nationalism achieved; no moves towards democracy or land reform because of reliance on wealthy landowners; some limited progress with schools and roads. ⚠️ A centre rebuilt without a relationship to the villages.

## 7. The rural turn and the Long March

✅ Strategy changed to winning mass support among the peasants; Mao responsible. ✅ 1931: Mao chairman of the Central Executive Committee; Chinese Soviet Republic proclaimed at Juichin; first All-China Congress of Soviets on 7 November 1931 with delegates from fifteen soviet areas. ✅ Five extermination campaigns 1930-34; base in the mountains between Hunan and Kiangsi; the Red Army built up. ✅ Early 1934 encirclement; breakthrough October 1934; almost 100,000 set out; about 6,000 miles in 368 days. ✅ Edgar Snow, quoted: 18 mountain ranges, 5 snow-capped, 24 rivers, 12 provinces, 62 cities, armies of 10 provincial warlords. ✅ 20,000 survivors reached Yenan in Shensi, the last surviving communist base, controlled by the guerrilla leader Kao Kang; the Shensi communists accepted Mao not entirely willingly; Mao controlled Shensi and Kansu. ✅ Jung Chang and Jon Halliday (2005): the march was vastly exaggerated and the breakout permitted by Chiang; historians generally conceded exaggeration but rejected the reading as "more fantasy than fact". ✅ During the following ten years the communists gained support while the Nationalists lost popularity. ⚠️ Four functions: survival, leadership settlement, strategic relocation, founding narrative. ⚠️ A military disaster that functioned as a political rescue.

## 8. The five recorded reasons for rural support

✅ (1) Nationalist inefficiency and corruption; little reform on offer; time spent on industrialists, bankers and landowners; no effective attempt to organise mass support. ✅ (2) Factory conditions unimproved despite laws such as those against child labour in textile mills; laws often unapplied; widespread bribery of inspectors; Chiang unwilling to offend industrial supporters. ✅ (3) Early-1930s droughts and bad harvests with widespread rural famine, while rice and wheat were hoarded in cities by profiteering merchants; high taxes; forced labour - against a communist land policy in two stages: seizure and redistribution of rich landlords' estates in the south, then, after the truce during the Japanese war, rent restriction and a small plot even for the poorest, winning smaller landowners as well. ✅ (4) The New Life Movement of 1934, claimed as a unique secular, rational and modern Chinese version of Confucianism to revive "innate morality"; Rana Mitter records that prescriptions such as not spitting in the street and queuing in an orderly fashion seemed trivial against the larger issues of national coherence; May Fourth supporters called it a backward step. ✅ (5) No effective resistance to the Japanese - the crucial factor.

## 9. The Japanese war and the Second United Front

✅ Japan occupied Manchuria 1931 and prepared to take neighbouring northern provinces. ✅ Chiang seemed to think destroying the communists more important than resisting Japan and moved into south Shensi in 1936. ✅ Sian incident: taken prisoner by his own troops, mostly Manchurians incensed at the invasion; at first unwilling; agreed to a fresh alliance and national front only after Zhou En-lai came to see him. ✅ Second United Front 1937-1945; extermination campaigns ceased; the party was secure in Shensi. ✅ 1937 full-scale war: Nationalist forces quickly defeated, most of eastern China occupied, Chiang retreating westwards; communists undefeated in Shensi presented themselves as patriotic nationalists leading effective guerrilla warfare in the north, winning massive support among peasants and middle classes. ✅ Base areas: five controlling 12 million (1937) to nineteen controlling 100 million (1945). ✅ Chiang admired Germany's military tradition and committed China to the Allied side only after Stalingrad 1942-3; Stalin refused to attend meetings at which Chiang was present. ✅ January 1943: rights and concessions renounced, Britain keeping Hong Kong; Manchuria and Formosa promised back; China treated as an equal great power and promised a permanent Security Council seat. ✅ Japanese offensive from Manchuria reached the Indochina frontier, cutting the south-east coast from the interior. ✅ August 1945 atomic bombs and surrender; the Chinese contribution was to keep hundreds of thousands of Japanese troops bogged down. ✅ Jay Taylor's revision: given the enormity of his problems Chiang governed with reasonable skill and understood the challenges better than his American advisers; Stilwell called him "Peanut" and Alanbrooke "a cross between a pine-marten and a ferret".

## 10. The civil-war outcome, 1945-49

✅ Victory was still not inevitable; many observers expected Chiang to win. ✅ Americans helped the Nationalists occupy former Japanese areas except Manchuria, taken by the Russians days before the war ended, where they obstructed the Nationalists and admitted communist guerrillas. ✅ 1948: communist armies abandoned guerrilla warfare for direct challenge; Nationalist armies disintegrated under direct pressure. ✅ January 1949 Beijing taken; Chiang and his remaining forces fled to Taiwan; 1 October 1949 the People's Republic proclaimed at Tiananmen with Mao as party Chairman and president. ✅ Communist strengths: restrained, locally varied land policy; well-disciplined armies; honest and fair administration; shrewd and dedicated leadership in Mao and Zhou En-lai; carefully prepared and tactically superior generals Lin Biao, Chu Teh and Ch'en Yi. ✅ Nationalist weaknesses: inefficient and corrupt administration with American aid reaching officials' pockets; galloping inflation from printing money, ruining many of the middle class; poorly paid armies allowed to loot; desertion under communist propaganda; terror alienating more areas; Chiang's refusal to order retreats, producing encirclement and surrender without resistance at Beijing and Shanghai. ⚠️ Six mechanisms all present. ⚠️ Thesis: one side demonstrated it could govern; the other demonstrated it could not.

## 11. State formation, land and collectivisation, 1949-56

✅ Inheritance: devastation after the civil and Japanese wars; railways, roads, canals and dykes destroyed; chronic food shortages. ✅ Agriculture transformed from small private farms to large co-operative farms like Russia's, 1950-56: land taken from large landowners and redistributed, no doubt with violence in places; by 1956 about 95 per cent of surviving peasants in collective farms with joint ownership. ✅ Competing accounts of the violence: some sources mention as many as two million killed; Jack Gray in 1970 described remarkable attention to legality and minimum physical violence; Frank Dikötter, using archives declassified around the 2008 Beijing Olympics, describes land taken from ordinary peasants and given to party activists with considerable violence, torturing and execution, quoting a Hebei document. ✅ The archives also showed official accounts exaggerated achievements and toned down or omitted unpleasant events. ✅ Industry: most businesses nationalised; Five Year Plan from 1953 on heavy industry with Russian cash, equipment and advisers; some success; communications restored and inflation controlled. ✅ Before it was complete Mao doubted whether China was suited to that sort of heavy industrialisation. ⚠️ Redistribution and collectivisation are separate operations whose social meanings are opposed.

## 12. The Hundred Flowers campaign, 1957

✅ Industrialisation produced a vast new class of technicians and engineers; cadres - who organised the masses politically and economically and carried out collectivisation - feared these experts would threaten their authority. ✅ The government, pleased with progress, invited open discussion; "Let a hundred flowers bloom and a hundred schools of thought contend". ✅ Critics attacked the cadres for incompetence and over-enthusiasm, the government for over-centralisation and the party for being undemocratic, some suggesting opposition parties. ✅ Mao hurriedly called the campaign off and clamped down, insisting his policies were right. ✅ It showed how much opposition remained and convinced him a drive was needed to consolidate socialism - hence the Great Leap Forward of 1958. ⚠️ Four-step mechanism, recurring in 1978-9, 1986-7 and 1989.

## 13. The Great Leap Forward, 1958-61

✅ Rationale: something new and different, not based on Russian experience; increase output; adapt industry to Chinese conditions. ✅ Communes: larger than collective farms, up to 75,000 people, brigades and work teams with elected councils, running farms and factories, performing most local government functions; the illustrative commune of 1965 with 30,000 people, a third children at school or in creches, a third housewives or elderly, the rest the workforce including 32 graduates and 43 technicians; family profit shares and small private plots. ✅ Industry: small rural factories for agricultural machinery; Mao talked of 600,000 backyard furnaces; communes built roads, canals, dams, reservoirs and irrigation channels. ✅ Failure: opposition to the communes; bad harvests 1959-61; withdrawal of all Russian aid; cadre inexperience; hardship 1959-63. ✅ Estimates: statistics emerging later suggested some 20 million may have died prematurely, especially in the famine of 1959-60; Frank Dikötter puts premature deaths at at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962, of whom two to three million were tortured to death or summarily killed. ✅ Mao resigned as Chairman of the People's Congress, succeeded by Liu Shaoqi, and remained Chairman of the party. ✅ Ledger from the official account: substantial production increases by the early 1970s; the population fed without further famine; communes as an efficient unit of local government; the labour-intensive strategic choice; education and welfare spread; infant mortality 203 to 84 per thousand; a definite improvement in the position of women. ✅ Jonathan Fenby (2012): Mao had brought the country to its knees and China was virtually bankrupt in 1976.

## 14. The Cultural Revolution and the Party's verdict

✅ Purpose: keep the revolution and the Great Leap on a pure Marxist-Leninist course and hit back at an over-bureaucratic party leadership under Liu Shaoqi. ✅ Rightists 1963-66, including Liu and Deng Xiaoping, argued for incentives - piecework, wage differentials, larger private plots - and an expert managerial class on the Russian model; Deng had grave doubts about the Great Leap. ✅ Maoists rejected these as the revisionism they condemned among the Russians, taking the capitalist road; the party must avoid a privileged class exploiting the workers and must keep in touch with the masses. ✅ Mao used the party chairmanship to rouse the young; the Great Proletarian Cultural Revolution; Red Guards, mostly students, with Little Red Books; in some areas schools and later factories were closed. ✅ Dating: 1966-9 for the mass upheaval in the core account; 1966-76 in the canonical owners. ✅ Damage: education, administration and social stability badly damaged. ✅ Gang of Four tried for "evil, monstrous and unpardonable crimes" and sentenced to life imprisonment; the Central Committee Resolution condemned the campaign as a grave "Left" error for which Mao was chiefly responsible, while praising him for smashing the "counter-revolutionary Lin Biao clique". ✅ Steve Smith: the Resolution exculpated the "overwhelming majority" of leaders and underwrote a shift from a single leader to collective leadership. ⚠️ The rightist programme of 1963-66 became the reform programme of 1978.

## 15. Maoism as doctrine and practice

⚠️ Five distinctive features with their failure modes: the peasantry as revolutionary base (no method for technical development); politics in command of expertise (execution by organisers without competence); rejection of the imported model (an untested alternative at vast scale); government by mass campaign (no calibration, reversal or audit); struggle against internal privilege (every policy dispute becomes a loyalty question). ⚠️ Achievement: unification, sovereignty, administrative capacity, rural transformation, welfare and education, the position of women. ⚠️ Cost: ideological overreach in economic policy; shattered institutions; the blurring of criticism, disloyalty and class war, which disabled error correction. ✅ Historiographical sequence: Gray 1970 (closed archives); the official account (later shown to exaggerate); Dikötter from 2008 archives; Smith on the 1981 Resolution; Fenby 2012. ⚠️ Structural verdict: the capacity that unified China made both catastrophes possible. ⚠️ Doctrine boundary: the philosophy of communism is owned elsewhere; the record of communist regimes in China and Asia is owned here.

## 16. The Sino-Soviet split and the American opening

✅ Treaty of mutual assistance and friendship 1950; deterioration steadily after 1956. ✅ Doctrinal charge: rejection of peaceful coexistence and of achieving communism by methods other than violent revolution, against Lenin's ideas - hence "revisionism", revising or reinterpreting Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs. ✅ Strategic charge: anger at Khrushchev's "soft" line towards the USA. ✅ Material retaliation: reduced Soviet economic aid. ✅ Territory: nineteenth-century Russian gains north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang demanded back without success; the main bone of contention once China softened towards the USA. ✅ End of the 1970s: both vying for American support for the leadership of world communism. ✅ February 1979: China attacked Vietnam, partly for Vietnam's December 1978 invasion of Kampuchea overthrowing Pol Pot, a protégé of China, partly over the frontier; withdrawal after three weeks having "taught the Vietnamese a lesson". ✅ 1984 grievances: Afghanistan; Soviet backing of Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea; build-up on the Mongolian and Manchurian frontiers. ✅ July 1985 five-year trade and economic agreements; May 1989 formal reconciliation on Gorbachev's Beijing visit; Vietnam withdrew from Kampuchea the same year. ✅ United States: hostility since the Korean War; 1971 table-tennis invitation; UN membership October 1971; Nixon February 1972 reopening contact and producing rapprochement without formal diplomatic relations; Ford 1975; Chiang died 1975; Carter withdrew recognition of Nationalist China in 1978 and recognised the People's Republic early in 1979, with diplomatic relations effective 1 January 1979; nuclear co-operation agreement 1985. ⚠️ The communist world behaved like a states system, not a church.

## 17. The Deng transition

✅ Hua Guofeng blamed for the trade gap and inflation; the Politburo decided in 1980 that he lacked the political and organisational ability to be Chairman; Deng undisputed leader June 1981 at seventy-three. ✅ Robert Service: as hard as teak; as many demotions as promotions; his son crippled escaping maltreatment in the Cultural Revolution; no time to waste. ✅ From June 1978: revolutionary committees abolished for more democratically elected groups; confiscated property returned to surviving former capitalists; more religious freedom; greater freedom for intellectuals. ✅ Economic opening: Western technical and financial help; foreign loans and contracts; IMF and World Bank membership 1980; private industrial companies; state farms given control of planning, financing and profits; bonuses, piece-rates and profit-sharing; higher state prices and reduced taxes; record grain output 1979. ✅ Zhao Ziyang: 80 per cent rise in Sichuan industrial production in 1979; communes broken up nationwide, land still state-owned but allocated to households keeping most of the profits; December 1984 compulsory state purchase abandoned, with surplus grain, pork, cotton and vegetable prices free to fluctuate. ✅ Democracy Wall from November 1978; four demands - open criticism, non-communist representation, freedom to change jobs and travel, abolition of the communes; dissidents imprisoned for up to fifteen years; the Wall abolished November 1979; "Without the party, China will retrogress into divisions and confusions." ✅ Costs: 1984 exports up 10 per cent and imports up 38 per cent, record trade deficit of $1100 million; duties of 80 per cent on cars and 70 per cent on colour televisions and video players; inflation 22 per cent in 1986. ✅ Deng, November 1986: income per head from about £280 to near £700 by 2000 and production doubled; "To get rich is not a crime"; sweeping industrial decentralisation needed; only capitalist investment could create a prosperous modernised state; internationally, to lead a peace alliance against the ambitions of the USA and the USSR. 📰 Official release of 24 October 2025: the 20th Central Committee's fourth plenary session adopted recommendations for the 15th Five-Year Plan covering 2026-2030 - the single current item, used only for institutional continuity.

## 18. 1987, Tiananmen and the two sequencings

✅ Deng balanced reformers Zhao Ziyang and Hu Yaobang against hardliners such as Li Peng, encouraging criticism only up to a point and stopping it when party control seemed threatened. ✅ December 1986 student demonstrations supporting Deng and the Four Modernizations - agriculture, industry, science and defence - but urging a quicker pace and more democracy; dispersed after bans and a five-days' notice rule were ignored; hardliners forced Hu Yaobang's resignation; Zhao replaced him; Li Peng became prime minister demanding a clampdown. ✅ The dilemma of January 1987: choice in buying and selling but none in policies and political parties? Western observers and Gorbachev thought it impossible. ✅ Inflation reached 30 per cent in 1988-9. ✅ Protests from April 1989 demanding democracy and an end to party corruption; on 4 May Zhao treated the demands as just and allowed press reporting; demonstrations continued through Gorbachev's visit in mid-May and into June; martial law declared 20 May; the army attacked on 3-4 June with paratroopers, tanks and infantry. ✅ Casualty records diverge: "killing hundreds" in one place; "at least a thousand students were killed and many later executed" in another; the canonical owners give no figure. ✅ Protest leaders sentenced to long terms in labour camps; Zhao removed and replaced by Jiang Zemin; hardliners triumphant with Li Peng the leading figure; worldwide condemnation. ✅ Leadership justification: giving way would have caused too much disruption; one-party control was needed to supervise the transition to a "socialist market economy"; Gorbachev's simultaneous reforms failed, the party lost control, the reforms were a disaster and the USSR broke into fifteen states. ⚠️ Same diagnosis, opposite prescriptions; each leadership cited the other's outcome.

## 19. Korea

✅ Japanese occupation and rule from 1905 after the Russo-Japanese War; a strong nationalist movement. ✅ Cairo 1943: the USA, UK and China promised a united, independent Korea. ✅ USSR declared war on Japan 8 August 1945, three weeks before the surrender; Russian troops from Manchuria entered the north before the official surrender of 2 September; Soviet forces worked with Korean communists and nationalists; the Korean People's Republic proclaimed; Kim Il-sung, Soviet-trained and Soviet-backed, emerged dominant. ✅ Americans sent troops to the south and proposed the 38th parallel; Syngman Rhee emerged there. ✅ Stalin poured aid into the north and withdrew troops in 1948; the Democratic People's Republic of Korea proclaimed with Kim as premier - before the communist victory in China; recognised by China, the USSR and eastern Europe in 1949. ✅ The unification deadlock was handed to the United Nations, which undertook to organise elections. ⚠️ The war of 1950-3 belongs to the Cold War owner. ✅ The Sino-Soviet rift from 1956 left Kim pro-Soviet, then pro-Chinese, then independent of both; Soviet aid sharply reduced at the end of the 1950s and Chinese aid cut off in 1966; no development plan then reached its targets. ✅ Excessive spending on heavy industry and armaments; rapid population growth; falling living standards; recovery in the 1980s; renewed difficulty in the early 1990s. ✅ Unification policy erratic: a federal proposal in 1980; the 1983 bombing; the 1987 airliner; the 1991 joint renunciation of violence and nuclear weapons. ✅ Hereditary succession announced 1980; Kim Il-sung died 1994 aged 82; Kim Jong-il died December 2011; Kim Jong-un named Supreme Leader. ✅ 1994 crisis: economy deteriorated, population trebled since 1954, on the verge of famine, heavy nuclear and missile spending, loss of the USSR. ✅ Indicators reported by the deputy foreign minister to a UNICEF conference in May 2001 for 1993-2000: under-five mortality 27 to 48 per thousand; per capita GNP $991 to $457; vaccination 90 to 50 per cent; safe water 86 to 53 per cent. ✅ July 2002 limited reform including family-unit farming for the first time since collectivisation, reversed by summer 2005; labour camps with thousands of political prisoners. ✅ Nuclear confrontation: KEDO 1994; "axis of evil" January 2001; NPT withdrawal December 2002; weapons claims April 2003 and February 2005; claimed test October 2006; further tests 2009; uranium plant revealed 2010; Cheonan sunk March 2010 with 46 lost; Yeonpyeong shelled November 2010 with four killed; the record here ends in 2012.

## 20. Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos

✅ Indochinese Union 1887; roads, railways, schools, hospitals and a Hanoi university, but very little industrialisation and mostly poor peasants; 1930s protest unceremoniously suppressed, bringing a rush of support for the unified Vietnamese communist party Ho Chi Minh helped to found in February 1930, renamed the Indochinese Communist Party later that year; Ho was a nationalist before he was a communist. ✅ 1940 Japanese entry; a southern rising crushed by the Vichy French and the Japanese; Ho moved north and organised the Vietminh. ✅ Japan surrendered 14 August 1945; the Vietminh took Hanoi, Saigon and most large towns; the Democratic Republic proclaimed September 1945. ✅ British forces, using undisarmed Japanese troops, suppressed the Vietminh in the south to avoid encouraging decolonisation; some 50,000 French troops arrived; the Americans were appalled. ✅ Grenville: had the south been allowed to follow the north, the trauma of the longest war in Asia, which led to at least 2.5 million deaths, might have been avoided. ✅ Hostilities began with the shelling of Haiphong; the French were defeated at Dien Bien Phu in 1954; Geneva recognised North Vietnam with the area south of the 17th parallel under a commission of Canadians, Poles and Indians and elections set for July 1956. ✅ The elections never took place; the Americans backed Ngo Dinh Diem, who proclaimed the Vietnam Republic in 1955. ⚠️ The war of 1961-75 belongs to the Cold War owner. ✅ Socialist Republic proclaimed July 1976; command economics in the south failed amid sabotage, unwilling and incompetent cadres and corruption; floods, drought and population growth caused shortages; hundreds of thousands fled, including the boat people. ✅ December 1978 invasion of Kampuchea; some 200,000 troops required; February 1979 Chinese invasion and withdrawal after three weeks; trade embargo by the USA, Japan and most of Western Europe; near-total isolation by the mid-1980s. ✅ December 1986: Nguyen Van Linh general secretary; Doi Moi, moving towards the free market as the Chinese had already begun to do; withdrawal from Cambodia September 1989; population almost 80 million by the century's end against around 17 million in 1950; first stock exchange July 2000; trade agreement and Clinton's visit November 2000. ✅ Cambodia: Sihanouk proclaimed independence March 1945 and then bided his time; Geneva 1954 recognised independence; the Communist Party formed 1951; abdication March 1955; a landslide; head of state from 1960; fifteen years of authoritarian and benign rule with peace and reasonable prosperity. ✅ Neutrality destroyed by American bombing of border villages, the break of relations in May 1965, the 1967 uprising and his overreaction, the Nixon-Kissinger bombings, and the American-backed coup of March 1970 while he was in Moscow; Lon Nol 1970-5; bombing tonnage in March-May 1973 more than double the whole previous year although the two states were not at war; Khmer Rouge took Phnom Penh April 1975. ✅ Democratic Kampuchea: cities emptied, deaths on forced marches, immediate total collectivisation to double the rice harvest where even Mao had taken years; money, private property, shops and markets abolished; schools, hospitals and monasteries closed; genocide against the educated. ✅ Leifer: around a million died from execution, starvation and disease; some estimates put the killing-fields total as high as two million; just over a third of a population of 7.5 million disappeared. ✅ Philip Short's 2005 cultural explanation, against the recorded objection that Vietnam and Laos experienced war and terror at the same time and never attempted such a radical social solution. ✅ January 1979 Vietnamese intervention; Hun Sen installed; occupation until 1989; the regime unrecognised; Parsons on the draft resolutions of January and March 1979, vetoed by the USSR; the USA and non-communist south-east Asia feared a powerful Vietnam more than the Khmer Rouge. ✅ Three resistance groups - Khmer Rouge about 35,000, Sihanouk about 18,000, Son Sann's front about 8,000 - formed a United Nations-recognised government-in-exile in 1982; Hun Sen prime minister from 1985; October 1991 agreement; Supreme National Council with Sihanouk president; 16,000 United Nations troops and 6,000 civilians; June 1993 elections; Ranariddh first and Hun Sen second prime minister; monarchy restored; July 1997 coup against Ranariddh. ✅ Laos: drawn into the Vietnam War and subjected to indiscriminate American bombing; communist power at the end of 1975; collectives, state control, several thousand in re-education camps, around 10 per cent of the population leaving by some estimates; collectivisation abandoned in the mid-1980s for family-run farms following China and Vietnam; better GNP per head than Vietnam or Cambodia by 1989 on United Nations statistics; a new constitution in 1991; February 2002 elections with 166 candidates for 109 seats and a reported 100 per cent turnout; unrest in 2003; Choummaly Sayasone from 2006. ✅ The definitional question: with socialist economics abandoned and party monopoly retained, these governments could just as well be called simply one-party states.

## 21. China and Russia compared

⚠️ Formulation: the Bolsheviks took a state and then had to learn to govern it; the Chinese communists learned to govern and then took a state. ✅ Russian stages used only as boundary material: abdication 2 March 1917; seizure on the night of 25-26 October 1917; Civil War 1918-20; New Economic Policy 1921; Stalin's consolidation 1928-29; purges 1936-38. ⚠️ Four differences: seizure against a twenty-two-year protracted war; urban theory applied against urban theory corrected into a rural strategy; civil war after power against civil war for power; a world war that destroyed a state against a war that damaged an incumbent and legitimised a challenger. ⚠️ Genuine parallel: single-party monopoly and organisational model, which the Nationalists also adopted. ⚠️ Dangerous parallel: collectivisation and famine - different evidence, estimates and mechanisms. ✅ Clearest divergence: the Soviet heavy-industry model adopted in 1953 and repudiated in 1958; doctrinal opposition after 1956; opposite reform outcomes in 1989-91. ⚠️ Four false equivalences to refute: both peasant revolutions; China followed the Soviet model; both revolutions were exported; both ended the same way.

## 22. Answer architecture

⚠️ Eight recurring demands with their real tests; five master theses on 1949, the Mao era, Deng, Asian plurality and the bloc; mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20 marks; six verdict scaffolds; eighteen factual-risk controls; seven judgements that constitute command of the topic; and four boundaries - the Cold War theatres, European decolonisation, the post-1991 order and the definition of communism as a philosophy. ⚠️ The three costliest errors: unowned mortality figures, survey answers that mention many things and explain none, and verdicts that treat achievement and catastrophe as an arithmetic balance rather than as products of the same institutional capacity.

## Book evidence and its controls

|---|---|---|
| Norman Lowe, *Mastering Modern World History*, fifth edition, Chapter 19 "China, 1900-49" | The chapter summary; section 19.1 on revolution and the Warlord Era including the background to 1911, the 1911 revolution and the Twenty-One Demands; section 19.2 on Sun Yat-sen and Chiang Kai-shek including the Three Principles, Whampoa and the purification movement; section 19.3 on Mao and the party including the early years, the Juichin republic, the extermination campaigns, the Long March, the Snow quotation, the Jung Chang and Halliday controversy, the five numbered reasons for communist support, the Sian incident and the base-area figures; section 19.4 on the communist victory including China and the Second World War, the non-inevitability heading and the reasons for the triumph | ⚠️ The chapter is legible except for the illustration page and the page carrying the opening of the Sun Yat-sen section, whose optical extraction is unusable; the Three Principles themselves are legible on the following page and are used from there |
| The same work, Chapter 20 "China since 1949: the communists in control" | The chapter summary; section 20.1 on Mao's handling of China's problems including the problems of 1949, the agricultural changes with the Gray and Dikötter assessments and the Hebei quotation, the industrial changes, the Hundred Flowers campaign, the Great Leap Forward with its features, failures, mortality statements and long-term ledger, and the Cultural Revolution's origins and instruments; section 20.2 on the period after Mao including Hua's removal, the Service characterisation, the Gang of Four trial, the Party Resolution, the Smith assessment, the policy changes from June 1978, the Democracy Wall and modernisation with its problems; and section 20.3 on the 1987 crisis and Tiananmen, including the leadership's own justification of the crackdown; and the chapter's four end-of-chapter questions. ⚠️ **Section 20.4, "The changing face of communism in China", was read during source discovery and is deliberately NOT used.** It carries the chapter's post-1989 Chinese record - the growth of the 1990s, the Five Year Plan of March 1996, Deng's death in 1997, Jiang Zemin's Three Represents and the opening of the party to capitalists, the leadership changes of 2002-03, the economic and social record to 2012 and the handover to the next leadership - and every one of those items sits beyond the June 1989 boundary this topic observes. ⚠️ The rationale, the citation and the asymmetry with the Asian comparator cases are recorded in the open-gap entry below | ⚠️ Two pages of this chapter are blank in the extraction - the page carrying the political changes of the early 1950s and the page carrying the closing phase of the Cultural Revolution and the opening of the post-Mao section. ⚠️ A further page covering the square itself in 1989 is heavily degraded; only phrases legible with confidence are used, and the casualty figure read from it is reported as a damaged reading rather than as a primary figure |
| The same work, Chapter 21 "Communism in Korea and south-east Asia" | The chapter summary; section 21.1 on North Korea including the establishment of the regime, the one-state-or-two question, the unification record, the economic difficulties, life under Kim Jong-il and the nuclear confrontation to 2012; section 21.2 on Vietnam including the struggle for independence, the two Vietnams, the isolated Socialist Republic and the change of course under Doi Moi; section 21.3 on Cambodia from the Japanese occupation through Sihanouk, Lon Nol, the Khmer Rouge years with the Leifer, Short, Stanley and Grenville material, the Vietnamese intervention with the Parsons quotation, and the settlement to 1998; and the section on Laos with its definitional question | ⚠️ Four pages are blank in the extraction - the page covering the Korean War, the page covering the opening of the Cambodia section, and two pages covering the Cambodian elections after 1998 and the opening of the Laos section. ⚠️ The Korean War gap does not affect this topic's scope because that war is owned by the Cold War topic; the other three are recorded as open gaps below |

## Discrepancies found in the evidence base and how they were handled

| Discrepancy | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| **The dating of the Cultural Revolution.** The core account dates it 1966-9; both canonical owners date it 1966-76 | ⚠️ Both are taught in Lesson 14 section 3 with the phenomenon each dates - the mass upheaval and the political era to Mao's death - and neither is silently preferred. The register notes carry both |
| **Great Leap Forward mortality.** The core account reports statistics emerging later suggesting some 20 million premature deaths, especially in the famine of 1959-60, and then quotes Dikötter's at least 45 million between 1958 and 1962 | ⚠️ Both are stated in Lesson 13 with author and period, and the text expressly forbids merging them into a range. Neither is presented as the settled toll |
| **Land-reform violence.** Three incompatible accounts - an unattributed figure of as many as two million killed, Gray's 1970 judgement of minimum physical violence, and Dikötter's archival findings of considerable violence, torturing and execution | ⚠️ All three are reported in Lesson 11 with their authors and evidentiary conditions, and the lesson states explicitly that no defensible middle figure exists. The accompanying archival finding that official accounts exaggerated achievements is used to qualify the positive ledger elsewhere |
| **Tiananmen casualties.** The same work states in its chapter summary that the army killed hundreds, and in the Cold War chapter that at least a thousand students were killed and many later executed; the main narrative page, which is damaged, appears to give a range of between 1,500 and 3,000 | ⚠️ The two legible statements are reported side by side in Lesson 18 section 4 as a divergence within a single account. The damaged reading is disclosed with its limitation and is expressly not used as a primary figure. The canonical owners give no figure, which is also recorded |
| **Cambodian mortality.** Leifer is quoted as giving around a million dead from execution, starvation and disease; the account separately records that some estimates put the killing-fields total as high as two million and that just over a third of a population of 7.5 million disappeared | ⚠️ All three statements are reported in Lesson 20 Part B with their sources, and the lesson forbids selecting one as the toll or merging them with Grenville's figure for the war, which counts a different thing |
| **Responsibility for the Khmer Rouge atrocity.** Philip Short attributes it to Cambodian popular culture rather than to the leader or the ideology; the same account records that Vietnam and Laos experienced war and terror at the same time and never attempted such a radical social solution | ⚠️ Both are given in Lesson 20 Part B, with the comparative objection identified as decisive against the cultural explanation as a complete account, and a combined explanation offered instead |
| **Assessment of Chiang Kai-shek.** The account's own narrative is severe about corruption, inflation, looting and tactical error, while recording Jay Taylor's view that he governed with reasonable skill given the enormity of his problems | ⚠️ Both are given in Lesson 9 section 6, and the balance is stated as genuinely open, with the structural point that he inherited a state that had lost its capacity in the 1860s |
| **Formation of the Vietnamese communist party.** The core account states that the Vietnamese Communist Party was "formed by Ho Chi Minh in 1929" | ⚠️ The event taught is the unification of the rival Vietnamese communist groups into a single party in **February 1930**, an organisation renamed the Indochinese Communist Party later in the same year, with Ho as its principal organiser rather than its sole founder. ⚠️ The corrected date and the corrected description of Ho's role are used in Lesson 20 Part A, in Maps 1 and 5, in the cumulative checks, in the sixth Mains model answer and in register note 20. ❌ No 1929 antecedent organisation is asserted, because the account names none and none is independently held here |
| **Ordinal of the Vietnamese party congress of December 1986.** The core account calls it the "Third National Congress of the Communist Party" | ⚠️ The congress that appointed Nguyen Van Linh general secretary and adopted Doi Moi in December 1986 is standardly the **Sixth National Congress of the Communist Party of Vietnam**, so the account's ordinal cannot be right. ⚠️ Because the ordinal carries no examinable weight and the corrected ordinal is not independently held in this session's bounded source set, the teaching text names the body neutrally as the party's national congress of December 1986 and the divergence is recorded here rather than resolved in the lesson |
| **What the Nixon visit of February 1972 achieved.** The core account states that the meeting "led to a resumption of diplomatic relations", while the same account records Carter giving formal recognition and exchanging ambassadors early in 1979 | ⚠️ The two statements cannot both describe the same step, and the second is the one the account develops. ⚠️ Lesson 16 section 7 therefore teaches February 1972 as the reopening of contact and rapprochement between governments hostile since the Korean War, and records that formal diplomatic relations took effect on **1 January 1979**. ❌ No claim that 1972 restored full diplomatic relations appears anywhere in this session, and a trap entry warns against it |
| **Location of Hunan province.** The core account places Mao's birthplace "in south-east China" | ⚠️ Hunan is in **south-central** China. Lesson 5 section 2 and register note 5 use the corrected description; the divergence is recorded here. ⚠️ The point is not merely geographical: the later rural strategy depended on interior and border-province terrain rather than on the south-eastern coastal belt |

## The single bounded material-fact pass

⚠️ One verification pass was conducted for this session on 29 September 2026, limited to a finite list of standard connecting names and dates fixed before searching. ⚠️ The list was declared as: the May Fourth Movement's date and its immediate trigger at the peace settlement; the standard designation and span of the First United Front; and the standard designation and span of the Second United Front. ⚠️ Each was needed because the extraction of the core account is damaged at exactly the places where the intellectual awakening of 1919 and the formal naming of the two alliances would appear, and their omission would have left a real syllabus gap in Lessons 3, 5 and 9. ⚠️ The minimum reliable source set was used, the pass stopped when the declared list was resolved, and no other item was pursued.

| Item verified | Where used | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The May Fourth Movement takes its name from student protests in Beijing on 4 May 1919, triggered by the peace settlement's transfer to Japan of Germany's former rights in Shandong rather than their return to China; China did not sign the treaty | Lesson 3 section 3; Map 1; register note 3 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account preserves only a later reference to May Fourth supporters and the page carrying the movement itself is unusable |
| The First United Front between the Kuomintang and the Chinese Communist Party, 1924-1927, directed at ending warlordism, collapsing in 1927 | Lesson 5 section 3; Map 1; register note 5 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account describes the co-operation and its collapse without naming the arrangement a question would refer to |
| The Second United Front, 1937-1945, formed to resist the Japanese invasion and persisting with mutual distrust until the end of the war | Lesson 9 section 3; register note 9 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed for the same reason as the first front |

⚠️ **Items identified but deliberately not pursued:** the remaining four groups of the Twenty-One Demands; the detailed content of the political changes of the early 1950s; the closing phase of the Cultural Revolution including the suppression of the Red Guards and the Lin Biao episode; the Korean War; the opening of the Cambodian section and the Cambodian elections after 1998; and the opening of the Lao section. ⚠️ Each is either owned by another topic or recorded below as an open gap rather than filled by inference or memory.

## Open gaps recorded rather than filled

1. ⚠️ **The Twenty-One Demands.** The extraction becomes unusable immediately after the first of the five groups. Lesson 2 states the first group and the pattern the demands illustrate, and records the remainder as unavailable. ❌ No reconstruction from memory is offered.
2. ⚠️ **Political changes of the early 1950s.** The page carrying the account of the new party-state's political consolidation is blank in the extraction. Lesson 11 uses the canonical owners' record - land reform and reconstruction with tighter one-party control, the weakening of landlord power and the rapid expansion of the party-state - and records the gap.
3. ⚠️ **The closing phase of the Cultural Revolution.** The page carrying the end of the campaign is blank. Lesson 14 therefore does not narrate the suppression of the Red Guards, the role of the army or the sequence leading to the Lin Biao episode, and uses only what the surrounding legible pages and the canonical owners supply, including the Lin Biao clique as named in the Party's own Resolution.
4. ⚠️ **The 1989 square narrative.** The page carrying the main account of April to June 1989 is heavily degraded. Lesson 18 uses the clean chapter summary, the clean Cold War chapter and the phrases legible with confidence, and discloses the degraded casualty reading rather than relying on it. ⚠️ Two further items on that page are not legible with confidence and are therefore not asserted anywhere in this session: the exact dates of Gorbachev's Beijing visit, which is recorded only as taking place in mid-May 1989 and as marking the formal Sino-Soviet reconciliation; and the reported size of the occupation of the square, which is recorded only as the square and surrounding streets being occupied.
5. ⚠️ **The Korean War.** The page carrying the account of the war is blank. ⚠️ This does not affect coverage, because the war is owned by the Cold War topic and Lesson 19 hands it over explicitly; the gap is recorded for completeness.
6. ⚠️ **The opening of the Cambodian section.** The page introducing Cambodia before the Japanese occupation is blank. Lesson 20 Part B begins from the legible record at 1945 and records the gap.
7. ⚠️ **Cambodian politics after the 1998 elections and the opening of the Lao section.** Two pages are blank. Lesson 20 carries Cambodia to the 1998 elections and uses the chapter summary for the opening of the Lao case, recording both gaps.
8. ⚠️ **Quantitative series of any kind for the reform era.** No growth, output, trade, population or poverty series is asserted beyond the specific dated figures the sources state - the 1984 trade figures, the 1986 and 1988-9 inflation rates, the 1979 record grain output and Deng's stated 1986 projections. ❌ No current figure of any kind is given.
9. ⚠️ **Russian comparative evidence.** Lesson 21 uses only the dated stages and stated mechanisms recorded by the cross-topic owner. ❌ No Russian mortality figure, policy detail or narrative is asserted, and no Russian and Chinese figures are combined.
10. ⚠️ **Post-1989 China: a deliberate bounded omission, not an oversight.** ✅ The core account's section 20.4, "The changing face of communism in China", supplies a substantial post-1989 Chinese record running to 2012. ⚠️ It is not taught here, and the decision is bounded rather than accidental, for three reasons. ⚠️ First, ownership: the canonical owner's boundary statement assigns the post-1991 order to the end-of-Cold-War topic, and the advanced owner's own current-link section is explicitly marked as continuity material rather than history; importing a large post-1989 Chinese lesson would annex material this topic does not own. ⚠️ Second, stability: the session's date-and-status discipline forbids asserting current Chinese economic, demographic, military or leadership facts, and almost all of section 20.4 consists of exactly such time-bound indicators, several of which were already dated when the account was written. ⚠️ Third, argumentative sufficiency: the topic's post-Mao thesis is that economic liberalisation was deliberately separated from political liberalisation, and that thesis is fully established by the material to June 1989; the later record illustrates the thesis but does not test it. ⚠️ **The asymmetry with Korea, Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos is deliberate and is required by the owners rather than chosen for convenience.** ✅ The two canonical owners define three of those cases partly by what happened to them after 1989: the basic owner records that North Korean centralisation and one-party control **endured** and that Laos's takeover was **later moderated by pragmatic economic adjustment**, and the advanced owner records Vietnam's **later market reform under party control** as the feature that makes it resemble China. ✅ For the fourth, Cambodia, the core account itself carries the record to the United Nations transition, the elections of 1993 and the restored monarchy. ⚠️ Their post-1989 endpoints are therefore constitutive of the comparison this topic owns, whereas China's post-1989 record is not. ⚠️ Lesson 22 states the same boundary in learner-facing terms without citing sections.
